title: The Thread Seers: Book One
author: Le Viet Hong
date: 2026-10-08
---

# Acknowledgments

This book exists because of threads—shimmering, unbreakable—that bind me to those I love most.

To my parents, who believed from the beginning. Your unwavering support became my strongest thread, golden and true.

To Katie, my partner in every adventure—thank you for standing beside me through challenge and celebration alike. The threads between us pulse brighter with each passing day, weaving patterns I never imagined possible.

To Mia and Lana, my brilliant daughters who teach me to see the world through wonder-struck eyes—your curiosity, creativity, and boundless joy are woven into every page. May you always follow the silver threads of your dreams, wherever they lead.

And to you, dear reader, for lifting this book and joining the luminous thread that connects us all through story. Welcome to the Weave.

# PROLOGUE: SAIGON, 1943

The bullet passed close enough that Mei-Hua felt the draught of it on her ear.

She did not move. The threads had told her a heartbeat before: hot, quick, a scarlet line running from the second storey of the warehouse across to the roof of the Customs House opposite, and along that line an intention with a shape she had learned to read the way other people read a mouth.

*He is going to shoot.*

"Hold," she said. "He has not fired yet."

Zhang, flat against the crates beside her, did not ask what she meant. In eleven weeks of working together he had stopped asking. He had also stopped suggesting that she was exaggerating, which she valued more.

Below them the Saigon River moved the colour of a bruise. Two patrol boats idled at the quay. On the far bank, under tarpaulins the size of ballroom floors, the cargo was stacked and labelled and starving. Somewhere up on Hậu Giang Street the evening market was closing, and the smell of it — shrimp paste, burning sugar, jasmine — came across the water mixed with the sharper stink of the rice that was no longer in the paddies.

"Third time this month," Zhang said. "They've taken the delta's whole allocation."

"I know."

He shifted, and the boards gave him. Mei-Hua put a hand flat on the wood without looking, feeling for the seam, and found it, and was grateful for the finding because her hands had started to shake and she needed something to be competent about.

"Third time," he said again, differently. "And the fourth time they'll send the manifest to Tokyo."

She turned her head a few degrees. On the water below, a knot of black-silver light ran from the Customs House down to a coaster tied at the second pier. It was thick as rope and it moved wrong: not the slow breathing of a bond between two people who had agreed to be bound, but the taut, purposeful pull of a line being paid out over a spool. Everything on it was being carried one way and nothing was being permitted to travel back.

"Master Nguyễn's contacts in the General Association got hold of something in August," she said. "They gave me the shape of it. I wanted to see it for myself."

"Can you see now?"

"Yes."

"Then tell me what you see, and let us both be frightened in the same direction."

She almost liked him for that.

---

The threads had two names in her family, and her grandmother had been very clear about the difference.

*Connections* — 线 (xiàn) — ran between people. Between a daughter and a kitchen, a teacher and a classroom, a girl and a boy who had not yet managed to say a thing out loud. They were visible. Everyone in the trade could see them; some people saw them without trying.

*Currents* — 势 (shì) — belonged to places. They were the accumulated weight of a neighbourhood's living: who had been born there, who had died there, who had eaten and quarrelled and gone to the temple on the same street for four hundred years. They were not visible. They were not, she thought, exactly audible. They were the pressure of a place on your skin when you stood in it and knew it.

Threads could be pulled. Currents could not. That was the whole of her training and the whole of her argument, and she had lost it more times than she could count.

She closed her eyes and put her thumb and two fingers on the crease of her left palm, and breathed in through the nose.

*In. Three. Out through the teeth.*

Her grandmother had walked up behind her once, when she was nine, and tapped her sternum with the flat of a wooden spoon — *again* — and had not said a word, and had gone back to her tea. Fourteen years, and she could still feel the tap.

She opened her eyes.

The world arrived in layers.

The nearest layer was the small and the personal: Zhang's pulse in his throat, quick and stubborn; her own hands; the pouch at her hip where the tools were; the woman asleep two streets over whose thread to her grandson had gone thin as wet paper in the last fortnight. Below that, braided through the whole quarter, the current of Hậu Giang Street — not a sound but a leaning, the way a room leans when a lot of weight is in one corner. It had been leaning since March.

Under all of it, and this was the part that frightened her, the mains.

"You see the cord," she said. "The black one, from the Customs House to the ship."

"I see nothing."

"A cord the colour of a knife gone dull. It's carrying the manifest and it's carrying the rice allocation and it is not carrying anything back." She swallowed. "It is being *paid out*. Somebody is drawing on it, and everything they take, they keep. Nothing comes back up the line. Do you understand what that means? It means the harvest is not shipping. It is being consumed where it is grown."

Zhang was quiet for a moment. "Then cut it."

"No."

"Then what?"

---

She took out the pouch.

It had been her mother's, and her mother's mother's, and the box inside it was older than the Republic. Her mother had embroidered the outside in blue silk at a time when blue silk was a serious decision, and she had done it in a single unbroken line, and it had taken her four years, and she had died before the pouch was finished.

Inside: a jade needle, worn smooth along one edge by her grandmother's thumb. A coil of red silk, unwaxed, undyed by anything but age. And a small knot of red silk, wound and re-wound so many times it had gone into a shape like a closed fist, which her grandmother had put in her hand on her sixteenth birthday and said nothing about at all.

*Choices that cannot be taken back*, her grandmother had said, when Mei-Hua finally asked. *You will know which ones they were.*

"There is a way to do this that does not announce you," Mei-Hua said. "But you have to hold very still inside, and you are not a person who holds still."

"I can hold still."

"You held still for four counts yesterday and then you told the fish seller his account was wrong."

"That was true."

"That is not the point."

She drew the needle out. The jade was cool and it had a weight that belonged to a smaller hand.

Zhang shifted his weight onto his other foot. "Tell me the plan. In order. Quickly."

"I put a fork in the line." She unwound about a forearm's length of the red silk. "The manifest still goes where it is going. The ship still sails. But a copy of the flow — an echo of it — comes down my silk instead, into the water, and along the water to the General Association, because the current on Hậu Giang runs out to the river and the current is willing to carry it if I ask properly. That is the part that takes the work. The silk is easy. The current is not."

"And the Japanese?"

"They will feel it by morning. The pattern will not sit still. Their own thread-walkers will know something has been done to that cord before the sun is up."

"Then we go now."

"We go after I finish. If I go now, the fork is half made and a half-made fork is worse than no fork at all." She looked at him. "Fifteen minutes. Count them for me and tell me when you are at eight."

---

It is not a large thing, to describe.

She knelt with the needle in her right hand and the silk in her left and she did not cut anything. Cutting would have been quicker and cutting would have been a declaration: a snapped line, a shock that ran backwards up the cord and lit up every ward the Japanese had laid between the river and the harbour, and every person on Hậu Giang Street would have felt the sky jerk like a held rope.

Instead she persuaded.

*You are carrying a list. You are only carrying a list. A list can be copied. Here is a second road. It goes to the same place. It is smaller and it is quieter and nobody has to know.*

The silk was old and the spool was old and she had soaked the thread in the herbs her grandmother used, and once a month she pricked her palm for it, because a thing you have paid for in blood will hold an intention you did not mean. The Western academies would have called that unscientific. Dr. Weber, in the good years, had said it was the most rigorous thing he had ever seen, and then had spent twenty minutes trying to write down what she was doing in a form he could submit somewhere.

She whispered in the dialect of the quarter — not a language, exactly; a way of putting the tones so the meaning leaned the right way — and the red silk began to pulse once, twice, and then at her own heartbeat, and the black-silver cord did not break.

It *bent*.

That was the whole trick, and the whole risk. A cord under tension will carry a great deal further than it should along a line you have introduced, if you are not careful, because a cord under tension wants to be straight more than it wants to be true. She felt it happen. She felt the Japanese have built that line to be efficient, and efficiency meant it took the path of least resistance, and she had just made a path of least resistance that went somewhere else.

Sweat ran into her hair. The current under her knees shifted, resentful, the way a neighbour shifts when you lean on their wall.

*Please,* she thought, and it was not addressed to the silk. It was addressed to Hậu Giang Street. *Please carry it. You have carried worse than a piece of paper for us.*

And the quarter, which had been leaning since March, leaned very slightly the other way.

---

"Eight," Zhang said.

She tied the last knot. She was aware of doing it and then she was aware of having done it, which is not the same thing and is usually the last useful sensation available to anyone doing this kind of work.

*It is done,* she thought. *But it is not as I meant it.*

The fork held. That was the good news. The echo was running — she could feel it going out under the river, thin and cold, toward the General Association, and somewhere up on Hậu Giang Street somebody who had been getting up every morning for two years to a market with nothing in it felt the current under her feet ease by a fraction, and did not know why, and slept a little better.

The bad news was in her hands. She had asked the cord to carry a copy and it had carried a *tone* as well, and the tone was still in the red silk, and it was not subtle, and it was hers.

"They will know by morning," she said. "Not what. Where. The disturbance will sit in the local current like a dropped stone."

"Then we have tonight."

"Then we have tonight," she agreed, and put the needle away, and put the red fist of a knot back in the pouch where it belonged, and noticed for the first time that her grandmother's hands in the embroidery, if you looked at them from this angle, were holding exactly the same thing.

---

They went out along the wall the way they had come in.

Mei-Hua did not look back at the coaster. She looked up, instead, at the roof of the warehouse where the thread had told her somebody was standing, and she was not surprised to see him, and she was very frightened, which is a different thing and which the years had taught her to keep in its own place.

He was tall. He was European. The lines around him were arranged the way they are arranged in a man who was once interested in something and then became efficient at it instead.

"Hello, Dr. Weber," Mei-Hua said.

She felt the shape of what he was doing to the mains under Hậu Giang before he had raised a hand. It was the same hand, and the same terrible competence, and the warmth that had once been in it had been taken out and replaced with something that worked.

*He is not doing this for the Japanese,* she thought, and the thought was worse than if he were. *He is doing it because he has decided that what he understands is more important than what is there.*

"We should warn the elders," Zhang said, when they were two streets down and the water was out of earshot. "All of them. Tonight."

"Yes."

"About the new thread-walkers. About the machines."

"About all of it."

"Will they listen?"

Mei-Hua thought about her grandmother, who had listened to a young German man for two years and had been right to.

"They will listen," she said. "They will not be quick. That is the other half of it, and it is the half that matters, and nobody ever warns you about it."

They turned into a lane that smelled of jasmine and hot sugar and the particular dust of old plaster, and the thread behind her — the one that connected a man on a roof to a woman standing in a lane, and which had been, for one moment, the most honest thing in the whole night — thinned and thinned and did not break.

It never broke. That was the trouble with it. It just got thinner, and thinner, and after a while there was nothing left on his side of it at all.

---

*Seventy years later, in a kitchen on the far side of the world, a girl of sixteen would sit at a table with a charcoal pencil and a bowl of jasmine tea, and an old woman who had her father's eyes would tell her that the silk threads ran through all things, and that the deep currents ran under the places themselves, and that both of these were true, and that the second one was the one the schools had thrown away. She would not believe her at first. She would believe her by the end of the afternoon. It would take her the rest of her life to work out what the believing had cost, and who had been asked to pay it.*

*The girl had a great-grandmother on her mother's side, and that woman had done a very small version of this, in a lane not far from here, on a night when the rice was being taken and nobody could stop it. She had taken a fork in a line she was not strong enough to cut, and asked a street to carry the copy. It had worked. It had also told the enemy exactly where the street was, and it cost her thirty-one years of a life she would have preferred to keep, and four people she loved who are not in this story.*

*Neither version is the whole of what happened next.*

# Chapter 1: The Bridge

*Tuesday 27 August. Westbrook General, then the house on Ellery Street.*

The bell at Westbrook High had a flat, unhappy note, and the hallway was still ringing from it when Madison Stone put her shoulder into Lyra Chen's.

Not hard. That was the thing about Madison — she calibrated. Lyra's sketchbook went down on the linoleum and slid under a bank of lockers and the pages fanned out, and half the corridor stopped to look, because half the corridor had been looking most of the day.

"Sorry," Madison said. "Didn't see you."

She had a way of saying *sorry* that made it a small door held open onto a larger room.

Lyra knelt and gathered the pages. She did not look at Madison, because looking was how it got worse. What she could see without looking was worse enough: a dense, knotted snarl of grey-white running from Madison's collar to the inside of Madison's wrist, and threaded through it, thin and steady and carrying on regardless, a filament of the same colour heading down the corridor toward a boy called Zach who was pretending very hard not to notice any of this.

*She's hurting him,* Lyra thought, and then, because she was sixteen and honest with herself in the privacy of her own skull: *He's letting her. That's not the same thing.*

"Okay," said a voice behind her. "I've got the rest."

Katie Doyle crouched down beside her and started picking up pages, and Lyra noticed, as she always did, without being able to stop noticing, that the space between the two of them was full of something. Not a colour she had a name for. A braided, warm, slightly fraying thing, gold at the middle, and thinning at both ends.

"Show-off," Katie said, holding out a corner of the sketchbook. "You draw people like they're made of wire."

"It's charcoal."

"It's not the medium, you know what I mean." Katie straightened up, dusting her knees. "Zach's over by the water fountain pretending to read. Are you going to say something to him, or are you going to do that thing you do?"

"Do what thing I do?"

"You tell me you do nothing, and then you do something."

Lyra's face went hot. "I don't do anything."

"You fixed his and Katie's — okay, not yours and mine, *mine and his* — you fixed the thing between us last month. In here." Katie touched her sternum, in the place where people put a hand when they mean *here*. "He was going to transfer schools over it, and now he won't shut up about my eyes. You said you did nothing."

"Things fray," Lyra said carefully. "They fray and sometimes they mend back on their own. Sometimes."

"Uh-huh." Katie looked at her for a moment too long, in the way that Ms. Rivera had started looking at her too, and Lyra decided, for the hundredth time, that she was imagining it. "Sorry about your dad. My mom said he's back at the hospital."

"He got home."

"He got back to the hospital." Katie's mouth went flat. "Lyra. That's a different sentence."

---

The phone rang at two in the morning and she was awake before the first ring finished, which she did not examine at the time.

"Miss Chen? Your father collapsed in the waiting room. You should come. Now."

---

The drive blurred. She was sixteen and had never driven, and she got on the wrong side of the car twice, and the whole time the thing in her chest was making a noise she had no word for.

*This is the night it's real,* she kept thinking. *This is where I find out if I've been doing this to him.*

She had been asking herself that question for two years and she had no way to ask it out loud, because the sentence *Dad, I think my brain is hurting yours* is not a sentence a person can say to her father.

What she had instead was this: for two years she had drawn people the way other people drew scaffolding, and the drawings had got worse — not less accurate, *worse* — the lines thicker, the connections denser, the whole thing starting to look less like a drawing and more like a wiring diagram of something that was already built. And for two years, on and off, her father had had headaches. And she had put the two facts side by side in her head, where she looked at them a great many times a day, and had never once been able to make the join.

The hospital had the smell of every hospital. She got lost twice on the way up. A woman in scrubs took her arm at the lift and said *third floor, honey, he's in 312* in the voice people use when they are being kind on purpose, and Lyra understood, immediately and completely, that people used that voice at funerals and not anywhere else.

He was smaller than he had any right to be.

That was the first thing. Everything else — the monitors, the woman with the clipboard, the strip of tape on his forearm — was furniture, and she could look past it. But Wei Chen had always taken up slightly more space than his actual body warranted, the way very tall people do, and in that bed he took up exactly as much space as his body warranted and no more.

There was dried blood under his nose. She sat down on the plastic chair because her legs had made the decision for her.

His eyes were open. They did not focus at first. Then they did, and they went to her face, and something happened in them that she had never seen before and did not know how to read, and it took her a moment to identify it because she had never seen it directed at her before.

*Guilt.*

"Dad—"

"Lyra." His voice was scraped out. "The headaches. I need to tell you about the headaches."

She put her hand on his, and the hospital noise went out of the room.

She could see it. She had been able to see it for months and had told herself she was imagining it: the silver-grey line between them, the one that had been the warmest and steadiest thing in her life since she was old enough to notice such things. It was not dim. It was *thin*, and thinning, and along the thin part there were dark places where the light simply stopped — gaps, like missing teeth — and at each gap the line buzzed with a fine, high, wrong sound that she had been hearing for a year and had finally let herself name as a sound.

*Shadows that move wrong. Lights that shouldn't be there.*

He was seeing them too.

"It started when you began drawing those connections," he said. "Didn't it."

It was not a question, and it was not an accusation, and that was the worst part. He had assembled this a long time ago, on his own, in a room where nobody was drawing anything, and had been carrying it alone, and had not said a word.

"How long?" she said.

His mouth did something complicated.

"Weeks," he said. "At first. Months, I told myself." He closed his eyes. "Last night it was days."

The monitor beeped. Somewhere down the hall a machine announced something in a voice designed not to worry anybody.

"Dad—"

"Promise me one thing." He got his hand up, a small effort, and she understood that it had cost him. "Don't sit here and decide this is your fault. I don't know that it is. I want you to hear me say that I don't know, because in about four minutes somebody in a white coat is going to tell you a great deal more than I can, and I want it to start from the right place."

Lyra discovered that she had put her hand over her mouth.

Outside the window, the parking lot was the colour of wet slate, and somewhere down at the far end of it a car alarm was going off in the specific stupid rhythm of a car alarm, and a nurse walked past with a coffee cup, and the ordinary world carried on being the ordinary world at the exact moment the ground came out from under it. Lyra looked at it, and away, and at it again, and understood that this was what her life had been doing for two years, and that she had only just now caught up with it.

"Okay," she said.

"Okay?"

"Okay. Not yet." She put her chin on the edge of the bed so that she was level with him, which was undignified, and she did not care. "But I'm going to be there when you find out. I'm going to be there for all of it. Whatever it turns out to be, I'm not going to be somewhere else."

Wei Chen turned his head on the pillow and looked at his daughter, and the dark places along the line between them did not get any better, and he said, "You are a strange girl, and I am extremely glad you are mine," which from her father was the equivalent of a parade.

---

She did not sleep.

At ten past four the neurologist who had been dealing with Wei Chen for two years came in and introduced herself as Dr. Ngozi Ekwueme of Westbrook General and Lyra said, automatically, that her father had already seen somebody, and the woman said, patiently, that she was the one, and had been for two hours.

"How much do you know about what he can see?"

"More than he thinks I do."

"Then you can save us both some time." She pulled up a chair, which Lyra would think about later, because it was the small hours of a Tuesday, and this woman had sat down next to a frightened teenager instead of standing over her. "Do you see threads, Miss Chen? Coloured lines between people, and places, and objects?"

Lyra's whole body went hot and cold at once, the way it had been doing about twice a week for a year.

"Yes," she said.

"Thank you." Ekwueme did not move, did not soften, did not reach for her hand. "And how long?"

"Since I was little. Faintly. Properly — about two years. It's been getting — " She stopped.

"It's been getting louder. Yes. That's the word I'd have used." Ekwueme turned a tablet around. "Your father started with the same thing. The visual cortex and the temporal lobe, here and here, are running hot. Not damaged. Working too hard. He has been interpreting information his brain was never built to handle, continuously, for years, and it is using energy it doesn't have a budget for."

"That's what I was afraid of," Lyra said. "That I did it to him."

"That's a very reasonable fear and it is not what this is." Ekwueme's mouth tightened a little, which Lyra later learned meant that she was about to be more careful rather than less. "This is called sympathetic thread bleed. When a seer's perception runs, it creates feedback. Not a metaphor — feedback, in him, in his own brain, and he is close enough to you, and you to him, that the loop closes. In most people it gives headaches. Confusion. In some cases it gives seizures, and in some cases it gives this."

"How long does he have?"

Ekwueme did not answer immediately, and Lyra found that she had been counting the seconds.

"Without treatment, a few months. I want to be honest with you about how few." She put the tablet down. "There are things we can do. Manageable things. But the underlying problem is that your father is standing next to a live wire, and I cannot insulate him from it, because the insulation would have to be him, and he does not want that."

"What does he want?"

"He wants to be there when you find out what you are." Ekwueme stood up. "That is a real answer, by the way. People find it easier to believe in treatment than in the alternative. It is not a stupid thing to want."

Outside, the sky had gone the colour of a nickel. Somewhere on the floor below, a door opened and closed.

"How do I stop it?" Lyra said. "Being what I am — is there a way to turn it down, to make it quieter, so that—"

"Miss Chen." Ekwueme waited until Lyra looked at her. "You are sixteen. The person who is going to have to decide whether you turn it down is you, and you are going to be in a position to decide it better than anyone in this building, including me. What I am going to do is find you people who can teach you how to use it without spending him, and I am going to tell your father that if he refuses, I will overrule him, because he is not in a position to make decisions about his own neurological future this morning."

She was at the door when she turned back.

"He asked me one thing, and I said I would pass it on, and I am passing it on. He said: *tell her it is not a debt. If it is a debt, she will spend the rest of her life trying to pay it, and there are better uses.*"

And then she was gone, and the room made the noises a room makes, and Lyra Chen sat in the chair beside her father's bed with her hands flat on the blanket and did not cry, and noticed that the line between them was still there, thin as wire, dark in three places, and still warm.

---

The last time she saw Katie and Zach's thread do anything was in April, in the art room, and it was the only other time she had ever deliberately touched one, and she had never told anybody, and she thought about it more than she thought about almost anything.

They had been fighting since September. The thread between them had been fraying for months — she could see it from across a crowded room, the gold gone pale and full of little breaks — and on the day it happened, Katie was crying at the back of the room and Zach was pretending to work on a piece that had forty minutes of work left in it, and something in Lyra's chest simply *went*.

She did not decide to. That was the part she could never get past afterwards. There was no moment where she thought *I will fix this*, and then a moment where she did it. There was only the sensation of a tangle in her chest coming loose, and then Katie had stopped crying, and Katie had looked up, and Katie had gone very still, and then Katie had said, in a voice with no breath in it, *oh*.

She'd crossed the room on her knees. She'd put her hand flat on the desk next to Katie's drawing, and she had not touched Katie, and she had not touched the thread either, not exactly. She had said, out loud, the way you talk yourself through a door: *hold on, hold on, that's it, there, that's it.*

The line had run warm and thick and whole, and Katie had looked at her and said *what did you do*, and Lyra had said *nothing*, and Katie had said *you did something*, and that had been the end of it, because on the following Monday they were in the corridor outside the art room and Katie had said *Zach says sorry about the thing with the duffle coat* and Lyra had said *okay* and Katie had said *do you want to walk with me* and Lyra had said *yes*.

The thing that had got her was that it had worked.

Not *well*. Not completely. In the corridor that Monday, the line between them was still fraying in two places, and it was still gold, and it was thinner than it had been in September, and Lyra understood — she had understood at the time, standing in the middle of the art room with her heart going like a bird's — that she had not fixed anything. She had pulled two loose ends together and knotted them.

Katie and Zach got back together that year. They broke up twice more before they left Westbrook, both times by text, both times within three weeks, both times for reasons that had nothing to do with threads.

But they had got back together. That was the part that Lyra could not put down, and that she had never been able to explain to anybody, not even to herself, and certainly not to Ms. Rivera, who had cornered her at the end of that April and said, *You drew something true in that assignment, Lyra. Something true and not artistic. Come and see me when you're ready,* and had then, with what Lyra now understood as an almost unnerving restraint, not mentioned it again.

Two gold threads. One September. One April.

And her father, in Room 312, with dark places in him.

Lyra sat in the chair and looked at the places where the line between them had gone out, and thought, with a clarity that arrived fully formed and without comfort:

*I do not know what I am. But I know what it costs, and I know I have already started paying it, and I have been paying it in other people's lives without asking any of them.*

That was the morning everything turned over. It did not feel like turning over. It felt like a Tuesday, in a hospital, with bad coffee.

---

*Thread Resonance Note: Thread-sight confirmed, multi-spectrum, late onset. Sympathetic bleed confirmed in a first-degree relative. Prognosis: guarded. Referral: see attached. Recommend Academy intake evaluation.*

# Chapter 2: Father's Equations

*Wednesday 28 August. The house on Ellery Street.*

*Ba, there were green tea sachets in the cupboard above the kettle and only the ones he liked in the box, and Lyra had been putting the wrong ones in for two years and he had never once said so, and she understood on Sunday morning that this was how he said so.*

The rain came in off the porch and went along the window frames the way it did, and Lyra sat at the end of the kitchen table with charcoal in her hand and a stack of Ms. Rivera's first-year portfolios in front of her, and she was supposed to be marking them.

She had marked four. She had spent most of the last hour on the same page.

The problem was that the kitchen was doing the thing.

It was doing the thing everywhere, always — that was the part nobody warned you about, if nobody warned you, which nobody did, because there was no warning, there was just a girl who at some point between being five and being six had noticed that her father was a person and not a shape — but kitchens were where she lost herself. Every surface had a line running off it. The fridge had a grey one to the back door and a warm, complicated one to the toaster, because he had owned the toaster for eleven years and it had never once surprised him. The window had a long pale one going out to the fence. Her father's teacup, the blue one with the chip in the handle that he refused to replace, had a gold line into his chest that Lyra could pick up from the far side of the room, and that was the line.

The line to her father was the line.

It was silver-grey, which she had read somewhere meant *old and structural* — load-bearing, like a beam, which was a stupid word for it but the only one that had ever felt right — and it was wide, and it was steady, and it had a texture like the grain in a plank. Under the kitchen table it was doing the thing it did when he worked, which was to run hot and bright at the exact point where he was thinking, so that she could tell from across the room which of his problems he had hold of.

Right now it was bright at two points at once, and one of them was where his hand was, and the other one was further in, and Lyra had learned over two years to read the distance between those two points as a rough measure of how hard a problem was.

This one was hard. It had been hard since Thursday.

She put the charcoal down and dragged the portfolios towards her and did the honest thing, which was to grade a portfolio by Marco Senn, who was fourteen and had drawn a dog.

---

"You're doing the thing where you hold the pencil and don't move it."

"I do not do a thing."

"You do a thing with your mouth." Wei Chen pushed his glasses up. "It goes a bit flat at the corner."

She put the pencil down deliberately. "You have a headache."

"I have a Wednesday."

"You have a headache and you're doing the thing where the headache is a legitimate reason to work."

He laughed. It was a good laugh, and it cost him something, and he covered it by reaching for the teapot and finding it empty, and Lyra was on her feet before she had decided to be, filling the kettle at the sink with the specific bad temper of a person who has been given a job.

"Do you want the big pot or the small pot?" she said.

"The small one. It steeps longer."

"You always say that and then you drink it too fast and say it's bitter."

"That's because I like it bitter."

"It's not bitter, it's *strong*." She set the small pot down. "Ba, you were discharged eight hours ago."

"I was discharged eight hours ago," her father agreed, "and I would like to be permitted one morning of not being discussed."

The line between them buzzed along its whole length — not loudly, not the way it had in the hospital, where she had been able to hear the gaps in it over a nurse talking. But it was there, and she heard it, and so did he, because he flinched, minutely, in the middle of pouring, and Lyra filed that away with a kind of cold anger she had been getting better at.

She put the small pot on the hob. She sat back down. She pulled the portfolios towards her again, and she said, instead of the thing she had been saying for two days, "Can I ask you a real question?"

"You can ask me a real question."

"Not a doctor question. You can put the doctor down for a minute."

Wei Chen took off his glasses, which he only ever did when he was about to be careful, and cleaned them on the hem of his shirt, which never worked.

"Ask."

"Are you afraid of it?"

He put the glasses back on. "Yes."

"Okay." She had not expected it to be that easy and she had to sit with it a second. "Okay. That's — okay. I'm afraid of it too, so."

"Yes," her father said, "I imagine you are."

"Not of seeing." Lyra turned the charcoal over in her fingers. "Of the seeing *being me*. I get to be sixteen and frightened and have a bad week, and then there's this thing that I can't put down, and it doesn't get tired, and it doesn't go to bed, and it's not — it isn't a skill, Ba. Skills have off switches. This doesn't have an off switch, and it's in my *head*, and you can hear it, and I can hear you hearing it, and neither of us has said anything about that."

The kettle did the thing it did before it boiled, the small climbing hiss, and neither of them moved.

"That's the fairest description of it I've ever heard," Wei Chen said. "Including mine."

"What's yours?"

"Mine is that I've been married to a woman who could do it, and I spent eleven years being polite about it, and then she disappeared, and now our daughter can do it, and I have spent a year and a half pretending I did not know." He turned his cup a quarter turn on the table. "I have been polite about it, Lyra. I want you to know that I know what that was."

She looked at him. He had not said *your mother could do it* in five years. He had said *your mother was sensitive*, and *your mother had a rich inner life*, and once, at a barbecue in the year before, *your mother saw things*, in a voice she had recognised and had then wished she could unhear.

"You knew."

"I knew."

"When?"

"Before you were born. Before you were four. I have watched you draw since you could hold a crayon, Lyra, and I have watched you stop drawing at about the age of nine, and I worked out what that meant, and I decided that not saying it would be a kindness, and then you were fifteen and it stopped being a kindness and became a lie, and I got very good at it." He said this without any self-pity at all, which made it much worse. "I did not think it was going to cost me my life."

The kettle clicked off. Neither of them got up.

"Did Mum know you knew?" Lyra said.

"Your mother knew everything I knew. Your mother is the reason I knew anything at all."

---

She did get up, eventually, and pour the water, and steep it too long, and he drank it too fast, and neither of them commented, and it was the best thing that had happened in the kitchen in a fortnight.

Then he turned back to the laptop, and the bright point in the line between them moved, and Lyra, who had spent two years learning to pretend she was not doing exactly this, gave up and looked at the screen.

"Ba, that's not converging."

"It is converging. It is converging *slowly*, which is a different complaint."

"It's not slow, it's wrong." She pulled the chair round to his side of the table, which she was not supposed to do, and which he had stopped objecting to in about March. "You're treating it as a function of the two variables separately and then adding them. It's not that. When they interact, the whole curve changes shape. It's — okay, look. Look at this."

She drew it for him on the back of a portfolio sheet, in charcoal, fast, without looking at her hand.

Two dots. Four lines. She added four more that made it wrong on purpose, so that he would see it.

"You're not looking for a function," she said. "You're looking for a *web*. You've got two things and you're forcing them into a line, and they don't want a line, they're a — they're a network. Everything's connected to everything else and the amount of connection isn't the same on both sides. It goes up and down. Some pairs are strong and some pairs are almost nothing, and if you average them you get a number that doesn't mean anything."

Wei Chen had gone very still.

"Where did you learn graph theory?"

"I've been in your study for two years, Ba, you leave things *open*."

"Thirty-one seconds," he said.

"What?"

"Show me again. From the top. And don't let me interrupt you this time, because I want to hear where you were going before I start criticising your notation." He was already reaching for a clean sheet. "Go on. Slowly."

She went again. Slowly.

He interrupted her four minutes in, and she had been right about the web, and he was right about the notation, and they ended up at half past nine in the morning with tea going cold and two pages covered in a hybrid of his graph paper and her charcoal, arguing — properly, hard, on the evidence — about whether the interaction term should be weighted by duration or by intensity.

And it was the best two hours Lyra had had since the hospital, and she understood, sitting on the end of his table with charcoal dust on the side of her hand, that this was the thing she was actually frightened of losing. Not the sight. Her father, at the end of a kitchen table, being *interested*.

"I would like you to do more of this with me," he said.

"It's not rigorous."

"It is extremely rigorous. It is rigorous in a way I cannot currently check, which is a different and more interesting problem, and I intend to check it." He was writing. He did not look up. "Will you come in on Sunday afternoons? Bring the charcoal. Bring the bad notation. I will bring the coffee and we will be wrong together for two hours."

He still would not say it. She noted that. She let it go. It was a Wednesday, in a kitchen, and he had promised her Sunday.

---

The studio door was at the end of the hall, and it had been shut for five years, and there was no lock on it, and they had never once discussed this.

Lyra went down the corridor at four in the afternoon with the rain still going and stood in front of it and put her hand flat on the wood, which was cool, and which was exactly the temperature of the rest of the house, and which was not, she thought, the temperature of a door and nothing else.

*You are being dramatic,* she told herself. *You are sixteen and you have had no sleep and you have just spent the best morning of your life being argued with by your father, and you want to go and look at your dead mother's things.*

She opened it.

The air came out at her like something held. Dust, cold paper, and under it, faintly, linseed oil and the ghost of turpentine, and something else that she had no name for and had smelled perhaps six times in her life, and which was simply *her mother in this room*.

The easel stood where it had stood. There was a half-finished canvas on it — a hillside, mostly done, the bottom corner still bare primed board — and the bare corner had a clean edge on it, not a torn one, which meant she had put the brush down in the middle of a decision and had never come back.

The desk had a journal on it. Leather, soft, the corners gone white. A circle with radiating lines stamped into the front, blind, no colour.

Lyra did not open the journal.

Not because she had decided not to. She got as far as putting two fingers on the cover, and the room *answered*.

It was not a sound. It was closer to the way the kitchen felt in the mornings — pressure, warmth, a leaning — except that the leaning had a direction, and it went from the desk to the walls to the ceiling and back down again, and it was silver-blue, and it moved the way water moves in a pipe that is not quite full.

Memory, she thought. Nobody's, specifically. The room's.

She took her hand off the journal and the leaning stayed exactly as it was, patient, and did not follow her, and did not leave.

In the tray on the desk, under four dead brushes, there was a chain with a small silver pendant on it: a circle with lines running out from the centre in a spiral, the same design as the journal's cover, in metal that had gone dull. It was not jade. It was heavier than it looked.

Lyra did not pick it up either.

Instead she stood in the middle of her mother's studio and let herself look, properly, for the first time in five years, and she took in what was actually in the room: a working room, cluttered, warm, used, a chair with a cushion worn flat on one side, a stack of library books with the spines cracked and a receipt from a shop in Chinatown serving as a bookmark, a chipped mug.

Nobody dies in a room like that. That was the thought that arrived, unwanted, and the worst thought she had had all day.

*Somebody left in a hurry and was never coming back.*

The floorboard in front of the easel creaked.

"*Lyra?*"

He was in the doorway. He had a towel round his shoulders and his reading glasses in his hand and an expression on his face she could not read in one go.

"Ba, I — "

"How long have you had that door open?"

"Since Tuesday. Since I was five."

The colour went out of his face. He put his hand up onto the frame.

"Then it has been open for five years," he said, "and I have been closing it."

---

He did not shout. Lyra had been braced for shouting and got something much worse: a man standing in a doorway trying to get his breathing under control, and failing, and being visibly ashamed of failing.

"I asked you not to go in there."

"I know."

"Not because I didn't want you to. Do you understand that? I did not close that door to keep something from you. I closed it because I could not — " He stopped. He took his glasses off and pressed the heels of his hands into his eyes, which was a thing he had never once done in front of her. "I could not stand in it."

"But it's just *her stuff*, Ba."

"Yes," he said. "That is exactly what it is, and that is exactly why I cannot stand in it, and I have not stood in it since the eleventh of September five years ago, and you have just told me you have known about the room for eight years, which means I have been lying to you since you were five, and I need a moment, and I am not going to pretend that I have one."

He went and sat on the bottom stair, which is not a comfortable place to sit, and which he chose.

Lyra stood in the doorway of her mother's studio and looked at the back of her father's head and understood that she had, without any particular wish to do it, taken something from him. Not the room. The *not knowing*. He had spent five years closing a door so that he would not have to stand in it, and she had, without meaning to, taken away the only reason the door had stayed shut.

"I'm sorry," she said.

"Don't be. Not for that." He did not turn round. "Lyra, I want to be very clear about one thing, because I think you have made an inference and I think the inference is reasonable and I think it is wrong. I did not keep you out of that room to keep you from seeing threads. If I could have stopped you seeing threads I would have done it, and I would have felt like a hero, and I would have been a fool, and I would have lost you into the bargain."

"Then why?"

"Because the people who see what you see end up *alone*, Lyra. Your mother told me that when we met. She said: the sight is not the problem. The problem is that it puts a pane of glass between you and every other human being on the planet, and you get to watch all of them through it, and nobody gets to watch you." He turned the glasses over in his hands. "And I thought: not her. Not our daughter. Not while I have anything to say about it."

"And yet."

"And yet." He stood up. "And yet she walked into that room on the eleventh of September, five years ago, and told me she had found something, and I told her she was tired, and she went back to the studio and shut the door, and the next morning she was not in it, and the next morning she was not in the house."

The rain had got louder.

"Ba, that's not—"

"I know that is not what happened. I have a whole file on what happened. Police reports, a search that went for eleven months, a lawyer who took our money for two years." He came back up the last two stairs and stopped in front of her. "What I have never been able to explain, Lyra, and what I have never once said out loud in this house, is that I was *relieved*. That when the police finally stopped coming, and when the neighbours stopped asking, and when we moved from the house on Ellery Street and I put the studio behind me, I was relieved. And I have never forgiven myself for it, and I am telling you tonight because you have been in that room twice now and you are not going to stop, and you deserve to know what you are walking into before you walk into it."

She stood very still. The silver-blue leaning in the room behind her shifted, faintly, the way water shifts.

"What am I walking into?"

"That room, for a start." He almost smiled. "And there is a great deal more than that room, if you want the whole of it. Come out of there. Sit down. I am going to tell you the parts I can bear to say out loud, and then we are going to have an argument about the rest, and then — perhaps, if I can find a way to do it that is not a coward's way — I am going to tell you about the letter that came six days ago."

---

He did tell her about the letter that evening, and it took him an hour and forty minutes, and he got most of it right.

It had come to the university address, because the university address was on file, and it was cream paper with a seal he did not recognise, and it was addressed to *Miss Lyra Chen* and it said that her abilities — *her rare and remarkable perspective*, it said — were a gift deserving nurture, and that an institution existed which had been teaching the disciplined use of that gift since 1798, and that it would be visiting her town, and would be glad to speak with her and with her father, and that no obligation of any kind was implied by the receipt of this letter.

"They gave you my name," Lyra said.

"They gave your name, your school, and the date of the last time you were taken off campus for something that looked like a seizure." He said it evenly. "That last detail is the one I would like you to sit with for a moment, because I have been sitting with it for three weeks, and I have not been able to put it down."

Lyra looked at the letter. It was very well made. That was what she kept coming back to. It was the best paper she had ever handled, and the seal was a single line, a thread, passing through an eye, and it had been embossed rather than printed, and somebody had thought about the weight of it in somebody else's hand.

"What did you do with it?"

"I put it in the drawer under the phone bill I did not pay." He was quiet for a second. "I am not proud of that. I want to be accurate: I am not proud of it, and I did it anyway, and the reason I did it is that I could not work out what I wanted to happen, and a man who cannot work out what he wants will do almost anything to postpone finding out."

"Are you going to answer them?"

"I have already answered them. This morning, before you were awake. I telephoned the number on the letter and spoke to a young woman called Eleanor Weaver, who was extremely gracious, and who told me that there would be an informal gathering on Saturday at a bookshop in town, that families were welcome, and that no one would be approached without me present." He turned his cup. "I said we would come. I said it before I had decided to. I would like it on the record that I have been an absolute coward about this for three weeks and that I have now stopped."

Lyra looked at him for a long moment.

"You're not sending me by myself."

"No."

"You're coming."

"I am coming." He almost smiled again. "Lyra, I cannot do the thing you do, and I will never be able to do it, and I have spent two years pretending to believe you about it and I am extremely tired of pretending. But I can sit in a room and be extremely unwelcome and extremely unhelpful, and I can be the person in the corner who is not from their world, and there is a version of this where that is the single most useful thing in the room, and I intend to find out whether there is."

The rain had stopped. Through the window the street smelled wet and green and completely ordinary.

"Ba," Lyra said. "They know my name. They know the date of the thing at school."

"Yes."

"Should we be scared?"

Wei Chen considered this with the seriousness he brought to everything, and Lyra loved him for it, and was frightened alongside him, and the two things sat down next to each other and stayed there.

"I think," he said, "that we should be careful, and not frightened, and that the difference between those two things is going to be the entire subject of the next six months of my life." He got up, and put the letter in his pocket, and stopped in the doorway. "And Lyra. One more thing, and then we are having dinner and I am not discussing it."

"Okay."

"The woman on the telephone. She knew your mother's name."

The kitchen went very cold in a way that had nothing to do with the window.

"She asked about her," Lyra said.

"She asked about her by name, Lyra. Not 'the mother.' Not 'her.' A name." He looked at her for a long moment. "Your mother went to that place. I know that now. I have known it for four years and I have not said it in this house, and I am saying it tonight, and that is the last of it. I am not sure any more what I am protecting. Come and eat."

---

*Thread Resonance Note: Academy file C-2214. Prior enrolment of a first-degree relative (L. Chen, 2008–2009, partial) retrieved. Note appended by E. Weaver: recommend the girl be assessed before the Council schedule closes. File flagged for review by [REDACTED].*

# Chapter 3: Recognition

*Friday 30 August.*

The folder was on the kitchen table when Lyra came down, face down, squared at the edges, and her father was sitting at the other end of the table doing the thing he did with the newspaper, which was holding it at arm's length and moving his lips.

"You're doing the thing where you hold the pencil and don't move it," she said.

"I do not do a thing."

"You do a thing with your mouth. It goes a bit flat at the corner."

He put the paper down. "I telephoned the woman yesterday."

"You told me you had. You told me twice, and then you told me the number, and then you told me to be careful with the number, which is the part I have been thinking about for eighteen hours." Lyra put her bag on the chair and did not take her coat off. "Did she say yes?"

"She said the family was welcome. She said no one would be approached without you present. She said her name is Eleanor Weaver and she has been doing this for eleven years, and she said — " He stopped.

"Said what?"

"She said, and I am quoting, *Tell your daughter that the number on the board is a severity and not a percentage of anything.* And then she said she was sorry, and she put the phone down." Wei Chen took his glasses off. "I have been a university professor for twenty-one years, Lyra, and in twenty-one years nobody in a position of authority has ever said anything to me like that, and I do not know what to do with it."

Lyra stood in the kitchen in her coat and found that she was not sure whether she wanted to cry or to laugh, and that what she actually felt was something in between and much worse, which was *relief*, and the relief was so large that she had to put a hand on the table.

"So you're going," she said.

"I have not decided that."

"Bàba."

"I have decided that I am going to sit in a room and be extremely unhelpful," her father said, "and I have not decided anything else, and I would like the distinction observed."

---

She made him say it properly. That took forty minutes and she was faintly ashamed of how good she was at it, and he was faintly ashamed of how easily he was managed, and between them they got through the whole of it, which is the sort of thing that takes forty minutes when you are both being careful and about four seconds when you are not.

The first thing she established was the diagnosis. "Dr. Ekwueme's letter says sympathetic thread bleed. She says it means my head is doing something to yours. I want to hear you say it."

"I cannot say it. I have a mechanism, a name, and a mechanism is not the same as a fact about my own skull." He turned the folder over. "What I can say is that the two things started in the same month and I have never been able to unstart them."

"The second thing," said Lyra.

The second thing was that the letter was addressed to *Miss Lyra Chen* at the university address, and that her father had taken it out of the drawer under the phone bill he had not paid on four separate occasions between eight o'clock in the morning and half past five in the afternoon, and had read it, and had put it back, and had done the thing he did at the end of a bad day, which was to stand at the sink for eleven minutes with the tap off.

"So you knew on the twenty-second."

"I knew on the twenty-second."

"And you didn't tell me for eight days."

"No." Wei Chen did not look up. "And I would like to be permitted to say why, because I know how it is going to sound, and I would like to say it anyway."

"Go on."

"Because if I told you on the twenty-second, you would have started planning. And there is a version of you that is much better at planning than the rest of us, Lyra, and that version is the one that is frightened, and I have watched you get frightened at me for two years and I did not want to be the reason you became organised." He put his hands flat on the table, which she had never once seen him do. "That is the true reason. The other reason is that I did not want to have the conversation. I have never in my life not wanted to have a conversation, and last Tuesday I sat in a hospital and watched a woman tell my daughter that the thing about her is killing me, and I discovered that I would rather have the conversation on a Saturday than on a Friday evening, and that is a thing about me that I am not proud of and that you are entitled to.

Lyra thought about that for a moment longer than she needed to.

"Right," she said. "Okay. That's — okay. It's not fine, but I can work with it." She pulled out the third chair. "Now do the other thing."

"Which other thing."

"You told me on Thursday that you wanted three things from me before I went, and you only said one of them, and then the folder came out and I forgot." Lyra put her elbows on the table. "You've been holding something since Thursday and it's about my mother and I have watched you not say it for two days. Say it."

Wei Chen looked at his tea.

"She was at that school," he said.

The kitchen was very quiet.

"For how long?"

"Four months, in the spring of 2002. She was thirty-three and she was the best thing I have ever met and she was, for about four months, extremely happy, in a way I had not seen her be before and have not seen since." He said it carefully, the way you walk on a floor you know is bad. "She came home and she was not the same and she would not tell me what had happened, and I did not ask, because I did not want to be a man who asks his wife that at the end of a dinner party."

"What did she tell you?"

"Two things. She told me their method was wrong, and that she had argued about it and lost, and that she was going to do it her own way instead. And she told me — " He stopped, and started again, and the second time it came out of him sideways. "She told me that if anything ever happened to her, I was not to let anybody put a *method* on her. She was very specific. She said: *it will not be a monster, Wei. It will be a spreadsheet.*"

Lyra's hand went to her sternum, to bare skin. The jade was still in her grandmother's house, with everything else of her mother's, and the fact that Nai Nai had said nothing at all about any of it was somehow much worse than saying something.

"And then?" she said.

"And then, five years ago this September, she went into a chamber under that building by herself, on a night, and told nobody she was going, and the next morning she was not in the house." He said it without drama, which was how he delivered the worst things. "They searched for eleven months. I was the one who told her not to take the position. I have done the arithmetic on that every day for six years and it comes out the same every time and I would like it noted that the arithmetic is not the problem."

"That's the third thing," Lyra said quietly.

"Yes."

"What's the fourth?"

Wei Chen took off his glasses and cleaned them on his shirt, which never worked, and which Lyra had watched him do perhaps four hundred times in her life and had never once seen fail to be the thing he did instead of crying.

"There is not a fourth," he said. "I have three and I have said two of them and I am going to go and stand at the sink now, and you are going to sit at that table and be sixteen at me for a bit, and then we are going to have eggs."

---

She was sixteen at him for eleven minutes.

She got the third condition out of him — the one about not doing it for him — and she heard it, and she did not agree to it, and she told him so, and he let her, and something in the kitchen got easier by about four per cent. Then she got as far as the practical part and hit the wall.

"Chợ Lớn."

"Yes."

"You emigrated when you were four."

"I emigrated when I was four. My mother was born there. My grandmother was taught there, in a house on a street called Hậu Giang, by a woman who was the grandmother of the woman who taught me." Nai Nai's chain sat on the table between them, coiled. "That is a very long sentence and I have never said it out loud to anybody, and I have been waiting since Friday to see whether I could."

"And Lin knew that. Lin knew all of that, and she was taught *here*—"

"Here, in this kitchen, by a woman with a jade needle and a temper, from the age of six, because her mother and my mother were the kind of friends who leave their children with each other." He turned the chain over with one finger. "Lyra. I want to be extremely careful here, because I can feel you already arriving somewhere, and I would rather you did not arrive there at speed."

"I'm arriving somewhere."

"I know. Everyone does. The family has a great many people in it who see things, and all of them ended up in the same building, and the building is the reason your mother is not in this kitchen." He looked at her. "Chợ Lớn is not where the gold is. It is where your family is. Those are different sentences and I would like them kept apart for a while."

Lyra looked at the window for a while.

"So the map is wrong," she said.

"Your mother's map is *her mother's map*, and it was drawn in about 1996, and it says north, near Canada, and it does not say why, and it does not say when, and there is a line through 'near' in her own hand as though she had argued with herself about it." He was going through the folder now, and the folder had a photocopy in it, and he had evidently had the photocopy made himself, at a copy shop, on his lunch, which was the single most likeable thing she had learned about her father in two years. "I have looked at it for eight days. I am not a person who sees anything. I looked at it for eight days and I could not tell you whether it means anything at all, and the reason I could not tell you is that I do not know what I am looking at, and I have spent my entire professional life resenting that kind of sentence."

"Then what do you think it is?"

"I think," her father said, "that your mother went looking for something, and that she told your grandmother about it in a panic, and that the person she trusted to help her find it is the person who runs the school, and that either she found it and told them and it was suppressed, or she did not find it and it frightened her into a chamber on a night when nobody knew where she was." He squared the folder. "Those are the two available stories. In the first one she is a martyr and I am a fool who kept her home. In the second one she is a woman who was frightened and made a bad decision alone, and I am a fool who kept her home, and the difference between them is nine years old and I do not get to find out."

---

At four o'clock she went out to the gate to meet him coming home, which she had not done since she was about nine and which neither of them mentioned.

"Ba."

"Mm."

"If I get in a room on Saturday and everybody in it is very kind and very interested and slightly better informed than me about my own mother, what do I do?"

Wei Chen stopped on the pavement, and took his briefcase off his shoulder, and set it down, which he had not had to do.

"You want the tactical answer or the true one," he said.

"I can't tell."

"The true one." He shifted the briefcase to his other hand. "You are going to be very good in that room, Lyra, and I want you to know that I have thought about it and I have concluded it, and I would like you to be warned, because it is not what you are expecting. You are not going to be able to tell whether they are lying to you. Nobody can. That is not a failure of their ethics, that is what a room full of people who know things is *for*. You will not be able to tell, and you are sixteen, and you are going to sit there and feel the whole structure of it, and there is going to be a moment — it will be a short moment, it will be about four seconds long — when you will be tempted to be certain."

"Certain about what?"

"About which of them is lying." He picked the briefcase up. "And I want you to know, before we get there, that *I* have not been able to tell for five years, and that I have used every tool I have, and that a man who has been a professor for twenty-one years and has taught four hundred students how not to trust a bad argument cannot get to the bottom of this either. So you will not be able to. That is not a thing to be ashamed of at sixteen. That is the actual condition of a person who loves somebody who has gone missing, and there is no technique for it, and everybody who tells you there is, is selling."

"Then what do I *do*?"

"You write down every single thing anybody says to you that you would not be able to guess, including the kind things." He started walking. "Including especially the kind things. In a school like that the kind things are the ones that have been shaped. You will feel the shaping and you will not be able to prove it, and so you will write it down in the shape it arrived in, and in ten years you will be glad you did."

---

That night she sat on the end of her own bed with empty hands, because everything of her mother's was still in her grandmother's house, where it had always been, and she had decided — out loud, to herself, in an empty room, which she was aware was not the same as deciding — that she was done waiting to be given any of it, and that tomorrow she was going to ask.

Instead she lay on her back and held her own hands up above her face and looked at them until the room began to do the thing, which it still did, and which was that the small warm box of her bedroom stopped being a small warm box and became a room with a second room behind it.

She had been having that since she was six.

She had never told anybody. Not Rivera, not Nai Nai, not her father. Ten years of a second room, and no words.

*Nobody has ever asked me what is in the second room,* she thought, lying there with her hands empty. *Not one person, in ten years. Not my father, who notices when I do not eat. Not Nai Nai, who counts my breaths.*

The second room was there. It had been there since she was six years old, and it had never once contained anything, and on Sunday she was going to find out what was in it, and she was not going to be frightened of that, and she was absolutely frightened of that.

She lay in the dark, and did not sleep, and did not turn the light on, and did not put the paper off the door.

---

*Thread Resonance Note: C-2214. Guardian conditions received and entered verbatim: (1) accompaniment, unconditional after the first hour; (2) no diagnostic disclosure to the school in advance of the guardian's consent; (3) explicit refusal of any inducement offered in the seer's interest of a third party. One further condition offered by the guardian and not accepted: the seer has stated she will not agree to it, and the entry stands as a matter of record rather than as an agreement. Note from E. Weaver: understood. I will raise the third with my own committee on Thursday and I expect to lose. Note appended by N. Ekwueme (external): please be aware that the referral to you was made on the fifteenth and that the letter of the twenty-second was not the trigger. Somebody had already made a decision about this child before either of us was told anything.*

# Chapter 4: The Invitation

*Saturday 31 August. Nai Nai's kitchen on Ellery Street, and then a corner on the way home.*

> "A tradition is not a set of rules. It is an argument that has been going on for a very long time between people who are dead."
>
> — Nai Nai, on being asked why her grandmother never wrote anything down

The bus to Ellery Street took fifty-five minutes and used to take forty, and Lyra spent the first ten of them watching a thread between the driver and a woman in the third row, and the next thirty working out why she could not switch it off.

She could not switch it off. That was the new thing. For two years it had come and went — a bad week, a bad month, an afternoon in a corridor when everything was a wiring diagram and she had gone home and put her head under a pillow. But since the fifteenth it had settled in and taken up residence, and it was as though somebody had turned a dimmer up in a room she had not known was there.

At the last stop before her grandmother's, a man got on with a bicycle, and the whole length of the aisle between the door and the back filled up with lines, and Lyra sat with her hands in her lap and her eyes shut and let it wash over her, and it was — she had to be honest, sitting there with her eyes shut — restful. It was the only quiet she got.

*That is the problem,* she thought. *I have found the thing that helps and it is the thing that is in my father's head.*

---

Anmei Chen was eighty-one years old and lived in a house on Ellery Street, four streets from where Lyra and her father had lived before, and the reason for the street name was that both houses had belonged to the same family and had been given the family name, which in this family was not a family name so much as an administrative decision taken in about 1911.

Her grandmother was standing in the doorway with a dish towel over her shoulder.

"You are wearing your school coat to a grandmother's house in August."

"It's raining."

"It is not raining. It has not rained since Tuesday. You are wearing your school coat because you do not know what else to do with your hands." Anmei Chen turned around. "Bà Sáu made *bánh tét*. You will eat. You do not get an opinion about the pork, and you do not get an opinion about the second helping, and if you say you are not hungry I will telephone your father and tell him you said so."

"Nai Nai—"

"You will eat and then you will tell me about the letter, and then I will tell you about the school, and we will not have this conversation in a doorway like two women at a market."

---

Bánh tét was the cylindrical rice cake from her grandmother's province, wrapped in banana leaf and boiled for hours, and the first thing Lyra had ever made with her own hands that anybody had ever asked her for. She had been eleven. She had put too much filling into one corner and had been told about it for six years.

She ate two. Her grandmother watched her do it with the flat satisfaction of a woman whose plan was working.

"You have a question," Anmei Chen said, "and you have had it since you got on the bus, and it is the same question you asked me on your fifth birthday, which was *how do you always know what I am thinking*, and I told you then and I will tell you now, and you will like the answer less this time."

"You count my breaths."

"Your breaths go fast when you are lying and slow when you are frightened, and there is a third kind where you are holding something very still so that nobody will ask you about it." Her grandmother dipped a piece of cake into the tea and did not eat it. "You are frightened. And you are holding something. And you are also lying, but about something small, and I will let that go, because you will tell me when you are ready, and I have never yet known a person to be harmed by being allowed to arrive at a thing in their own time."

Lyra put her chopsticks down.

"Nai Nai. The letter came nine days ago and I found out on Friday and I have not told you because I did not want to be told things."

The dish towel came off the shoulder and went on the back of the chair, very deliberately, the way you put down something that is about to be needed.

"Sit back," said Anmei Chen.

---

The box was mother-of-pearl inlaid, and the pattern on the lid was a knot that Lyra had been looking at in dreams since she was small and had never been able to draw.

Inside, on faded blue silk, was a jade pendant.

Not large. About the size of a thumbnail, in a shape that was almost but not quite a disc, drilled at the top and worn on a cord that had been replaced at some point in a colour that did not match.

Anmei Chen did not lift it out. She put one finger on it and left it there.

"Whose is it?"

"Your mother's. It was her mother's, and her mother's mother's, and it came down the Ho line in the time when there was a line, which is to say that it went north out of Saigon in 1975 in the bottom of a tin, and then it came south again when your mother came to this country, and then it stopped with her." The finger did not move. "It is not jade of any great worth. It is not a treasure. It is a *key*, and it is a key to a thing that does not exist, and the reason it still works after four generations is that everyone who has ever held it wanted it to."

"What does it open?"

"Nothing." Anmei Chen finally looked up. "That is what I am about to tell you, and I have saved it for eighty-one years and once, so you will listen to the shape of it before the content."

She sat down.

"Your father's grandmother — my mother, whom you never met, who died in the winter of ninety-nine — used to be able to see two kinds of thing. She would tell you, if you asked, that she saw *threads* and *currents*, and if you were a stupid child you would write that down and think it was poetry. It is not poetry. It is one thing, seen from two ends."

"Explain."

"*Threads* are the individual. The line between a person and a person, a person and a place, a person and what they intend to do next. The oldest ones are old: a grandmother and a granddaughter, a house and the family that has lived in it, a teacher and the student she is going to fail. They are *yours* — you can see them, and in the old way you can touch them, and everything you do to a thread is a thing you have done to a person." Her grandmother's finger was still on the pendant. "*Currents* are the other end. They are what a place has been. They are not made of people; they are made of all the people who have ever been in a place and are not there any more. They are slower, they do not belong to anybody, and they are the only thing in this family that cannot be hurt by a person who means well."

"And Mum?"

"Your mother had the whole of it." A pause. "Full-spectrum. Both ends, at once, and the connections between them, which is the third thing my mother could not do and your mother could do from about the age of eleven. When your mother looked at a room she was not seeing the room. She was seeing the room *and everything that had ever happened in it*, and she could not switch either off, and from fourteen she could not switch off the part where the two of them did not agree with each other."

"The currents didn't agree?"

"Never once. Not in eighty-one years." Anmei Chen picked up her tea. "That is the part your father cannot accept, and it is the part I would have you take to that school on Saturday. Your mother was not a woman with a beautiful gift. She was a woman with a *contradiction* in her, from the age of eleven, and she spent twenty-nine years learning to hold a contradiction without resolving it. There are three people alive who have ever seen that, and one of them is sitting in front of me eating my rice cake, and one of the others is a woman called Mei-Hua, who is your father's sister, and who works at the school you are going to on Saturday, and whom I have not spoken to in five years. The third is a man in Lyon who has never met your mother and who has read everything she published, and I have never told him any of this, and he does not know whether to believe me."

Lyra put her hand down.

"I don't have an aunt at that school."

"You have exactly one, and you have never met her, and I am going to explain that in about ten minutes and you are not going to like any part of it." Her grandmother drank. "Eat."

---

The explanation took eleven minutes and was worse than Lyra had been able to imagine.

"Your father is my son," Anmei Chen said. "Your mother was my *daughter-in-law*, and before that she was my student, from the age of six, because her mother and I were the kind of friends who leave their children with each other. Her mother was a Ho. My mother was a Ho. There are three of us living who can do the whole of it, and two of us are in this family, and the third is your mother's sister, and the fourth — the fourth was a woman called Lin Mei-Hua, who was born in nineteen twenty in Chợ Lớn and who did something in this quarter in the autumn of nineteen forty-three, and who was your great-grandmother on your mother's side, and who died in nineteen ninety-four, and whom I have known about since I was twenty-four years old, and whom I have told you about exactly once in my life and will not tell you again today."

"Four. And Mei-Hua is one."

"Mei-Hua is one, and she is your father's sister, and she is the one who taught your mother the things I could not teach her, because Mei-Hua is *fast* and I am *correct*, and your mother needed both, and there is a great deal of that in the two of them. I have one end where she has two, and that is the whole of the difference between us, and I have never once resented it, and I have resented it constantly." Anmei Chen's mouth moved. "And your father met your mother in nineteen ninety-six, at something neither of them thought was worth mentioning, and married her the next year, and that is how two families that had been arguing with each other across an ocean for forty years became one family with two surnames and one very tired grandmother."

"So when my father says—"

"Your father says *we do not have a large family*, and he says it in the tone of a man who has been saying it for sixteen years and has never once been contradicted, and he is aware that he is lying, and he has never worked out who he is lying to." She put the cup down. "Which brings us to the part you will actually want. Mei-Hua is the Registrar of that Academy. She has been for eleven years. She is the person who signed your file, and she is the person who did not answer one of the eleven questions I put to her in the spring of 2007, and she did not answer it because she was frightened, and I did not press her, because I was frightened too, and because I was a coward with a granddaughter in the room."

---

"What happened to her," Lyra said.

"What happened to her."

"She was going to speak to the Council. You told me. She came to you at night with a map and she said *patterns of decay* and *institutional blindness*, and the next night she was gone."

"She came here on the eleventh of September, five years ago, at about eleven at night, and she had been in the house for four hours and she had not unpacked, and she was not going to unpack." Anmei Chen's voice did not change. "She put a map on this table. It is a bad map — I have never seen the like of it, it is not a map of a place, it is a map of a *quantity*. And she said: it converges somewhere it has never been looked at, and the counting is a wall built by people who need a place to point at. And I said, *Lin, who is coming with you*, and she said, *nobody is coming with me, that is the entire problem*, and then she said a thing I have thought about every day for five years."

"What did she say?"

"She said: *if I go alone, they will write it up as a woman alone, and it will be the correct reading, and in ten years there will be a girl who can do what I can do and she will go alone too, and it will be the correct reading again.*" Anmei Chen's hand had gone flat on the tablecloth. "And I said: *then do not go alone.* And she said: *I have been alone for eight years, Bà. You are the only person I have not been alone with.*"

The kitchen was extremely quiet.

"And then what happened," Lyra said.
