"She went out of this door at about half past one in the morning and she did not come back, and I sat at this table until the sky came up, and at eight o'clock I telephoned your father, and your father was the worst thing that ever happened to me, because he said *I told you*." Her grandmother's hands were not entirely steady, and she dealt with that by reaching for the teapot and pouring badly. "I have not forgiven him for it and he has not forgiven me for telephoning him and we have had five years of Sundays. He comes. He sits there. He reads. We do not talk about it. It is the most civilised household in this city and it is built on top of a thing nobody can say out loud."

---

She went to the other cabinet.

"Three sketchbooks. She left them in her studio and the police took nothing, because the studio was not evidence of anything, and I went and got them eleven days later, before your father had finished the search." Nai Nai put a leather satchel on the table, repaired at some point with a different colour of thread. "I have not opened them. I do not read the sight; I never had the full thing, only one end of it. But I have looked at her drawings every day for five years, and I have understood perhaps a tenth of what is on those pages, and that tenth is enough for me to know that she was not a woman who lost her way."

She went to a drawer, and took out a small mother-of-pearl box, and set it beside the satchel.

"That is yours as well. It is nothing. Silk, and a needle, and a woman in a shop in a Chinatown who looked at a boy of about nine drawing on a tablecloth and said, *You have the hands for this. Do not let anybody sell you a better one.*"

Lyra looked at the two objects, and then at her grandmother.

"Why now?"

"Because on Monday morning you are going to get on a train, and because I have been sitting on this table for five years, and because you have just been to a hospital and been told that the thing about you is in your father, and there is a version of this in which I say nothing for another five years and you are twenty-one and you are still carrying a secret and you still have a second room behind your eyes that you have never told anybody about."

Lyra's head came up.

"How do you know about the—"

"Because I built it, child." Anmei Chen said it without any drama at all. "I put it there when you were six. I sat on this floor with you and I taught you to find the room behind the room, because that is where the *currents* are, and because the currents are the only thing that cannot be taken from you, and because I thought — " She stopped. She wiped her eyes with the towel, briskly, as if that were a thing you did afterwards. "Because I thought that if I gave you that, then whatever else I failed to give you, you would have a place to go that nobody could put in a school."

---

Lyra did not open the sketchbooks until she was on the bus.

She opened the first one on her knees, with the satchel on her lap and the window going grey past it, and turned the pages slowly, and it was worse than grief: it was a woman's mind, laid out flat, in order, with the dates on them.

Drawings of a single teacup, annotated across eleven months, with the colour and weight of the connection between the cup and the hand and the hand and the day and the day and the price of the tea, in a notation system that used a symbol she did not know and a small drawing of a wave. Notes in English, in Chinese, and in Vietnamese, sometimes in the same sentence, as though the ideas had not yet agreed on a language.

And then, in the third book, on a page she turned past and came back to, a map.

Not a map of anywhere. It was a diagram: a great many fine lines drawn in silver on a ground of silver, all of them running inward, meeting somewhere out past the right-hand edge of the paper, and it took her a full minute to work out that the thing at the right-hand edge was not a decoration. It was a place, drawn in ordinary cartographic lines, at a scale where a whole hemisphere was a thumbnail.

And a note beside it, in Lin's careful hand:

> *Not here. Everyone assumes here, because here is where the counting is done, and the counting is wrong. It converges somewhere it has never been looked at. There is an island in the Pacific that does not appear on most charts, and the charts that have it disagree with each other about its position. I have not been there. I have spoken to two people who claim to have seen the water there move in a way that water does not move, and neither of them could give me a date, and both of them were frightened of me by the end of the conversation.*
>
> *Do not trust the counting. The counting is a wall built by people who need a place to point at.*
>
> *B. — if this reaches you, and it will not reach you, because you will do what I did and go alone: the thing that is counting is not a place and it is not a person. It is a habit, and the habit is older than the school, and it is the reason they need the number.*

Lyra read it four times. Then she closed the book and put it back in the satchel and looked out of the window at a wet industrial park going past in the dark, and felt, for the first time in her life, the specific vertigo of holding a piece of evidence she was completely certain was important and had absolutely no idea what to do with.

Then she got out her phone and typed out one message, and thought about it for a long time before she sent it.

To her father: *I'm going to ask you something tonight and I want you to actually answer it. Have you ever read any of Mum's notebooks?*

And then she did not send that either, because she was sixteen, and because she was on a bus, and because she had a second room behind her eyes that she had never once told anybody about, and because on Monday morning a man with a very good memory was going to square a sheaf of notes on a lectern in exactly the way her father squared a sheaf of notes on a kitchen table, and she was going to have to sit in a room and be sixteen at him, and she could not think about her father tonight.

The pendant was round her neck before she got to the corner. It was warm within a minute, and stayed warm, and did the thing it had done in the art room at school, once, for a split second, which was that the street got *deeper*, as though there were a second street behind the first one, and somebody in the second street put out a hand.

She put her own hand over it, on the cord, and said, out loud, on a wet corner in a city she had never thought about leaving, in a language that nobody within a thousand miles would have understood her saying:

"Mum. I'm going to be careful. I want you to know that. I'm going to be careful and I'm not going to do it for anybody."

The warmth did not change, and did not answer, and did not have to.

---

*Thread Resonance Note: C-2214. Second guardian Anmei Chen (paternal household; silk-vision by instruction, not by blood) to be contacted through the seer and not directly. Instruct that no member of staff is to describe the family, correct the family, or characterise the family's practice to the seer as tradition, as folklore, or as data. Family materials released to the seer at guardian discretion. Note appended by N. Ekwueme (external): guardian has asked me to record that the referral to you was not the trigger for your letter of the twenty-second, and that this household has understood why, and would like it noted that it was not the trigger for the referral either. Note appended by E. Weaver: I am going to have to tell her, on Saturday, that her aunt is forty metres away and has been for eleven years. I have been putting it off for six.*

# Chapter 5: The Severance

> "The question is not whether we are able to cut a connection. We are able. The question is who we are willing to be afterwards."
>
> — Lin Chen, notebook, autumn 2002

## THE GATHERING

*The Iris Bookstore & Cafe, 12 Willow Creek Lane. Saturday 31 August, ten minutes to two.*

They had put out four chairs at the front and a rope across the middle of the room, and there were perhaps sixty people in it, and Lyra understood within about a minute of walking in that this was not a school and had never been dressed up as one.

It was a bookshop. There were books on the walls, being sold, with the price still on them. There was a woman at the back with a coffee urn who did not stop knitting when a woman in a grey coat stood on a chair-stool and said, into a microphone that was slightly too loud, that her name was Eleanor Weaver and that she had a great deal to say and would try to be brief.

Her threads came off her the way light comes off a window. Deep green, and under the green something colder and much steadier, and the whole of it held in a shape that Lyra could not look at directly and did not try to. She had the room the way a person has a room they have been in before.

"We are not a school," Weaver said. "We are an institution that has been teaching one thing since 1798, which is that a person who perceives connections between things is not ill, and is not imaginative, and is not a metaphor. Some of you already know that. Most of you have been told, politely, by somebody kind, that there is no such thing as a thread."

A ripple. Lyra felt her grandmother's hand arrive on the back of her neck, lightly, without looking, the way you steady a table.

"Before I go any further," Weaver said, "I am going to do the thing our doctors tell us to do at the start of a difficult appointment, and then I am going to let everyone watch me do it, because I have been told that this organisation has a habit of arriving in communities already holding an opinion."

"I don't know what you mean," said somebody at the back.

"I know exactly what you mean, Mrs. Whitlock, and it does not matter, and I am going to do it anyway." Weaver turned to the side of the room. "Dr. Osei. Would you come up."

---

Dr. Amara Osei was younger than Lyra had expected and did not look like a doctor, and it took about ninety seconds for Lyra to work out that this was a professional decision.

There was no lectern. Osei stood in the aisle with nothing in her hands, and she did not look at Weaver once.

"I am going to describe a man I saw last Tuesday in Westbrook, with his consent, and I am going to use his name because he asked me to and because there is a boy in this room who is roughly his age and I would like somebody to be frightened on his behalf for a moment."

"Dr. Osei—" somebody started.

"Fifty-three years old. Professor of applied mathematics. What he has is called sympathetic thread bleed, and it is not a rare thing, it is a *common* thing, and the reason it is common is that it is the price of loving somebody who sees." She looked at Weaver, and something went between them that Lyra could not read and did not need to. "The perception runs. It is not passive, and anybody who tells you it is passive is selling something. And the running of it makes feedback in the people it is bonded to, and the feedback is measurable, and in a man of fifty-three with a pre-existing heart condition it is not a matter of *if*."

The room had gone completely silent.

"I have written to the four families in this room who have somebody with the condition," Osei said. "I have written to them, today, before I came here, because I am not a brave woman and I would rather not be in a room with a surprise in it. Three of you are here. You already know. One of you does not, and that is the young woman by the window with the jade, and I am sorry."

Lyra felt her grandmother's hand come off her neck and then come back.

"Dr. Osei," said a man at the front, and it was her father, and Lyra discovered that she had not known in advance that he was going to speak. "Before we go any further, may I ask what your college would consider a success here?"

"Professor Chen." Weaver's voice was very slightly different. "May I say that I know your wife."

"You may not," said Lyra's father, pleasantly. "You may say that you were at the same institution as my wife at the same time, and you may say it in front of sixty people, and I would prefer that you did it now rather than at some point in the next four years when it would be more expensive."

Nobody breathed.

"That is fair," Weaver said. "I was there in 2002. I was not her friend. I was on the committee that overrode her, and I have thought about that every day for eleven years, and it is not a thing I came here today to say out loud in a bookshop, and I am not going to." She squared her notes. "Answer the man's question, Amara. He has earned it."

Osei turned round.

"Success," she said, "is a number. For a seer, a bleed can be arrested and the perception kept, at a cost in range and precision that is permanent and that nobody can predict in advance. For a relation, the bleed stops and the organ that was being strained gets to keep working. That is the entire trade. It is not a cure and it is not small and it is not reversible and the person whose perception is being cut is the only one in the room who gets no vote, which is the part I have spent eleven years failing to fix, and which is why I stood up in front of sixty people instead of sending a letter."

---

That was how it happened. Nobody planned it.

A boy in the third row, four seats from the end, stood up.

He was seventeen. He was wearing a Westbrook High hoodie and he had the loose, unhurried shoulders of somebody who did not find school difficult, and Lyra saw the threads on him before the room did: a dense warm web, gold and green, the signature of somebody who read people easily and had never once been asked to stop.

"Ben," said a girl beside him, in a whisper that carried.

"I've got it." He was already going. "Sorry. Sorry. I just—"

And then he was on the carpet on the Persian rug with his limbs arranged the way a dropped coat is arranged, and the room made a noise Lyra had never heard a room make.

She was standing before she knew she was standing, and what she was looking at was not Ben.

It was the thing on Ben.

It came off him in a colour she had no word for and had never seen: not black, not grey, but the specific dead light of a filament that has burned out and is still, structurally, present. It was not attached to anything in the room. It went from Ben, inward, down — and it was eating. That was the only description that fitted. It was laid along his other threads the way a wire is laid along a wall to hold a door shut, and where it touched, the light underneath stopped.

Zara Washington would have called it *drawing*. Lyra, who had never met Zara, would not have used that word for another eleven days, and would then never be able to think about the phenomenon without it.

"—someone get the nurse—"

"Get him *space*," said Osei, from the aisle, in a voice with a great deal of the past eleven years in it. "Everybody back. Not touching him. Do not form a circle, that is the worst thing you can do, do not form a circle—"

But the noise in the room was not a room's noise. It was a *thread* noise, and Lyra could hear it, and it was the sound of about forty people feeling something happen to somebody and having no word for it, and it came off them in long pale spikes of pure adrenaline with no colour at all.

And then a woman in a grey coat put her hand on a boy's forehead and it went quiet.

Not gradually. *Quiet.* The way a room goes quiet when somebody finds the right note.

Lyra watched Marcus Harlow do it, because she could not stop watching him do it, and she had a perfectly good view.

He moved the way water goes down a drain. There was no hurry in it anywhere. The smell arrived before anything else — ozone, and under the ozone something sweet and green and completely wrong, like jasmine kept in a room where nobody has opened a window — and it was that smell, and the hair standing up on her arms, that told her he was the man from the tape. Not a person. A *category*. A thing she had seen twice in her life in photographs on a shelf and had not, until this second, understood that it applied to a human being standing eleven feet away from her.

His hand was on Ben Ruiz's forehead. His threads were not there.

She had spent sixteen years looking at threads. She had looked at her mother's drawings until she could have drawn them from memory. She had looked at the four foot of her grandmother in a kitchen and at the two thousand feet of a high school corridor and at the six inches between a girl and a boy in an art room, and she had *seen* people, all her life, everywhere, in layers, the way you see a landscape.

Marcus Harlow had no threads.

That was not the same as having dim ones, or shielded ones, or ones she could not interpret. He had a hole where a person was supposed to be, and the edges of the hole were very tidy, and the hole did not look at her, and Lyra had to put her hand on the table.

"No," she said, and did not hear herself say it.

"Miss Chen." Weaver was beside her, and had been for some time. "Sit down."

"He's—"

"I know what he is. So do I. So, apparently, does your grandmother, and she has gone very white." Weaver's hand closed on Lyra's arm, and it was not unkind and it was absolutely not negotiable. "Dr. Ekwueme wrote to me on Friday evening and told me what you do, and told me to have somebody near you today, and I did not have anybody near you because I did not know who you were, and that is the single worst thing I have done in eleven years of doing this, and I am not going to argue about it in a bookshop. Sit down."

Lyra sat down, because her grandmother had said something in Vietnamese that she had not heard her grandmother's voice make before, and whatever it was, it was not for her.

What she did not sit down through was the last eleven seconds of it, because she was looking at the floor, and the floor is where the threads go when they are under strain, and what came up out of the Persian rug in a single line was Ben Ruiz's, coming apart.

It did not detach. That was the thing she could not stop seeing for the rest of her life, and it was the reason she was, in the end, able to say the things she said to him in the room under the mountain. It did not detach. It went along its own fault line, all at once, the way a bone goes, and the silver-black on it ignited from the far end and burned backwards, and it was over in about a second and a half, and there was no sound at all.

Ben Ruiz made a sound.

He came up off the floor in stages — shoulder, then head, then one knee — with the expression of somebody surfacing from very cold water, and he looked around at sixty people who had all gone white, and said, in a cracked voice, "Sorry. Sorry, I—"

He stopped. His hand went up to his own face, and then went round the back of his own neck, and Lyra watched him do it, four times, in nine seconds.

"What's—" Ben Ruiz's voice went flat and young. "Where's my aunt? Has anyone — where's—"

"I would like everyone to remain seated," said Weaver, into a room that had stopped listening to her some time ago, "and I would like Dr. Osei to say something about what has just happened, and I would like nobody to leave, including Mr. Stone, who has his phone out."

The room emptied itself over the following twenty minutes in the way rooms do, and the shape of the emptying was itself information: the parents went first, in pairs, and the *seers* went last, and two of them went out of the side door, and one of those two was a girl of about eighteen with her aunt's coat over one shoulder, and Lyra saw her go, and filed it, and did not know yet that she was looking at Elena Vance, or that Elena Vance had planted something in the store's back room in the four minutes while everyone was looking at the floor.

---

They were not allowed out of the back room.

"We have to be fair to you," Weaver said. "You are the guardian of record and you have asked a fair question and I have given you a bad answer, and I am not going to let you leave with it. Dr. Osei? The scans."

Osei put a tablet on the table and turned it round, and Lyra looked at it, and understood about a fifth of it, and was grateful for the other four fifths being a shape rather than a sentence.

"Your father," Osei said, to Wei Chen. "The twenty-ninth of August. This is the first time I have seen the ventricular numbers move, and I want to say three things about it and I want the order preserved."

"Please."

"One. There is no bleed-related mechanism visible in this that we can reverse, and I am aware that that is a sentence you did not want, and I have been saying it in this family for two weeks and I have said it to him as well, and he has taken it better than you are going to." Osei did not look up. "Two. What is visible is a muscle that has been dying quietly for a long time, and that has nothing to do with a gift, and that is why the arrest on the twenty-seventh of August—" she glanced at Lyra, who had not known it was the twenty-seventh, "—was not a bleed event. Three. The bleed is still running, and it is still costing him, and it is *also* not what is going to kill him in the next three months, and if anybody in this room tells you that severing your daughter's perception is going to save his life, they are telling you a thing that is arithmetically true and completely false, and I would like you to notice the shape of that sentence, because it is the shape of everything this industry says."

"So what is the shape," said Lyra.

Osei looked at her for the first time.

"The shape is that a real improvement to a real problem was available to you, and it cost something that could not be paid for in money, and the person who had to pay was you, and everybody in the room had a good reason." She put the tablet down. "That is the shape. It is always the shape. I have been in this for eleven years and I have never once seen it come out any other way."

---

"Miss Chen."

She had not seen him come into the room. That was the first thing, and she would think about it later at three in the morning for a long time.

Marcus Harlow was standing with his hat in his hands like a man at a funeral, and he was not a large man, and he had a face that gave nothing away and a small silver cicatrix at his left temple, and his voice, when he spoke, was so quiet that Dr. Osei had to stop to hear it.

"Professor Harlow," said Weaver, from the doorway, in a tone that made the back of Lyra's neck cold.

"Headmistress. I am not here in any capacity, and I am about to say something that will be more useful to you than anything I could say in one, and I would ask you to let me finish before you stop me, which you are fully entitled to do." He did not look at Weaver. "In 1997 a team of five of us ran a controlled extraction under a populated valley, in a garden, in April, with a child in the garden. The extraction was interrupted. Two of my colleagues died. My wife and my daughter were in the garden and they did not die, and the collapse did the other thing to them, the thing that is technically possible and that nobody in our field will discuss in front of a first-year." He set the hat down on the table. "Mr. Ruiz is going to be all right. He is going to be *entirely* all right, in the sense that the vessel is intact and the closure is clean and he will not scar, and he will not be able to perceive the connections between himself and the people he loves ever again, and he is seventeen years old and he has just found out, in front of sixty people, and he is going to be humiliated about it for the rest of his life, and he is going to be one of a great many."

He pushed the folder across the table.

It contained two sheets. The first was a child's drawing in coloured pencil: five pink petals, a stem, a careful and slightly crooked attempt at a bee. In the corner, in a seven-year-old's letters, the word *Mei*.

The second was a charcoal study of blossom branches, and the edges of the paper were scorched and blackened as though held too near a fire, and it was signed, bottom right, in a confident adult hand: *A.H.* Beneath it, in a third hand, and in ink: *For Mei — may you always remember the beauty before the burning.*

"That is my wife's," Harlow said. "That is my daughter's. They were drawn the same afternoon, four feet apart, about a hundred metres from where I was standing when the floor came up." He straightened. "Kyoto. 1997. Those are the only two facts about that day that anybody in this building is authorised to give you, and I have just given them to you in a bookshop, and I would like somebody to tell me why I did that."

Nobody did.

"Because you have just cut a boy in front of sixty people," said Lyra.

"Yes."

"And the boy was going to die."

"He was going to go under," said Harlow. "In about ninety seconds. It is not a rare thing. It looks like your friend described it and it is not a metaphor and it is not a mystery; it is a *load*, and a young man with a strong signature and a strong bond has a load like a rope, and the rope caught on something, and I was the only person in that room who had seen it happen before." He did not move. "So yes. I cut him, and he lived, and he is going to be humiliated for the rest of his life, and I have done that on eleven occasions that are on a list, and the list is in my office, and the list is the reason I would like to spend the next hour of your life talking to you, Miss Chen, and I would like to do it honestly, which nobody who has ever spoken to me about this has wanted."

---

He put his hand on the table. Not on her. Beside the folder, about nine inches from it, and he did that on purpose, and she saw him do it on purpose.

"I am going to offer you something and I am going to tell you the price before I tell you what it is, and I am going to do it here, in front of your father, in front of the headmistress, and in front of a doctor, because that is the only way this offer is any use to anybody."

"Please don't," said Lyra.

"Three to nine months," Harlow said. "That is Dr. Osei's figure and I am not going to improve on it. I want to sever the connection between your perception and his. The cost is that you will be cut, in the way that boy was cut, and the cut is clean, and it does not hurt, and you will be alive, and he will be alive, and he will get the other years." He stopped. "That is the offer. There is a second half of it and I am going to tell you the second half as well, because the only people who ever accept this from me are the ones who were told both halves."

"What is it?"

"I do not know which part of you will be missing afterwards," said Harlow. "I have three prior cases. Two of them are alive. Neither of them is recognisable to themselves. I have never been able to predict it, I have never been able to choose it, and I have never been able to do it twice the same way, and I would be lying to you if I told you the cost was your thread-sight." His hand had not moved from the table. "The cost is that somebody decides that about a child in a bookshop, and that the child does not get a say, and that the reason is that I have run the numbers and the numbers are on my side."

He looked at her father.

"Professor Chen, you are a mathematician, and you have a right to ask the obvious, so let me make it easy for you. I have not shown you a number. I have shown you two children in a garden and a boy on a carpet. If I had shown you a number you would have believed it, and that is the thing I am trying to teach you not to do."

Wei Chen had not moved for a long time.

"My wife's last note to me," he said, "was four words long. *Don't let them quantify you.* I did not know what it meant. I have had five years." He put his hands flat on the table, in a gesture Lyra had never once seen him make. "I have understood it, and I would like to say, since nobody else in this room is going to: you have just told my daughter the price of something, accurately, out loud, in front of witnesses, and you have told her that the person who cannot afford it is her. That is the whole of it. There is nothing else in the five years."

He stood up.

"We are going to go and sit in my car," he said, "and then we are going to have a conversation, and then we are going to decide about Monday, and I would be grateful if Dr. Osei would telephone me at home tonight at any hour at all, because there is going to be a question I cannot answer on my own and I would rather have it in writing."

---

They got as far as the door of the back room.

"Miss Chen."

Lyra turned round, and it was the girl of eighteen with the coat over her shoulder, standing in the aisle with the lights behind her, and Lyra understood with total clarity that this girl had been in the room for the entire conversation and had said nothing.

"You're the one who's been holding that piece of paper," the girl said. "Since the eighteenth. I'm not going to ask to see it."

Lyra's hands went cold.

"I'm not going to say how I know, either, because that's a piece of paper of my own and I'd like to keep it." The girl glanced at the back room, at Weaver, at the door. "My name's Elena Vance. I'm a third-year, and my sister came here for an open day in the spring and asked me to put a word in for her and I said no, and she has not spoken to me since March."

"...oh."

"So." Elena Vance pulled her coat straight. "Two pieces of advice, free, from somebody who is about to make a much bigger mistake than you are. One. He's telling you the truth about the price. That's the thing you should be frightened of — not that he's lying to you. If he were lying to you this would be a much easier afternoon." She put her hand on the doorframe. "Two. You're going to spend the whole of next week deciding whether to trust him, and you're going to do it alone, and it is going to feel like respect. It isn't. It's just quiet."

She was gone before Lyra could answer, and the bell over the door went twice, and it was only afterwards that Lyra realised she had never once seen the girl's threads, and had not looked.

---

*Thread Resonance Note: Outreach event, Westbrook, 31 August. Attendees: 61. Seers present: 14. Interventions: 1 (uncomplicated; 4 min; good outcome; **prior consent not obtained and not obtainable — subject was nineteen minutes unconscious and no relation present**). Attending clinician: *not* Dr O. Osei (the room was cleared before she arrived). On-site medical: school nurse, one. On-site thread-qualified responder: M. Harlow, whose authority this office has never been able to establish and has twice declined to inquire into. Family briefed: yes. Referral to Dr A. Osei: yes, by Dr M. Harlow, over the objection of the family. Note appended by the attending registrar's office: the Authority for the Care of Persons of Perceived Thread, section nine, requires a named second responder at any public outreach. There was no second responder. There was a rug.*

# Chapter 6: The Weight of Memory

> "The cruelest thing about arithmetic is that it is right. It was right in 1993. It has been right every day since. The only variable that ever moved was me."
>
> — Marcus Harlow, private journal, entry 2,206

*Harlow's office, East Tower. Saturday 31 August, 11:47 p.m.*

The security monitors showed him the rug for the ninth time.

That was the thing he could not get past, and he had tried, and he had a bottle of something in the bottom drawer that he had been not-drinking from for eleven years. The rug was Persian. Ben Ruiz had landed on it diagonally, left shoulder first, and the pattern under his face had been — and here was the part that would keep him awake — *a palm tree*, and Harlow had known that, had known it while he was crossing the room, and had thought, in the middle of the most dangerous ninety seconds of the last four years, *that's unfortunate.*

Not *that's a boy.* Not *that's somebody's son.* A carpet.

He shut the monitors off and sat in the dark with his hands flat on the desk, and the desk was cold, and the silver in his arms did what it always did at night, which was to be quieter than the rest of him and more honest.

Right up to the elbow now. On Friday it had been at the wrist. He had a chart for it, in his own hand, in a book that was not in the building, and the line on the chart was not a curve any more. It was a straight line with a date on it.

*Six months*, he had written in March. *Possibly five. It does not matter; I have what I need and I have four years.*

It was August. He did not adjust the figure. He had found that he could hold the number perfectly still only by not looking at it, and that he was looking at it every day, and that on the days he looked at it he was a man doing arithmetic on himself, and there was no version of that man in which the arithmetic improved.

---

He had three folders out. He had not opened the third.

The first was Ben Ruiz. Seventeen months of assessment, a signed consent form from a mother who had signed it in a hurry, a velocity report and a resonance band in the low twenties, a boy who read people the way other boys read football, a voice that went flat and young when he came up off a floor, and a single instruction in his own hand at the bottom of page four of the consent, which was *I want to be told everything, and if I am told everything I am going to ask you questions you will not like, and that is the deal, and I am seventeen and I am allowed to make it. *Resonance and a line on a chart that had gone flat on the eighteenth of August, and beside all of it, in his own handwriting, the sentence he had written in the margin and had not shown to anybody:

*This is the one I will not be able to carry.*

Because that was the honest arithmetic, and nobody ever wanted it. Eleven interventions, nine clean, two that had left marks, and the eleven was a list in a book in a drawer, and the list was not a list of triumphs. It was a list of *substitutions.* In each case a young person had been carrying a load that the vessel was not built for, and the load had found a soft place, and a soft place is not a fault in a boy; it is a fact about a boy, and it is discoverable, and the discovery is cheap.

Eleven times he had found a soft place in a person and cut the wire that was pulling on it, and eleven times the boy or the girl had come up off the floor and said *sorry* — which was the detail that undid him, every time, without fail, in front of witnesses: the apology. The load did not apologise. The boy apologised, for having been a soft place.

He had read the Authority's section nine on Sunday evening. He had read it eleven times over four years and had found, as he had known he would find, a line in it about the consent of a subject in extremis, and he had found, as he had also known he would find, that the line did not say what he needed it to say, because the line said *where practicable* and nothing in the world was practicable at Rug Two.

He had cut a boy in front of sixty-one people with no second responder and had known exactly what the file would say, and had done it anyway, and would do it again, and the reason he would do it again was the arithmetic, and the arithmetic was correct, and he had made his peace with that a long time ago in the specific sense that he had stopped pretending it was a peace and started calling it a *method*.

---

The second folder was the Council.

Eleven years. A committee that met, and a classification system with a *priority number* on it, and a number he had argued with since the spring of 2002, when a woman of thirty-three had stood up in a lecture hall in this building and asked who decided, and had been told *a committee, of which I am one*, and had believed it the way you believe anything a person in a building is saying about a building.

He had believed her.

*That is the part,* he thought, *that is the part I will have to say out loud on Thursday, and I have practised it four times and I still cannot get through it. Not: they did it to her. They did not do it to her. I was on the committee. I signed the classification schedule in 2009 that put a triage number next to her daughter's name in a drawer, because in 2009 I believed I was the man who would use it to keep her safe, and I have never once in eleven years written anything in that drawer that was not a warning.*

Lin Chen had been thirty-three. She had been, in the four months she spent in this building, the most frighteningly competent person he had ever worked with, and he had argued with her in front of eleven people about a method, and lost, and she had gone home, and six years later she had gone into a chamber on her own and had not come out of it, and he had written a report about that in the passive voice for two pages.

He still had the report. It was in the folder. He had taken it out perhaps four times in five years, and he had never once been able to get past the second paragraph, and the second paragraph said: *The subject was known to be at elevated risk and was not accompanied.*

Twelve words. He had signed it.

---

The third folder he did not open.

It had a photograph in it and it had been in the drawer since 1999 and the drawer had a lock on it, and there was a reason for the lock, and the reason was that the last four times he had opened this folder he had sat in this chair afterwards for a length of time he could not account for.

He knew what was in it. He had looked at it every night for fourteen years. He did not need to look at it again tonight, and looking at it again tonight would not make him any more able to be the man he needed to be in about nine hours, because in about nine hours he was going to have to sit across a table from a boy of sixteen and say the word *severance* out loud and mean it, and there is no way of doing that with a photograph of a seven-year-old in the front of your mind, and he had learned that in 1999 the hard way.

So: April. The garden. Aiko with a book she had not opened. Mei, seven, with cherry blossoms in her hair, because it was the second week of April and there had been a festival, and both of them had been within forty metres of him when the floor came up, and Mei had *walked towards it*, which he would be told about for the rest of his life by people who were not there.

*Just like her mother*, he thought, and then, in the dark, at his own desk, sixteen years later: *No. I am going to stop doing that. I am going to stop finding her in every frightened girl, because that is how a man ends up treating a person as a *use*, and I have been treating a person as a use since 1997, and the woman I did it to first was the best of us, and she has been gone for six years and she is not here to be angry with me about it.*

Aiko had died in the spring of 1999, of the injuries, in a bed in Kyoto, with her hand on the side of the cot where a seven-year-old had been sitting two days earlier. That was a fact he had carried for fourteen years and had never once used, not in a report, not in a justification, not in a conversation at four in the morning with himself; and the reason he had never used it was that it was not *evidence*, it was a *reason*, and reasons are what you have when the evidence has run out.

Mei was twenty-three. She was in Portland, or had been in Portland, or was in one of three other places that the last four years had given him. He knew that she was alive. He knew it because the woman who had come to him in 2002, the *recovery* team, had told him so, in the specific tone of people delivering good news they had been asked to deliver, and had then, in the same conversation, told him that the memory work on his daughter was complete and irreversible, and had not used the word *grief*, and had watched him to see whether he would use it.

Aiko had been thirty-two. Mei had been seven. Two colleagues had died at the console. That was five people in the report and he had been the one who wrote the report.

He put both hands flat on the desk.

*Right. Here is what happens in about nine hours.*

He had a card in his drawer that he had been carrying since the sixteenth of August, and it was a proposal, and it was genuinely good, and he had checked it eleven times, and he had never once written down what it would cost.

He wrote it down now, because it was half past midnight and because the woman who had taught him that a number on its own is a thing with no weight in it was six years dead and he had promised her, in a kitchen in this city, on a Sunday, in the voice he used for promises he intended to keep.

*What it will cost: a child of sixteen loses a part of herself that nobody can predict, cannot reverse, and was not consulted. She will be alive. Her father will be alive. I will be the man who did it and I will be the man who told her the price first, and those are not the same man, and the second one is the one I am going to have to be.*

He read it back.

He then wrote, underneath: *and I will not sleep tonight, and I have not slept properly since the eleventh of September five years ago, and that is a cost too, and I have been keeping it in a different column.*

---

He got up at ten past one, and put the folders away, and did not open the third.

The security desk had left two things for him, which was normal, because Ines Blackwood ran the desk better than anybody he had ever worked with and he had been systematically ruining her life for four years in order to keep it running.

The first was a request from a third-year, Elena Vance, for four hours of unsupervised access to the restricted stacks on the grounds that she was writing a comparative paper. The form was filled in correctly, which was the frightening part; Elena Vance filled in forms the way other people breathe.

The second was a medical line from Westbrook Neurological, relayed by the campus clinic, and it was flagged *family* because Wei Chen had given his number as the family contact and had not given anybody else's, and the flag had been set by a records clerk who was doing her job.

> *CHEN, W. — 31 Aug, 21:40. Re: V. (file C-2214). The outside file note of 15 Aug uses a term I do not recognise and have queried twice. I have had no reply. He has asked me this evening whether the school sends anybody to him or expects him to travel, and when I told him the school does not travel he said, and I am quoting, "Then I will go." He is not a fool. He has understood that he is a data point, and he has decided to be one at close range. Recommend somebody from that institution telephone this ward before the end of the week. I am not chasing it. I have chased it twice.*

Harlow read it four times.

*Then I will go.*

He sat in the dark in a building full of sleeping children and thought about a man of fifty-three with a failing heart and eleven years of silence, saying that sentence to a nurse on a telephone, on a Saturday night, about a Saturday's conversation in a bookshop, three hundred miles away.

He thought: *he is going to drive here. He will be in the car in the morning. He will get here in a fortnight, on a Monday, with a bag and a folder and the specific expression of a man who has read something in a woman's notebook and been frightened by it, and he will want to be useful, and I will have to decide in about ninety seconds whether to let him.*

He thought: *if I let him, he will read the machine. He is a mathematician. He is the only person in this building who is going to be able to read the machine, because the machine is not a thread and it is not a current and it does not have a *thread signature*, it has a *transfer function*, and it will be sitting in a room four hundred metres from where he is going to be standing, and nobody else in the building has ever written one down.*

He thought, with a clarity that was almost physical: *and in eleven years I have not once permitted myself to be helped, and I have built a machine on the strength of that, and the machine has been holding an open circuit on nothing since 1997 and the wall board says nine days, and there is a man with a heart condition coming here on Monday who would read it in an afternoon, and I am going to have to decide whether I am the man who stops him at the gate.*

He turned the light off.

"Harlow," he said, to the room, in the voice of a man doing a very long sum. "You have fourteen years of not asking. It is now or never and the arithmetic does not care."

Then he went home, and did not sleep, and did not open the third folder, and did not turn the pendant over, because it was a crane folded out of silver thread-paper by a seven-year-old's hands in the second week of April, and he had worn it for sixteen years, and it was the only object in his possession that was not a tool.

---

*Thread Resonance Note: M.H. — Interventions 11, cumulative. Declines: 0. Requests for second responder: 0 of 11. Outstanding: Ruiz, B. — outcome stable, perception absent, will require a statement of explanation at 18; the mother has not been given one and will be told by a letter unless somebody in this building does it by hand. Chuang, S. — referred. Okafor, D. — declined at intake; see file. Outstanding and not in file: E.V. request for unsupervised stacks, approved by M.H. without reading the form. Recommend that somebody who is not me read that form in future, and I am aware that this note is a system of which I am the only member.*

# Chapter 7: The Father's Shadow

*Saturday 31 August.*

## THE CLINIC

Elena Vance had found Priya Raman face-down in a corridor outside the west showers at twenty past nine at night, which was where the first-years went when the field in their chest got too loud to sit inside, and which was therefore, in Elena Vance's considered opinion, the single most useful corridor in the entire building.

She knelt down beside her and did not touch her, which was the first thing she got right, and which she had learned the hard way in her second week.

"Priya. Prija. I'm Elena, third-year, and I'm not going to touch you, and I'm going to talk for about ninety seconds and you can tell me to stop."

"...oh."

"That's it. That's the whole floor. Do you know where you are?"

Priya's thread was a bad colour and getting worse — not a colour Lyra would have had a name for, and not a colour Elena had a name for either, and this was the part of her gift that nobody had ever wanted: she did not read the *content* of people. She read the *pressure*. She knew, standing in a corridor, that the girl on the floor had been frightened for four days and had run out of room.

"Do you know what day it is?"

"It's Sunday." Priya's voice was very small and very sarcastic, which was, Elena thought, extremely good. "Because the food's rubbish and the chapel's shut and I can't work out why the whole building's *wrong*."

"It is Saturday," said Elena, "and you are in a corridor, and you are not the first person this month to have got that wrong, and I would rather you got it wrong than that you decided you were ill. It is not you, and it is not the shower."

That got her up. Elena got her into the first-aid cubicle with its door that did not lock properly, and shut it anyway, and put the light off, and then spent forty minutes doing the single most useful thing she had ever learned to do, which was to count.

Count out loud. Count the tiles. Count the seconds between the light and the dark. It is a child's trick and it is in a book somewhere and Elena Vance had found it in a grandmother's kitchen, and it worked on Priya Raman, and it worked on Elena Vance, which was the difference between them.

"You've had it four days," Elena said, when Priya could hold a sentence. "You came in on the twenty-eighth and you have not been able to turn it down since, and you have been lying about it because you thought you were doing something wrong."

"How did you—"

"Because I did the same thing in September of last year and a second-year found me in a laundry and did not say a single word about it, just started counting, and I have wanted to say thank you to her for about eleven months and I do not know her name." Priya's shoulder had come down half an inch. "The thing nobody tells you — and I am telling you because you will not believe it from a handbook — is that it does not go away. It never goes away. What changes is that you stop being *in* it, and that takes about a year, and the year is not fun, and the only thing that made mine survivable was that somebody knew I was in it and did not make it a moral event."

Nurse Thorne came and went twice and was, as usual, efficient and faintly appalled.

"You don't have to do that," Thorne said in the doorway, when Priya had gone. "The discharge notes."

"I know."

"She's a first-year who can't turn a gift off, Elena. That's not an emergency, that's September."

"Sure." Elena wiped the chair down. "It's September."

---

And then, because she was Elena Vance and because she had four hours of unsupervised access approved by a man who had not read the form, she went along the corridor to the medication console outside Priya's cubicle and put her hand on it.

It was an old model. Scheduled for replacement next term. Less secure than the ones in the main wing, and the difference between the two was a quarter of an inch of bezel and a person who knew which quarter of an inch to look at.

She took a chip out of her sleeve. Non-conductive silk against the skin; she had had the gloves made, which had taken nine months and a great deal of money and a conversation with her uncle that she had ended by hanging up on.

*Protocol Kheredu, item seven. Diagnostic logs — thread degradation and severance — thirty days, thread-blind excluded, self-erasing at twelve hours.*

The chip went in with a click she felt in her teeth.

And then she stood in an empty corridor with her hand on the console and asked herself the question she had been asking for four years, which was: *what are you actually doing, and would you do it if nobody ever found out.*

The answer, as usual, was in the other room.

---

She was the last of three, and the last of the last, which was the part nobody outside the family understood and the part she had never once been able to say to a classmate.

*Elara.*

Her younger sister was fifteen and had a gift like a lit window and had been at a demonstration in May and had asked Elena — asked her, in a corridor, in April, in the specific way that a fourteen-year-old asks a person they have decided is the adult in a room — whether it was *really* going to help, and Elena had said yes.

Elena had said yes because Elena had not known, at fourteen, that there was a question.

Tobias Vance had been a curator in the oldest continuously-operating collection of displaced thread material on the continent, and he had gone into the Kheredu vault on a Tuesday in 2011 and had not come out, and the collection had been in the family ever since, in a room above a garage, in eleven crates, and every item in it had been taken by an institution and not returned, and every item in it had come out of a community that could not afford to buy it back.

*Three cousins. Two hundred years of somebody's work, in crates, in a garage, in a town where nobody had ever heard of any of it.*

And the Syndicate wanted her. That had been true since she was sixteen. They wanted the Vance archive and they wanted the granddaughter who could find it, and they had offered her — very politely, in a restaurant, in a tone of great civility — a great deal of money, access, and a copy of everything.

And so Elena Vance had been useful to two people who were destroying each other, for four years, by being a person who could sit in a corridor and be patient and say nothing to either of them.

Her uncle had used the word *Steward* and had cried, and had been right, and she had said *I will do it properly and I will do it alone*, and had then, at some point in the third year, stopped being able to tell the difference between the second sentence and the first.

She put her hand on the console again, and the charm on her wrist — her mother's, a knot her mother had tied on a beach, which was nothing at all, and which she wore because it was hers and not anybody's — gave off its small warmth.

*Twelve hours*, she thought. *And then the chip is gone. And nobody in this building knows that the boy in the rug and the girl on the floor in Ward C and the two in the second-year dorm are the same kind of damage, and in about three weeks somebody senior is going to stand up in a hall and tell two hundred students that what they saw was a medical event.*

She took her hand off the console.

And then, because she was eighteen and alone and had a four-hour window in a restricted building and no adult in the world who would have understood what she was about to do, she went up two floors and let herself into a room, and started reading.

---

## THE FATHER'S SHADOW

*Professor Harlow's office, East Tower. Saturday 31 August, 11:52 p.m.*

He was not in his office.

He was in the training room at the end of it, which had a practice field in the middle of it and a glass wall on three sides, and he was on his knees on a mat with a sixteen-year-old boy in front of him who had been at this school for nine days and who had just tried to hold a connection and had made it scream.

"All right," Harlow said. "Stop."

"Am I doing it wrong?"

"You are doing it *hard*, which is the same mistake in a better costume."

Rafi Oyelaran put his hands in his lap. He was very dark and very slight and had spent nine days being frightened in a manner that was, Harlow thought, more disciplined than anything he had seen in thirty years of teaching, including his own daughter's. When the first-years came in, most of them looked at the field as though it might notice them. Rafi looked at it as though it was a document he would have to sign.

"You keep trying to make the connection hold," Harlow said, "because it slipped. But the connection did not slip. *You* slipped. You went to hold a thing you have not read yet, and when it did not arrive in the shape you expected, you pulled, and a pull is the only thing in this room that a thread cannot survive."

"So what do I do instead?"

"Read it."

"Read it *loosely*?"

"No." Harlow shut the practice field off. It went out. The room was suddenly an ordinary room with a glass wall and a table. "You are doing it loosely. Loosely is a word for people who have decided that accuracy is a chore. I want you to do it *exactly*, and take as long as it takes, and find out what is actually there before you decide what it should be."

"That'll take a week."

"Yes."

"That's ridiculous."

"Yes," said Harlow. "It is. Sit down. I am going to tell you a thing and then I am going to make you write it down, and you are going to hate it, and in four years you are going to have it on the wall."

Rafi sat down.

"In 1997, five people ran a controlled draw under a populated valley, in a garden, in April, with a child in the garden." Harlow kept his hands on the table where they could be seen. "Four years before that, a woman of twenty-three ran the numbers that predicted it, correctly, on incomplete data, and wrote the word *fails* in the margin, and put her name to it, and then left the institution and did not publish. Nobody has ever been able to establish whether she was frightened, or negligent, or correct and unloved. All three readings are defensible and I have read all three."

He turned his head and looked at the boy's face, which was seventeen kinds of puzzled.

"She did one thing right," he said. "She was *slow*. She took a whole season over a field notebook. Everybody in that building wanted a number by Thursday and she gave them a number in March, and by March two people had died, and the number was the only thing anybody in this field has ever had that has not turned out to be a lie." He put one finger on the table. "You have nine days. You are going to be here four years. You have exactly the amount of time available to be slow, and it is *all* of it, and you will spend it on gossip if I let you, and gossip is what people do here when they are frightened and there is nothing else to do with their hands."

"...so read it loosely."

"*Exactly*," said Harlow. "Write it down. And put the date on it, and the date is the important part, because in ten years the only thing that will have saved your life is being able to prove that you were careful on a particular afternoon."

Rafi wrote it down. He wrote it down carefully, with the pen held wrong, and it took him four minutes, and Harlow let it.

And then, halfway through, the boy looked up.

"Professor — my abuela says the field is an instrument. She says you only get to calibrate an instrument, you don't get to *argue* with it."

"Yes."

"And she says the ones who argue with instruments end up in the newspapers."

"Your abuela," said Harlow, "is describing my entire discipline and is not aware that it is a compliment, and I would like you to remember that she was right and that I said so, because there is a document coming in November and I am going to have to be read to a committee, and I would like one witness."

---

He walked Rafi out to the dormitory at half past midnight and stood at the bottom of the stairs in the rain, and the boy's footsteps went up, and he stood there a good deal longer than a man standing in rain requires.

There was a version of this where he told them. There was a version where he told the whole first-year cohort, in a hall, in September, and watched the room go cold. He had in fact drafted it, in March, in the blue book, and the draft was four pages long and the fourth page was the reason he had never sent it, and the fourth page was a paragraph about a girl of fifteen.

Her name was Elara Vance. She was fifteen and in the first-year group and she had walked into his office in April and asked him a direct question and had been, for four months, the best student he had had in nine years, and her uncle was a curator who had gone into a vault in 2011 and had not come out, and the family had a collection of two centuries in eleven crates, and the Syndicate had offered that girl a great deal of money for a list.

*The only person who has ever been inside that vault is a man who is not a member of this family and who has been extremely careful, and I do not know his name, and I have not asked, and Elena Vance has never told me, and I have not asked her either, and I would like the Committee to understand that I have not asked her.*

He had four months of her. He had the fourth page. He had a machine that needed a resonance and eleven years of not asking anybody for help, and a boy in a corridor on a Saturday who had said *I'm not going to ask to see it* and had known, somehow, that there was a piece of paper.

*Two of them,* he thought, on the stairs, in the rain. *Two people in this building under eighteen who have found out that the adults are running something, and both of them have decided to manage it themselves, and they have both done it in the last four days, and I did the second one.*

He got back up to his office at ten to one and there were two things on the desk.

The first was a message from the clinic that had been routed oddly, via a records clerk, flagged *family*:

> *CHEN, W. — patient ambulatory with assistance. Asks re: transfer. Attending advises strongly against travel. Patient has been informed. Patient has noted that he has been informed.*

The second was a request, from a third-year named Vance, E., for four hours of unsupervised access to the restricted stacks, approved by him at six o'clock that evening without reading the form, and sitting on his desk now with a tick in the box next to *supervisor present: N*.

Not N.

He looked at it for a long time.

Then he opened a drawer, and took out the blue book, and turned to a page near the back where he kept a list he had never shown anybody, and at the top of it he wrote a date, and under the date he wrote a name, and then he wrote a second line:

> *14 Sept. Wei Chen. Westbrook. Permitted or not permitted — decide, and write down the reason either way, because in November I am going to be asked and I am not going to remember.*

He closed the book.

*The problem with a method,* he thought, *is that it is very good at everything except the one thing it is for.*

---

*Thread Resonance Note: M.H. — Practice field used 21:50–23:20, one student, supervised (the only time in four years). Field log shows one involuntary projection at 22:06 lasting under two seconds: involuntary, uncontrolled, aimed at a first-year's *safety*, not at a target. Recommend immediate review. Recommend that this office be given the authority to request a second clinical opinion and be unable to refuse the request. Oyelaran, R. — referral to the long programme. Vance, E. — stacks request approved by me without reading the form. I am recording that I did not read the form, because in November somebody is going to ask me what my standards are and I want to be able to say that at least I wrote them down.*

# Chapter 8: The Arrival

*Wednesday 4 September. The train, and then Threadweaver Academy.*
*Weave-Quake Counter: 17% instability. Node-failure projection: T+9 d, 14:00. Source: 1997 survey, re-verified 2009.*

> "In moments of crisis, artificial barriers dissolve. True connections form not in comfort, but in the crucible of shared vulnerability."
>
> — Dr. Eleanor Weaver, "The Psychology of Thread Bonding"

*The Weave-Quake Counter stands at 17% instability. The Academy's ward network is within normal tolerance.*

## THE TRAIN

The train came into Threadweaver Valley through morning mist, silver on the rails, and Lyra did not look up.

That was the first thing she had learned to do in the four months since Ms. Rivera had said *rarer than you might think* and the three days since a woman with grey eyes had sat down beside her in a hospital and asked her to be completely honest: you could not look up. Not here. Not anywhere with this many people in it.

She looked at her own thumbnail instead, and counted, and let the valley come to her.

It came anyway.

Threadweaver spread out below the treeline the way a stain spreads through cloth — stone first, then glass, then a school. The thread density over it was so thick that even with the blue-filter technique a third-year named **Wren Zhou** had taught her on the platform at Westbrook, Lyra's eyes began to ache. Ley lines ran the valley floor like rivers seen from a great height, silver-white, braided, and every one of them carried something moving along it.

*In for four, hold for four, out for four,* Wren had said. *Don't try to take the valley in. Take the one thread in front of you and let the rest be weather.*

*And if I can't?*

*Then you'll have a headache and a bad first afternoon.* Wren had grinned. *Everyone does. It's not a test. Nobody's marking you.*

Lyra had not entirely believed her.

"You have that look," Wren said now, from the seat across the aisle, "a potent cocktail of excitement and terror."

"Is it that obvious?"

"It's that obvious. I'm Wren Zhou. Third-year, resonance specialist, and I've been doing this for six years, and I still make that face at the gate." Wren turned her uniform cuff over and showed Lyra a burn scar the size of a coin, pale and very smooth. "First thread-quake I ever felt. I was eleven. I sat down in a corridor for forty minutes and refused to be helped up, and my halmeoni was *furious* with me about it, which was the only good part."

"Wren Zhou."

"Wren. Zhou is my grandmother's name to give away and I guard it jealously." Wren studied her. "You're Chen. The late one."

"That's me."

"Four months?"

"Since the last week of April."

"Late is unusual. Most manifest by twelve." Wren tilted her head, and Lyra had the unnerving sense of being weighed. "Modality?"

"Visual. Mainly. Light and colour, and—" Lyra hesitated, and then heard herself being exactly as imprecise as everyone at home had always been. "And more than that. Some things are texture. Some are almost temperature."

Wren's eyebrows went up. "Multi-spectrum. With a strong silver component." She said it like a diagnosis. "Do you know how rare the silver component is?"

"My grandmother's tradition has a name for it."

"Does it." Wren was quiet for a moment. "I asked my grandmother the same thing, once, and she said my seeing and my threads were in a *relationship* — that by perceiving them I was already taking part in what they did. I thought that was the kind of thing grandmothers say." She looked out at the valley. "The Academy teaches the opposite. It teaches that the threads are real and the perceptions are just windows. Both of those are in the curriculum. They are not reconciled, and anyone who tells you they are reconciled is selling something."

"Do you think they're the same truth?"

"I think it's a better question than the one on the exam." Wren stood, gathering a bag that had been repaired so many times the seams had their own seams. "Control your perception, Chen. Not the other way round. Whatever they tell you on your first afternoon."

The train began to slow.

## THE STATION

Threadweaver Station was built out of the mountain and had opinions about it. Lyra's eyes filled at the platform, and she did what she had practised on the train, which was nothing at all, and let the noise be weather.

Four boards, mounted in the vault rather than along the walls, so that you could not walk past them without looking up:

> **WEAVE-QUAKE COUNTER** — 17% · NOMINAL
> **NODE PROJECTION** — T+9 d, 14:00 (1997 survey, re-verified 2009)
> **LOCAL DISTORTION (EAST TOWER)** — 0.4% · nominal
> **COLLECTOR** — 4% of capacity

*Four different numbers,* Lyra thought. *Four different questions. The big one is the severity of the whole ward network, and it is a severity, not a percentage of anything you could point at. The second is the Council's model of the day on which the Southern Nexus gives up, and there is a date on it, and a date on a wall is a different kind of frightening from a number. The third is how much trouble this one building is in. The fourth is how full the old fear-collector under the gym is, and nobody knows what it's for, and it is not in the student handbook.*

She had read all four the way other people read the weather.

A boy of about her own age came past with a handcart of luggage and a thread signature like a struck bell, and Lyra flinched away from him before she could decide not to. A girl in a first-year's grey cloak was standing perfectly still in the middle of the concourse with her eyes shut, lips moving. Two third-years were arguing cheerfully about whether the coffee stall was cheating.

*All of them seeing. All of them on the first day.*

"Okay," Wren said, shouldering her bag. "Gate's that way, shuttle's after it, don't get on the shuttle before you've found your hall, they'll dump you in the wrong valley and it's a forty-minute walk in the wrong direction. Which is—"

"Look at the paper."

It was Wren who said it, and it was Wren who was looking at Lyra's hands, and Lyra looked down.

She had been holding her sketchbook the way she held it everywhere, flat against her stomach, and a folded sheet of rice paper had worked its way loose between the covers, and it was lying against her forearm in the open like a small white flag.

Thin. Almost translucent. Written in a careful classical hand in black ink. A seal in dark red at the foot.

"That paper—" Wren's voice dropped. "That's Security Chief Blackwood's stationery. Where did you get it?"

"I didn't. It was there on the eighteenth of August, when I opened the book to look for a charcoal stub, and I have been carrying it for seventeen days, and I have not shown it to one person."

"It's not in the book now."

It was. Lyra had shut it in the dark in her room, and it had been there when she'd packed, and it was there now; she had sixteen days of not looking at it and one day of looking at it. She had folded it in twice and put it in the back of the sketchbook under a page of charcoal studies, and that was a decision she had made and then not looked at since, which she understood now was a different thing from a decision.

Wren read her face and made a decision of her own. "Whatever that is, you have about ninety seconds before the shuttle doors close and forty-five minutes of walk if you miss it. So: read it, or burn it, or put it back and decide later. I am not going to tell you which, because I have known you for forty minutes and I don't know you at all."

She went.

Lyra opened the book.

The shuttle doors closed.

> *Board Members,*
>
> *The Academy's approach to thread knowledge remains fundamentally flawed. Their "preservation" is merely appropriation under academic guise. What was taken from my family — from countless traditional practitioners — cannot be recovered through institutional channels.*
>
> *The Syndicate's extraction methodology offers a more direct solution. When knowledge is hoarded, it must be reclaimed. The Magnus Conduit project progresses despite Council resistance. Our asset is now fully embedded and reports significant advancement in thread harvesting.*
>
> *The Chen girl's arrival presents both opportunity and risk. Her thread signature shows remarkable similarity to her mother's — multi-spectrum perception with silver resonance. Monitoring protocols are in place. If she manifests Animus Argenti affinity, extraction priority elevates to Alpha.*
>
> *Knowledge is power only when controlled. The Academy "preserves" but does not protect. We will do both.*
>
> *— M.B.*

The shuttle was warm. Somebody's shoulder was against Lyra's, and it was not her father, and her father was two hundred miles away in a house with his shoes by the bed and his tea going cold on the counter because he could not remember whether he had made it.

*Alpha.*

M.B. Security Chief Blackwood's stationery. The Academy's own security chief. *Our asset is now fully embedded.*

Her father had said, on the phone, the night before, in the voice he used when he was trying to sound reasonable: *If the people who want to help you are the same people who study people like you, then the question isn't who to trust. It's who's doing the asking.* And she had said *Bàba, I'm scared* and he had said *Good.* And she had not asked him what that meant, because it had been late and he had been tired and she was sixteen and it had been the night before a train.

*I'm scared too,* she thought. *I just don't have anyone to hand that to yet.*

She closed the sketchbook.

At the checkpoint a woman in dark uniform stood over the ramp, and her thread signature was a black-silver thing Lyra could not look at properly and so did not look at, and it was that refusal, more than anything, that told her the letter on the shuttle had not been a mistake.

"Letters out," the woman said. "New students, acceptance letters ready."

Lyra fumbled, and found the wrong thing, and the woman took it from her.

"Chen." The black-silver signature moved. Not much. It did not need to. "Any relation to the Lin Chen who was on the faculty here?"

"Yes." There was no version of this that was a lie. "Eleven years ago."

"Interesting." The signature probed Lyra's, the way a hand goes into a coat pocket, and she stood very still and let it. "Welcome to Threadweaver Academy, Ms. Chen. I trust you'll find it illuminating."

At her wrist, a silk charm the colour of twilight pulsed once. Just once. And the woman looked at it, and looked at Lyra, and said nothing at all.

## ORIENTATION

The Grand Hall held two hundred and eleven first-years, and Lyra had been in it for six minutes when the alarm went.

It was not the polite chimes she had been expecting. It was a sound like a blade drawn along a length of chain, and the thread-quake signage above the arch pulsed from amber to red, and four hundred threads of fear went up off the floor like heat off a road.

> **BREACH. THREAD-QUAKE DETECTED — EAST TOWER. ALL STUDENTS TO EMERGENCY POSITIONS.**

The practice matrix dropped out of the ceiling. Lyra had not expected anything like it — a lattice of a thousand spun filaments, eighteen feet across, and *wanting*, in the way a dog wants.

It reached for her.

She did not decide to answer. That was the part she would think about later, at three in the morning, for a long time. One moment she was standing in a doorway being frightened, and the next there was silver going out of her in a rope and the whole hall had gone bright as the inside of a bell, and two hundred and eleven people were folding up around her, and somebody was screaming, and it might have been her.

*I am going to hurt them.*

She tried to let go. There was nothing to let go *of*. Three months of not being able to do this, and it turned out that not doing it had not been practice.

"Hey." A voice, close, unhurried. "Look at me."

Milo Reyes had warm brown skin and a thread signature like a struck glass, and he had put himself directly between her and the matrix, and he was not moving away from her. That was the whole of it. That was the entire reason she did not go under.

"Breathe with me. In for four." He breathed. "Hold for four."

She breathed. In for four. Hold for four. Out for four.

"It's not you," said a girl at her other side, and put a hand flat on her arm, and stopped. "Sorry — you have to say yes first. It's not you. I can feel you from here and it's not you, it's the *frequency*. You're not too loud, you're just—" She hunted for it. "You're in a key nobody else is singing."

"Eleven point eight," said a boy with glasses who had appeared on her other side with a tablet he was clearly reading upside down. "Eleven point eight kilohertz. That's not random. That's a harmonic. If we can get her output down to match the room instead of fighting the room—" He pushed his glasses up. "Eli. Eli Park. And I want it on the record that this is the most interesting thing that has ever happened to me and I am *extremely* frightened."

"Eli," said the girl at her arm, with feeling. "Not now."

"I'm not saying now. I'm saying eventually. Zara. Zara Washington." She took her hand off Lyra's arm, waited, and put it back, and Lyra felt the difference: a warmth that was not a temperature, and *underneath* the warmth a structure. Cartouches. The tight blocks of a story being told around a name, a life, a debt.

"I'm Zara. My mother's family is from Egypt. We don't read people, we read the stories around them. You're a very short story, and it's not finished." Zara's mouth went tight. "Also, you're hurting about nine hundred people and I'd like that to stop before you decide anything else about yourself."

"Then we're here," said Milo, "and she's not doing this alone, and Eli, whatever eleven point eight is, use it."

"Already on it. Zara, I need you to hold her at four. Milo, I need you to give her a beat to aim at. Lyra—" Eli crouched, so that he was looking up at her, which she would think about later too. "You don't have to be quieter. You've just got to be *tuned*. There's a difference. Can you feel the difference?"

She could not.

"Okay, then do it the other way. Don't aim. Just—" Eli snapped his fingers, once, small. "Match that. And stop everything else."

She matched the snap. Not with a thread. With herself.

The matrix let go.

Later — in the shuttle, in the queue for the hall allocation, in the corridor outside the refectory, in the small hours of the night when it came back and she could not get it to go back again — Lyra would go over those four minutes and be unable to find the moment where she had been rescued.

Because that was not what had happened. Eli had given her a note. Milo had given her a place to stand. Zara had asked. And Lyra had taken the three of them and put herself in the middle of them, and the hall had gone quiet, and two hundred and eleven strangers had watched four people she had met eleven minutes earlier hold a door closed with nothing but attention.

*That's not me,* she kept thinking. *That's the three of them. I was only the noise.*

She had not yet understood that this was the shape her life was going to take.

## THE ARGUMENT

They were on the lawn with the rest of the two hundred and eleven, in the strange golden hour of a day that had already contained an alarm, and it was here that the quartet happened, properly, in the way that quarrels happen.

"I want to say something and then I want to be finished saying it," Eli said, "and I want to say it to you specifically, because you are the only one of us who has to hold anything."

He had a diagram on his tablet. She could not see it properly; she could feel it, a hard bright grid against her attention like graph paper.

"Numbers," Eli said. "You fed the matrix for eleven point eight seconds before we got a hand on you. Nobody in that hall was hurt. But the *East Tower* was. And the East Tower is where the loudest readings are coming from, and there has been a thread-quake every week since I arrived, and the Council says 'nominal' on a board in a station because nominal is under nineteen percent."

"We don't know that they're connected," Zara said.

"I know that I don't know they're *not*," Eli said, and for the first time his voice went flat and hard in a way that made Lyra's stomach drop, because he was frightened and Eli Park did not do frightened out loud. "And I know that the only reason we can have this conversation is that the alarm didn't kill us. That's not a foundation. That's a *miracle*, and people build on miracles and then they blame the miracle when it doesn't come back."

"Then what do you want to do?" Milo said. "There's a hundred and ninety kids here who don't know what any of us are."

"I want one of us to be *wrong* out loud, in public, where it costs something, and see what happens." Eli lowered the tablet. "I nominate Ms. Washington."

"Chur." Zara folded her arms. "Fine. I'm wrong. It's stress, it's the altitude, it's a hundred and ninety kids in a small room, and if the East Tower is draining us then the East Tower is not going to be fixed by four first-years in a fortnight." She said it flatly and without heat. "Now you tell me how that's *useful*."

"I said I wanted to see what would happen," Eli said.

"Everything happens," Milo said, and shouldered his bag. "Come on. If we're going to be wrong together, let's be hungry together first. I think there's soup."

They walked. It was a very ordinary walk, across a lawn, in the last of the light, in a valley full of silver rivers, and it changed the direction of Lyra's life, and the four of them did not know it, and the only one of them who had a way of knowing said nothing, because the thought that the afternoon had been *about* them, that anything at all in this enormous silver landscape had noticed two hundred and eleven people, seemed to her like a piece of vanity she did not have the right to.

But she walked between them. And when they reached the refectory she found she had already, without deciding to, taken the seat on Milo's left, which was the seat next to the wall, and Eli was already in it.

She put the sketchbook on the table under her tray, with the rice paper still folded inside it, in the back, under a page of charcoal studies.

*Tomorrow,* she thought. *Not now. Tomorrow I'll have a room, and a shelf, and somebody to ask, and then I'll take it to somebody who can do something with it.*

She ate her soup. It was very hot, and it was the first hot thing she had eaten in three days, and she had to stop and breathe for a moment.

*Seventeen per cent,* she thought. *Nine days. And a woman on a shuttle who already knows my name.*

She had come to the Academy for answers about her mother.
