What she had found, on the first day, was four people, a great deal of soup, and a piece of paper in her sketchbook that somebody very dangerous had written by hand, and had lost.

She had not decided yet what to do about it. She was sixteen, and it was not even six o'clock, and she had a whole term in front of her.

# Chapter 9: The Silver Burn

> "The threads reveal themselves in the way each mind is best equipped to understand them. A curriculum that assumes one kind of seeing is not a curriculum at all."
>
> — Eleanor Weaver, "Perception and Difference"

*Weave-Quake Counter: 18% instability. Node-failure projection: T+9 d, 14:00. Source: 1997 survey, re-verified 2009.*

## THE LECTURE

*The next morning. Thursday, 5 September. Lumina Lecture Hall.*

The hall felt wrong to her now, which was a thing she could not have told anyone about a week ago. On her first day it had been a large room. It was still a large room. But she had spent one day in it, learning that the stone under the floor carried thread the way a riverbed carries a current, and that the tall keystone above the arch was threaded so densely it had a hum, and that Professor Armitage's introductory jokes landed in that hum and came out changed.

She knew the room. That was the problem. You cannot sit in a lecture and also be in the walls.

Professor Armitage was not his usual ebulligent self. He introduced the afternoon's session in the tone of a man reading a weather forecast he did not believe.

"—no lecture notes. Professor Harlow has asked us to work from first principles. His work after Kyoto is not on the reading list. It should be. It isn't, and I have raised that four times." Armitage's eyes went briefly to the back of the room and came away. "He has asked us not to take notes."

"Sir," somebody said, with the specific optimism of a first-year.

"I have asked him three times. He has asked us four times. Three days into the term, Ms. Chen, and already the two of you are the only people in this hall who have noticed that the number on the board has not moved."

A low murmur. Lyra had noticed. She had been looking at it in the station, in the queue for the hall allocation, in the refectory, on the way back from the refectory. Seventeen on the train. Seventeen yesterday. Eighteen this morning, and the model had shaved two hours off the forecast without saying why, and nobody in the year group had reacted to that at all, because they did not know that the two hours was the news.

She had a folded piece of rice paper in her sketchbook that somebody very dangerous had lost, and she had told nobody, and she had told herself four separate times that she would tell somebody today.

She had not told anybody.

Harlow came out.

She had braced for something. She had braced, since the train, for the ozone and the jasmine and the particular quality of attention that came off him like a hand laid flat on the back of her neck. What she had not braced for was that he looked ordinary. Grey suit, no jewellery except the small paper crane at his throat. He set a sheaf of notes on the lectern and squared them, and the gesture was so exactly like her father's that her chest hurt.

He did not look at her. That was the other thing she had braced for, and it did not happen either. He looked at the whole room, evenly, the way a lecturer does who has decided to be fair.

"Threads," he said, "are not a metaphor."

He let that sit for exactly as long as it needed to.

"They are a medium. They have properties. They carry, they attenuate, they interfere, they can be induced, they can be damped, and — this is the part your textbooks are coy about — they respond to intention with a measurable lag of four to nine tenths of a second. Four tenths. The time it takes to flinch."

He wrote on the board without turning around.

`AN INTENT IS NOT A CAUSE. IT IS A DELAYED CAUSE.`

"That number is the most important thing I will teach you this year. Every disaster in our institutional memory traces back to somebody who believed that wanting a thing hard enough was the same as doing it. The gap between those two is four tenths of a second, and in four tenths of a second a city can be opened."

Somewhere behind her, a student laughed, uneasily, at somebody else's joke.

"I want to be precise about what I am not saying. I am not saying measurement is cruelty. I am saying that a discipline which can count things and a discipline which is frightened of counting things are not two points of balance — they are one point and no point, and the no point is where the bodies go."

*He's talking about Kyoto.* Lyra's hand had closed around the edge of the bench. *He is standing in front of two hundred people and talking about Kyoto and not saying Kyoto.*

"You are, I am told, unsettled by the quantitative framing." His gaze came up. It found her. "So let us test it."

He did not name her. He did not need to; in a first-year lecture there is precisely one person who has filed a written objection.

"Some of you perceive texture where others perceive colour. Some perceive temperature. Some perceive sound." He said it lightly, as though he were describing a preference in a restaurant. "I am told the plurality here is unusually high. Very well. Question: does the fact that your perception is a *kind* of texture establish that the thing you are perceiving is *only* texture?"

Milo, two seats along, had his hand up. "Professor — is that the same as asking whether the medium is the message?"

"It is a prettier version of the same question, and I like Mr. Reyes for asking it, and the answer to both is no." Harlow set down his chalk. "The medium is the message in the sense that you cannot get the message out of the medium by standing further back. But the medium is not the *whole* of what is being said. A nerve does not know that it is a nerve. It knows heat."

Eli's hand went up. "Then what is the Observer Effect doing in our first-year texts? Because at minute forty of the syllabus it says—"

"Minute forty of the syllabus says that a sufficiently sensitive instrument changes the system it measures. That is *true*. It is also the most useful sentence in your year and the most dangerous, and I will spend an entire afternoon on it in three weeks and I will spend it on the difference between *a measurement being taken* and *a decision being made about a person on the basis of a measurement*." He looked around the room. "Write that down. You may write that down."

Two hundred pens.

"Final matter, and then questions."

He returned to the board. The hall leaned in.

"The failures in our records are not failures of imagination. They are failures of arithmetic. Kyoto was not a surprise. It was a *projection*, run in 1993, correctly, on incomplete data, and it said that a nexus under a populated valley, held above tolerance by a controlled draw, will fail catastrophically when the draw is interrupted and the lag is longer than the reserve." He wrote it. "Nine years later the draw was interrupted, by a blade, in a garden, for the reasons that blades in gardens are for. The projection was right. It was right in 1993. The only person who did not believe it was me."

Nobody moved.

"So when I tell you that an acceptable attrition rate of two to three percent becomes a moral imperative when the alternative is global collapse —" and here his voice did the thing Lyra had felt through three walls and a floor, the small drop into something much colder and much more sincere — "I am not being cold. I am being the third person to run the numbers, and the second of us to be believed, and I would like very much for this generation to be the first for whom being believed is not a precondition of being reasonable."

He stepped back from the board.

"Questions."

The silence lasted a beat and a half.

Eli nudged her. She had not noticed she had stopped breathing. "Your observation," he said, low, urgent. "The thing you said to Ms. Rivera. The *feel* of it. Ask him."

She put her hand up. It felt like putting a hand into a fire and then finding the fire was on her side.

"Professor Harlow. You said a measurement is not the same as a decision made about a person on the basis of a measurement. But the Animus Argenti classification is a measurement." She heard her own voice come out steadier than she had any right to expect. "It's in my file. It's on the station board's sibling — it's in the intake system, and it has a category, and the category has a *priority number* attached to it, and priority numbers are for triage." She swallowed. "Who decides which measurements get a priority number, and are they told the number exists?"

She heard her own pulse in her ears. She heard, distinctly, Milo make a small injured noise beside her — he could not hear a pulse; the fact that he had made that noise anyway told her what his face was doing.

Harlow looked at her for perhaps a second and a half longer than a lecturer needs to.

"Miss Chen." He inclined his head. Not a hawk this time. Something more careful. "That is a very good question and I am going to answer it in a way that is administratively useless to you on purpose. The answer is: a committee, of which I am one, and which meets, and which I have argued with for eleven years, and which I have never once won. That is the truthful answer. It is also, I expect, a more useful one to you than any other answer I could have given in this room."

*Eleven years.* The number landed somewhere in her chest and stayed there.

"And on the classification itself: a priority number is a *triage instrument*. It was designed, in 1999, for a collapse in the Pacific Northwest, to decide who receives a scarce stabilising agent first. It was never intended to order people. It has been used to order people. That is not the same as saying it was designed to do it, and I am aware of how much cheaper that distinction is in a courtroom than in a valley."

"Then stop using it," Eli said, before he could stop himself.

Someone laughed. It was not a nice laugh, and it was not aimed at Eli, and Harlow let it stand.

"Mr. Park has been at this school three days." He almost smiled. "On Friday that sentence will be three days old, and he will be right, and in three weeks I shall have been at this for sixteen years, and I shall still not have stopped. Perhaps that is the answer to Ms. Chen's question, and perhaps it is worth a mark."

The questions after that were ordinary, and Harlow answered them ordinarily, and Lyra sat with her hands flat on the bench and understood something she had not understood on Monday.

*He is not lying to me.* The thought arrived with a small cold shock. *He is lying to me — he has lied to me, he is lying to me right now, about the pendant, about my mother, about the file. But he is not lying about the committee. He is going to be the villain in this and he is telling me, on the record, in a lecture hall, that he lost an argument about me eleven years ago and lost it again today.*

*That is what it looks like from the inside. That is what it has always looked like from the inside.*

She was aware, distantly, of an irony. Yesterday she had sat on a lawn and let four people tell her she was not the problem. Now she had sat in a lecture hall and let a man tell her, in front of two hundred witnesses, that she was the only student in the building asking the right question.

*Both of those things are true. Neither of them cancels the other.*

Milo found her in the corridor with his hands in his hair and an expression she had never seen on him.

"What did you just do?"

"Ask a question."

"You asked a question in front of Harlow about a classification with a priority number in it." Milo put his hands down. "Lyra. That is not a question. That is a *statement* you dressed up so he couldn't object to it."

"Yes."

"And?"

"And I don't know yet." She shifted her sketchbook under her arm. It was heavier this week. "That's the honest answer. I have a thing I should show somebody and I keep not showing it, and I don't know whether that's because I'm frightened or because I'm wrong about who to show it to."

"You've been strange since yesterday."

"I've had a strange week."

"You've had a strange *life*, and you keep pretending it's a strange week." He said it without any edge, which somehow made it worse. "Eli and Zara want to know. Tonight. Common room. Bring the thing."

"All right."

"All right." He turned off down the corridor, and then turned back. "For what it's worth — the answer's yes. Ask him things. In front of everyone. That's the only lever you've got and you're the only person in that hall who can reach it."

He was gone.

Lyra stood in a corridor of a school she had been in for one day and took the sketchbook out and looked at the back of it, at the page of charcoal studies, and thought about a woman writing *extraction priority elevates to Alpha* in a careful classical hand on very expensive paper.

She still did not show it to anybody.

That afternoon, at 15:47, her Academy tablet lit up on the bench beside her and would not stop.

> **MEDICAL ALERT — HIGH PRIORITY**
> **CHEN, WEI — WESTBROOK NEUROLOGICAL INSTITUTE, ROOM 247**
> **Cardiac arrest 15:41. Resuscitated 15:47 — ROSC achieved. Stent placed. Admitted to CCU.**
> **Attending note: the arrest was not seizure. It was a ruptured vessel in a heart already failing. Nothing that happened today caused it. This was coming.**
> **Prognosis: guarded. The damage to the muscle is old. There is no bleed-related mechanism here that we can reverse.**
> **Family contact required. Immediate.**

The tablet fell out of her hands onto the flagstones, and the sound it made was very small, and went on being small.

She got down on the flagstones to pick it up, which took a while.

*Nothing that happened today caused it. This was coming.*

She had spent a day being afraid in a new building, and a man had spent a day looking at her, and a woman on a shuttle had written a word in a careful hand, and none of it — *none of it* — was what was happening to her father, and she stood in the corridor of Threadweaver Academy and discovered that the fear she had been managing was the wrong fear, and that she had been managing it very well, and that this was not a relief.

# Chapter 10: The Gauntlet

*Weave-Quake Counter: 19% instability. Node-failure projection: T+9 d, 14:00. Source: 1997 survey, re-verified 2009.*

*Friday 6 September.*

## THE CALL AT SEVEN

The telephone in the east corridor of the clinic had a cord on it, and the cord was too short to reach the stairwell, and Lyra discovered that she had to stand in a specific doorway to be heard and not be overheard, and that she stood in it for forty minutes on Friday morning, and that Professor Tanaka found her there at ten to eight and said nothing at all about it, which Lyra would think about later.

Her father was in Ward C at Westbrook and had been for a day. He was not allowed to travel and had stopped arguing about it on Thursday night, which was new and worse.

"They've put a number on me," he said. "Thirty-one. And the woman who says the number has said the word *ejection* in a corridor, twice, in front of my mother, and my mother has started taking notes."

"What are the notes for?"

"Your grandmother. She rang me. She has a great deal of experience of people who are frightened in institutions and none whatsoever of cardiac patients, and she has decided to run this." A pause; the sound of a man turning a cup. "She has also said a sentence to me that I am going to repeat to you exactly once and then never again. She said: *Bàba ba. Ask them the number for the other thing. Not the heart. The other thing. Ask them the number for the other thing and write it down, and do not let them put it in a drawer.*"

Lyra stood in a doorway with a receiver against her ear and looked at the corridor.

"The other thing is the bleed," she said.

"The other thing is the bleed, yes."

"They said on Saturday that on its own it would have given you years."

"They said that. I have it in writing, because I asked for it in writing, and I would like you to notice that I asked, because I have never once in my life asked for anything in writing, and I did it on Saturday morning in a corridor outside a bookshop, and I did it because a woman I have never met looked at me and said *the shape of that sentence is the shape of everything this industry says* and I wanted the sentence in a drawer that was mine."

Line seven said *the transfer requires a Council countersignature and a consultant cardiologist's letter and there is no consultant cardiologist on a Sunday.*

"Tell them a teacher is coming," Lyra said.

"Lyra."

"Tell them my grandmother is coming and she is eighty-one and she has been extremely unpleasant to two doctors already, and tell them that if anybody at that hospital says the word *ejection* in front of her again she will ask them, in front of the ward, to explain the number, and that she will do it politely."

Her father laughed, and then had to stop, because of the laughing.

"I'll ring you tonight," he said. "Go to your examination. Lyra — the other number. Whatever it is, when they give it to you, *do not let them put it in a drawer.*"

---

## THE TRIAL

"You have one hour and forty minutes and seven stations, and I want to say something about that before we start."

Professor Tanaka had them in a semicircle in the wet grass below the East Tower with a flask of coffee and a clipboard she did not consult, and she had the air of a woman who had been running this for nineteen years and had stopped trying to impress anybody.

"Every year a first-year cohort comes down here having been told that they are a team. Most of them are not. Most of them are four people who arrived on the same bus. You are going to find out which you are in about forty minutes, and I am not going to help you, because the help is the part that does not count."

"Station one is the Mirror Walk. Station two is a bridge of held sound. Station three is the Well. Station four is a set of equations that will not sit still. Station five is a room with a door in it that is not a door. Station six, I am not going to describe, because the last cohort that described it to themselves did it badly for a fortnight afterwards. Station seven is Professor Harlow."

Silence.

"Two things about station seven, and then I will let you get on with it. One: he is authorised, he is supervised, and I will be in the room. Two: he will offer you something. Everyone who reaches station seven is offered something. Accepting it is not the wrong answer and refusing it is not the right one, and the only thing I am going to assess is whether you can hear the offer and still know what it costs." She looked along the row. "Miss Chen. You are going to be sent through first. Do not argue; I have been sent through first. It is unpleasant and it is over in ninety seconds and then I will not be able to help you at all for a week, and the second time will be much worse, and that is the design."

---

Station one took the shape of a corridor with mirrors down both sides, and the mirrors did the obvious cruel thing, which was to show each of them the thing they had been most careful about.

Lyra's corridor showed a hospital at four in the morning and a woman with grey eyes saying a number, and then showed her, that same morning, standing in a doorway with a receiver against her ear and *not telling anybody*, and being unable to make the second image less accurate than the first.

Zara's showed a room in Alexandria with nine people in it, all of them shouting, and underneath, in much smaller writing, *and one of them is you*.

Eli's showed a proof he had got wrong in a way that could not be got wrong in, and his own handwriting, saying: *this is what it looks like when you decide a thing is true before you have checked it.*

Milo's was silence, and then a piano, and then the same silence.

"Do not look directly at them," Zara said, in a voice that had gone thin. "They're not showing us the past. They're showing us the assignment."

"Is it showing us what we'll do?"

"It's showing us what we'd *like* to have done," said Eli, "which is worse, and it is why the station is called the Walk and not the Test. Keep moving. Don't answer anything it says."

The mirrors began, around the second corner, to say things. Lyra heard her mother's voice — not the memory of it, the *recording* of it, right down the consonants, saying *if I go alone they will write it up as a woman alone* — and she stopped walking, and had to be touched on the shoulder by Milo, and made a noise she was embarrassed about for a week.

"They're feeding on the pauses," she said.

"They're feeding on the pauses," Eli agreed, and wrote it down, and Lyra noticed that he wrote it down and thought: *he is frightened and he is taking notes, and both of those are the same thing.*

At the far end of the corridor a boy from the row behind them was standing with his hands over his mouth and not moving, and there were seven of them, in a knot, in the second mirror.

"We are not leaving them," Lyra said.

"You are not leaving them," Milo agreed. "But we are going to need a way to walk them out of a corridor that only opens for a group, and I would like it noted that *I have not been consulted about that*, and that I have already worked out a way, and that it is extremely stupid."

"Do it."

"It is extremely stupid. Zara, take the two worst. Eli, take the ones who are following Eli. Ms. Chen—" he looked at her. "You have to be the one who talks them out, and not me, and not them, and I would like it noted that I know what I am doing."

---

Station two was a bridge of held sound, and Milo built it.

That was his station and everyone knew it within about nine seconds, and it was the first time in Lyra's life she watched somebody do something with a gift that made her understand what *competent* meant, as distinct from what *powerful* meant. He did not hold the whole bridge. He held the beat, and the beat was a low four, and everybody else found their place in it.

Eli counted and told them when to step. Zara took the two who were crying and stood in the gap next to them and did not speak, and Lyra — who had been told on the platform at Westbrook not to look up, and who could not have read a room if she had, and was therefore, as she discovered, extremely useful, because she was the only one of them who had to walk backwards — walked backwards in front of the seven for ninety metres above a drop, watching their feet.

"Talk me through it," she said. "Somebody talk me through it the whole way or I'm going to look down."

"Four," said Eli.

"Four," said Milo.

"Four," said Zara, from somewhere behind, and a boy of fifteen said "four?" in a voice like a man asking for water, and said it four times over the next ninety seconds, and did not put his hand on the rail, and got all the way across.

---

Station three was the Well, and it was Zara's, and it nearly finished her.

It was not a test of empathy. Lyra worked that out at about the second minute, from the sound of it. It was a test of *boundary*. There was a resonance in that shaft that was eleven years of every student who had ever gone into it, and it was not hostile, and it was not asking anything of them, and it went on without them, and it would go on if every one of them walked out.

Zara went in. Zara came out at eleven minutes and sat down on the wet grass and said, in a completely level voice, "I can feel every single person in this building, including Professor Harlow, including the woman at the security desk, including somebody in a sub-basement who is very frightened, and I want to be alone for an hour, and I would like somebody to tell me when the hour is up, and I would like it to be somebody who is not being kind at me."

They told her when the hour was up. Nobody was kind at her. Milo, who had brought soup and put it outside the door and gone away again, was the only one who got thanked.

---

Station four was Eli's, and it was mathematics, and he did badly and knew it.

"They are affected by our emotional states," he said, about four minutes in, with his glasses off and his hands in his hair. "No. No, that is what the wall is *telling* me, because the wall wants me to believe the problem is me. The equations are not affected by me. The equations are *being changed* by something in this room and the something in this room is a projection and I am the one projecting it, because I am the only one here who would rather be told the maths was the problem than admit that the projection is mine."

"Which means?"

"Which means *I am generating the obstacle and then solving it, and every solution I produce is a real solution to a problem that does not exist, and that is the most Eli Park thing that has ever happened in a room.*" He sat down on the step. "Halmeoni used to say that the hardest thing about a mistake you make with your own mind is that it *works*. I would like the record to show that I got through station four on a problem I invented."

"You still got through it."

"I got through it *on a lie*," said Eli Park, "and I have written that down, and I am going to think about it for years, and it is the single most useful thing anybody has made me do at this school."

---

Station six was a room with a door in it that was not a door, and Lyra has never been able to describe it except by saying that it was about wanting, and that the four of them argued about it afterwards for two days.

Station five she skipped, because the Well had taken it out of her, and Tanaka let her, and wrote *5 — deferred* on the clipboard without comment, which Lyra understood at the time as kindness and understood about a month later as a decision.

---

Station seven was a circle of silver light in the wet grass, and Harlow standing in the middle of it, and Tanaka on the outside with a folder under her arm.

"Forty minutes," he said. "You have forty minutes and I am going to spend them asking you to come and work with me, and you are going to refuse, and both of those things are going to be true, and I am going to be honest with you about why I am doing it, which is that I have asked four hundred and six students this and roughly one in nine says yes, and I would like to know what the other eight are made of."

He did not make an argument. That was the thing Lyra could not put down afterwards. She had braced for an argument, and for a threat, and for the ozone-and-jasmine thing; and what she got was a man standing in a circle in the rain being reasonable at her.

"You are the strongest signature I have seen in a first-year since 2002," Harlow said. "I would like to be able to say that I asked. I am not going to dress this up. There is no clause. I am asking because I have a machine under this mountain that is doing arithmetic I cannot finish on my own, and you would finish it in a fortnight, and I would pay for your mother's outstanding fees, which are nothing, and I would pay for your father's transfer here tomorrow, which is not nothing, and I would do both of those things and you would be able to check."

Eli said, "That is an offer."

"That is an offer," Harlow agreed.

"And you are going to refuse it," Zara said, "and I want to know what you are refusing, exactly, before you refuse it, because you are not refusing *him*, you are refusing something much more useful, and I have watched you do this twice this week."

"I know what I am refusing."

"Say it out loud, then, so that there is a record."

Lyra thought about the doorway on Ellery Street, and about a woman counting her daughter's breaths, and about a jade pendant that was four hundred years old and had only ever worked for one person at a time, and about a woman who had said *nobody is coming with me, that is the entire problem* and had then said, four hours later, *if I go alone, they will write it up as a woman alone.*

"I'm not refusing you," Lyra said. "I'm refusing being the only one who decided."

She heard it go round the circle.

Harlow's head came up very slightly.

"Again," he said.

"You want it in the form you can file." Lyra found that her hands were shaking and did not try to stop them. "My mother is the woman on the recording from the second corner. She told my grandmother *nobody is coming with me, that is the entire problem*, and four hours later she said if she went alone they would write it up as a woman alone, and that in ten years there would be a girl who could do what she could do and she would go alone too, and it would be the correct reading again. I'm not her. I've got three people standing in a circle in the rain. So the honest answer to your offer is: yes, and not like that, and you have to sit down and tell me what the machine is for, and I will take it to my father and to the doctor and to a person I have not met yet whose name I am going to find out, and *then* you can have me. Or you can carry on asking in circles, and I will carry on refusing, and we will both be extremely polite about it for years."

Tanaka's folder shut.

For about four seconds nothing in the circle moved, and in those four seconds Lyra had a completely clear, and completely unwelcome, thought: *I have just made an enemy who is going to be the most useful person I ever meet.*

Then Harlow laughed — one syllable, entirely without warmth, and not at all a performance — and said, "Miss Chen, that is the first time in eleven years that anybody has refused me in a form I could not file."

"Sir?"

"That was a compliment. It is also a serious problem." He was already walking. "Tanaka, your four are dismissed. Ms. Chen — a word, outside, and it will take four minutes and you will not enjoy it."

---

He was entirely correct; she did not enjoy it.

"Three things," he said, under the colonnade, with the rain going past. "One. Your refusal was correct and it was also very nearly too late, and I am not going to explain what I mean, because if I explain it you will carry it around and it will get you hurt. Two." He took out a card, and did not give it to her, and put it away again. "Somebody in this building has been reading your file since the twenty-second of August and has not filed a report, and it is not me, and it is not Dr. Osei, and I would like you to find out who, because I have an interest in you finding out and that is the reason I am telling you at all. Three."

"Three?"

"Thirty-one per cent." He said it the way Dr. Osei had said *stable*. "That is the number the model puts on the capacity of the thing under this mountain that your mother's notes in that room this afternoon were about, and it is the number I would give to the Council on Thursday, and it is a *severity* and not a percentage of anything, and the difference between those two things is the difference between a doctor and a murderer, and somebody in this building is going to have to explain it to two hundred people, and I am not going to be the one who has to, because I have been the one who has to for four years and I am finished."

He walked off into the rain.

Lyra stood under the colonnade for a while.

Then she went and found a telephone, and telephoned Westbrook, and asked for Ward C.

---

*Weave-Quake Counter: 19% instability. Node-failure projection: T+9 d, 14:00. Source: 1997 survey, re-verified 2009.*

*Later. The east corridor, 22:40.*

> *Number for the other thing, since you asked: the bleed is at nineteen per cent of a load that a healthy adult runs at about four, and the load went up when you arrived, because you are the first multi-spectrum in this building since 2002 and everything in this valley is leaning toward you, and it is not going to come back down on its own, and it has been at nineteen for eleven days and it has been at nineteen for eleven days because I have been putting something in his chart every morning at 08:00 and taking it out at 08:15, which I have been doing for eleven days without a doctor's order, and if you tell anybody I will deny it, and I would like you to decide tonight what you are going to do about it, and I have written it on this side of the paper so that you cannot say you did not have it.*
>
> *Get him here. Do not ask them to send anything.*
>
> *Bàba*

---

*Thread Resonance Note: Gauntlet, cohort 1, 6 September. Seven of eight completed; 5 deferred by invigilator on medical grounds (see note). Station 7: offer made in full, terms stated, declined in a form recorded verbatim by the invigilator. Recommendation from M.H. that the file for C-2214 be reviewed by somebody other than this office. Recommendation from this office that the C-2214 file be reviewed by a person who is not a member of this office, is not the family, and is not the Headmistress. # Chapter 11: The Magnus Conduit

*Weave-Quake Counter: 20% instability. Node-failure projection: T+9 d, 14:00. Source: 1997 survey, re-verified 2009.*

## THE DOORWAY

*Tuesday 10 September.*

"First-years, into the courtyard, and stand in a line and do not talk."

Professor Tanaka had a way of arriving that Lyra only later understood was a technique: she never walked into a room, she arrived in it, and the room had about four seconds in which to decide what it was.

Fifty of them stood in the wet on flagstones. The East Tower was behind them, a dome of stone and glass with a doorway cut into it by somebody who had wanted, very badly, to be fair to everybody, and the doorway was a list.

Chinese knots, deep crimson, tying and untieing themselves in a slow and beautiful rhythm, and Lyra understood after four seconds that they were *wrong* — that they were knots seen from the outside, from a book, by somebody who had never had to tie one with cold hands.

Below them, yantras in pale gold, geometric to a degree that was almost aggressive.

Adinkra, in flat black on the pale stone, stamped rather than drawn, and near the right-hand post one of them had been left half-erased at some point and was coming back.

And framing all of it, holding all of it, running round the arch in three straight bands: Latin script. Sans serif. A classification index.

"The arch has been rebuilt four times," Tanaka said, without looking at it. "The last time was 1954. I have asked for the 1954 drawings and been told that the 1954 drawings are in the 1954 drawer. The whole of this institution's relationship with your grandmother's practice is in that archway, and it is a good archway, and I have never once seen a first-year look at it and see anything except decoration, so I am going to say the thing out loud, once, and then never again: *half of that stonework was made by people who were not permitted to write anything down, and we have kept the stonework.*"

Ninety seconds of silence.

"Morning," Milo said, cheerfully. "Everyone's fine. Nobody's sick. It's raining."

"He'll do that all term," said Zara, low. "It's his whole—"

"I know. It's *disgusting*." She was watching Lyra. "You've gone white. Which bit."

"The knots," Lyra said. "They've been done from a picture."

## THE CALIBRATION

"Late awakeners go first," said Tanaka. "Not as a kindness. Because the field is calibrated against the cohort and a late awakener is the only person in the room whose number is not already in the file, and I would like one honest reading before the day gets a shape to it."

Fifty heads turned to Lyra, which was the single most unpleasant eleven seconds of her life to date, and which she was to remember for the rest of her life as *the moment I understood that a gift is a scheduling problem before it is anything else*.

"Chen, Lyra."

"Why me—"

"Mr. Reyes has been explaining it to you for forty seconds and doing it badly," said Tanaka. "You are the only person here whose instrument has not been calibrated this term. Come and be honest at me for ninety seconds and then go and be terrified in private like everybody else."

---

The archway was cool, and the stone under her hand was cool, and the air going in smelled of a dry cellar and old paper and, very faintly, of cardamom.

She put her hand on the left-hand post, where a Chinese knot was tying itself in crimson, and it was *loose*.

That was the whole of it. Everything else — the light, the density, the way the Maze seemed to be made of a thousand overlapping systems all talking at once — was the most extraordinary thing Lyra Chen had ever stood inside. But what she actually remembered, for the rest of her life, was that the knot on the left-hand post was loose, and that nobody in the building had looked at it in fifty-nine years.

"Ms. Chen." The voice was not in the room. It came from the stone, or from behind her sternum, or from some third place that she would spend a lot of the following week trying to describe to Osei. "The Maze sees your signature. Multi-spectrum, with a strong silver component. Late awakening, four months, which is late. Deep ancestral structure — four lines, two of them very old and one of them in a country I am not going to name." A pause that felt like a held breath in a machine. "Begin."

---

It was not frightening. She had prepared to be frightened for eleven days.

The first chamber had a table in it and three objects on the table, and the room asked her to find what joined them, and she found it in eleven seconds and did not enjoy it. The second asked something about weight. The third about sequence. The fourth was about a thing she had done, badly, in an art room in April, and the Maze had a copy of it, and it showed her the copy, and Lyra stood in the middle of a chamber under a mountain and looked at a diagram of herself pulling two loose ends together and knotting them, and had to sit down.

*It has been keeping records.*

The fifth chamber was a corridor of standing figures, and as she went down it they turned, one after another, and each of them was somebody she knew, and each of them had a thing in their face she had not seen, and she got to the end of it having learned nothing and having felt everything, and stood in the archway afterwards shaking so hard that Milo had to hold her by the shoulders.

"That's four," he said. "That's four minutes. You okay?"

"No."

"All right." He handed her the flask. "Brew made that an hour ago and it's gone cold and it's got a skin on it, and you're going to drink it anyway, because the alternative is that you pass out in front of Tanaka and she writes *5 — deferred* on a clipboard and then feels bad about it for a week."

---

The sixth chamber asked her to hold something still.

She got it wrong for about forty seconds, and then she got it right, and the reason she got it right was the sentence a woman in a kitchen on a Sunday had put in her hand with a piece of silk and refused to explain.

*The eye sees the ripple. The hand feels the tide.*

The Maze seemed to consider this for a while.

"Perception test. Unconventional approach." Its voice had changed fractionally, which she noticed and would later be unable to persuade anyone of. "Proceed."

The seventh chamber was empty, and almost entirely dark, and it asked her to find a path that was not lit.

She almost made it. She got about two-thirds of the way along a passage of patterns too fine to see properly, by closing her eyes and walking on the faint resistance of the air against her skin — and then the pattern *moved*, and the air went wrong, and every thread in the passage came alive at once and shrieked, and Lyra went down on the polished floor in the dark with her hands over her ears.

And in the middle of that, a silver line.

Not in the wall. Not on the floor. In the air, about a foot in front of her knees, at the height of a person who was sitting down.

She had never seen anything like it. Everything else in the Maze was *about* something. That line was a thing in itself: quiet, fine, older than the stone, and going on without her, and it did not care in the slightest whether she looked at it.

"Oh," said Lyra Chen.

She followed it, and it took her to a door that was not one of the seventeen, and the door opened, and beyond it was a room with patterns on every surface and a stone pedestal in the middle of it and a crystal the size of a fist lying on the pedestal, silver, and turning very slowly.

*Animus Argenti chamber. Restricted access. Signature recognition: Chen, Lin. Accessed once before. Eleven years prior. Similar resonant signature. Access window: four months.*

*Four months.* Spring 2002. The months her father did not talk about. The months in which, by every other account, her mother had been extremely happy.

The silver line came off the crystal and reached for her, and where it reached her she felt the thing she had felt in a kitchen and in a school corridor and in a hospital corridor, and it was the thing the jade did: the room got *deeper*.

The crystal was cold. It was also, unmistakably, *pleased*.

"Animus Argenti fragment," said the Maze, and the voice had come a great deal closer. "A silver bridge. Remnant. Left by previous visitor. She was here for one hour and eleven minutes and she did not take it, and she wrote on the wall behind you, in English, and I have not shown it to anybody in eleven years, and I am going to show it to you, and then this chamber is going to stop existing, and I would like you to understand that I am not being kind. I am closing a file."

Lyra turned round.

There was pencil on the wall, very old, very faint, and it said:

> *It is a door. I am not going to be the one who opens it, because I have worked out that the reason nobody has opened it in eighty years is that everyone who gets here has been told it is the only way. — L.C., 4 May*

---

She reached for the crystal with her right hand.

She was two inches from it when the floor came up, and everything went white, and there was a noise like a bar of iron being bent, and the wall behind her — the wall with the pencil on it — went.

"Exit now," said the Maze, and it was not calm any more. "Exit. *Now.*"

The pedestal dropped into the floor. A compartment came up out of the place the pedestal had been — a narrow one, a service one, with a pneumatic hiss — and inside it there were four rolled tubes of paper, and Lyra's hand went in and came out with one of them, and the compartment shut on her fingers, and the crystal went down, and the archway at the far end turned red.

She ran.

Behind her, something very large and very old finished coming apart, and the sound of it went on in her right ear for the rest of her life. The arch itself held. She did not know that yet, and neither did anybody else in the courtyard, and the difference between those two facts was the whole of the next three days.

---

She came out of the archway into the courtyard into ordinary rain, holding a roll of paper, and stood in the middle of flagstones with two hundred people in a building and the east half of a stone arch down across the flagstones in pieces, and the span still up, and the two posts still standing in it, and the passage through it open to the weather.

And standing about four metres away, in the rain, in a dark uniform, absolutely still, was a woman Lyra had seen once, on a ramp, on Monday.

Security Chief Blackwood.

She had not been there when Lyra went in. Lyra was certain of that in the way you are certain of things you have checked. And Blackwood was not looking at the archway.

Blackwood was looking at the paper in Lyra's hand, and then at Lyra's face, and then — and this was the part that Lyra would take into every room for the rest of the year — Blackwood's left hand went, very briefly, to a silk charm at her wrist, and gave it a small deliberate tap with her thumb, and stopped.

It was a gesture. It was not a tic.

"Ms. Chen." Professor Tanaka was coming across the flagstones at a speed that was not dignified and did not pretend to be. "Are you hurt. Look at me. Are you hurt."

"I'm fine."

"Say it properly."

"I'm not hurt." Lyra heard how it sounded and could not improve it. "There was an eighth room. It wasn't on the list. And the board in the antechamber went from twenty to twenty-three in about nine seconds, and the word NOMINAL off the end of it, and then the floor—"

Tanaka stopped.

She stood in the rain and looked at the archway, and then at the board over the anteroom door, and something went across her face that Lyra could not read and would think about for a long time.

"Twenty-three," Tanaka said, very evenly. "That is nine seconds."

"That's what I—"

"Ms. Chen, the wall board has not read twenty-three in the eleven years I have been on this staff." She turned. "Security Chief, I am going to need the sub-level instruments for the East Tower, not the campus board, the ones on the buried line, and I am going to need them now, and I am going to need a witness for the request, because I have learned this year how to ask for that."

Blackwood did not move.

"Chief."

"The buried line is sealed under an order from the Council's own office," said Blackwood. Her voice was level and pleasant and had a smile somewhere inside it that Lyra could not locate. "Signed in 2009. I would be glad to raise a request, Professor. It would take some days."

"Would it."

"It would take some days," said Blackwood, "and there is a form, and I am sure the registrar would be glad to countersign it." She turned, and looked at Lyra one more time, unhurried, the way you look at something you have already decided about. "It is very hot for September."

And she went, into the colonnade, and Lyra stood in the rain and understood two things at once.

The first was that the woman had known about the eighth room.

The second was that she had told somebody, in that gesture, in that second, that she had known.

---

Milo came out of the archway with his hair on end and his shirt half out of his trousers and the enormous uncomplicated happiness of a boy who has just done a thing well.

"**Nine**," he announced, to nobody, to everybody. "Nine chambers. *Nine.* There's a first-year record of seven and I have beaten a first-year record of seven by *two* and I want it written down before somebody comes out here and tells me it doesn't count."

"Milo—"

"I know. I *know.* The arch. Half of it came down." He stopped, and looked at the archway, and at the rubble, and up at the span, which was still there, and then at the two hundred faces appearing along the colonnade, and then at Lyra, and then at the roll of paper under her arm, which she had not realised she was still holding. "What have you got?"

"Nothing."

"You've got a—"

"I said I've got nothing," Lyra said, and heard her own voice come out in a register she did not recognise, and Milo Reyes, who was sixteen and deaf to about a third of what people said and missed none of the rest, went completely still.

"Okay," he said.

"Milo—"

"Chur," said Milo Reyes, and took his shirt out of her hand, and did not ask again, and that was the single kindest thing anybody did for her that entire week.

## THE BLUEPRINT

*Her room. That evening. Lights off.*

The paper came apart in her hands like something alive, because it was old, and because it had been rolled inside a warm stone room for eleven years, and because Lyra had been carrying it against her ribs since four o'clock in the afternoon and had sweated through it.

Four sheets. The largest was about eighteen inches by thirty and it was covered in a dense black mesh of drawings that made no sense to her at all: concentric circles, transfer functions, something that looked like a spine laid flat, and a great many numbers.

The second was a diagram of a machine, and the machine was the archway. She was certain of that within four seconds, because the archway had a socket in the second course of stone on the left-hand post and the diagram had a socket in the second course on the left-hand post, and it had a name next to it, and the name was—

*Animus Argenti chamber. Rim node. Cache: 4.*

The third sheet was a list. Lyra read it four times and her hands went cold on the paper.

> *INTERVENTIONS, CUMULATIVE. N = 11.*
> *Soft load identified: 11. Vessel intact: 11. Perception absent after: 11.*
> *Consent: signed 9. Not obtainable 2 (subject in extremis, no relation present). Not sought 0.*
> *Second responder required by s.9: 0 of 11.*

The fourth sheet was a note in three hands, and the third of them was her mother's.

> *This is not a machine. I want that on the front of it. A machine has a purpose and can be switched off and does not have a schedule of appointments. This is a piece of arithmetic that has been running since 1941 and has acquired an appetite, and the appetite is not the arithmetic's, and I cannot find where it was put in.*
>
> *Control is not the solution. Communion is the only road.*
>
> *He has built the stabiliser around a person-shaped hole. I have found the person-shaped hole. It is not in the Council's inventory. Ask the Registrar. Ask her twice, and then ask her in writing, because that is the only way anything in this building has ever moved, and she is a good woman and she is frightened, and those two facts have been the whole of my life in this place.*
>
> *I am going to be reckless in May. I am telling nobody because I have promised.*

Lyra read the last line four times.

*I am going to be reckless in May.*

April. The four months. Her mother's best months.

She got out her phone and found, in a photograph her grandmother had sent her in June with no message attached, the back of a card in a hand she knew. MEI-HUA CHEN, REGISTRAR. And underneath, in a much smaller line: *For enquiries regarding records, appointments, and the rights of persons under the Care of Persons of Perceived Thread, ask at the desk.*

She typed out a message to three people, and looked at it, and added one line at the bottom, and sent it, because she had promised a boy in the rain that she would.

> *Nai Nai says I have to tell you things. I have three. I have had one of them in my bag for twenty-three days and I am telling you about it in this message and I am not going to read your replies tonight, and if that is cowardice then it is cowardice and it is at least consistent.*
>
> *Eli — I have a list. Eleven. It has a column headed "consent". Nine are signed. Two are "not obtainable (subject in extremis, no relation present)". I want to know what the second one is.*
>
> *Zara — I cannot see threads in a crowd any more, or in a room from across it, or at all unless I touch something, and I have had three days to work out why and it is because the Maze had a copy of what I did to Katie Doyle in April and showed it to me, and I have been sick about it, and I want you to ask me about it in a room, not on a telephone, because I do not think I will manage it on a telephone.*
>
> *Milo — thank you for not asking. I am going to tell you anyway, and not tonight. Station five was about a door. That is all I am going to say about it tonight.*
>
> *One more thing. My mother wrote on a wall in 2002: "It is a door. I am not going to be the one who opens it, because I have worked out that the reason nobody has opened it in eighty years is that everyone who gets here has been told it is the only way."*
>
> *I would like somebody in this school to tell me, to my face, in a room with witnesses, whether that is still true.*

She put the phone face down on the bed and lay on top of the covers in her coat with her boots on.

At ten to two, the board on the wall — the small one, the one that showed a severity and a date and nothing else — changed.

*20% · MODERATE* went to *23% · MODERATE* in about four seconds, and then to *31%*, and held, and then began, very slowly, to climb.

> **COUNTER** — 31% · CRITICAL
> **NODE PROJECTION** — T+13 d, 14:00 *(re-run on partial data)*
> **LOCAL DISTORTION (EAST TOWER)** — 9.1% · elevated
> **COLLECTOR** — 87% of capacity

The fourth board was the one nobody had explained. The collector under the gym had been at four per cent on Monday.

Lyra got up, and put her coat on over her pyjamas, and went down three floors in her bare feet, and read the fourth board for a long time, and then went back up and lay on top of the covers in her coat with her boots on, and did not sleep, and did not take the paper off the door.

---

## THE CORDON

*Wednesday 11 September - Thursday 12 September. The courtyard, and then the file.*

Nobody went back under the arch on the Tuesday night.

The Consultant Engineer came on the Wednesday with two men and a torch, and what he wrote is short, and the file contains the whole of it. What was still traversable was named, and the naming is the whole of the document: the span, the left-hand post, the threshold, and the passage between them. What was not traversable was named the same way: the east side, the rim, the housing, and the eighth room. The eighth room was not damaged and was not shored and was not going to be shored, because there was nothing left of it to shore.

The reference set was in the Maze. That is why the Council's counter has said *partial data* in every header since Wednesday morning, and it is why nobody since has been able to say what the number means, and it is why a boy's number in a room in the East Tower could be taken for the Academy's.

They ran a cordon along the colonnade and propped the span on the Wednesday, and the timber was called temporary in every document that mentions it, including the one in which somebody asked for it to be called something else and was refused.

The arch was not declared safe. It was declared traversable, on the Thursday, by a man with a clipboard, and those are not the same sentence, and it is the second one that a Registrar signed an access condition on the strength of, in the courtyard office, on the Thursday afternoon, in a hand that is not difficult to read.

---

*Thread Resonance Note: Maze run, cohort 1, 10 September. Nine candidates entered. Eight returned. ELEVEN YEARS: the term "Animus Argenti" has been scrubbed from the assessment vocabulary in 2009 on the instruction of this office and I have been aware of the instruction. — withdrawn 14 Sept, see minute 44-B/3. RE: C.L., first-year, unattached to a range. The pattern of her reading is the pattern of a candidate for something. I do not know what and I have not asked, and I want it minuted that I have chosen not to ask, which is a decision, and that the last person who chose not to ask in this building was her mother, and that I was her mother's colleague, and that I did not know her well enough to stop her, and that I have thought about that for eleven years.*

# Chapter 12A: The Demonstration

*Weave-Quake Counter: 34% instability. Node-failure projection: T+13 d, 14:00. The reference dataset was destroyed in the Maze collapse of the tenth of September; this is the Council's re-run on partial data, and nobody has said out loud that it is a guess.*

*Wednesday 11 September. The Grand Demonstration Hall.*

## THE ABSENCE

The notice went up at nine o'clock and it was four lines long and it changed the shape of the morning entirely, because everybody in that hall had been told in August that Dr. Chen would be demonstrating, and everybody in that hall had therefore not read anything about thread ethics since the start of term, and there were two hundred and eleven first-years and about sixty of them had already prepared questions.

*Dr. M-H. Chen will present Thursday's seminar. In her absence, Professor Harlow has asked for the floor.*

Lyra read it four times and did not know why it made her feel as though somebody had taken a chair out of a row.

"You've gone quiet," said Milo.

"I've got nothing. It's a notice."

"It's a notice about a *woman*." Zara had not looked up from a page. "Two hundred people in this hall and about forty of them can read a thread signature, and every one of them is going to spend the next hour watching two men who both outrank everybody in the room, and there is going to be a *thing* about it, and you are going to be in the middle of it, and I have known you for nine days and I would like to take this moment to say that I find you extremely difficult to be in a room with."

"Chur," said Milo Reyes, who had been in the room for nine days and had already worked out that the three of them were going to be a thing whether they liked it or not.

---

## THE FLOOR

It was not a duel. That is the first thing, and Lyra wrote it down in the back of a notebook on the Thursday because she wanted to be sure of it, and because a great many people told her afterwards that it had looked like one.

Elder Adebayo Adeyemi took the floor first, unasked, and did it in a way that made the request irrelevant: he came down into the circle, and he sat down on the obsidian, cross-legged, in robes the colour of a September afternoon, and he waited.

"I teach what I know and I do not know what you teach," he said. "We will both be wrong. There is a bench at the back for anyone who becomes faint, and I am seventy-one and I have a knee and I will be using the bench, and I would like it noted that I have said so in advance, because a man of my age who does not say so is performing."

Then he made something.

He did it slowly, and he narrated it, and he got two things wrong on purpose and corrected them, and the effect of watching a competent man correct himself in public was the single most disarming thing Lyra had ever seen a person do.

*Ẹ̀mí àgbájọ.* The living gathering. He said the words and translated them and then said that the translation was a *reduction* and that the reduction was the fault of English and not of him, and that he would like somebody in the room to write down that the fault was the fault of English.

Amber and green, and they did not snap into being — they *arrived*, the way water arrives at a thing, and Lyra's eyes filled and she had to stop and do the count that Wren Zhou had taught her on a platform, and behind her four hundred threads of frightened attention went up off the floor like heat off a road.

"It's feeding on the room," Eli Park said, from two seats along, in a whisper. "It's not taking. It's *feeding*, and it's the same as what Ms. Washington does with a room when she walks in and everybody goes quiet, and it's completely different from what the professor is about to do, and I want to watch both."

A ward went up in front of Adeyemi. It was not a wall. It was a thing with roots in the floor and a great deal of patience in it, and the pattern was nothing like anything in the textbooks, and it was, Lyra thought, the first time in her life she had seen a *structure* that was obviously alive and obviously not interested in her.

"Hold that in your mind," Adeyemi said. "That is what a *relationship* looks like from the inside. It is not stronger. It is more expensive to run and it lasts longer and there is no on/off and you cannot get it in an afternoon. Hold it."

---

Marcus Harlow stepped into the circle, and the temperature in the hall went down about two degrees, and Lyra's whole body reacted before her mind had got to the word.

He was not a large man and he moved like something poured, and he did not have the ozone smell in a room of four hundred people; that was in a bookshop in August, at a boy on a carpet, and she had spent a month being frightened of a smell and now there was nothing at all, which was somehow much worse, because it meant she had been frightened of the wrong thing and had got lucky.

"Threads are not a metaphor," he said, and the textbook said that too. "They are a medium. They respond to intention with a measurable lag of four to nine tenths of a second, and every disaster in our institutional memory traces back to somebody who believed that wanting a thing hard enough was the same as doing it. I have said that sentence in this hall once. I am not going to say it again. I am going to *demonstrate* it."

Silver threads came into being in the air in front of him, and they came into being the way a nail goes into a board, and Lyra felt her stomach go cold, because she had seen that gesture forty times in her mother's notebooks in a kitchen on a street called Ellery, and in every one of them it had been described in words like *ploughed* and *driven* and *forced*, and Lyra had never once thought about why.

He built a lattice in about nine seconds. It was beautiful. It was, without any question, the most competent thing Lyra Chen had ever seen a person do with their hands, and it hung in the air over the obsidian and it was a *fortress*, and every line in it was straight, and the whole of it was beautiful in the way that a suspension bridge is beautiful.

"Efficiency," he said. "Minimal expenditure. Maximal effect. That is what you are looking at and it is what I have been trying to teach this institution to do for eleven years, and I have failed approximately two hundred times, and the reason I have failed is on that bench."

Adeyemi had not moved. His amber ward was still up, patient, rooted, entirely indifferent.

"Marcus," he said, in Yoruba-accented English, in the tone of a man who has known somebody a very long time. "That is a *fence*."

"It is a *wall*," Harlow agreed. "And a wall is what is between my daughter and a collapse."

"Between your daughter and a collapse is a *wall*," Adeyemi said, "and the wall is made of your daughter, and she is seven, and she is standing in a garden, and she has been standing in that garden for sixteen years, and you are the only person in this room who still believes she is standing in a garden, and I have watched you build eleven of these and I have watched what happens to the garden every time."

*Eleven years.*

Lyra's hand went to the jade at her throat and stopped there, because she had been about to do something else with it, and she made herself not, and did not entirely understand why, and understood four weeks later.

---

Then Adeyemi stamped his foot, and a hum went through the stone, and the amber flared, and what came out of it was a bird.

It was enormous. It had a wingspan of about nine feet and it was made of light and it was not a *symbol*, which was the thing Lyra could not stop thinking about afterwards: Harlow said the word *animistic* twice, dismissively, in the voice of a man filing a document, and the bird was not an animistic projection. It was a *structure* with a job.

It took his lattice apart in the air, in about four seconds, without touching a line of it — Lyra worked that out afterwards and could not work out how — and it came back and folded itself over Adeyemi's shoulder like a bird that has come home.

"Recycling," said Eli Park, from two seats along, in a voice that had gone somewhere else entirely. "Oh. *Oh.* He isn't making a wall. He's making a *roof*."

---

## THE WRIST

It went wrong at eleven minutes, and it went wrong because of a man's hand.

Lyra saw it happen and she could not afterwards explain to anybody why it was the hand, because a room of four hundred people saw a demonstration and a woman of sixteen saw a man in a grey suit move his cuff down about an inch too far, in the specific, practised, entirely involuntary way of a person who has been wearing something that is not theirs.

Adeyemi's eyes went to it. Lyra saw the elder's gaze arrive on that wrist and stay there, and saw the elder's face do something that four hundred people could not see, because it took about half a second, and then Adeyemi said a word, quietly, in Yoruba, and Lyra did not know it and has never been able to find it in a dictionary, and has never needed to.

Harlow froze.

Every silver thread in the air went out at once, not in a pattern and not with a bang — they *went*, the way a held breath goes, and eleven or twelve of them tore as they went, and there was a sound in the hall like a door being shut in another building.

The name came out of the old man like a stone out of a wall.

"Kyoto."

And the hall did not breathe.

---

Lyra went out into the corridor because she had to, and because four hundred people were standing up, and because the woman she wanted to speak to was going to be at the front of it and could not be got to.

There was a man against the wall with his forehead on the stone.

It was her father, and he had a stick, and he had come from Ward C on his own at eleven o'clock in the morning on the advice of a doctor who had lost that particular argument, and he had not told her, and the reason he had not told her was that he had been in Ward C for two days and had been watching a woman of eighty-one on a speakerphone teach a girl of sixteen to say a sentence in Vietnamese, and had decided that he could not interrupt that.

She stood four feet away from him and could not get a word out.

He was not weeping. That was the thing she would remember. His shoulders were shaking and his knuckles were white against the stone and he was not making any sound at all, and Lyra stood there and understood that she was looking at her father being *remade*, in a corridor, in about ninety seconds, by a word said by an old man in a hall.

"Ba—"

He put his hand up. Not to her. To the wall.

"Lyra. Go back in."

"I can't. Ba—"

"*Go back in.*" It came out of him like something breaking. "I have been sitting in Ward C for two days reading your mother's papers, all of them, the ones she left and the ones Nai Nai has had for six years, and I want to tell you what I have learned, and I cannot do it in a corridor, and I cannot do it now, and I am asking you for the only thing I have ever asked you for, which is to come back in a minute."

"Bàba—"

"Nineteen ninety-seven," her father said, to the stone. "April. A garden. A draw, and a man with a blade, and a child of seven who walked *towards* it." His hand slid down the wall about a foot. "That is what I have learned, and I have known some of it since the beginning of the year, and I did not tell you, and the reason I did not tell you is that I was afraid of a specific thing, and the specific thing was that you would go and find him."

Lyra stood in a corridor of a school she had been in for nine days and did not move.

"Bàba, I am going to find him."

"Yes," said Wei Chen. "I know. That is why I have been *reading*."

---

Behind them the doors opened, and about forty people came out into the corridor, and among them was a girl of about nineteen with silver in her hair who was taking notes at a speed that was frankly indecent, and a girl of eighteen with her aunt's coat over one shoulder who said "Ms. Chen" in a voice that was not a question, and a boy who had followed Lyra out of a hall of four hundred people without being asked and without any idea at all what he was doing.

"There is a bench," said Lyra, "in the Demonstration Hall, and it is not for the students. It is for an old man's knee, and he used it, and he said he would in advance, and he did."

Milo Reyes said, "Right," in the voice of a person who has decided to be useful before he understands the situation, which is, Lyra thought, exactly what he had done on the first day with a matrix, and she would spend the next nine days learning that it was a habit and not a fluke, and the rest of her life learning that it was the best thing about him.

---
