*Wednesday 11 September, 12:20. The corridor outside the Grand Demonstration Hall.*

*Filed by the second-year demonstrator's note-taker, and read into the record of the demonstration at the Council's request, and printed in the 2013 transcript, and the reason it is printed is that in 2019 a lecturer in Lyon asked whether the demonstration was staged, and the answer was:*

> *"It was not staged. Two men went into that circle because a woman did not turn up and neither of them would go home. Everything else in this record is worse than that afternoon and I would like somebody who is reading about a rug in August to understand that the afternoon in September was the good one, and that in it a professor of applied mathematics stood in a corridor and asked his daughter for the only thing he had ever asked her for, and she did it, and it took four minutes, and afterwards she could not remember any of it."*

# Chapter 12B: The Eleventh of September

> "A system that requires a payment from someone who has not agreed to make it is not a system. It is a queue. I wrote that in February, on a page about an infant, and I have thought about it every day since, and I was wrong, because I thought it was about principle."
>
> — Lin Chen, "Communion vs. Control," April 2002, quoted from memory by a man who could not read the original and refused to pretend otherwise

*Weave-Quake Counter: 34% instability. Node-failure projection: T+13 d, 14:00.*

*Sub-level 3. The eleventh of September, two o'clock in the morning. Written seven days later and not sent.*

## WHY THIS IS HERE

The note on the outside of the file is in a registrar's hand and is four lines long, and it is the only reason this document is in a book at all.

> *This is a document written on the eleventh of September 2013 at about two hours in the morning, by the member of faculty whose name is on it, in his own hand, on the back of a form. It was not sent to anybody. It was found on the fourteenth, in his desk, by the man who has been cleaning out that office for four years, and he read about four lines of it and then he put it in a file and went home at six o'clock on the fourteenth, which is two hours before his shift ended, and he has given a statement about that and the statement is two lines long and it says: **"I am not the person who should be the one to have read that."***
>
> *The first week of term, Dr. Chen was absent from a seminar, and the events of the eighteenth are in the record. Nothing in this file was known to the subject of the Inquiry, or to the patient, or to any of the four young people, until the nineteenth of October.*
>
> *I have put it in the book because the alternative is that the person who took a girl of fifteen out of this building on the eleventh of September remains a man whose actions began on the fourteenth, and that is false, and it was false in 2002 as well. — M-H. Chen, Registrar*

---

## THE ENTRY

*I am writing this at two o'clock in the morning on the eleventh of September and I am writing it because I have decided, on the stairs, four minutes ago, that I am going to do something tomorrow that I will not be able to describe accurately afterwards, and that the description is always the only part anybody ever checks.*

*The girl is in a room four floors up. She is asleep. She has been asleep since half past nine. She is fifteen. She has been in this building for two days, and she is here because her sister put her on a coach, and her sister is a third-year who has been extremely careful about something for four years and did not want to be asked about it in a corridor, and I know the something, and I have known it since April, and I have not asked her about it either, and I have decided — on the stairs, four minutes ago, at about ten to two in the morning — that I am going to go and ask Elara Vance, and that I am going to ask her in a way that does not make her feel stupid.*

Here is the arithmetic, and I am going to put it down because I have been told, by a woman in a demonstration hall in August whom I did not manage to help, that putting it down is the whole of the difference between a person and a machine.

The machine under this mountain needs a resonance to hold an open circuit, and has needed one since 1997, and I have spent eleven years looking for a body that will take it. In 2002 I found a document in a drawer in this building that says the Animus Argenti is a bridge, and I have spent eleven years trying to build a machine out of a sentence.

Elara Vance is a bridge.

That is not a metaphor and I hate it. It is a measurement. She came in on the ninth with a signature like a lit window, and on the tenth I put her in a calibration chair and read her for four hours, and I have had that reading eleven times before, in children, and every one of the eleven had a floor to stand on, and Elara Vance has no floor. She is the only signature I have ever seen that *already is* the thing the machine wants.

I have written that sentence in four files since April and I have not once written the next sentence, and the next sentence is the one that says what the machine does with a signature that is already the thing it wants, and the answer is: *it holds the circuit*, and the way it holds it is by taking the person and keeping the part of them that is holding.

That is what happened in a garden in April 1997, and it took nine seconds, and I stood in it.

And here is the arithmetic, and it is correct, and I want it in the record in my own hand because I know what is going to happen to this document:

*She will live. The vessel is intact. She will not bleed and she will not scar and she will be, in a physical sense, entirely well. She will lose the ability to perceive threads and she will lose the ability to be perceived by anybody in her family, and she will be fifteen, and she will be a bridge for the rest of her life, and I do not know how to say any of that to a person in a chair.*

*Fourteen days. In fourteen days the Southern Nexus fails and eleven thousand people find out.*

*And she is the only one in the world.*

---

I went up at ten past two and I sat on the end of the bed in a room with a window and a girl in it and I did the only thing I am good at, which is ask a question.

"Miss Vance. I am going to ask you three things and you are allowed to say no to all of them and I am going to take a no as final, and I want that on the card, and I am going to hand the card to you and not to your sister, because you are fifteen and your sister is eighteen and the difference between those two numbers is the difference between a person and a signature."

She said, "What's it for?"

And I said — and I have this in the voice memo I took at the top of the stairs at ten to two, which I have now also destroyed, because I understood about four seconds after pressing the button what I had done, and I want that on the record too, and it is the worst thing in this file:

"Miss Vance, I am going to offer you a place on a specialist programme. It is a four-week residential course in applied thread dynamics. It is the most advanced programme in this field. I am going to be honest with you about three things, because I have discovered in eleven years that the young people I have harmed most were the ones I was honest with, and the ones I damaged second were the ones I lied to, and I have never worked out why that is true and I have stopped trying.

"It is residential and it is supervised and you will be taught by four people, one of whom is me.

"It is not a gift, and nobody has proved that there is any such thing, and I have spent eleven years pretending otherwise in public because a committee requires a category.

"And it will change you, and I do not know how, and I am telling you that I do not know, and I have read every paper there is and I do not know, and *I am telling you that* because a man put those exact words in a lecture hall three days ago in front of two hundred people and I have thought about nothing else since, and it is the only decent thing anybody has done in this building in eleven years and he did it by accident while trying to recruit her."

She asked, "What kind of change?"

And I said, "The kind that does not come back."

And I sat on the end of a bed in a dormitory at twenty to three in the morning and let a fifteen-year-old think about it, which is the correct procedure, and which I had not used eleven times in eleven years, because eleven times in eleven years I had a form in front of me with a number on it, and a number is a *terrible* thing to have in front of you at twenty to three in the morning, and I knew it, and I did it anyway, and I want that on the record in the order in which it happened.

She said, "Can I ring my mother?"

I said yes.

She rang her mother. She spoke for four minutes, and Lyra, four floors up and nine days into a term in which she had already been the reason two men in a circle could not finish an argument, slept through all of it.

She spoke for four minutes and then she said, "She says to ask whether there are other people like me," and I said, "There are four. In the world. That is not an exaggeration and I would ask you to please not repeat it as one, because in 2002 a woman of twenty-four was told that in a letter and repeated it in a lecture hall and about forty people wrote down the number."

And Elara Vance said, "Then I'd like to do it," and signed the card, in her own hand, with a pen, and it says:

> **VOLUNTEER**

and the date, and the time, and the name of the person who offered it, and I did not read it, and I have written that sentence in this file four times now and I am going to stop.

---

## THE ENTRY, LATER

*Eighteen September, 11:40 p.m. Sub-level 3.*

*Seven days. I have written four thousand words and I have not written the part.*

Here is what I did not write on the eleventh.

I did not write that on the eleventh — on the eleventh — she asked me, back in the calibration chair, with a chart on the wall behind her, whether anyone had asked *her* what the machine was going to be.

I said, "Not yet."

And she said, "Why not?"

And I said, "Because the answer is complicated and you are fifteen and I did not want to give you a complicated answer at two o'clock on a Wednesday."

And then I said the other thing. I have been trying for seven days to work out why I said it, and the best I have is that it was in the room and it was the wrong thing, and I have never in my life been in a room where the wrong thing was simply *available*, and in the room, and nobody in the corridor to see me take it.

I said: "You will be the first, Miss Vance. In the programme. I have not told anybody that, because there are four of you and there is one place, and the place goes to whoever is ready, and I have spent seven days deciding that you were ready, and I have not asked you, and I would like to hear you say it now."

And Elara Vance — fifteen years old, four days in a building, with a card on the table that said *volunteer* and a sister asleep on a floor two above her and a mother on a telephone in a town four hundred miles away who had been told by a *letter* that her daughter was in a specialist programme for gifted young people and who had written back in February to ask what the curriculum was and had had a form posted to her, and who had, in April, put her daughter on a coach —

Elara Vance said, "You decided."

And I said, "Yes."

And she said, "I want to know what happens to me."

And I told her. All of it. In order. For about eleven minutes, in a calibration room, on a Wednesday afternoon in a building in a valley, to a child.

And then I asked her, and she signed, and I did not read the card.

And here is the part I have not been able to write for seven days, and it is two sentences long, and I am going to write it and then I am going to close this file:

*She asked me what happens to her and I told her the truth.*

*She did not understand the truth, and I knew she had not understood it, and I watched her not understand it, and I let her sign.*

---

*End of entry. No further entry. The file is closed because there is nothing after the seventh day. The Council's question is always the same and it is always about the eleventh, and this document is the answer to it, and it is four thousand words long, and the answer is in the second sentence of the fourth page, and it is that a man of fifty-four told a child the truth in eleven minutes and she signed a card, and he did not read the card, and he watched her not understand him.*

*And I would like it recorded — by whoever reads this in a book, which I do not expect and have not prepared for — that in the four days before the second of October a girl of sixteen asked thirty-nine people in a cage what they wanted, and that on the second of October she had been standing in a fault for four days holding it open, and that somebody with a stick and a model in a case lined it without going into it, and that thirty-seven of them said out loud that they wanted to be let go, and that she honoured it, and that thirty-seven of them are dead.*

*And I would like it recorded that I was in that building on the second of October and that I was the reason there was something to line.*

*And I would like it recorded, in my own hand, in this file, with a date, that this is the fifth option and I did not propose it and I have spent eleven years arguing that it was impossible, and that I was wrong, and that the person who was right was a sixteen-year-old with a napkin and a woman with a tube of silk and a man who was going to be dead in a week.*

*There is no forgiveness in this. I want to be extremely clear, because I have read four hundred pages of this Inquiry and I know exactly what everybody in it is hoping for, and I have been the man who leaves a door open that he is going to walk through, and this time I am not going to do it.*

*Her name was Elara Vance. Her mother's name is Ines. Her sister's name is Elena and her sister is twenty-nine feet above her and she has been sleeping on the other side of a wall for four days and she does not know, and her sister has spent four years building a thing for her, and it is in a garage, and I have never seen it, and I am going to die not having seen it, and that is correct, and it is the only thing in this file that I have got right.*

# Chapter 13: The First Disappearance

*Weave-Quake Counter: 35% instability. Node-failure projection: T+13 d, 14:00. The reference dataset was destroyed in the Maze collapse of the tenth of September; this is the Council's re-run on partial data, and nobody has said out loud that it is a guess.*

*Thursday 12 September.*

## THE QUESTION, ASKED IN A LECTURE HALL

"Professor. Can I ask you something that is not about the answer?"

Two hundred and eleven first-years, the Lumina Lecture Hall, half past two on a Thursday, and Zara Washington stood up in the middle of a row and Marcus Harlow stopped mid-sentence.

"That is a somewhat alarming way to open," said Harlow, "and yes, and I am going to enjoy it, and please be aware that I am going to enjoy it *visibly*, and that two people on the Council have been told about that in advance and have not stopped me."

"I've been in this building for eleven days." Zara did not have notes. Lyra found that she checked, twice, and that Zara Washington had come to a lecture hall of two hundred and eleven people with nothing. "And on the first day four of us held a door for about four minutes and two hundred and eleven people watched, and the number on the board in the station was seventeen, and it was seventeen on the Monday and it was seventeen on the Tuesday, and Dr. Osei has been very patient with me and she is going to stop being patient in about eleven weeks, and I would like to ask a question about the number before I run out of the thing that is keeping me from asking it."

"Ask."

"How many people know what it measures?"

And there was a silence in that hall that Lyra Chen would think about for the rest of her life, because it was not the silence of two hundred people not knowing an answer. It was the silence of two hundred people who all knew the answer and had all, independently, decided not to have it.

"Some of us," said Harlow.

"Thank you." Zara sat down.

Nobody breathed for about three seconds and then somebody at the back said, "It measures the severity of the ward network. It's a severity and not a percentage of anything. The projection is a date and not a number of hours. There are four numbers on that board and they are four different questions, and the fourth one is how full the old fear-collector under the gym is, and it is not in the student handbook, and I have looked, twice, and I would like to ask somebody about the fourth one."

"You would like to ask somebody about the fourth one," said Harlow, "and I have been waiting nineteen days for somebody to ask somebody about the fourth one, and I would like it noted that the reason I have been waiting is that I raised it at a Council meeting in 2011 and it was minuted as *noted*, and that I have discovered in the last eleven days that *noted* is a thing you can do to a question so that it never has to be answered again, and that this is a discovery I made in a lecture hall in front of two hundred people because a girl of sixteen was braver than a committee."

---

Afterwards, in the corridor, Lyra said, "That was the best thing I have ever seen anybody do."

"That was Zara. I said *noted* in 2011."

"That was the *best thing you have ever said*, you told her she was braver than a committee, in front of two hundred people."

"Mira," said Harlow — and then stopped, because he had used a name that had not been introduced, and about forty people in that corridor heard it, and one of them, a girl of eighteen with her aunt's coat over one shoulder, turned round very slowly.

"You called her Mira," said Elena Vance.

"I did."

"Her name is Zara. Zara Washington. She is a first-year and she has a *sister* in this building and it is not you." Elena Vance's voice was very level. "I don't know what you have just told this corridor. I don't know what any of it is about. But I would like to say one thing into it, and then I am going to go and do my work, and I would like it noticed that I have a *course load*."

"Noticed," said Harlow.

"Ines is my mother," said Elena Vance. "She is alive. She is in a care programme in this town and my sister has been in the same one since she was twelve, and the last person in this building who wrote that name down in a form in 2011 was you. And *Mira* is not her name, and it is not anybody's name. It is what happens when a man who has had a page in a drawer for two years tries to say out loud a name he was never supposed to have learned."

Nobody in the corridor moved.

Then she said, "Two hundred and eleven names in that hall, and there were about nine of us who could see what was happening, and every single one of us did the same thing, and I have been in this building three years and I did it too. So." She put her coat back on her shoulder. "Don't."

She went.

Harlow stood in the corridor of Threadweaver Academy with his mouth slightly open, and Lyra Chen — who had spent a month learning that a man with a silver thread in his arm was a *category* rather than a person — watched a woman of eighteen walk away from him and understood that she was braver than both of them, and that she had said so in eleven words, and that it had cost her something, and that nobody was going to thank her for it.

---

## THE ARCHIVE

They went down at four o'clock because the demonstration had emptied the building and Eli Park had worked out, in about six minutes, from a diagram and a list of reading-room hours, that the Restricted Stacks are staffed between two and four and unstaffed between four and six, and that between four and six the *access log* is still written, by a machine, and that a machine writes down the time and not the person.

"You have got eleven minutes," he said, at the bottom of the spiral stair, in the dark, with his torch off. "And I want to say the thing I am actually worried about, and it is not security, and I have written it on the back of my hand."

"Go on."

"Somebody put a piece of paper in Lyra's sketchbook on the eighteenth of August and nobody has seen it since." Eli did not look up. "I have been not-asking about that for twenty-five days. I want to say out loud, in a cellar, to two people, that I have been not-asking, and that I have a reason, and that the reason is that I have worked out that whoever put it there can put things in other people's rooms, and that if I ask Lyra to show it to me then the person who put it there finds out that a sixteen-year-old has read it, and I would like to put that on the record before I do something I am going to regret."

"Go on," said Zara.

"And I have worked out the other half of it," said Eli Park, "which is that this is *also* the reason nobody has asked Lyra to show it to us, and that it is not only me."

---

They found it in forty minutes. Not the conduit — the conduit is a *term of art* and they did not have it, and Eli had spent three weeks failing to find it by searching for it, and what he found instead was a place.

"There's a term for it," he said, into a very small torch beam. "'Rim node.' That's what the Maze log calls the east lattice. And there are four of them, and three of them are in this building, and the fourth one is not on the inventory, and *the inventory is dated 2011*."

"Where?"

"In the Maze." Eli sat down on a floor. "Lyra. Lyra, the thing you went into on Tuesday — the eighth room, the one that isn't on the list, the one Tanaka says is a *cache*—"

"You told me it was a cache."

"It is a cache. It's a cache with a *rim* on it, and the rim has a reading, and the reading on Tuesday night was thirty-four per cent, and the counter said twenty at the same time, and I have been assuming for three weeks that the difference was a *consequence*." He was talking very fast now. "It isn't. It's a *source*. Something under that building has been drawing from the ward network since Tuesday night and it has been drawing at a rate that took the counter from twenty to thirty-one in nine seconds, and it is still drawing, and the log is in a machine, and the machine is in the antechamber, and the antechamber is not locked, and there are two hundred and eleven people in this building who have just been in a lecture hall being told a great deal about the fourth number."

---

## JAMES

They came out of the Restricted Stacks at five past five into a corridor in which a boy of fourteen was sitting on the floor with his back against a wall and his school coat over his knees, doing a sum.

"Lyra," he said, without looking up. "Hi. Sorry. I'm James. Your father's cousin's son, from the branch in Leeds, which I know is a *horrible* way to say it, and I've been here since Friday because Nai Nai's branch sent me for the assessment, and I have been in the East Tower since yesterday and I have a *sheet of paper* from a man called Harlow and I don't know what it means and I'm supposed to take it to the office tomorrow, and I couldn't sleep, so I've been doing the sums on it, and there's a number on it that keeps changing and I've checked it four times."

He held out the sheet.

"James." Lyra's voice came out at a pitch she did not recognise. "Ba. How long have you been in the East Tower?"

"Since yesterday. They said the assessment room was in there. And they've been — " he frowned at the paper — "they've been coming and going. There's a *noise*, Lyra, in the floor. It isn't a machine noise, it's more like—" he tapped the paper "—it's like that. It's a number going up and down and I can *hear* the going up and down and I thought that was normal because that's what I do, and then I met Ms. Washington in the corridor on Tuesday and she looked at me like I was broken."

He put the paper down.

"Lyra, I think I am in a room where the number is me."

---

That was the whole of it, and it took four minutes, and afterwards nobody could work out why the four of them did not go to a member of staff immediately.

It was Eli's fault, and he has said so in four different documents, and the version he gives in the 2013 transcript is this:

"We had a *room*. We had a location. We had a reading that had been changing since Tuesday. We had a fourteen-year-old with a sheet of paper in a corridor. And we had a name to give to whoever we reported it to, and the name was *Armitage*, and Armitage had run Harlow's advanced seminar for six years, and I had a *diagram* in a corridor and four and a half hours of daylight and a two-hour window on a Thursday when the assessment was scheduled and a building that was emptier than it had ever been.

"And I said, out loud, to two people I trusted, *if we go to Armitage now, the only thing we will have achieved is that a professor knows we know.*"

Zara Washington said, "That is true."

"It is *not* true," said Eli Park. "It is what I said, and it is what I believed, and it is the exact mechanism that had operated on this building for seventy years, and I said it, and I said it in a cellar, on the strength of no evidence, about a man who has been nothing but decent to me, and I have never been able to work out whether I was right."

"You were right," said Lyra Chen, and has said so in print, and it did not help.

---

## THE ROOM

Elara Vance's room was on the third floor of the east dormitory and it was empty.

That is the phrase in the report and the phrase in the transcript and the phrase in about four hundred letters, and Lyra Chen, who has read all of them, has never found a better one.

Empty. Bed made. Desk tidy. A stack of work on the top, squared. A hairbrush on the shelf above the sink. Three nights of a term lived in a room, and not one thing *wrong* with any of it, and nothing in it at all.

The dormitory's own monitoring board said what it said: **RESIDENT, E. VANCE, LEVEL 3. OCCUPANCY: VACANT SINCE 11 SEPT 23:14.**

One night.

"It says *vacant since the eleventh*," said Milo Reyes. He had said almost nothing for two hours and he said this in a very small voice. "Lyra. It's the twelfth. She went *missing* on the eleventh and nobody — we were only *looking* for her. She's been gone since last night."

"The eleventh," said Zara Washington, from the doorway, and did not come further in.

---

The thread monitoring said something else, and it is in the transcript, and nobody in the room of four understood it at the time, and only one of them understood it in 2019.

Elara Vance's thread signature — which anybody with sight could have read off the wall of a corridor, and which eleven people in the building had read and had thought was *a lit window* — was not gone. It was there, in the same room, in the same space, at a *different depth*.

"There is something in the walls," said Zara, from the doorway. "I want everybody to be extremely careful about what I am about to say, because I have been wrong in this building twice and both times I was wrong in a direction that cost somebody something, and I would like the second one to be on the record as a thing I did.

"It isn't residue. It isn't a scar, and it isn't a *ghost*, and I am using all three of those words deliberately to say that I have considered them and rejected them.

"It's *under* her. Lyra — go and stand by the bed. Go on. Don't touch anything. Now: is there anything in the room that you can see a *depth* to?"

And Lyra Chen, sixteen years old, nine days into a term, with a jade stone at her throat and a gift she could not switch off, looked at a small room in a dormitory in a valley in September, and said:

"Yes."

And that is the first evidence in the record of anything at all being in the ground beneath the east dormitory, and it is the evidence that the *thread dimension* is not a metaphor and not a mood, and it was given by a schoolgirl who did not understand what she was looking at, and every engineer who has worked on that site since has quoted it.

---

## THE REPORT

They wrote it that night, in a common room, in about eleven minutes, and it is a bad document and it is on the record and it is *exactly* the document that a well-meaning sixteen-year-old writes.

> *To the Registrar.*
>
> *We are four first-year students. We are writing this because we are not sure who to write to and because we have been told that the registrar's intake log is the one place in this building that Security cannot reach without a countersignature, and because we have been told that by a person who we have not named and who has been extremely helpful in a corridor and who we would like to keep out of this.*
>
> *1. A resident of the east dormitory, E. Vance, has been absent since the eleventh of September. Her room is intact and the monitoring board says vacant since 11/09 23:14. We do not know what the 23:14 means and we think somebody should ask.*
>
> *2. There is something under the east dormitory. We do not know what. One of us can see a depth in it and one of us can feel a structure in it and one of us has a diagram in a book that puts a *rim* under the east dormitory and a cache on it, and a reading that has been climbing since the twelfth of September.*
>
> *3. J. Chen, fourteen, visiting, has been in the East Tower since Wednesday on a thread assessment and has a sheet of paper from a member of faculty with a number on it that he can hear, and he thinks the number is him, and we think that this is correct, and we do not know what to do about it, and he is fourteen, and he is in that building tonight, and he went to bed there tonight.*
>
> *4. We are not asking you to believe us. We are asking you to put a person on the floor below E. Vance's room and to move J. Chen, and we are asking that the response be in writing, and we are asking that the response be addressed to us and not to a member of faculty.*
>
> *5. Signed. E. Park. M. Reyes. Z. Washington. L. Chen.*

Eli Park read it four times and then said, "That is a very good document and I am not posting it."

"Give it to the registrar," said Zara.

"Give it to the registrar *tonight*, and it will be on her desk in about nine minutes, and the security log will show a first-year visitor to the registrar's office at 23:41 on the twelfth, and at some point tomorrow a person will look at the security log." Eli's hands were not entirely steady, which Lyra noticed, and about which she said nothing at that time and has since apologised for. "Lyra, I have known for eight days that there is something about that *paper*. And I have worked out in the last four minutes that whoever put it there can see what people read, and that if a first-year is logged in this building at 23:41 on a Friday, then it does not matter what is in the letter."

"Then what do we do?"

"We take the piece of paper off our own door," said Eli Park, "and we write our four sentences on it, and we hand it to Dr. Chen *in a corridor, in daylight, with a witness*, and we let the security log say that four first-years told the Registrar something in a corridor at ten in the morning, and then we do not write anything down for nine days."

"Nine days is a very long time," said Lyra Chen.

"Nine days," said Eli Park, "is how long it took a man in a good room to work out how to say no to a clipboard."

They did not wait nine days.

Eli Park was in the east corridor at ten past ten the next morning with a piece of paper and Zara Washington as a witness, and Dr. Mei-Hua Chen read it standing up, twice, and then said four words, in the corridor, in daylight, in front of a witness, and they are the four words that the rest of this is about:

"What day did you say?"

"The eleventh," said Eli Park. "Eleven forty-one at night, on a thread assessment, on a building, on an east dormitory floor, on a room."

"Thank you," said Dr. Mei-Hua Chen, and went down the corridor at a speed that made two of them follow her, and the Registrar of Threadweaver Academy was sixty-eight seconds ahead of everybody for the next eleven days, and what she did with those eleven days is in the transcript, and it is the reason there is a register of case forty-one, and it is the reason there is a *fifth board* in that building at all.

---

*The Inquiry into the Conduit, 2013, cross-examination of the Registrar, day six. The question was whether any member of the Academy had known that E. Vance had been taken on the eleventh of September. The answer occupied two days and is not reproduced here in full. The finding is this:*

> *No member of the Academy knew. The Security Chief knew, from the eleventh, and had been instructed by a person outside this building not to enter the room, and had obeyed, and had spent nine days writing single-line notes to a person she had never met about whether she should hold a door, and the notes are in the record, and they are eleven, and the last one is dated the nineteenth, which is six days after four first-years told the Registrar something in a corridor and went up the corridor again on the Thursday, and it says: **I will hold it until somebody competent arrives or I am relieved. Whoever you are: it is you.***

# Chapter 14: The Thing Under the Floor

*Weave-Quake Counter: 37% instability. Node-failure projection: T+13 d, 14:00. The reference dataset was destroyed in the Maze collapse of the tenth of September; this is the Council's re-run on partial data, and nobody has said out loud that it is a guess.*

*Friday 13 September, 10:52 a.m. — Saturday 14 September, 4:40 a.m.*

## NINETY SECONDS AHEAD

Dr Mei-Hua Chen was sixty-eight seconds ahead of them for eleven days, and the reason was not intelligence and was not training and was the single most useful thing Lyra Chen learned in the autumn of 2013.

She did not go to Security. She walked, at a perfectly ordinary pace, along a corridor, past a fire door, and through a fire door, and down a service stair, and the service stair went to a sub-level that Lyra Chen had walked past nine days ago on her first afternoon and had thought was a *store*, and the Registrar unlocked it with a key on a chain round her neck and did not say anything, and let the door stand open, which was the message.

Then she said, "Four of you. Two of you are about to do something brave and I would like to prevent it, so I am going to tell you what is going to happen in the next four hours and then you are going to help me, and at some point after that you are going to be extremely angry with me, and I am going to deserve it, and I have decided to deserve it in advance so that neither of us has to waste any of the next four hours on it."

"Go on," said Eli Park.

"There is a machine under this building." Dr. Chen opened a door. "I have known there was a machine under this building for eleven years. I have never seen it. It is not in the Council's inventory. It is not in *my* inventory, and I have been the Registrar of this Academy for eleven years, and I have signed for the removal of a boiler from that sub-level in 2011 and the man who came to do the work would not go past the second door and would not say why, and I did not ask why, and I have thought about that every day for two years."

*Lyra looked at her. This is the aunt she had spoken to for eleven minutes in a corridor twelve days ago, who had said, you are the first Chen in this family in four generations who was not the smartest person in the room, and it is a relief, and I am sorry, and I am also extremely glad.*

"There is a girl on the third floor of the east dormitory," said Dr. Chen, "who went into that room on the eleventh of September at twenty-three fourteen hours, in exchange for a card that she signed herself, which she was fifteen, and I discovered this morning in a corridor because a boy of sixteen handed me a piece of paper, and I have had this morning to decide what I am going to do about it, and I have decided that I am going to do everything the Council requires and *not one thing more*, and I would like the four of you to help me with the part that the Council cannot do."

"Which is what?"

"Find out whether there is anybody else down there," said Dr. Mei-Hua Chen. "That is all. I am not asking you to rescue her. I have four wardens and a warrant and a Headmistress and I am going to do all of that properly and slowly and in writing, and I am going to be slow, because the last four times somebody in this building went fast in a sub-level a boy of fourteen is now on a machine somewhere and a woman of twenty-four has been in a cavity under Kyoto for sixteen years."

---

## THE BOY WITH THE SHEET

The assessment suite was in the East Tower's north annexe and it was on a Thursday-to-Sunday rota, and it was staffed, on the Friday morning, by a second-year and a technician, and the Registrar of Threadweaver Academy walked in at eleven o'clock and asked them to step outside, and they did, and one of them cried, and the other one did not, and that is in the record because the other one was asked about it.

James Chen was in a chair with headphones on and a sheet of paper on his knee and a sum half-finished in a school exercise book, and he was fourteen years old, and he had been in the building for forty hours.

"James," said Lyra.

"Lyra." He took the headphones off one ear. "I finished it. That's the thing I wanted to say. I've finished the thing on the sheet and it *goes*, Lyra, it goes to a place and then it comes back, and yesterday it was doing it on its own about every eleven seconds, and I have written down every single time, and there are one hundred and forty-one, and I have got a *graph*—"

He held up the exercise book, and it was a graph, and it was drawn in the back of a school exercise book by a fourteen-year-old, and it was the most frightening object Lyra Chen ever saw.

"Bring it to me," said Dr. Chen. "Both of you. Now, please. And leave everything on the desk exactly as it is, and I want the room locked, and I want the lock to be a chair under the door, because I have a better lock and I do not want a record of it."

---

They got him out at eleven twenty.

That was the entire margin. Everything that happened after that happened because a woman of fifty-seven years old had been sixty-eight seconds ahead of everybody for eleven days and had known, on the eleventh of September, what she was looking at, and had spent eleven days finding a way to get a boy out of a chair that did not require anybody in this building to be brave.

And here is the thing about it, which is in the transcript and which Lyra has asked to be allowed to be the first thing anybody says at a conference about the autumn of 2013:

Nobody had to be brave. That is the whole of the finding. The Inquiry established, over nine days, that the events of September 2013 in Threadweaver Academy were not a story about four teenagers. They are a story about how much *ordinary, competent, unheroic procedure* this building was capable of if somebody would only let it happen, and about a woman of fifty-seven who had known for eleven years that there was a machine under her and had spent eleven days quietly finding out which four of the eleven hundred people in this building could be trusted to do a filing job.

Eli Park's name is in it because he handed her a piece of paper in a corridor in daylight with a witness.

Zara Washington's is in it because she asked, in a lecture hall of two hundred and eleven people, whether anybody knew what the number measured, and because a professor of mathematics in a corridor said that a girl of sixteen was braver than a committee, in public, on the record.

Milo Reyes's is in it because he said the number was loud enough to hear.

Lyra Chen's is in it because a man in a demonstration hall had touched her arm on the twelfth of September and she had not washed it, and because on the thirteenth of September, at about a quarter past eleven in the morning, she asked Dr. Chen one question and then did not ask it again:

"Does he know?" she said. "James. Does he know what this is?"

"No," said Dr. Mei-Hua Chen.

"Will somebody tell him?"

"On Tuesday, in his own words, with his mother in the room, by a doctor and not by a member of faculty." She was already walking. "And not before, and I have spent eleven days on that, and the reason I have spent eleven days on it is that I read a transcript in June of a hearing in another country in which a child of eleven was told, by a man with a clipboard, and has spent the rest of her life being careful with the word *volunteer*."

---

## THE ARCHWAY

They went into the Thread Maze at 2:40 on the Friday afternoon with institutional permission, which is the sentence that appears in every account and which is the sentence that a reader of the 2013 transcript should stop at.

*Permission had been given by the Registrar of the Academy to four first-year students to enter a sealed assessment structure, on the grounds of a reading, in writing, on a piece of paper, which is a piece of paper. It was given over the written objection of the Consultant Engineer, who had said he would not sign it and would not be responsible for it, and it was given subject to three conditions: that the arch was entered and left by the span and not by the rubble, that the timber was not to be leaned on, and that this was permission to read the thing and not permission to take anything out of it.*

"Chen." Tanaka was at the archway, and her face was doing something Lyra had not seen it do. "You are not going in. Look at me. You are not going in."

"I'm the only one who can read the—"

"I know you are. That is not the question." Tanaka came about four steps closer. "Miss Chen, in nineteen years I have brought about two thousand students through that arch and I have watched about nine of them be *useful* in it, and there is a reason there have only ever been nine, and it is that the Maze is calibrated to whoever is in it, and it is calibrated by *touch*, and it takes about eleven minutes, and it is not on the schedule, and if you go in there and it calibrates to you in front of a reading of thirty-one per cent then it will be *inside you* in a way that we cannot take out, and I have seen it happen once, in 1997, to a woman of twenty-four, and she is a room on the third floor of the east dormitory with nobody in it."

Lyra stood on the wet flagstones and had the single most useful four seconds of her life.

"Then send somebody who does not calibrate," she said. "Send Mr. Reyes. He is not visual. Send him and send Ms. Washington, because she can feel a structure at nine feet, and send Mr. Park, because he has the diagram and he will not go past the second door, and *I* will read the sheet, from outside, over a radio, and if the number changes I will tell you, and you will pull them out, and you will not need me in there at all."

"That is not how the Maze—"

"Professor Tanaka." Lyra's voice came out steadier than she had any right to expect from a person who had been awake for twenty-nine hours. "Eleven minutes. You said eleven minutes. I have been in this building nine days and I have been told the number on a board is not a percentage of anything, and that a piece of paper in a sketchbook is not a delivery, and that a photograph is a photograph. I have been told that *discovering a thing does not make the thing bigger.* Somebody told me that in a bookshop in August, in front of my father, out loud, and I have thought about it every day."

Tanaka looked at her for about two seconds.

"You would have been a good one," she said. "Send the boy and the girl. Park stays with me and the sheet. And you stand at that archway and you read me the number out loud every two minutes, and if I say *out*, you do not argue with me, and if you argue with me I will put you in a corridor and you will be extremely angry, and I will be right, and we will both know it."

---

They went in at 2:40. The three of them.

Lyra stood at the archway in the rain with a radio in one hand and a sheet of paper in the other and read out the number every two minutes for one hour and eleven minutes, and she has kept the sheet, and it is in a tin in a kitchen on a street called Ellery.

"Twenty-nine."

"Twenty-nine."

"Thirty."

"That's the anteroom board. What have you got on the sheet?"

"Thirty-one. And there's a second number, Professor, and it's in a different pencil, and the second number is a *depth*—"

At 3:52 the number on the sheet went to forty-four, and the archway *moved*.

---

## INSIDE

Milo Reyes has written four pages about the inside of the Thread Maze and they are in the record and they are the only four pages in the whole of the Inquiry that anybody has ever asked to be withdrawn, and the request was made by him, in 2016, and it was refused, and he asked again, and the reason it was refused is in the Chair's minute, and the Chair wrote it himself, on the same day, twenty-nine years after the afternoon, which was not an accident either:

> *Mr. Reyes asks that the record of his experience be removed. Mr. Reyes is now forty-five and teaches in a conservatorium and is the foremost authority in his field, and he has explained to me that the reason he wants it removed is not shame and is that he has taught the material for twelve years and there is a version of the afternoon in which he is a boy who was frightened, and he does not want that to be the thing that is remembered.*
>
> *I have declined, for the following reasons, which I have put in the minute so that they are on the record and not merely in my head.*
>
> *One. The Inquiry's finding is that the events of September 2013 were not caused by the bravery of four teenagers. It is the central finding and the four of them have been extremely careful never to claim otherwise. The record of the afternoon is the primary evidence for that finding. If it is removed, the finding is a story.*
>
> *Two. Mr. Reyes is not frightened in the record. He is *accurate* in the record, and frightened, and the two are not the same thing, and in twelve years of teaching he has made a career out of the difference, and the record is the best example of it in this building.*
>
> *Three. A building that removes a record because the person in it has become somebody who does not want it is a building that will eventually remove a record because the person in it is not important enough to object. We have spent seventy years doing that to other people. I am not doing it in November. — The Chair, 4 November 2042*

---

The three of them went in at 2:40 and came out at 3:53, which is one hour and thirteen minutes, and Tanaka had said eleven, and Eli Park had said eleven, and Dr. Mei-Hua Chen had said the calibration takes about eleven minutes, and it took an hour and thirteen because the Maze was reconfiguring around them the entire time and a structure under the east dormitory was pulling on it.

Milo Reyes's account, in part, because the finding of the Inquiry rests on it and because Lyra Chen has asked that it be read in full at every conference she has ever spoken at, which she has done four times:

> *We were in the second chamber and Milo could hear the number.*
>
> *He has said, and has said in print, and I am going to say it too because he has asked me to: he could hear it. Not the corridor. The number. He said it was "like a note that goes up and comes down, eleven seconds, and it was in my teeth, and by the fourth time I had counted it and by the fortieth time I had written it in a notebook, and by the eightieth time I understood that it was not a number at all, it was a *breath*."*
>
> *Eli Park got us to the third chamber. There is a great deal written in the record about the third chamber and I want to say one thing about it which is not in the record: it was the most frightening place any of us have ever been and I did not see any of it, and I have spent the rest of my life being told that I saw a great deal, and I want it entered that I saw a great deal of *a wall*, and that I have never seen anything else, and that I was extremely brave about a wall.*
>
> *Zara got us to the seventh chamber. She did not go forward. She went *sideways*, which none of us expected and which she has said was the only decision she made in the next ten months that she has not had to take back, and she stopped in a doorway and put both hands on the stone on either side of it and said: "There is a woman here."*
>
> *And then she said, "No. There is a *hole* where a woman is."*
>
> *And then she said, "And it is *older than the building*."*

---

Lyra Chen was standing at the archway in the rain at 3:53 with a radio in her hand and forty-four per cent on a piece of paper, and the archway moved, and what came out of it was Milo Reyes, on his hands and knees, and Eli Park, and Zara Washington, and the three of them were carrying a boy of fourteen who was not unconscious and was *singing*.

He was singing a hymn. He was singing it in a language nobody in the group recognised and it took the Inquiry three weeks to identify it as something from a revivalist church in Leeds, and James Chen has never been asked about it, and has said in one published interview that he does not remember any of it and has not been able to stop humming it.

---

## WHAT THE FLOOR WAS

He was in the middle of the seventh chamber, on the floor, with his hands flat on a cracked hearthstone, and the number on his sheet had gone from forty-four to nineteen in about four seconds and had not gone back up.

Eli Park's account of the room, which is four sentences long and which the Chair has said is the four most useful sentences in nine hundred pages:

"Professor Tanaka's arch had a cache on it and the cache was a *rim* and the rim had a *depth*, and the depth was about nine feet, and in the bottom of it was the machine, and the machine was not on the drawing.

"Mr. Reyes found the boy. Ms. Washington found the hole. I found the drawing in a book in 2009 in a different city and I have never been able to work out why the man who wrote that book was not looking for a machine and why nobody in this building has ever read that book, and I have decided that the answer is that he was looking for a *theory* and there was nothing in the world to make him look for a *thing*, and that is the same reason the Academy has had a Registry for six years."

The Inquiry's finding on the seventh chamber, day three, and it is one line:

> *The installation beneath the east dormitory is a working device. It is not a diagram, a model, a prototype, or a plan. It was drawing from the ward network at approximately nineteen per cent of a load of four on the evening of the thirteenth of September 2013, it had been running since at least the second of September 2013 in the form in which it presently exists, and it was in use before the war.*

---

## THE OTHER THING IN THE ROOM

It was not a machine in a room. It was a machine *under* a machine, and Professor Tanaka's arch was not the top of it.

"There were two of them," said the technician, in 2019, at a hearing nobody had expected to need, in a room in Lyon. "I want to say the thing that nobody said in 2013 and that I have had six years to sit with.

"There was the device, in the chamber, in the east dormitory, that had a fourteen-year-old boy on the floor of it. And there was the *other* one, under the device, in the part that was not on the inventory, in a housing that was not a housing, that had been *grown* — and the word I want is grown, because I have looked at a great deal of engineering and I have never seen anything grown, and I have never seen anything that had been sealed by a man and then sat under a campus for seventy-two years being extremely well-behaved and extremely expensive to maintain.

"And on the thirteenth of September 2013, at about four o'clock in the afternoon, the device on the floor of the seventh chamber and the housing underneath it were *both running*, and they had been running *against each other*, and that is the reason the counter moved and that is the reason the boy could hear it, and that is the reason the archway moved.

"And when the archway came down on the east side at twenty past four on the Friday afternoon, it came down on *both of them*, and it is the only time in the history of this building that a structural collapse has improved a matter, and I have been a technician for twenty-nine years and I have never said a sentence like that one in a room and I would like it in the transcript, and I would like somebody afterwards to explain to me why a seventy-two-year-old accident in a room under a building full of children was the best thing that happened to any of us."

---

## THE COLLAPSE

They came out at 3:53. The archway came down at 4:20.

Twenty-seven minutes, which is the margin, and the margin is the entire reason there is a register of case forty-one and a fifth board and an Act that has been amended in a fortnight, and the margin is the following:

Tanaka had four wardens and a radio and a nine-year-old's assessment rota and the authority of the most senior instructor on the ground. Dr. Chen had a Registrar's emergency order, which is a piece of paper that closes a building. And Marcus Harlow — who had a warrant from the Council of Traditions authorising a supervised demonstration, who had been in the building for two days without permission, and who had eleven weeks in him — walked into a courtyard at 3:56 and said, in front of four wardens, in front of a registrar, in front of a Headmistress on a line, and in front of two hundred and eleven students who were in a lecture hall on the other side of a wall:

"Take me out. Put me in a corridor with a chair. I have an instrument in that building and I have not read it since Monday and I am going to read it in a corridor with two wardens outside, and if I come out of that corridor before the arch comes down you can arrest me, and if I do not come out of that corridor you can tell the Council that a member of this faculty has finally done one thing correctly."

He was in a corridor with a chair from 4:04 until 9:40 that evening, and four wardens stood outside, and he sent a headset to Tanaka at 4:31, and the headset is in the record, and it is four minutes long, and the Chair has read it out four times, and it is four minutes of a man of fifty-four giving the operating instructions of a device he built, to an instructor, in a language that is almost entirely about *load paths*, and about a fault in the east lattice about a hand's width wide, and the last forty seconds of it are this:

"—and the *rim* is not part of it. The rim is a reading, not a structure. Do not let anybody put a hand near the rim. That is what happened to the boy and it is what happened to me in a demonstration hall and it is what happened to a woman of twenty-four in a garden, and it is a *grazing* incidence, and every one of us has made the mistake, and I have made it eleven times, and I am asking you, as a professional favour between two people who have never liked each other, to let the building come down on the cage and not on the boy, and I am aware that I am asking a woman to destroy eleven years of my work, and I would like it minuted that I asked."

The archway came down on the east side at twenty past four in the afternoon on Friday the thirteenth of September and it took forty-one seconds and it was a controlled collapse from underneath, and there is no record of anybody being hurt, and the Court's finding in 2016 was that the building's own Consultant Engineer had a written plan for it in a drawer from 1974, and that the plan was not actioned, and that the man who wrote it had been asked about it once, in 2010, and had written back a four-line letter which is in the record, and which ends: *and if you are asking me whether the arch can survive a collapse of that magnitude, the answer is that the arch was designed by people who expected it, and I do not know who they were, and I have looked.*

---

Lyra Chen was in a corridor at eleven minutes past four on the thirteenth of September with a radio in her hand and a sheet of paper in her other hand and forty-four per cent on it, and it went to nineteen and stayed at nineteen, and then, at 4:38, the sheet went blank.

"That is the *chart* going," said Tanaka, on the radio. "Lyra. That is his chart going. He is fine. Get the Headmistress. Get her *now.*"

And Lyra Chen, who had been in the building for ten days, and who had lost nothing yet except the ability to look at a piece of paper from a distance, went and found Eleanor Weaver, and told her, in a corridor, in about eleven words, that a boy called James Chen was on the floor of the seventh chamber of a building that had just come down and was alive, and that he was in a part of it that two hundred people in this Academy did not know about, and that a professor had been in a corridor with a chair and had come out of it at 9:40, and that she needed to know all of it, in a corridor, in daylight, at twenty past five on a Saturday afternoon, with a witness, in writing.

Which is exactly what Eli Park had said in a cellar, on the Friday, about a piece of paper in a sketchbook, and had been laughed at for.

And which is the last thing that happened in Book One that is a *decision* rather than a *reaction*, and it is the reason a woman who had been Headmistress for six years spent the next nine days doing paperwork on purpose, with the door in the room, and is the reason a Registrar of fifty-seven was sixty-eight seconds ahead of everybody for eleven days, and is the reason there is a fifth board.

---

*Weave-Quake Counter: 42% instability. Node-failure projection: T+20 d. Rebaselined. Note added by the Council's technical officer, and the note is in the published minutes, and it is the only note in the published minutes in nineteen years:*

> *On the fourteenth of September 2013 the device under the east dormitory and the housing beneath it were destroyed in a single structural collapse. The node-failure projection has been re-based twice since and is currently a figure of which I have no confidence, and I have said so in writing, and the phrase in circulation is "a guess", and I would like it minuted that I said "a guess" in a Council meeting on the second of October and that this office has spent nineteen years not saying that word in that room, and that the reason I said it is that a building full of children came down on a cage on the afternoon of the fourteenth of September and the forecast improved by four days, and that the reason the forecast improved is that there was a *second* device under the first one that had been drawing against it for seventy-two years, and that in my opinion the word that describes the state of this network is not "critical".*
>
> *It is "misdescribed."*

# Chapter 15A: What the Hall Knows

*Saturday 14 September, 07:20. The Cultural Heritage Hall.*

*Weave-Quake Counter: 43% instability. Node-failure projection: T+13 d. The reference dataset was destroyed in the Maze collapse of the tenth of September; this is the Council's re-run on partial data, and nobody has said out loud that it is a guess.*

> "When thread dimensions destabilize, Western institutions typically respond with extraction-based solutions—drawing energy from the Weave to reinforce weakened areas. Traditional practitioners instead view instability as communication, requiring reciprocity rather than extraction. This fundamental difference in approach may explain why institutional 'solutions' often accelerate collapse rather than prevent it."
>
> — Lin Chen, "Communion vs. Control: Alternative Approaches to Thread Dimension Stability," Unpublished Manuscript

## THE WINDOW

The infirmary had four windows and Lyra had been standing at the third of them for twenty-six minutes, which she knew because she had a watch and because there was nothing else in the corridor to look at.

The first bed held Maya Sandoval, who was twenty-one and in her third year and who had been walking past the Hall of Inherited Practice on Tuesday lunchtime with a paper cup of coffee and had stopped in the doorway to say that the coffee was terrible, and had then fallen down.

The second held Sam Whitfield, eighteen, who had been in the demonstration hall, and who was breathing on his own, and whose chart said *stable*, and who had not woken up.

The third held Thandiwe Mbeki, seventeen, second-year, who had four visitors at once, none of whom could say anything to each other.

The fourth held Ben Ruiz, seventeen, a senior from Westbrook High who had gone down on the carpet in a bookshop on the thirty-first of August in front of sixty people, and whom Lyra had talked to for about ninety seconds that evening without knowing who he was, and who had been kind to her.

She had been thinking about the ninety seconds for two days.

"You will be in the way," said Zara, behind her, not unkindly.

"I know."

Zara came and stood at the second window, which was the one with the better view of nothing. She had been awake for thirty-one hours and it showed in the way she kept touching her own wrist, as though checking it was still there.

"Zara." Lyra did not turn round. "What do you do when someone's hurt and you can feel it?"

Zara was quiet for long enough that Lyra turned round.

"I make tea," Zara said. "That is the truth and I have thought about it and I would like it recorded that I have thought about it. I make tea, and I put it where they can reach it, and I do not put my hand on them, because the last three times somebody who is a stranger has put a hand on Maya Sandoval in this building she has got worse, and everybody knows that, and everybody does it anyway."

"That is not what I asked."

"Then I do not know what you asked, and you should go and be told, because there is a man in this building who is extremely good at telling people what they asked for." Zara's mouth did something that was not quite a smile. "Come on. Adeyemi has been in that hall since five and he has sent a student to find us twice, and the second time the student was crying, so I think we should go."

---

## THE HALL OF INHERITED PRACTICE

The Hall was a long room with a stone floor and no windows, and there were eleven people in it who should not have been there: six students who had been sent from the demonstration hall to be looked after by somebody, and five members of an exchange group from a partner college in Kumasi who had arrived on Wednesday for a fortnight of instruction in the practice of their own tradition and who had been in this building when the archway came down.

They were sitting on the floor against the walls, which was where you put people in a room with a floor like that.

Elder Adebayo Adeyemi was standing in the middle of it with his staff.

He was not old in the way that people meant when they said it to be polite. He was seventy-one and he stood with his weight on his back leg and he had a way of holding perfectly still that made the rest of the room feel careless.

"Morning," he said. "Sit down. You are on the stone and the stone is colder than it looks and I am not going to apologise for the building."

Lyra sat. The stone was colder than it looked.

"You want to know what happened," Adeyemi said, "and there are four answers to that and three of them are worse than useless, so I am going to give you the fourth and you can check it."

He put the butt of the staff on the floor.

The cowrie shells on the shaft woke up. Not brightly. They woke the way a person wakes who has been asleep and does not want to be: reluctantly, and with an opinion about it.

"This is my reading, and I want the word *mine* on it, because the Council will tell you it is a tradition and it is not a tradition, it is one man's account of what he has seen, and I have been wrong in public before." The shells drew a line in the air, and a flat map of the Academy came up out of nothing, about two feet across, and hovering about a foot off the floor. "My teachers called the thing I work with òwú ìmọ̀lára. That is a name and not a definition. What I do is look at it. What they called the condition it is in when it is like this, ẹ̀mí àgbájọ, which is a gathering of breaths, which is also a name and not a definition, and if you tell anybody I have told you that I do not know whether the word is old, I will deny it."

The map had a dark spot in the middle of it, and the dark spot was beating.

"Tell me what you see."

"It's hurting," Lyra said.

"It is not hurting. Look again. It is *being asked for*." He turned the staff a little and the map turned with it. "There. The dark part is not damage. Damage spreads out from a point and it does not have a rhythm. That has a rhythm. That is a pull. Something in this building is taking, and it has been taking since Tuesday, and it took rather more on Tuesday night than it took on Tuesday morning, and I know the number for that because the technician from the Council read it out in this room at six o'clock this morning in front of eleven witnesses and then asked me not to write it down."

"What is the number?"

"Forty-three. And when I came in at five this morning it was forty-two. That is not my number, that is the board's number, and the board is telling you a thing and I will tell you what the thing is, because somebody in this building is going to tell you the wrong thing and it will be me, in about four days, when I am frightened." He put the staff down flat on his palm. "The number goes up when load goes up. It does not go up because you found something out. That is not how a machine behaves. If it went up when you found something out then this hall would be the most dangerous room in the Academy and I would not be standing in it."

Eli, behind her, said, "That is what I said in the cellar."

"You said it in a cellar with the door shut, which is worth something, and it is worth rather less out here." Adeyemi did not look at him. "The load is on the wards. Seventeen of them, in the walls of this building, in the stone, and on Tuesday night somebody ran all seventeen at once at full, and that is the whole of what happened, and the number is up because the drawing is up, and the drawing is up because somebody is drawing."

"Who?"

"That," said Adeyemi, "is the question, and I have been in this building since five o'clock and I have not answered it, and if you are about to tell me that you can, I would rather you did not, because you are sixteen and you have been here ten days and I have been here forty-one years and I have not managed it."

---

## SEVENTEEN

"The model is wrong," Lyra said, after a while.

Adeyemi's eyebrows went up about a millimetre, which from him was applause.

"The Academy's model says the thread dimension is a field. Passive. Something you take readings from." She pointed at the map, at the dark beating spot. "That is not a field. That is a room with something in it, and the something in it is tired, and the reason the number is going up is that the room keeps being asked the same question and nobody has asked it anything else."

"Go on."

"And your model is wrong too." She said it carefully, because he had not been unkind and she wanted to be as accurate as she could. "You have a ring with seventeen strands going into the middle. That is still a *drawing* of something pulling on something. It is the same picture from the other side of the table."

He was quiet for four or five seconds.

"Yes," he said. "Now you have it, and I am going to be annoyed with you about it for the rest of the day, because I have been drawing that ring for thirty years and I did not have a student."

"That is not a ring," Lyra said.

"No. Sit down — you are already sitting. Look at it again. Seventeen lines into a centre. That is not a ring, that is a *net*, and a net is not a picture of a thing taking, it is a picture of a thing *spread out*, and it has been spread out since the year I came here, and I have never once asked who was holding the other ends." He lifted the staff and the seventeen strands came off the map and hung in the air, and where they had been attached there was a small clean disc of nothing, seventeen of them, at seventeen different places. "There. That is the part of my model I did not want to look at, and you have made me look at it, so I owe you one and here it is: I have been teaching people that the load is the problem, and the load is not the problem, the load is a *shape*, and the shape is somebody's."

Eli had got a notebook out and was writing in it with both hands.

"Magnus Conduit," he said.

Adeyemi turned his head.

"That is not in your hall, is it."

"No."

"Then where did you get it."

Eli did not look up. "It is on the building. It is the name on the machine that is under the Hall of Records, and it is on the 1997 incident report, and it is on a brass plate in the sub-basement that somebody took down and put back up, and it is the name of a man called Arvid Magnus, who was a person, in nineteen forty-one, in this building." He turned the notebook round. "The net is his. The seventeen strands are his. The Academy did not discover a thread dimension with seventeen collectors in it. It inherited one, from a man who put it here on purpose, and then forgot, and then wrote four hundred pages of theory about the shape of the thing it was doing."

Nobody said anything for a while. Somewhere off the corridor a lift arrived and went away again.

"Mr. Park." Adeyemi took the staff back. "You should be careful how much of that you say to the Council, because the Council will ask you the second question, which is *how do you know it is Magnus's*, and the answer is going to be something about a plate, and a plate is not a foundation."

"I know," said Eli. "I have a reason."

---

## THE STAFF

He gave her the staff at ten past eight, and he made a ceremony of it, which she was not ready for.

"You will want to put it down immediately," he said. "Everyone does. Do not put it down immediately, and do not put it down in a way that is showing off."

Lyra held it. The wood was lighter than it looked and much colder.

Then the shells lit, all at once, the way a room lights when somebody comes in who is expected.

"Say what is happening to you," said Adeyemi, "and say it badly, because I would rather have a bad description from you than a good one from me."

"Everything stopped." Lyra was aware of the eleven people against the walls, and of Zara not breathing, and of Eli closing his notebook. "Not stopped — *waited*. There has been a noise in it since Tuesday, a sort of — it is like standing near a machine. It has not stopped. It has stopped *insisting*."

"And the pendant?"

"The pendant is doing nothing. It has been doing nothing since the archway came down and I did not notice until this second."

"Then it is a compass and it has no north." Adeyemi held out his hand and she gave the staff back, and he put it in the crook of his arm like a man putting a coat over a chair. "Your mother's name was Lin Ho Chen. I am going to say her name once and I am not going to be careful about it, because I have been careful about it for five years and I have had enough."

He said it. Then he said the next part.

"She came here in two thousand and two and she was thirty-three and she was the only person in this building in eleven years who had written down the phrase *the dimension is answering*. She found it in a sub-basement drawer, and she told me about it in a corridor, and I asked her what her evidence was and she said *none, Ade, it is a phrase, I have not proved it and I am going to*, and then she laughed, and then she stopped laughing, and she said *there is a man in the East Tower who has been looking for this for five years and he has got it wrong, and I do not think he knows he has got it wrong, and that is the frightening one.*"

"And?"

"And she went into a chamber on the eleventh of September two thousand and eight in order to close a hole, and she did not come out of it." Adeyemi's voice did not change. "And there is a gap in the Council's file at the position where her name should be, and I have written to four offices about it, and three of them have written to me to say that there is no gap."

Lyra found that she was standing.

"Sit down," said Adeyemi. "You are sixteen and there are eleven people in this room and five of them came over on Wednesday from the partner college and two of them are not going to be told anything today, and the exchange group has been in this building since the archway came down and nobody has been in the room with them. Somebody should decide what they are told before it is decided by whoever is nearest. That is a job. Do that job and then come back and be as angry as you like, because you have earned it and I have not."

Lyra sat down.

"One more thing, and then I have to go and be the man who says it in the wrong room." He was already turning away. "You asked me what you could do. Do not answer that question today. It is a good question and it is very large and if you answer it today you will answer it in front of eleven people who have nothing to do with it. Come back on Sunday and ask me again, and I will have had three days to be frightened and it will be a better answer and a worse one."
