# Chapter 15B: Nine Minutes

*Saturday 14 September, 06:20. Three point two kilometres north-east of the Academy.*

*Weave-Quake Counter: 45% instability. Node-failure projection: T+13 d. The reference dataset was destroyed in the Maze collapse of the tenth of September; this is the Council's re-run on partial data, and nobody has said out loud that it is a guess.*

> "The cruelest irony of love is that sometimes protecting someone requires becoming the very thing they will learn to fear."
>
> — Marcus Harlow, Private Journal, Entry #847

## THE CRATER

The site was three point two kilometres north-east of the Academy and it was not a crater so much as a *fold*, and Marcus Harlow had seen four folds in his life and knew the difference.

He stood at the rim with his breath going silver in the cold and his instruments reading sixty-seven per cent local distortion, which was a number he had seen once before, in a garden in Kyoto, in April, sixteen years and five months ago.

The micro-fractures were still walking outward. That was the part that mattered. A collapse that has finished is a hole. A collapse that is still propagating is a *process*, and processes can be told to stop by something with a pulse.

His tablet said: *Site secured. No survivors detected. Recommend immediate quarantine.*

He had read that sentence in a different hand, in a different month, in a report that had come back over a telex, and he had gone on to spend three days in the wreckage with his hands because a piece of paper had said the same thing about two people who were not dead.

He was not going to do that again. He was going to do it differently and he was going to do it now, and the difference was going to cost somebody, and he knew which somebody, and he had written the name down in a notebook he carried and did not look at.

There was a noise.

It took him a while to accept what it was, because it was such a small noise and the microphones on his belt were picking up the folding itself, which sounds like a cloth being drawn slowly across a table.

A child, whimpering, about two hundred metres down the western face.

---

## THE BOY

He went down it fast and badly, and his left knee took a piece of the wall that would be a bruise for a week.

The boy was wedged where a section of the Academy's boundary wall had come down across a service road, and he was about eight, and he was wearing a coat with a school badge on it that Harlow did not recognise, and there was a great deal of blood in the coat and none of it seemed to be his.

Kenji Nakamura, aged eight, of Hokkaido, and his sister Ren, aged nine, who was under a metre of the same wall and who was not coming out, and whom the site team had found at nine o'clock the previous night and had written *no survivors detected* at nine o'clock the previous night, and the order those two entries had been written in was the single worst administrative fact Harlow had ever encountered.

The boy's threads were a wreck in a way that Harlow had only seen once.

He knelt.

Standard medicine would not work. He had been the one who established that, four years ago, in front of a committee, and he had been shouted down by the committee, and the committee had been right, and the boy under his hands was going to die in about six minutes.

There was the other way. The splice.

It took your own coherence and pushed it into somebody else's torn places, and made a bridge, and the bridge held while the child's own system came up. Nobody had ever established what the bridge was afterwards. That was the part the committee had actually been frightened of.

Harlow put his palm flat on the boy's sternum and did it, and it was warm, and it was the last warm thing he did that he was ever going to be proud of in an uncomplicated way.

The boy's eyes came open. Brown. Startled.

"Who's—" He coughed. "Where's Ren?"

Harlow's perception went out across the fold, fast, the way he had taught it to, and it found nothing, and he had known it would find nothing before he started and he did it anyway because a person who has already lost two people will check, every time, for as long as they are alive.

"Ren is not here," he said. "I am sorry. Look at me. You are all right. You are going to be all right."

"...sister."

"I know. I'm sorry. Her name was Ren and I have written it down." He said it because he had learned in Kyoto that the thing people are not told about the ones they have lost is not the loss, it is the fact that anybody bothered to learn the name. "What's yours?"

"Kenji." The boy got it out and then held on to it, the way children do. "Nakamura."

"Mr. Nakamura." Harlow gathered him up, and the splice settled, and it did not feel like a temporary thing, and he noticed that it did not and he did not do anything about it for eleven days. "Can you hold on to my neck? I am going to take you up about thirty feet and it will be the worst ten minutes of your life and I am sorry about that."

"Ten minutes?"

"Under-exaggerating," said Harlow, and started to climb.

---

## WHAT HE DID WITH THE BOY

He had him in the ambulance at 07:11 and on a gurney in the Academy infirmary at 07:34, and at 07:36 he was standing in a corridor outside the ward with a boy who was asleep and eleven items on a clipboard and nowhere to put any of them.

*The site report says no survivors.* That meant the site team's report, which was signed, and which was now contradicted by a living child on a gurney in the Academy's own building, by a stretcher-bearer, and by a nurse who had written the time on a board.

He could say the boy was found at 06:20 and let the record sort itself out.

He could take the boy somewhere the record did not reach, keep him, and keep the connection, and have a child of eight who could not stop feeling his chest for the rest of both their lives.

He did not want to do the second one. He was aware, in a distant and unhelpful way, that not wanting to do it was evidence, and that it was the only evidence he had ever had that he was a person and not a procedure.

He filled in the eleven items. He wrote *08:40, child recovered alive from the western face at 06:20, condition stable, spliced* — and then he took the pen back and wrote the name, and the age, and the school, and the sister's name, and the time the site team had logged *no survivors detected*, because somebody was going to read this file in twenty years and they were going to want to know what he had known and when.

Then he did the thing he had been putting off since 06:20, which was to send it upward, and it took him forty minutes and four refusals, and by 09:20 the boy was in a ward with a security officer outside the door and a notation on his file that read *do not transfer without the written consent of Prof. M. Harlow*, which he had caused, and which he had done deliberately, and which he was going to have to be able to explain.

*He would be able to explain it. That was the whole of his defence, and it was not a good one, and it was the only one he had.*

His tablet chimed. Academy emergency response, Priority Alpha: *Report to the Restricted Archive. Council observers in attendance. Bring nothing.*

He read it twice. Then he read the time on it, which was 09:20, and the time he had spent forty minutes getting a child onto a form, and he understood that he had just missed an appointment that was more important than one child, and he understood that this was a *decision* and not an oversight, and he wrote it down as a decision.

*Nine minutes.* In a garden in Kyoto he had been nine minutes late getting to a bench where two people were standing, and everything since had been the correction of those nine minutes, and here they were again, in a different field, with a different child, and he had spent forty minutes, and the forty minutes had not been for the boy.

He put the tablet in his pocket and went to the Archive, and on the way he stopped at a window and looked out at a building in which two hundred and eleven people had been in a hall yesterday learning what a number meant, and he felt, clearly, for about four seconds, that he was the villain of the story.

Then the four seconds ended, the way it always did, and he went to the Archive.

---

*Thread Resonance Note: Splice bridge established, 06:20–07:11. Subject: K. Nakamura, 8. Stability: not assessed, as no method exists. Bridge is permanent. Recommendation: none available.*

# Chapter 16: The Mathematics of a Daughter

*Saturday 14 September, 21:40. Harlow's laboratory, East Tower.*

*Weave-Quake Counter: 47% instability. Node-failure projection: T+13 d. The reference dataset was destroyed in the Maze collapse of the tenth of September; this is the Council's re-run on partial data, and nobody has said out loud that it is a guess.*

> "The most dangerous thread manipulators are not those who wield the greatest power, but those who have experienced the greatest loss. For in their hands, thread magic becomes not a tool of creation, but a desperate attempt to undo what cannot be undone."
>
> — Emerson Harlow, "Theoretical Limits of Thread Manipulation," Private Journal, 1941

## WHAT THE OBSERVERS LEFT BEHIND

There were two of them and they had been in his laboratory for four hours and they had spent three of those hours in silence, and Marcus Harlow had discovered over the course of the afternoon that he found this restful in a way that ought to have worried him more than it did.

Councilor Yuen had asked one question. It had been at two o'clock and it had been this:

"Professor, when you say the lattice has been load-bearing since nineteen forty-one, do you mean the physical structure, or do you mean the *arrangement*?"

And Harlow had said: "Both, and I can show you, and you will not enjoy it."

And Yuen had looked at him for a moment too long and then had said, "No. Tonight, please."

They left at six. The door had a seal on it that was not physical and he watched it settle.

He put the kettle on, and then did not make tea, and then made it.

---

## THE BOX

There was one object in the laboratory that was not an instrument. It was a lacquer box with a chip out of one corner and it sat where he could see it from the chair, and he had moved it eleven times in four years and it had ended up in the same place every time.

Inside: a photograph, a hairpin, a stoppered vial with four dry petals in it, and a paper crane, folded out of thread-paper, grey at the creases.

The photograph was of a woman and a girl under cherry trees in a garden in Kyoto, taken in the spring of nineteen ninety-six by Marcus Harlow, who was in the frame in those days and who was not in this one, because he had handed the camera to a neighbour and had gone to fetch the good tea and had come back forty minutes later to a garden with a hole in it.

Aiko was thirty-one in that photograph and looked about twenty-six, and she had put her hand flat on the girl's head the way she did when the girl was concentrating.

Mei was six, and would be seven in April, and in the photograph she had both hands in the woman's coat pocket and was looking at the camera as though it had personally offended her.

He did not take the photograph out often. He took the *hairpin* out, more, and never the photograph, and he had worked out the reason for that some years ago and had not enjoyed working it out.

---

## THE THING THAT HAPPENED

The collapse was in the garden at approximately four in the afternoon on the eleventh of April, nineteen ninety-seven, and Harlow had been running a calibration for a Syndicate cell in the Annexe, forty metres away, and the Annexe had gone first.

Mei had been in the garden because it was April and there was blossom on the thing by the wall and she was seven.

The facts he could verify: he had arrived at the garden at four minutes past four. The fracture was already open. Aiko was nineteen metres into it, on her knees, holding the two ends of it apart with her hands, doing with her body the thing that a weaver is not supposed to be able to do with her body. Mei was beside her, and Mei was weaving. Mei was seven years old and was weaving, and Harlow came round the side of the house and saw his daughter at the centre of an open dimensional fracture with her arms out and her eyes shut and her whole face *quiet* in an expression he had never seen on a child and has never been able to describe to anybody, including himself, and that was the moment.

The fracture did not close. It took her instead.

What happened to him is the part that is genuinely unusual, and it is the reason he is a professor at this Academy and not a ward somewhere, and it is the part he has never once told anyone.

He was not injured. He was not wounded, not scarred in the ordinary sense, and the physicians in Kyoto found nothing, which they were extremely pleased about, and the silver in his hands and up his forearms is a consequence and not a wound.

What happened was that he was *severed*. Every thread-connection he had ever had — to his wife, to his daughter, to the eleven people on his mother's side who had taught him the practice he was the finest living practitioner of — went out of him in the space of about forty seconds, and it did not snap. It came apart cleanly, like something withdrawn, and it took the record of it with it.

Aiko and Mei survived.

He found this out in nineteen ninety-eight, from a recovery team the Syndicate had hired, and then he found out the rest of it over the following year, and the rest of it was this: they had no memory of him. Not suppressed — *absent*, in the specific and total way that a name is absent from a person who has never had a brother. He had been removed from them at the level of the thread, which meant at every level, and the reason was that the fracture did the only thing a fracture can do to a man who is standing in one: it took what he was made of and it put it somewhere else.

He found them in the autumn of that year, and it cost him the last of his money and a considerable amount of a skill he had sworn in nineteen ninety-four never to use on another person. They were in a coastal town under new names. Aiko was a librarian. Mei was at school.

He went to the library, and he stood in front of her for ninety seconds, and she was thirty-three years old and she had his hands.

"I'm sorry," she said. "Do I know you?"

He did not go near her again. He did not go near the child either, and there was a reason for that one that he has never stated, and the reason is that she was eight years old and she had looked at him in a street in a coastal town in the November of that year and had walked to the other side of the road.

The order came in 2011, nine years after he joined the faculty here, and it was filed from Portland by a woman of twenty-one, and it was signed in her own hand using a handwriting he had taught her when she was five. He read the first line eleven times. He has never told anyone that he read the first line eleven times.

She was twenty-three when he ran the survey that put her name on the list.

The Syndicate's people in Portland had been watching her for four years. They had a file. The file said, in the second paragraph, on a page he has read so often that he can recite the layout: *female, 23, thread sensitivity unconfirmed, no institutional contact, no training, no community.* Which meant a woman who could probably see and had been told by everyone she knew that she was mad.

He has a photograph of her at twenty-three, at a bus stop, in a coat.

---

## WHAT HE TOLD HIMSELF IN 2013

The argument he had been making to himself for eleven years, and which he had got very good at, was not that it would work. It was that it was *correct*.

He sat with it in the way you sit with a thing you have carried a long way.

There was a girl in the east dormitory who had been in this building ten days and who had, on Thursday, in front of two hundred and eleven people, asked a question about a number and made a professor of mathematics stand up in a corridor and say that a girl of sixteen was braver than a committee. He had watched that on the internal feed at half past two on Thursday with a cup of tea going cold in his hand, and he had felt something he had not felt in sixteen years, and it had taken him two days to identify it, and it was not admiration.

It was *relief*, and it was the relief of a man who has been waiting for a specific tool.

Here is the calculus, and he set it out on paper every evening, and the paper is what the Inquiry will later find, and he has never denied a line of it.

The Animus Argenti is a rare thread type. It is not, as the popular accounts have it, a bridge to the dimension's mind. It is a *tone* — a pure resonant frequency, rare enough in the population that a working seer's lifetime will contain one or two. It is, in the terms of the 1941 material, a stabilising reference, and the lattice in the basement under the Hall of Records has been load-bearing for seventy-two years on a resonance it has been starved of since the day it was built, and that is the whole technical content of his life's work. He is not mad. He has been right since April, nineteen ninety-seven, about a machine, and everyone in this building has been very careful not to look at it.

A reference tone is required at the moment of transfer.

The subject will lose her thread-sight. He has modelled it. The probability of survival is high; the probability of permanent perceptual loss is higher still; there is a residual probability of neurological damage that he has written down, in his own hand, in a document he has signed, and he read that document to himself in June and then went and had dinner.

And if she has some of what Mei had — if the girl is what the incident report says she is, a full-spectrum late developer with an unremarkable family and no training at all — then what she has is the one thing that was standing in a garden in April nineteen ninety-seven with her arms out, and it will do the same work.

One thread-seer's sight.

Against: two lives, neither of which is available to be saved. His wife, who is not walking around in 2013 and has not been since 1999, and his daughter, who is twenty-three, and is on a bus, and will be twenty-three when the bus arrives.

He wrote the sentence down on the sixth of November, two thousand and twelve, and he has a copy, and the copy is the first document the Inquiry read out, and he asked for it to be read out.

> *One thread-seer's diminished capacity, against the restoration of two people, is arithmetically trivial. It is not morally trivial. I want it entered that I knew the difference between those two things for the whole period and that I went on doing it anyway, and that if you are looking for the moment at which I became the villain of this, it is not that sentence. It is a corridor, in nineteen ninety-eight, in front of a woman with my hands.*

The seal on the door lifted at eight minutes to ten.

---

## WHAT CAME UP THE STAIRS

Security had brought him three things and left them on the bench, which was how Security did things, and which he had stopped minding in about the eleventh year of doing it here.

The first was a Council minute, countersigned, giving him standing authorisation for a live draw on the seventeen campus collectors for a period of seventy-two hours, renewable once. It had gone through at six o'clock that evening. The Security Chief had not voted it down. The Security Chief had abstained, and had filed a minority note, and the note was in the packet, and the note said that she could not support a draw on a campus lattice containing minors without a medical protocol, and that no medical protocol had been produced.

He read the note four times. He would think about it for a long time afterwards and it never once changed what he did, and he could never work out whether that made it more forgivable or less.

The second was a signal strength. A campus alert, filed at twenty-two minutes past eleven that night, from the Restricted Archives: a breach on the dimensional-incidents shelf, four parties, dampening wards defeated. It had gone to his console and to the Security Chief's, and the Security Chief's copy had been opened first, at twenty-four minutes, and not forwarded for eleven minutes.

Somebody had opened the Kyoto file tonight. Somebody had gone to the shelf, and had not gone to the shelf by accident, because the shelf is at the back of a room and the file is in a box and the box is not labelled.

The third was a list of names, and the second name on it was in a hand he had not seen for sixteen years, on a form that had been filled in by a woman in Portland who did not know what she was filling in, for four hundred dollars, in March.

He put all three down and stood at the window and looked at the east dormitory, where four lights were on and three of them belonged to children who had no business in the Archives and every business in a cell two floors above the one he was standing in.

*Good*, he thought, and the word arrived without any argument attached to it, which had not been true of the last few.

He had spent eleven years being the man who asks a question and then waits, because the waiting was the ethics, and the answer to a question that was not asked was nobody's fault. In six hours he would be a man who had simply done it.

He took the paper crane out of the box and put it in his breast pocket, where it had been since nineteen ninety-four, and it was the only thing in his life that he had not once been tempted to use.

*Weave-Quake Counter: 47% instability. Node-failure projection: T+13 d. The reference dataset was destroyed in the Maze collapse of the tenth of September; this is the Council's re-run on partial data, and nobody has said out loud that it is a guess.*

# Chapter 17A: Four People and a Box

*Saturday 14 September, 23:47. The Restricted Archives, three floors below the library.*

*Weave-Quake Counter: 47% instability. Node-failure projection: T+13 d. The reference dataset was destroyed in the Maze collapse of the tenth of September; this is the Council's re-run on partial data, and nobody has said out loud that it is a guess.*

## THE STACK

The Restricted Archives were three floors down a spiral staircase that had been built for people carrying books and not for people running, and the last flight had a landing on it that nobody cleaned, and Eli Park found that he could feel the shape of the drain in his shoe.

The wards down here were so strong that Lyra could barely get a reading on Milo four feet away. It felt like being put into a coat two sizes too large. She had not known that you could lose *people* and still be in the same room as them.

"Dimensional Incidents," said Milo. "Classified. That is the whole shelf. That is the entire shelf and there are eleven boxes and nine of them say nineteen ninety-seven on the spine and one of them says nineteen forty-one and I have not opened the nineteen forty-one one and I would like to record that I have not opened it."

"Open the nineteen forty-one one."

"No. I would like to *record*—"

"Open it after."

He opened the nine ninety-seven ones instead, and they put them on a reading table under a lamp that came on when Milo put his hand near it, and it was, for about four minutes, the calmest any of them had been since Wednesday.

Then Eli found the second set of blueprints underneath the incident report and everything after that happened at once and to everybody.

## THE FIRST THING, WHICH WAS A PHOTOGRAPH

It was a group photograph, taken before, and there were five people in it, and it was the ordinary kind of photograph that institutions keep: a group outside a building on a day that went wrong later.

Harlow was second from the left, and he was younger, and he had his hand round the shoulders of a Japanese woman of about thirty with her hair up.

Beside her, holding both their hands, was a girl of six or seven with her hair in two plaits and an expression of open hostility towards the camera.

"She's got his jaw," said Zara. "That's the daughter. She's the daughter, isn't she."

"Missing, not dead." Eli had the incident report. He had it before Lyra did, because he always got there first and then waited, and she had worked out over three days that this was not coldness. "Two confirmed dead, three missing presumed dead. And then the missing list, and the missing list says *Aiko Harlow, thirty-four* and *Mei Harlow, seven*."

"Thirty-four is not thirty-two," said Milo.

"Thirty-four is what the file says."

"Thirty-four is two years out in the same direction as everything else in this room," said Milo, and he was not being clever, he was being aggrieved, and Lyra understood the aggrievance, which was that in the last three days a boy in this building had been on a machine and a girl had signed a card for herself and the file had said *no survivors detected* nine hours before the boy was found alive.

Zara had both hands flat on the table. Her eyes were shut.

"Zara."

"Sorry. Sorry. Give me a second. It's not — I'm not doing the thing." She opened her eyes. "There's residue on all of it. Not information. It feels like a room after an argument."

"That's not an answer."

"It's the only honest thing I've got and I'm not going to pretend it's a reading." She took her hands off the table. "Lyra, they've been *erased*. Both of them. That's not sad, that's — that's the specific texture of a person who's had something taken out. I've felt it on people whose dead have been stolen out of them and it doesn't feel like grief, it feels like a *hole with a shape*. If that's what's on this file then he isn't a monster, he's a man who's had his family taken and not told."

"He has a machine that harvests students."

"Yes. Both. Can they not both be true, and can we stop *arguing about which one is more real*, because we have about ten minutes before somebody comes down those stairs?"

They looked at each other across a reading table in a basement.

"I want to burn it," Lyra said.

"Say that again."

"The whole box. I want to put it in a corridor and burn it, and I have wanted to do that since about half past eleven, and I am telling you because I think if I don't say it out loud I'll do it and then I will not have told anybody." She was aware that her voice was doing something she had not authorised. "Because every single person who has ever gone near this has got it in their head for the rest of their life. You have got it now. You have got *Kyoto* in your head and a photograph of a child and you are sixteen, and he did that on purpose, and that is the *point* of it. He put it in a box and he put the box where a sixteen-year-old would find it, and that is not an accident, that is a *method*."

Nobody said anything.

"That's the most frightening thing anybody has said to me this week," said Eli.

"Then I will say it again, because it is true and because I am not going to pretend I did not say it." Lyra put her hands flat on the table as well. "But I am not burning it. Because if I burn it then the next person finds nothing, and the next person is whoever he picks next, and there is no box that stops him, there is only this one."

"Then what," said Milo.

"Copy everything. Photograph all of it. Leave the box exactly as it was so that nobody can say we took it, and go to Weaver tonight, before the shift change at midnight, and if she will not do anything then we go to the Council in the morning and we go *public*."

"That is a good plan," said Eli.

"It is a *procedural* plan," said Milo. "It has got a box in it and a signature in it and it assumes that a woman we have never met, at eleven forty at night, will act on the word of four first-years. It is also the only plan any of us has said out loud, and I hate it, and I am not going to be the one who says no. Say no, Lyra. Say it and give me a reason."

"I don't have one."

"Then we do yours." He took out his phone. "Both of us can be wrong, and we can both be doing it, and the *four* of us can be wrong in four different directions, and then nobody can shut it down with one phone call." He looked up. "That's four numbers and four addresses and four people who do not know each other's names. I'm not being brave. I'm being annoying. It's a different skill and it is the one I have."

Eli put his phone face down on the table with both hands on it, deliberately, and Lyra understood that he had been out-argued by somebody using his own tactic and that he was going to be fine about it later and unbearable about it now.

"Do it," she said. "All four of you. And Eli—"

"Yes."

"The photographs establish that she was there. That's all they establish. Remember that, because in about a month I am going to want them to establish more than that, and they won't."

## THE SECOND THING, WHICH WAS A DRAWING

The Conduit schematics were under the incident report, folded twice, and the paper was forty years old and had been unfolded and refolded so many times that it went soft.

Eli spread them flat and put his hands on the table either side and did not touch them for eleven seconds, which was the longest any of them had ever seen him not touch something.

"There are two sets of handwriting on this," he said. "Look. The heavy one is his, and it's the same hand as the signatory line on the letter of authorisation. And this—" he put a finger down beside the margin, without touching it "—this is small and fast and it argues with him. In the margin. Nine times."

The letter of authorisation was dated the eleventh of June, two thousand and nine, and it was signed by a Council officer, and the name at the bottom of it was Yuen.

"That is a person," said Lyra.

"That is a *person*, and she is on the Council, and she signed the thing that lets him do this, and I want to know whether she read it." Eli read down the margin in the small fast hand, and his voice went strange. "'*This assumes the reference tone is a person. It is not. No living subject has been located. Do not proceed on the assumption that one will be.*' And then, in his hand: '*She will be.*'"

Nobody spoke.

"And again, in hers: '*Whose daughter?*'"

It sat on the table.

"There's a page missing," said Eli. "Between this and the next sheet, there is a page missing, because the numbering jumps from forty-one to forty-three and the binding has been cut and re-cut and I have looked at the cut with my own eyes."

"Then somebody took it out."

"Somebody took it out, and then somebody put the whole thing back in a classified box where a sixteen-year-old would find it on the fourth worst week of her life." Eli sat down on the edge of the table. "Lyra. There's a scheduling line at the bottom of forty-three. It says *subject selection — on the basis of a lineage match, when a candidate of sufficient tone is identified*."

"Is there a name?"

"There's a reference." He turned the sheet so she could see it. "*See file: L.H.C. — Animus Argenti index, 2002.*"

L.H.C.

L.H.C.

"Lin Ho Chen," said Zara.

Nobody said it for a moment, and then Lyra said it, and it was, she understood afterwards, the moment the evening turned over.

"She's been in the file the whole time. She's not a warning in the margin. She's the *index*."

## THE THIRD THING, WHICH WAS THE PHOTOGRAPH THEY SHOULD HAVE READ FIRST

At the back of the box, under the last incident sheet, there was a print that had been folded small and folded often and unfolded so many times that the folds had gone through.

It was a photograph of the garden at the Zen school in Kyoto on the day of the collapse. It was a bad photograph, taken from a low wall, probably by somebody's brother, and it showed a hole in the world about nine feet across with a woman on her knees beside it.

Lyra had to put the lamp on her hand to keep her hands still.

"That's her."

"Lyra—"

"That's *her*. That's the way she's standing. That's—" She stopped. "That's how she stood at the top of the stairs in the studio in Ellery Street, doing the thing with the cup, with her weight on her back leg. I have been copying that stance since I was five years old and I did not know I was copying it."

Zara had gone white. "Lyra, there's somebody at the *edge* of it. On the left. That's not part of the collapse, that's a person standing next to it on purpose."

Eli took the lamp.

He looked at it for some time. Then he said, in a voice that had gone carefully flat:

"There's no name on this photograph. There's no caption. It was not in the incident file — it was in the *box*, behind everything, folded small. And Lyra — your mother is not on the roster of the Kyoto research team. There is no roster entry. I have been through the roster twice."

"So she wasn't there."

"She was there. That is *exactly* what I am saying. A photograph establishes that she was in that garden on that afternoon and not one other thing. It does not establish that she was running the experiment. It does not establish that she was a warning or a conspirator or a victim. It establishes that she was *there*, and there is a document somewhere in this building that says a woman was not, and one of those two is a lie and we cannot tell which from a photograph in a basement."

"Eli—"

"And that," he said, "is the first time in my life anybody has needed me to say a thing I did not want to say, and I am going to want it noted that I noticed I was doing it."

## THE ALARM

It was Zara.

She put her hand flat on the wall and said, very quietly, "Something in the wall just woke up."

There was a sound like a match being struck very far off, and then a sound like a match being struck, and then the reading lamp went out.

In the dark, the black-silver thread came down out of the stonework of the ceiling — not fast, not dramatic, *thoroughly* — and it went along the shelf, and it went along the reading table, and it stopped at the box, and it began, patiently, to record.

"How long," said Eli.

"It was asleep at eleven o'clock when we came down," said Zara. "It has been asleep every time we have been in this room and it is not asleep now and I would like everybody to notice that nobody has told us what wakes it up."

Nobody had told them.

"It is us," said Milo. "It is obviously us. It is a floor sensor and we are four warm bodies and one of us is a—"

"I know what I am."

"Then stop, because I am not doing it, and Eli is photographing, and Lyra is standing there, and you can feel the wall. That is enough. That is more than enough." Milo's voice cracked in the middle of it. "I am not — Eli. Tell her to stop feeling the wall."

*Weave-Quake Counter: 48% instability. Node-failure projection: T+13 d. The reference dataset was destroyed in the Maze collapse of the tenth of September; this is the Council's re-run on partial data, and nobody has said out loud that it is a guess.*

# Chapter 17B: Forty-Nine Minutes

*Saturday 14 September, 23:52. The Restricted Archives, and then the stairwell.*

*Weave-Quake Counter: 48% instability. Node-failure projection: T+13 d. The reference dataset was destroyed in the Maze collapse of the tenth of September; this is the Council's re-run on partial data, and nobody has said out loud that it is a guess.*

## WHAT THE WALL DID

It did not come down the way Lyra had imagined things coming down, which was the problem, because she had spent four years imagining things coming down and she had a whole vocabulary for it.

The stairwell rearranged itself.

Not dramatically. That was the horror of it. One second there was a landing with a lamp on it and a window at the bottom, and the next second there was still a landing and a lamp and a window, and the window was nine feet higher, and the flight they were on had become two flights with a turn in the middle that had not been there.

"Protocol," said Milo. "The campus protocol. That is not the Maze, that is the *protocol*, the Maze is the building in the courtyard and this is the whole *building*—"

"I know what it is." Eli had his phone out and it was photographing, and his hands were completely steady, and his face was not. "Forty-one. Forty-three. I have the authorisation and forty-three and the two photographs. That is what I have. Everything else in this room is the Academy's."

"Lyra." Zara was on the floor. "Lyra, the light—"

The emergency lighting came up red. It came up *everywhere*, all at once, in a building with four floors and eleven hundred people in it, and the whole campus went the colour of a photograph of a fire.

And then the corridor began to make sounds, and the sounds were people.

---

## THE HALL

Milo got them into the archive corridor, which was the wrong door, and then into a stairwell that went down, which was worse, and Lyra got them stopped at a landing and made them sit down, and none of them wanted to, and she made them anyway.

"Four things," she said. "One, we do not split up, because the Maze works on people who are alone and I do not know that, I am guessing, but I am not gambling. Two, Eli, phone in your pocket, the photograph is taken, you do not need it again tonight. Three, we are going to be slower than we want to be. Four—"

She had not got to four.

Something came round the corner above them.

It was not a person. That was the thing none of them had been ready for and it was the thing she would remember for the rest of her life: it was shaped like a person and it was about the size of a person and it was made of the *space* where a person would be, and it was walking, and it was walking the way people walk in a corridor at two in the morning when they know where they are going.

Eli Park made a sound that was not a word.

"Threads," said Lyra. "That is threads. That is a bundle of threads at about standing height, and it is moving, and it is *aimed*—"

Zara was screaming.

The screaming was the worst part and it was the part that Lyra kept afterwards: not the thing, but the fact that it worked, that it was designed to, that somebody in this building had built a thing that made a person's fear into a shape and then walked it at them down a corridor.

"Zara. *Zara.* Listen to me—"

"There is — I can *feel*—" She was on her knees with her hands over her ears. "I can feel how much it's *getting*—"

"I know. I know. That is the point of it, that is what it is *for*, and it is not going to work, because being frightened of it is not the same as being alone, and we are not alone, and you are Zara Washington and you stood up in a lecture hall in front of two hundred and eleven people on Tuesday and made a professor of mathematics answer a question."

Zara stopped screaming.

She said, in a very small voice: "I can still feel it."

"I know. Move anyway."

---

## THE STAIRWELL

They lost forty-nine minutes in the stairwell. Lyra could tell afterwards, because the infirmary board and the clock on the wall of the transit office and three separate statements all said 23:52 to about 00:41, and because Milo had kept a count, out loud, of the landings, on the grounds that somebody had to, and because counting was the only thing any of them could do that did not involve being afraid.

They went down eleven landings. They came back up nine. They went down four more and arrived at a floor whose number was painted on the wall in a hand she recognised from the register, and which was three floors *above* where they had started.

The thing came past them twice.

The first time it went by without stopping and Lyra understood that it was not hunting, it was *sweeping*, and that there was a rhythm in the sweeping, and that the rhythm had a gap in it.

The second time, it stopped.

"It has seen us," said Eli.

"It has seen *Zara*," said Lyra.

Because Zara was the one who was making the sound. It was not a scream any more. It was a continuous low sound, the sound of somebody whose fear has become an engine, and it was coming out of her at a rate that made the red light on the walls shimmer, and the thing in the corridor had turned towards it the way a dog turns towards a noise.

"Zara. I need you to do something I am going to be ashamed to ask for."

"If you say *calm down*—"

"I am going to ask you to be frightened of the right thing." Lyra got down on the landing beside her, so they were both on the floor, which made her own terror much harder to hold and she did it anyway. "You are frightened of that thing. It is real and it is coming. Now be frightened of *me*, because I am going to touch your face, and I am going to do the thing my grandmother taught me on Wednesday in a hall, and it is a communion gesture and it is not a control technique, and it works in a room, and it is the only thing in this building that I have ever been taught that I would bet money on."

Zara looked at her.

"Do it," she said.

Lyra put her hands on either side of Zara Washington's face, and did the pattern, and it was not magic and it did not feel like magic, it felt like *asking*, and the cold of the stairwell went out of Zara's face in about nine seconds and the sound stopped.

The thing in the corridor dissolved.

"He'll come back," said Zara.

"Yes."

"Then why did you—"

"Because he was harvesting you and I interrupted the harvesting, and you are still frightened, and both of those are true at once, and we have got to keep moving." Lyra took her hands off. "Eli. It's gone. Now move, and do not stop to write anything down."

---

## THE BURN

The fourth landing they came up was not a landing.

Lyra got there first and put her left hand flat on the wall to stop and the wall was not stone.

It was the black-silver thread, laid into the stonework, the whole surface of it, the way Harlow laid his wards over a boy in a fold, and it did not hurt her at first — it *read* her, fast, the way you read a page, and it read her and found what it was looking for and it took hold and it did what it did to the boy from Kumasi in ninety seconds and it had her for four.

Zara hit her. Not her arm — her shoulder, with the flat of both hands, at full weight, which was the single most useful thing anybody did for Lyra Chen that month and which nobody has ever properly thanked her for.

Lyra came off the wall and went down onto the boards and the skin on her left forearm had gone the colour and the texture of a page that has been in a fire and it did not hurt at all, which she understood about eleven seconds later to be a very bad sign.

"Left arm," said Eli.

"I know."

"You are not going to be able to use it."

"I *know*, Eli—"

"Water," said Milo. "There is a drinks fountain on every floor of this building because I have been here ten days and I have already drunk from all of them—"

---

## THE LOSS

They made the ground floor at twenty to one.

The corridor outside the Restricted Archives had a ceiling that was not a ceiling any more. It had come down in one piece, and it had come down *across* the archive doorway, and it had come down at some point in the previous eight minutes while they were floors above it in a stairwell going the wrong way.

Eli Park got his phone out and looked at the forty photographs on it and then put it away, and did not say anything, and then said, to nobody:

"Forty-one and forty-three are gone. The authorisation, the margin, the schedules, the two of us in the garden. All of it."

"The photographs," said Lyra.

"Four photographs. Three sheets of forty-one. None of forty-three. None of the margin." He was not shaking. "I have got his handwriting saying *she will be* and I have got a signature and a date, and I have got a photograph of a woman at a wall, and I have got us. That's the whole of it. That's what four of us are taking out of this building."

"And the original," said Lyra.

"Under the stair," said Eli.

They went back for it. It took six minutes and it was the stupidest thing any of them ever did and Lyra has said so herself, in print, at length, for years. They went back down a stairwell in a maze for the *original* of a book, because it was her mother's book, and because Eli said — correctly, and it is the last useful thing he ever said in that building before his shoulder — that a photocopy of a thing is a report of a thing, and a report can be said to be a fabrication, and the original is the original.

They got two-thirds of the way down.

The floor of the stairwell at that point was the *inside* of the collapse and the middle of it was the hole, and the hole was eight feet across and there was a draught coming up out of it that smelled of cold stone and nothing else, and Eli Park went over it.

He went over it sideways, in the way a man goes over a fence, and his left arm went into it and his shoulder came out of the socket on the sound of it, and he made no sound at all except a short breath, and Lyra caught him by the collar with her right hand because her left arm was not available.

It is in the record that the original of Lin Ho Chen's working journal was destroyed on the fourteenth of September, in a stairwell, under four hundred tons of the Academy's own roof. It is also in the record — Lyra insisted on this, and fought a Registrar about it for nine days — that the reason it was not carried out is that it had been on a reading table three floors down, and that the reading table was three floors down *because of a decision taken four hours earlier in a basement*, and not because anybody had been careless.

That is in the record. The Registrar wrote it down. It is on page eleven.

---

## THE LAST SIX MINUTES

They were outside the library at nine minutes past one with Eli's shoulder out, Lyra's arm ruined, Zara shaking, and Eli Park refusing to be helped down a step, and they were in an Academy in which two hundred and eleven people had been in a lecture hall on Thursday and in which, at that moment, somebody had the machine that had done this.

Milo sat down on the bottom step and put his good arm on his knee and said, to the four of them:

"So. I put us in a stairwell."

"Yes," said Lyra.

"It was four numbers and four addresses. I said that." He put his face in his good hand for a second and then took it out again, because he was sixteen and he was going to remember that he had said it. "It was still a good idea."

"It was a good idea," said Lyra.

"Okay." He looked up. "Then what's the plan now, because it is one in the morning and I have not got anything."

"I have," said Eli, from the step below, with his arm held against his chest by Zara with the collar of his own coat. "Four of us, four numbers. And the address for number two is the Headmistress, and the address for number three is not a person, it is a *register*, and Council resolutions have to be minuted inside seventy-two hours or they lapse, and it is Monday."

"Which is what day?"

"Sunday," said Zara.

"Then it is Monday tomorrow." Eli got his breath. "I'll go to the Registrar. She is a registrar and it is her job and she is not in the machine. Eli Park is not important enough to be in the machine."

*Weave-Quake Counter: 50% instability. Node-failure projection: T+13 d. The reference dataset was destroyed in the Maze collapse of the tenth of September; this is the Council's re-run on partial data, and nobody has said out loud that it is a guess.*

# Chapter 18A: Through the Vent

*Sunday 15 September, 03:47. A service corridor above Security, one floor below the Headmistress.*

*Weave-Quake Counter: 50% instability. Node-failure projection: T+13 d. The reference dataset was destroyed in the Maze collapse of the tenth of September; this is the Council's re-run on partial data, and nobody has said out loud that it is a guess.*

## THE LOCKED DOOR

Headmistress Weaver's office door was locked at twenty to four in the morning, which Lyra had known it would be, and there was a light on under it, which was new information and worse than the lock.

She stood in the corridor outside it for eleven minutes with four broken ribs of paper in her phone and a left arm she had wrapped in a strip of her own shirt because the clinic had been shut and the boy at the desk had taken one look at the burn and told her to come back at eight, and she had said *it is that kind of burn* and he had said *I know what kind it is, come back at eight.*

There was no plan after that. The plan was on a step outside the library and it was Eli's and it began at eight in the morning at the Registrar's office, and it was eight in the morning and she was here at twenty to four, and the distance between those two facts was about two hundred metres and eleven hours and she could not make it smaller by standing still.

So she went down one floor to find out whose light it was, and the answer to that was on a door at the bottom of the service stair, and the answer was that the Headmistress's night was being spent in Security.

The door to the Security antechamber was ajar. That in itself was information; the antechamber was alarmed and the alarm was off, and Lyra noted that, and then went through, because the two doors at the back of it were shut and there was a ventilation register above the left-hand one about nine feet up.

She got the chair from the antechamber. It was a good chair. She had never resented anything more.

She got it up onto the desk and she put her ear against the register and she heard, in the first two seconds:

"—six per cent, Marcus. Six. You told me four in June."

"I told you four in June in a corridor and I told you seven in a document, and you are now telling me about the six at three in the morning, which tells me that you read the document."

"I read every document you sent me. That is my *position*."

---

## WHAT SHE HEARD

What follows is what Lyra Chen heard through a ventilation register at twenty to four in the morning on the fifteenth of September, and it is set down here because she set it down herself, that night, in the back of a notebook, in eleven words at a time, and because she was careful about the difference between what she heard and what she worked out, and because the difference turned out to matter for a very long time.

She heard two voices. One was Harlow's, which she knew. The other was a woman of about fifty-five with a slight west-coast flattening on the vowels, and it was not Security's voice — she had heard Security all week — and when the woman finally said a name, out loud, to a man, it was *Marcus*, and it was said the way people say a name they have been saying in private.

She heard the words *thread-burn*, and *sixty-seven per cent*, and *cardiac involvement at eighty*, and she wrote all three down and underlined the third because that was the one that was about a person rather than a percentage.

She heard a photograph described: *a woman of thirty-four in a garden and a child of seven with her hand in her coat pocket*, and she wrote *he told her*, and then she wrote *no: she had it. She already had it. She brought it out*, and she underlined the second version because she had been in a basement four hours ago and she had watched a man put a hairpin back in a box.

She heard the woman say the words *reappropriated, absorbed*, and describe a database with four hundred and six entries in it, and the word she used for the entries was *assets*, and Lyra wrote that down and then put a line through it and then wrote it again, because it was true and because she had a rule now, from four hours ago, about not making a thing worse by making it bigger.

She heard the woman say *Kheredu-Seven* twice and not explain it, and Lyra did not know what it was then and did in March.

She did not know the woman's name that night, and has not been able to put it down since, and she has had it since the nineteenth of October, from the last line of a document four thousand words long that was not in any book until that day, a line that gives a girl's name and then gives her mother's: *Her mother's name is Ines.* She has written down the four other things she has about that woman, and she has not written down what she thinks they come to, and she has been asked twice, by people who were not in the room, whether the mother of a fifteen-year-old girl was in it, and she has answered both times, and both times she has said that she heard two voices and that one of them was a man's.

And she heard the words *Demonstration Hall Seven* said by the woman and *this morning* said by the man, and at that point Lyra Chen put both hands over her own mouth, because she had been standing in Demonstration Hall Seven on the Thursday at half past two in the afternoon with two hundred and eleven people in it when Zara asked a question about a number, and she knew what was in that room, and she had stood in it.

She did not hear either of them express a motive. She wants that written down, because she is going to be asked about it for years and she will be asked it by people who were not there and who want it to be simpler than it was. Nobody in that room said the word *revenge*. Nobody said *for Kyoto*. Nobody said *because they took her from me*. They talked about yield, and cardiorespiratory, and a budget cycle, and a child who lives in Portland.

She has thought about that for a long time. She thinks it is the worst fact she knows.

---

## THE PART SHE DID NOT HEAR

The last four minutes she had them and she did not understand a word of it, because it was in the register and it was low and it was two voices speaking at once.

What she could get was this: a name, three times, that she wrote as *Lin* and underlined twice. A word she wrote as *tone*. And a sentence, three times, in a flat west-coast voice, that she copied out word for word on the back of a page in a corridor at ten past four in the morning and which read:

*You are not doing this for her. You are doing this because you have spent sixteen years being the only person in every room who knew, and I am offering you the one thing you cannot buy, which is a witness. When it works, you will be believed. And a man who is believed does not have to be right.*

She wrote that down and she did not know what to do with it for nineteen hours.

---

## THE STAIR

She came down off the chair and the chair made a noise and she had four minutes.

What she did in those four minutes was the thing she has been asked about more than any other, in inquiries and interviews and on the three occasions it has come up with people she was fond of, and the answer is short and it does not make her look good.

She did not go up to the Headmistress's office. The light was still on. The Headmistress was forty feet away behind a locked door, awake, at twenty to five in the morning, on the worst night the Academy had had since nineteen ninety-seven.

She went to the stairs, and down, and across the courtyard, and up to the east dormitory, and she told the others, and she told them everything she had heard, in the order she had heard it, and she did not tell one of them about the four minutes she could not understand, for eleven days.

There is a version of this in which a sixteen-year-old girl bangs on the Headmistress's door at five in the morning with a burnt arm and a notebook full of a conspiracy and it is the beginning of something.

There is a version in which she goes back to her own room with four broken ribs of paper and waits five hours for a boy with a dislocated shoulder to go and see a registrar about a Council resolution, and Lyra Chen chose the second one, on a stair, alone, at ten past five in the morning, and gave as her reason that she did not know whether the woman behind the door was in it.

And the reason she did not give, which she gave instead to Eli Park in October and which is in the record, was this: *if the Headmistress is in it then I have told her I know, and I have four friends. If she is not in it then I have wasted the only four hours I had, and I would rather waste four hours than be wrong about a woman who could otherwise be the only adult in this building.*

She wrote that down at the time, in the notebook, in the eleven-word hand, and underlined it, and then she went to bed and slept for fifty minutes, which she was going to need.

---

## THE NOTE SHE WROTE AT SEVEN

She was not the one who went to the Registrar's office.

The four of them sent it by terminal, at twenty-one minutes past seven, from the west corridor, with a copy to the Registrar, and then they all went to Demonstration Hall Seven because the notice said 8:00 sharp and no exceptions and because in the end nobody in this account ever found a way to make a rule mean anything other than exactly what it said.

Eli Park went to the Registrar's office on the sixteenth, with his arm in a sling made out of Zara Washington's cardigan, and he was in it for two hours and eleven minutes, and he had already read the letter, because it had gone to her the evening before, which was the last useful thing anybody did for that office in September.

The Registrar of Threadweaver Academy is Dr. Mei-Hua Chen, and Lyra had met her once, for eleven minutes, in a corridor, on the fourth day. Lyra had not told the other three that this was true, on the grounds that she could not be the person who raised it, and the other three had worked it out on their own inside about four minutes and had then been so careful with her about it that she has described them, in print, as *appallingly decent*.

*What none of the four of them knew at twenty-one minutes past seven on the fifteenth of September was that Dr. Chen had already been asked eleven questions about this building by a grandmother in Westbrook in the spring of 2007, and had not answered one of them, and had known there was a machine under her for eleven years, and had been waiting, since the eleventh of September, for somebody to hand her something she was allowed to act on.*

# Chapter 18B: The Name in the Box

*Sunday 15 September, 06:05. The Observatory, and then Harlow's laboratory.*

*Weave-Quake Counter: 52% instability. Node-failure projection: T+13 d. The reference dataset was destroyed in the Maze collapse of the tenth of September; this is the Council's re-run on partial data, and nobody has said out loud that it is a guess.*

> "In watching her courage bloom, I see the ghost of my daughter's smile—and remember what it means to hope for something beyond my own redemption."
>
> — Marcus Harlow, Private Journal, Entry #1,891

## SIXTY-SEVEN

The thread-burn had been at sixty-one on Thursday and at sixty-seven at three o'clock this morning and it was at sixty-nine when he put his sleeve back down over it.

The rule was simple and he had written it down in nineteen ninety-four and had never broken it: eighty is cardiac involvement. He had said that number out loud, in a corridor, at three in the morning, to a woman who was taking notes, and he had said it because he wanted it written down, and he had been annoyed for eleven years about wanting it written down.

He had nineteen days, at that rate, and he was aware that the rate was not a constant and had not been a constant since roughly Tuesday.

He got up to the Observatory because the Observatory was the only room in the building with a telescope in it, and because a man at seventy per cent does not sleep, and because there is a particular kind of tiredness that comes from lying still.

The morning was coming up grey off the roof of the Hall of Records and there was still a haze over the courtyard where the archway had been, the better part of two days on, and if he looked at the wrong part of it he could see the shape of what had fallen.

He had looked at it every morning since the eleventh and he had got very good at it.

## WHAT HE SAW

Lyra Chen's window was on the fourth floor of the east dormitory and at ten past six she was not in it.

He had learned, over four days, a great deal about her, and he was aware that a large part of what he had learned had come from the fact that she had no idea she was being watched, which is the most useful condition a person can be in.

She was not sleeping. She had not slept more than fifty minutes at a time since Wednesday and he had the numbers, because the numbers were on the same feed as the numbers, and because he had built the feed.

That was a real thing and he would not pretend otherwise later, in the room where he said the true things. He had built a system that told him where a sixteen-year-old was and how long she had been asleep and how fast her heart was going, and he had built it in order to protect a girl, and the protection had never once required the numbers, and he had looked at them anyway, every day, and had felt the ordinary specific shame of a man reading something he had no right to read.

She was the only person in this building doing what she ought to be doing.

That was the finding he had not been ready for. He had come into the eleventh of September with a *candidate* — a tone, an index entry, a reference frequency in a box — and he had been in the building four days and had watched Zara stand up in a hall of two hundred and eleven people and refuse to accept a number without a unit, and had not at the time known which of the two of them he was watching, and had watched a professor of mathematics stand up in a corridor afterwards and say so in public, and had watched a first-year boy with a bad shoulder go and ask a registrar about a Council resolution because he had decided that it was her job and not his.

He had told himself on the sixteenth of November, two thousand and twelve, in a document he has a copy of, that the arithmetic was trivial. It had never once occurred to him to check what she would *do*.

## THE FILE

The Syndicate's offer had come up the stairs at nine o'clock the previous evening and it had been this, in writing, in a hand he had never been able to trace:

*Site report 14/09 corrects on receipt of instruction. No survivor. No splice. No medical protocol. Standard.*

Four lines. It would have taken the site team four hours.

He had sat with it from nine until midnight and at midnight he had written back three words, which were *not required thank you*, and he had understood while writing them that he was not refusing it for Kenji Nakamura.

He was refusing it because of the eleven seconds in the corridor in nineteen ninety-eight.

*I'm sorry. Do I know you?*

If he took the four lines, then on the twenty-seventh of November, two thousand and twenty-six, there would be a boy of twenty-one in Portland who was not in a record, whose sister was not in a record, and who would spend the rest of his life with a permanent bridge in his chest that nobody had authorised and no doctor in the world could remove, and who would not be able to find the paper that said *he did this to me*.

Harlow had had his own taken. He had spent the last eleven days deciding that he would not take another, and he had decided it at midnight, alone, having calculated that the boy in the crater was worth less to him than the girl in the dormitory by an enormous margin.

He wrote that down too. It is page four of the file.

---

## SEVEN IN THE MORNING

The tab on his bench at ten past seven said *draw schedule — live, 15/09 14:00 to 18/09 14:00* and *subject: see 43/41* and he looked at it for a while, and then he opened the drawer and took out the paper crane and put it in his breast pocket, and then he did the thing he had done on every working day for sixteen years and which had a ritual to it, which was to put the photograph face down and not open the box.

Then he stopped with his hand on the lid, and he opened it again, and he took out the photograph, and he looked at the girl in it, who was six, and whose jaw was his own.

She was twenty-three. She had her mother's hands. She was on a bus. She was showing thread sensitivity, unconfirmed, untrained, and entirely alone with it, and a great many kind people had been telling her for four years that she was unwell, and the only thing Marcus Harlow had to offer her in exchange for what he was about to do with a girl in the east dormitory was the truth.

He put the photograph back, face down.

In the anteroom, outside the door he had not opened, somebody had left a tray with four mugs on it, and the four mugs were the kind the Academy gave you at a coffee morning, and there was a piece of paper under it in a fist of capitals: *WE HEARD THE WHOLE BUILDING. WE ARE ALL RIGHT. WE ARE GOING TO THE REGISTRAR AT EIGHT. DO NOT COME DOWN.*

It was in Harlow's handwriting. It was a note he had written at eight o'clock in the morning on the fourteenth, yesterday, and left on his own bench, and someone had picked it up and put it on this tray and left it where he would find it.

He read it twice.

Then he took the crane back out of his pocket, and looked at the grey creases, and said, out loud, in an empty room, in a tone that was not steady:

"No."

And then he said, immediately afterwards, and he heard himself say it, and he wrote it on page four as well:

"But I am going anyway, and it is because of that note, and I want it on the record that a child wrote *we are all right* on a piece of paper in a drawer yesterday and that is the reason I am going to do this, and that if I had read it at the time I would not have."

He sat down and put his sleeve back over the arm and looked at the schedule, and at seven in the morning on the fifteenth of September, Marcus Harlow began the seven days in which he would tell himself that the thing he was doing was almost over.

---

## THE SAME MORNING, FOUR HUNDRED METRES AWAY

*Sunday 15 September, 06:20. Academy clinic. Lyra Chen's handset, which she has not looked at in nineteen hours.*

Dr. Osei had not telephoned the dormitory. She had telephoned her own registrar, which was a thing Lyra only learned about in December, and the reason she had not telephoned the dormitory was that there was a bell on the fourth bed and Maya Sandoval had been making noise all night, and Dr. Osei had decided at about four o'clock that the amount of noise one floor could make was a finite resource and she was not spending it on a first-year who was asleep for the first time in four days.

So the message came at seven twelve, to a handset on the table in the common room, and it said this, and Lyra read it twice, and the second time was worse than the first:

> *CHEN J. — second deterioration, 14/09 04:40. Confusion, intermittent, disoriented to time x3. Two memory gaps documented by bedside staff, both retrograde, both concerning to me. I am not going to telephone you to ask whether your gift is responsible, because it is not, and because a doctor who asks that question on a Saturday morning is asking it in order to be able to write it down later.*
>
> *He asked me for a pen at six and he wrote four words on the pad and asked me whether I had read them, and I had not, and I read them, and I told him the truth, and he said "no, but read them again," and I read them again, and he said "no, read them until they are true."*
>
> *I am transferring him to the Academy clinic on Monday. He is refusing. I have put the refusal in writing and I have put the neurological opinion in writing and the second is not the opinion I gave in Westbrook and I will not pretend that it is.*
>
> *Bring your grandmother. Bring the elder from the Hall of Inherited Practice, whose name he will not say to me and who I have now guessed at. Do not bring the other one.*

# Chapter 19: The Channel

*Sunday 15 September, 07:12. The east dormitory common room.*

*Weave-Quake Counter: 52% instability. Node-failure projection: T+13 d. The reference dataset was destroyed in the Maze collapse of the tenth of September; this is the Council's re-run on partial data, and nobody has said out loud that it is a guess.*

> "When the foundation cracks, the wise do not ignore the tremors. They prepare for the earthquake."
>
> — Ancient Chinese proverb, often quoted by Nai Nai

## WHAT WAS IN THE NOTE

Four of them, one working arm, one arm in a cardigan, and a sheet of paper on the table with a list on it that was the entire product of the worst night of four people's lives.

"We do not go to the Headmistress," Eli said.

He had it propped on the table at an angle that was going to hurt him by ten o'clock and he did not adjust it.

"Explain that, because we spent four hours in a basement and the Headmistress has a light on."

"The Headmistress has a light on at five in the morning, which is either a crisis or a *schedule*, and we do not know which, and if it is a schedule then somebody in this building is meeting the Headmistress at five in the morning about something they do not want in the minutes." He put his pen down. "Worse than that. Do you know what a Headmistress is? She is a *signatory*. That is what she is. She can suspend a professor and she can open an inquiry and she cannot authorise a human-subject protocol, and if I walk in there with a photograph and a signature I am asking her to spend the one credit she has, and she will have to spend it on the *inquiry* and not on us, and she will have to do it in writing, and it will take eleven days."

"Eleven days," said Milo.

"Eleven days is fine. Eleven days is nothing." Eli almost smiled. "The point is not the eleven days. The point is that the letter goes from me, a first-year with a bad arm, to a member of the Council, and the *only* route it can take is through this office, and this office is Security, and Security is a person, and the person is *Blackwood*."

Nobody said anything for a moment. Outside the window the courtyard was still grey and there was a crane on it, and the crane had been there for four days.

"So we do not use the office," said Zara.

"We use the office," said Eli, "and we do it in a way that leaves a mark, and that is what I want to argue about for the next twenty minutes, because it is the only decision we have got and everybody in this room wants a different one and I would like us to do it as an argument rather than as a mood."

## THE ARGUMENT

Zara wanted them to warn the students. Not the Council, not Weaver — the *students*, in the refectory, by name, with the photograph, today, at lunch.

"They will believe me," she said. "Not about the machine. About *him*. If I stand up in front of four hundred people and say that what happened on Saturday night was done to us on purpose, and that it is going to happen again, and that it is a person and not a fault — they will believe that. I have never in my life had that and I am not throwing it away to go and write to a committee."

"That is not a small thing," said Lyra. "That is the biggest thing anybody has said all week. Say why it isn't the plan."

"Because in four hours, every adult in this building will know who said it, and every one of them will have a reason it was wrong, and the *reason it was wrong* is the only part anybody will remember." Zara put both hands flat on the table. "Lyra. If it goes wrong it goes wrong on my name. I am not doing that to Eli."

"Nobody's name is going to—"

"It is absolutely going to be somebody's name, and it is going to be in a file, and in a month there is going to be an inquiry, and at that inquiry somebody is going to ask *why did the first-year with the gift tell four hundred people in a refectory* and I will have to answer it, and the answer will be *because I wanted it to be my fault*." She took her hands off. "I have been the one who wanted that since Wednesday. I am putting it down."

Milo wanted to go to the press. There was no press. What he wanted, it turned out, was to *go outside* — to the gate, to the road, to the eight hundred metres of public footpath where the visitors' cars were, and to stand on it.

"There are forty-one people in the car park from the exchange programme," he said, "and the bus is at nine, and if a coach of forty adults sees four teenagers on a road at eight o'clock in the morning with photocopies, that is not a leak. That is a *witness*, and you cannot put a witness back in a box."

"That gets us killed," said Eli.

"That gets the *file* out of this building."

"That gets the file out of this building and Ms. Sandoval in the fourth bed out of that building too, because if four first-years can get onto a public road then nobody is ever going to believe a medical ward again." Eli was not raising his voice. He was doing something worse, which was being completely reasonable. "Milo. I have been awake for thirty hours and I am not going to insult you by pretending I have not thought about exactly that, and I have rejected it, and here is the actual reason, which is not caution: a coach of forty adults who see four teenagers is a photograph in a newspaper, and a photograph in a newspaper is *a story about four teenagers*, and this is not a story about four teenagers. I have written that sentence down four times in the last two days and I keep writing it because it is the thing I am worst at and I am best at."

"And if the letter goes nowhere?"

"Then it goes nowhere," said Eli, "and we do it anyway, and we do it in a way that leaves a mark, because a mark is the only thing that has ever worked in this building."

## THE MARK

He wrote it out, and it took forty minutes, and here it is in his own hand, and it is two hundred and thirty-three words long, and the second paragraph is the one that matters:

> *To the Council of Threadweaver Academy, from four first-year students, and we are giving you our names because we understand that an unsigned thing can be removed.*
>
> *On 14 September, at approximately 23:47, the Restricted Archives on the lower level registered a dimensional-incident alert and the file "Dimensional Incidents — 1997" was opened. On 15 September, between 03:00 and 04:00, the campus containment protocol was activated for a period of not less than one hour, and in that period the corridors of this building produced physical phenomena consistent with the phenomena described in the report of the Kyoto Collapse of 11 April 1997.*
>
> *We hold four photographs, three sheets of a document numbered 41 in a series whose page 43 is not in our possession, and the signature and date of a Council officer on an authorisation dated 11 June 2009. We will lodge all of it with the Clerk of this Council and with the Registrar of this Academy, in two separate places, at 08:00 today. We are not asking for anything. We are telling you where it is.*
>
> *We are aware that this letter names a person. We are naming him on purpose. We have thought about it for four days and we have decided that a person who has done these things should be named by people he cannot reach, in a building he controls, before the day he intended.*

Eli read it out loud twice and then said, "That last paragraph is stupid."

"That last paragraph is the whole letter," said Lyra.

"That last paragraph is going to be the only reason anybody gets convicted and it is going to be the only thing that gets the four of us thrown out of this Academy." He put the pen down. "Read it again and tell me it is not stupid."

"It's stupid," said Lyra, "and we are sending it."

They sent it at twenty-one minutes past seven from the terminal in the west corridor, addressed to the Clerk of the Council for onward transmission, with a copy to the Registrar of this Academy, by name, and the copy is the part that mattered, and none of the four of them knew that yet.

Eli hit send and then sat back and looked at the ceiling.

"That's the first time I've ever sent anything to anybody," he said.

---

## WHAT HAPPENED TO IT

Here is what happened to it, and the four of them learned all of it over the following nine days, and none of it was reported to them directly; they reconstructed it out of dates on documents that other people had signed.

The terminal logged the message at 07:21 on 15 September. Every terminal in the building logs to Security. This is not a secret and is not presented as one; it is in the student handbook, on page four, under *conduct*.

Security received it at 07:21. The Chief read it at 07:38, which Eli established from a read-receipt on the copy addressed to the Registrar, which the Registrar's office was obliged to return.

The copy to the Registrar arrived, and Dr. Mei-Hua Chen read it at eight o'clock the next morning, and it is the reason a woman who had known there was a machine under her for eleven years was in her office at eight in the morning on the sixteenth with four photographs and a boy's bad arm and a written list of everything they did not have.

The copy to the Council did not, for eleven hours.

At 11:41 that morning the Council minute authorising a live draw on the seventeen campus collectors — a minute that had been passed at 18:00 the previous evening, in a room with two observers in it — was filed, and the version that went into the minute book carried a marginal note in the Security Chief's hand, which read: *forwarded to Prof. Harlow, 11:40, per standing instruction.*

The four of them did not learn that until the Inquiry, in December, in a room with a recording running, and by then it did not matter to anybody, which was, in the end, the whole of what they had wanted.

---

## THE NOTE ON THE BACK

The last page of Lyra's notebook for the fifteenth of September is a list, and it is in her hand, and it is short, and it is the thing she actually decided that day:

1. *The letter is in. It has a time on it.*
2. *Milo is right that a mark is the only thing that has ever worked in here and he is wrong about the road and I do not know yet why he is wrong.*
3. *Zara put her name down. I did not put mine down. That is a thing to look at later.*
4. *Eli will not sleep today and will not say so and I am going to have to notice it myself.*
5. *There is a woman forty feet above me who had a light on at five in the morning and I did not knock.*
6. *A boy of fourteen came out of that building yesterday and the Registrar got him out in eleven minutes and nobody had to be brave. A girl of fifteen went into it on the eleventh and has not come out, and there is no margin on her at all, and both of those are true at the same time in the same corridor and nobody has said so out loud.*

She did not write down what any of them were going to do next. She had been going to, four times, and each time she had stopped, because the last time she had written down what she was going to do next she had been fifteen and she had written *ask Dad what to do* and had not done it, and had gone into a hospital alone.

So she did not write it, and at nineteen minutes past seven that morning the notice came under the door, and it was slid rather than pushed, which she noticed, and which she has never worked out.

*Emergency Assembly — All First-Year Thread Students.*
*Demonstration Hall Seven. 8:00 AM Sharp.*
*Mandatory Attendance. No Exceptions.*
*Subject: Critical Thread Stability Protocols.*

# Chapter 20: The Trap Springs

*Sunday 15 September, 08:00. Demonstration Hall Seven.*
*Weave-Quake Counter: 54% instability. Node-failure projection: T+13 d. The reference dataset was destroyed in the Maze collapse of the tenth of September; this is the Council's re-run on partial data, and nobody has said out loud that it is a guess.*

## THE SUMMONS

The message arrived at dawn, slipped under dormitory doors while the Academy slept. Official Academy letterhead, Headmistress Weaver's seal, urgent red borders:

*Emergency Assembly - All First-Year Thread Students*
*Demonstration Hall Seven - 8:00 AM Sharp*
*Mandatory Attendance - No Exceptions*
*Subject: Critical Thread Stability Protocols*

Lyra stared at the notice, the jade pendant cold against her skin. After their discoveries in the archives, after witnessing Harlow's alliance with Blackwood, every official summons felt like a trap.

But it was more than that.
