"It is." He said it without any anger at all now, which was worse. "I am not angry that you lied. Everyone lies, and you lied badly, which at sixteen is a normal standard. I am angry that you did not believe that three people who liked you would rather know something terrible than be polite about it. That is the whole of it. That is the entire content, and I would like it minuted."

The room got very quiet. Zara's hand was still on the back of Milo's neck, where she had put it minutes ago and had apparently forgotten about.

"An anchor," Lyra said. "Something to hold onto when the storm hits."

"Exactly," Eli said. Lyra reached out, her hand finding Eli's. It was cool, steady. Then she reached for Zara's, then Milo's, until their four hands were clasped together in the centre of their small circle.

"If one of us falls," Lyra said, her voice clear and strong despite the tremor that ran through her, "if one of us is lost in the darkness, or overwhelmed by the storm..."

Eli's fingers tightened around hers. "The others will be the anchor."

Zara's voice joined his, firm and unwavering. "We hold fast."

Milo did not complete it. He was turning the paper cup over again and looking at the corner of it, and after a moment Lyra understood that he was not refusing; he was waiting to see whether anybody was going to help him say it.

Zara put her hand flat on the cup and stilled it.

"We pull them back," Milo said — to her hand, not to Lyra — and his voice was rough and it was not steady, and he finished the sentence anyway, which was the entire content of Milo Reyes at sixteen and would be for some considerable time afterwards.

"If one falls," Lyra repeated, "we anchor."

They held on for a while. Nobody counted it.

And it was not a solemn thing, and it was not a vow, and it was not forgiveness; Eli had not forgiven her and he had said so, out loud, and was not going to unsay it. It was a piece of procedure, agreed by four people who had just discovered they needed some: that nobody in this room would ever again take a piece of paper off a door without showing the three of them, and that nobody in this room would ever again decide, alone, what another one of them could afford to know.

*If one falls,* Lyra thought, *somebody has to have been told. That is all a pact is. Somebody has to have been told.*

---

## THE INFIRMARY INVESTIGATION

*Later. The expanded infirmary, Ward C.*

Dr. Amara Osei moved between beds with a healer's signature extended ahead of her like a lamp carried into a dark room. The Academy infirmary had become a different building. Emergency protocols, four extra beds in a space built for six, a whiteboard at the end of the ward with a hundred and ninety-eight names on it in three colours, and thread-infused walls that had been soothing blue in September and were now pulsing a slow, embarrassed amber.

"Stable," she said, at the fourth bed. "And I want the word *stable* on the record as meaning what it says on a chart, which is that the heart is doing its job and the thread signature is not coming back on its own. Ninety-eight to sixty per cent on visual. Three at total. And a cochlear bleed in two, which will heal, which I am including because otherwise the only thread loss anyone mentions will be the thread loss."

"Thread-blind," said Dr. Mei-Hua Chen.

"Thread-blind. Yes. And I would like it said out loud in a room with these children in it, that thread-blind is not the same as blind, and is not the same as dead, and is not a thing they did to themselves by working too hard in a laboratory." Osei wrote something on the board in the third colour. "That is the third colour. That is what I am actually saying. Every one of these names is going to leave here able to do something. It will not be the thing they came for."

Zara was in the doorway. She had asked, and had been told no, and had asked a second time in a different direction, which is a skill she had.

"Supervised," Chen said. "No direct contact without Dr. Osei's clearance, and not on the first two days, and not with anybody in the first six."

"I can be careful."

"You can be *supervised*," said Osei, "which is a different thing, and I know it is a different thing, and I am still doing it."

Zara came to the fourth bed. Maya Sandoval, third-year, who had collapsed in a corridor on the tenth of September and had not opened her eyes since. Zara's threads went out — Lyra felt that too, the way you feel somebody's hand on a door in another room — and stopped about a foot short of the girl, and came back wrong.

"There's something in the way," Zara said.

"Obviously."

"It's not *in* her, it's *on* her." Zara's voice had gone very flat. "It's a mesh. It's not scar tissue, it's not residue. It's *laid against her signature*, the way you'd lay wire against a wall to keep a door shut. And it's still connected to something. It's still—" She put her hand flat on her own sternum, and then took it off again, fast, as though it had burned. "It's still *drawing*."

"Drawing what."

"I don't know. I could feel it taking and I couldn't feel where it was putting it." Zara's face had gone the particular colour of somebody who has understood something and would very much like to be wrong. "It felt like it was feeding something that isn't her."

The four of them looked at each other in the particular silence of people who have all, independently, been thinking about a sealed case in a sub-level with an inventory number on it.

"There is a containment case," Dr. Chen said slowly, "which was removed from a sub-level at approximately half past ten last night by order of the Council's compliance office, and which contains an object of unknown function that was found inside a housing that was not on any inventory, and which I have been trying to get into a locked store since six o'clock this morning."

"Is it—?" Zara started.

"I do not know. I am not permitted to open it, and I have asked to be permitted to open it, and the answer I have received is that a student advocate will attend the opening and that the student advocate has not yet been appointed." Chen's jaw tightened. "Which is the whole of the difficulty, isn't it. Everything this week has come down to somebody competent who was not quite competent *enough*, and being a corridor away from the decision."

Eli had come in without anyone noticing, which he later denied.

"Thursday," he said.

"Thursday the Council sits on a vote," said Chen. "On the Nexus Stabilization Protocol. Which was drafted in 1994, amended in 2009, and which every member of this Academy voted for, and which is going to be ratified on Thursday on the grounds that the alternative is worse." She looked at the whiteboard. "And on Thursday they will be told that a machine under this mountain was rewritten last night, and they will be told what it now costs, and they will be told it costs her, and they will ratify it, because it is the only number they have."

"Then somebody had better have a second number," Eli said, "before Thursday morning."

Osei wrote **CASE 41** on the board, in the fourth colour, and underlined it.

"Supervised," she said again, to Zara, and it was not a refusal this time.

---

*Weave-Quake Counter: 45% instability. Node-failure projection: T+34 d. Rebaselined against a held circuit. The load is off the wall and onto a machine, and the model has never been run on this basis before.*

# Chapter 24A: Silver Echoes

*Tuesday 17 September.*

> "The eye sees the ripple. The hand feels the tide. The eye was never the only way we ever touched anything."
>
> — Lin Chen, notebook, undated

*Weave-Quake Counter: 45% instability. Node-failure projection: T+34 d. Rebaselined against a held circuit. The load is off the wall and onto a machine, and the model has never been run on this basis before.*

## THE ROOM

Darkness.

Not the night's texture. Not the thing behind her own eyelids. This was *absence* — void where a vibrant threaded world had been dancing, every day of her life, in front of her without her permission.

She sat in her dormitory room in the dark because the light was unbearable, which was not the same as not being able to see it. Her eyes worked. Her eyes had always worked. It was the *other* thing that was gone, and the trouble was that the other thing had been in the room with her since she was six years old, and now it was not, and so the room was a small warm box with a window and a bed and a girl in it, and there was nothing behind any of it.

She reached for her charcoal.

*Draw,* said the part of her that was Nai Nai, and had been Nai Nai since she was a baby, and was the only voice in her head that had never once been embarrassed. *You have always drawn. You have drawn through crying and through chemistry and through the year after your mother went. Whatever your eyes are doing, your hands are still doing.*

The charcoal snapped. It was the wrong length. She had broken the only two decent sticks in September and had not replaced them because she had not been in this room since.

*Fine.*

She drew the window.

Not what was in it — the actual view, the roofs, the treeline, the Weather, all of that still there and still perfectly ordinary, and she looked at it with ordinary eyes and it looked like roofs. She drew what was *behind* it. She drew the absence. She drew a rectangle of paper and then she went over it and over it with the flat of the charcoal until the paper was warm and the surface had gone furry and dull, and there was a black field where the light should have been doing something interesting.

Then she stopped, because her hand had started to report.

---

That was the discovery, and it was smaller than the shape of it would suggest, and it took her nine days to be able to hold it.

It was not a new sense. It was the old one, moved. She had spent sixteen years reading threads the way you read a page — everything at once, all of it, the whole dense interference of a room, from across it, in an instant, like looking at a photograph. She could not do that now. She could not do it across a room. She could not do it at all unless she *touched* something, and then she could read that thing, and only that thing, and only as far as her hand reached.

She tried it on the window frame. Cool paint, slightly ridged where the brush had gone over, a faint greasy warmth where a hand had rested that morning, and — under that, very faint, very far down, so faint that she was not certain it was there at all — the residue of a person. A temperature of attention. Not a colour. Not a name. A *weather*.

She tried it on the bed. Old linen. Her own, four nights of it, a heavy wool blanket that smelled of the room and of her, and beneath that nothing, because a bed is a bed and does not care.

She tried it on her own wrist, and got her own pulse, which she already had, and put the charcoal down for a while.

The whole of it, she worked out over those nine days, could be summarised as: *it is like being given the ability to read a book, in a language, with your hands, one page at a time, in a library where somebody has taken all the light out.* And the second part, which took longer: *it is not the gift, and it is not nothing, and I am going to have to be extremely careful for the rest of my life not to pretend otherwise.*

She wrote that on the back of a schedule.

---

Nai Nai had telephoned on Wednesday. Lyra had not told anybody that she was not eating, and had not told anybody that she was not sleeping either, and had been eating and not sleeping for nine days, and had answered the phone in a voice she had built.

*Con là của con.*

*Yes, Bà.*

*Did you eat?*

*Yes, Bà.*

*Lyra.*

*I had toast.*

There was a silence on the line of the particular Vietnamese kind, which is not empty.

*Nai Nai listens to your Bàba on the telephone on Sunday and he says the same thing. He says *I am managing*. He has said *I am managing* every Sunday for eleven years and I have never once asked him a question about it because I was afraid of the answer, and now my granddaughter is doing the same thing eleven thousand miles from her kitchen and I have run out of ways to be afraid quietly.* A rustle. Jasmine, always, even in October, because she put the dried leaves in her cardigan pocket and Lyra had never told her that she could smell it over the telephone, and neither of them had ever mentioned it. *So I will ask you the question I did not ask him. What do you need, and it may not be a person.*

*I need—*

*Say the true thing. Not the brave thing. The true thing.*

Lyra had stood in a cold corridor at the end of a landline in a student union with a receiver against her ear and a burn in her hand, at the age of sixteen, and had discovered that the true thing was very much smaller and much more embarrassing than the brave thing.

*I need somebody to not be kind at me for about a week,* she said. *Because everyone's being kind at me and I am going to lose my mind. And I need somebody to be angry. Not at me. Just — somebody else being angry about it, out loud, where I can hear it, because I can't be angry, Bà, I am so frightened that if I let the anger out there won't be anything left and then what will I use.*

Nai Nai was quiet for a long moment.

*Yes,* she said. *That I can do. I have been saving it for eleven years and I have got quite good at it. Your Bàba thinks I am a calm woman.*

---

The knock came on the fourth day, and Lyra knew who it was from the way it was done, which was twice, unhurried, with a pause in the middle, because Eli Park knocked the way he talked.

She had a sheet of charcoal in her hand and a bad drawing on the desk and eleven days of nothing on her face, and she thought about saying she was asleep, and then thought about the fact that she had said *I am managing* to her grandmother on Wednesday and that this was a house where that phrase was now a family joke.

"Coming."

Milo was in the corridor. He was on a stick, in a way he was going to be on a stick for another six weeks, and he had a folder under his arm, and he had not come in without knocking twice, and he had not asked how she was.

"I've been told I can't be here more than twenty minutes," he said. "Osei. It's in writing. I've got it in the folder, I made her put it in writing."

"You made—"

"I made her put it in writing," Milo said, "because I have discovered that I now have administrative skills, Lyra. That is what has happened to me. I am going to be a man who photocopies things. I have accepted it."

He came in and looked at the drawing, and did not say *it's beautiful*, and Lyra was so grateful for that that she almost said something.

"That's not threads," he said.

"No."

"That's the space where the threads were."

"Yes."

He put the folder down and pulled the chair round so that it was facing her, and sat in it — carefully, with the stick, in the way a person does a thing carefully that they will be praised for not doing, and then said:

"Right. Two things, and I want to do them in this order because the first one is the one I'm bad at."

"Okay."

"One. I can't hear it." He looked at his own hands. "Any of it. I've been in this room for forty seconds and I don't know what this room is doing. I have not known since Sunday. Osei says there's a chance it grows along a new route, and she says it in a voice that means she doesn't think so, and I have decided that I'm going to be the sort of person who plans for the boring answer." He looked up. "I can play. I'm going to be *ordinary*, Lyra, and I'm going to be an extremely good ordinary musician, and I have decided to decide that in advance so that nobody else gets to do it for me."

"Milo—"

"And two." He reached into the folder and put a single page on the desk, on top of her bad drawing. "Osei wants you to have this before the Council gets here, and she made me bring it, and she said, and I am quoting, *tell her the twelve-week review is in December and I am not going to soften it, she should plan her year around the real number and not the kind one.*"

It was a schedule. Twelve weeks, laid out in columns: review dates, a hearing, a named keyworker, an accommodation, a review of the accommodation, and a line in the bottom right corner that said **reassess: partial return possible; do not plan around it.**

"That's kinder than a straight no."

"It's the opposite of kind," Milo said. "It's *the same as what you just did to me*." He tapped it. "Lyra. You have spent a week deciding to be fine before anyone can tell you that you're not, and you are so good at it that I didn't notice for four days. I'm not going to be managed. Neither are you. If we're going to be a thing then the *thing* is that we say the real number out loud even when it costs us."

They looked at each other in a dark room.

"Okay," Lyra said.

"Say it."

"December. Twelve weeks. Partial return possible, don't plan around it." She took the page. "And you can play, and you're going to be ordinary, and you decided that in advance so that nobody could do it for you."

"Yes."

"That's the most frightening thing anybody has said to me in a fortnight."

"Good," said Milo. "Now show me the drawing, and tell me the thing you are not saying, and then I have to go, because I am counting the minutes, because the folder says twenty and I have been here for fourteen."

---

She worked out the rest over the following five days, alone, badly, and with a small mother-of-pearl box that had been in a drawer of her desk since the eighteenth of August and that she had not opened since she was a child.

Inside it: spools of silk. Red, silver, undyed. Not a magic kit. A *granny* kit, bought in a shop in a Chinatown in a city three hundred miles from her mother's grave by a woman who had looked at a boy of about nine drawing on a tablecloth and had said, *You have the hands for this. Do not let anyone sell you a better one.*

*The eye sees the ripple, the hand feels the tide.*

She had tied knots in silk for years, the way you learn a thing because somebody older is sitting next to you. She had never once tied one and *meant* it.

She tied one now. Badly. In the dark, by feel, with a charcoal stump and her teeth. A plain overhand, four times round, pulled tight.

Nothing happened.

She laughed, which was the first thing she had done in the room that was not thinking.

Then she tied another one, and this time she thought about the fact that her grandmother's grandmother had tied silk knots in Chợ Lớn in a room with a table and a lamp and four people in it, and that the *point* had never been the knot, and that the point had been that you did it in a room with other people in it, and that there was no version of this that anybody had ever intended to be done alone on purpose.

So she sent a message to three people.

It said: *Come to my room. Bring your own hands. Bring nothing else. I want to try something and I want to do it with you in the room and I have absolutely no idea if it is going to work and I would rather it did not.*

Eli arrived in nine minutes with a diagram. Zara arrived in eleven, out of breath, apologetic, with her shoes in her hand for no reason she could later explain. Milo arrived last, forty minutes late, on a stick, and had brought soup.

It did not work.

That was the first evening, and the only thing any of them learned on it was that a knot is a knot, and that a girl who had spent her whole life being told that her way of seeing was real, and had just lost it, was not going to get it back by wanting it in a dormitory room with three of her friends and a bowl of soup.

But it did not work *in front of them*, and on the fourth evening Zara said, absently, without looking up, while tying something: "Oh — Lyra, don't move,"

and Lyra was sitting with her palm flat on the desk, and the lamp had been knocked half off the table, and Zara had tied herself a knot with silk Lyra had handed her, and there was something under Lyra's hand that was not the desk.

Warmth. Not heat. A warmth with a direction.

*Oh,* Lyra thought. *Oh, it's like —*

*It is like the fork,* she said out loud. *It's like the tuning fork. It's not a thread. It's a* resonance *, and it's in the silk because the silk is a thing that was made by hand and a hand leaves—*

"A hand leaves *what*?" said Eli, who had a diagram, and who would not be talked out of it.

And Lyra said: "I don't know. I don't know yet. But it's not in me. It's in the *thing*."

And Eli Park, who had been holding a diagram, and who had spent nine days in a building with a broken arm and no gift and had been quietly, privately certain that he was now the useless one, put the diagram down on the floor and said, in a voice with a crack in it:

"Say that again. Slowly. Because I've been thinking in the wrong direction for nine days and I think I've been wrong about *who* has the hands."

## WHAT IT COSTS

The things she learned in October, and the things she wrote at the back of the schedule, were these:

**It is close range.** Touch to skin, or to metal, or to a doorframe. She could not read a room. She could not read a crowd. She could not do the one thing she had come here to do, which was to walk into a hall full of frightened children and see what was happening to them, and there was no version of this that got it back, and she had better stop hoping there was within the year.

**It is slow.** Where the old sight had been an instant photograph, this took four seconds to five, and then a decision about whether to believe it. Dr. Osei's word for it was *interpretive*, and Lyra's word for it was *guesswork*, and neither of them was wrong.

**It is exhausting and it is not symmetric.** An hour of it left her with the specific headache of someone who has been concentrating very hard in a language they only half speak. Two hours and she got it wrong — confidently wrong, in detail, the way she had used to be confidently right, and the specific horror of that was its own thing, and she wrote it on the back of the schedule in a hand that was not steady.

**It is useless without a hand.** Which she found funny, and then did not.

**It is hers.** The jade at her throat had gone cold some time in the last week and had stayed cold, and one morning in October she noticed that it had warmed when she put her thumb against the back of it, and did not warm otherwise, and she understood that her grandmother's mother's stone had been doing this for four hundred years for exactly one person at a time, and that she was not going to be told about it, and that she was not going to ask.

She did not tell anybody about the jade.

She told somebody about everything else.

---

On the twenty-ninth of October the Council of Traditions sat, and Lyra Chen went into the room at the back with a folder under her arm, because she had asked to be allowed and Dr. Chen had written a very short letter saying that the student advocate for the opening of the case had been appointed, and had asked whether the person appointed would be the person affected, and had not been told no.

*Weave-Quake Counter: 45% instability. Node-failure projection: T+34 d. Rebaselined against a held circuit. The load is off the wall and onto a machine, and the model has never been run on this basis before.*

# Chapter 24B: The Moment of Choice

*Tuesday 24 September. Sub-level 2.*

> "The most dangerous hour of my working life is not the one in which I do the thing I intend to do. It is the one in which I discover that I have never been the one who decides."
>
> — Marcus Harlow, Council deposition, week 3

*Weave-Quake Counter: 45% instability. Node-failure projection: T+34 d. Rebaselined against a held circuit. The load is off the wall and onto a machine, and the model has never been run on this basis before.*
*Harlow's detention: Sub-level 2, medical wing, ward locked, no devices, no unsupervised contact.*

## 04:23

He stood in front of a machine he had not built, in a room he had never been cleared for, at twenty past four in the morning, and he put his hand flat on a cold crystal housing and said the word that started the end of the most important project of his life.

"Cancel."

And it worked.

That was the part nobody ever believed afterwards, including the four hundred people who read the transcript: it *worked*. The draw schedule, which he had spent eleven years building, terminated in four seconds. The extraction matrix, which had been running continuously since the fifteenth of September, went quiet. The intake readings, which he had known by heart, dropped to nil.

Marcus Harlow stood in the dark and listened to the machine not be on, and felt something go out of him that he had not known was load-bearing.

*All right,* he thought. *All right. Then it was never necessary and I was never necessary and it can be unmade.*

Then he sat down on the floor of an unclassifiable room under a mountain, in a building where he had been a professor for eleven years, and put his face in his hands for the first time in sixteen years.

---

He had been in a locked ward on Sub-level 2 for nine days.

It was a good room. That was the thing that undid him, over and over: it was a *good* room. There was a bed with a cotton sheet. There was a window, which was a panel that showed a recorded valley, and he had watched it nine days running and had begun, in the afternoons, to find that he believed in the light in it. There was a chair, a table, a jug of water, a book nobody had selected, and at 07:00 and at 19:00 a woman named Osei came in with a blood-pressure cuff and asked him how he was, and meant it in the small administrative way that meant it.

Two guards outside. No devices. No visitors except the four on a list, all of them from the Council, all of them from outside the institution.

The Headmistress had said one sentence to him on the second day and then had never come again, and he had thought about that sentence every day since, and it was not the one he expected.

*Marcus, I am not going to ask you whether you are sorry, because you will say yes and I have no way of testing it, and I have watched a great many people say yes. I am going to ask you one question every week, and it is always the same question, and I would like you to get ready for it.*

*What is the first thing you did on the fifteenth of September, and who told you to?*

He had got that one out on the fifth day. He was working on the second.

And in between — in the long, unstructured, supervised middle of it — he had begun, helplessly and stupidly, to *do the thing he had always told himself he was doing*.

He had written to Emerson Harlow's sealed inventory list. He had begun a chronology of the 1941 housing, from the secondary sources and from his own family's papers, and he had found, in a ledger in the reading room in 1943, an entry in a hand that was not his great-grandfather's, recording the removal of a lattice "in unsatisfactory condition" and its reassignment. He had worked out who else in the world had a 1941 lattice, and there were four, and three of them were in museums, and the fourth was under a car park in Lyon.

He had written to Aiko.

He had written eleven letters to Mei and had sent none of them, and the reason he had sent none of them was not courage or cowardice but something he had only been able to name in the eleventh: because he did not know what he wanted from her, and because he wanted *everything*, and because everything was a twenty-three-year-old in a coastal city whom he had located in nineteen ninety-nine and approached once, in a municipal library, and who had asked him — politely, and in perfect good faith, and with a perfectly pleasant smile — whether he might help her find a book on tidal erosion, and who had then telephoned the police, and whose restraining order he had signed a receipt for in the same week.

And then, on the ninth night, Mirembe Blackwood had come through the door of his locked ward with a paper seal and a smile, and had shut it behind her, and Harlow had understood with total clarity that his life was about to resume.

## THE VISITOR

"You are not on the list."

"No. I am the Security Chief, and I signed off your transfer, and I have not been near this room, and I am going to be able to say that truthfully for about eleven more hours." Blackwood sat down in the visitor's chair without being offered it. "Do not sit up. You look dreadful and I have a schedule."

"Blackwood."

"Mirembe. Marcus, we are past surnames." She set a small case on the table. "The Council has a question for you every week, and the question is always the same, and it is always the fifteenth of September, and it is always about who told you. Do you know why?"

"Because they are building a case."

"Because they are building a *chain*." She opened the case. Inside, on grey foam, was a black-silver filament about the length of a pencil. "You are the last link that can be cut, and the reason you are the last link is that in eleven years you never once committed an act that could be evidenced. You severed. You did not harvest. You did not take a child. You built a machine, and drew power through other people's accidents, and let somebody else's hand do the last inch, and by the time the Council asks you what you did you have an extremely good answer, which is *nothing I could prove*."

"I did it deliberately."

"I know you did. There are four people in this building who know you did, and two of them are sixteen." She took the filament out. "And I have spent nine days watching a woman in a good room write letters she does not send, and I want you to understand something about the Council's weekly question, Marcus, because nobody has told you and I would like to be the one."

He waited.

"Five weeks," said Blackwood. "After the fifth question, the tribunal stops asking and starts testifying. Your deposition closes. And you go to the vote, and there is a vote, and the vote is on the Nexus Stabilization Protocol, and every member of the Academy is on that Council, including the woman who asks the questions." She put the filament on the table between them. "So the question is what you would like to happen on Thursday, and I would like to know the answer before Thursday, because I need to be able to promise it."

"Promise me what."

"That the machine comes out of that sub-level and runs in the open, with a Council observer, with a charter, with a stop condition and a name on the paperwork." Her face did not move. "That is the promise. I can get it. It is the only thing on offer. And in exchange you will do one thing, and it is a small thing, and it will take four minutes."

He looked at the filament on the table.

"Case 41," he said.

"Case 41."

"The sealed lattice. Case 41. The object the Council cannot open because they cannot agree who opens it, because they cannot agree who is affected, because *there is nobody left to be affected*, Marcus, that is the entire reason it is sitting in a store room — thirty-nine people, and thirty-seven of them were in there before I was born, and the two who were not, were put there by me, one on the eleventh of September and one on the eighteenth of May, and I know their names and I know what they wanted and I can hear them say it, and there is nobody to *ask*."

"Case 41," said Blackwood, "goes back into the machine. Not destructively. As a *component*. It is a seventy-two-year-old power source, Marcus, it is the only one of its kind on this continent, and the machine is a translator with no power source, and the Council will not ratify a protocol that is carried by a dead woman. If the lattice is not in the circuit, the machine cannot be argued into existence. If it is, everything you and I have built for sixteen years has a legal standing and a budget and a *name*."

"You want to put thirty-nine people back in a cage because the paperwork is easier with a power supply."

"I want to put *two* people back in a cage," said Blackwood, "and I want the other thirty-seven to have their deaths confirmed, and I want that signed by a tribunal rather than argued by a student, and I would like it done by a man who signed a warrant he knew to be false rather than by an eighteen-year-old with a folder."

"They said no," said Harlow. "In the circuit. I heard them. They said no, and I have been sitting in this room for nine days with the two no's in my head and I have not been able to put them anywhere—"

"They said no in a lattice with no power and no listener and no way to know whether the no would survive the morning." Blackwood stood up. "Marcus, I am the enemy and I am telling you this as a fact rather than as a threat: if the Council opens that case, the two of them are released, and they are *released*, and the machine has no source, and the vote goes against us, and the immediate collapse is held by nothing, and somewhere in this valley eleven thousand people find out about it in the worst possible way. And if the case goes back into the circuit, the two of them stay where they are, and the machine has a source, and the vote goes the other way, and eleven thousand people do not have a bad week."

"And the two of them stay."

"Yes."

Marcus Harlow looked at a black-silver filament on a table for a long time.

"You are asking me," he said, "to do to a person what I have spent sixteen years telling myself was a *calculation*, in a room where nobody can hear me refuse."

"No," said Blackwood. "I am asking you to do what you did on the fifteenth of September, one more time, having spent nine days of a good room finding out what you actually believe." She picked up the case. "I will be at the sub-level door at five-fifteen. You have fifty minutes to decide, and I would like you to decide in them, and not to lie to yourself in a comfortable chair and then spend the rest of the week being a better man than you are."

She went.

## THE FIFTY MINUTES

He did not decide in fifty minutes. He had given himself a very long time to decide, over sixteen years.

He sat in the good room with the recorded valley behind him, and he thought about his daughter's voice, and he thought about Emerson Harlow's ledger entry — *reassigned* — and he understood, with a clarity that was almost funny, that his great-grandfather had not written *reassigned*. Somebody had edited it, later, in a different hand, and his great-grandfather's guilt was therefore sixty years older than he had believed and had been sitting in a ledger the whole time.

He thought about a girl in a room under a mountain saying, in a register that was not words, *tell me what is happening*.

He thought about Lyra, at sixteen, in a demonstration hall, reaching up a wire in a shape nobody had taught her, in order to burn out her own gift, because a doctor had told her at four in the morning that her father was dying of the thing she was.

*That is the one,* he thought. *That is the one that is the answer. Not a calculation. A girl with a deadline she invented because the real one was too slow to run at.*

He got up. He washed his face in the basin. He looked at the man in the mirror, who had a scar like frozen lightning running up both forearms, and did not particularly recognise him.

At five-fifteen he went down to Sub-level 7 on his own, because refusing to be at the door was also a decision, and he wanted to be the kind of man who stood at doors.

Blackwood was there with the case. Beyond her, four wardens and a very tired woman in grey with a port seal on her collar.

"Marcus," said Tarrant.

"You are eleven hours early, Mr. Tarrant."

"We are always eleven hours early for you." He looked at the case. "What is that."

"Four minutes," said Blackwood.

"Do not," said Harlow.

He stood in the corridor of Sub-level 7, in front of four wardens and a compliance officer and a woman who was not even his friend, and he made the decision.

"I am not putting the case back," he said.

He felt it go. Sixteen years, and it went in a sentence, and the sentence was not even a sacrifice; it was a piece of arithmetic he had got wrong for a very long time and had only now finished.

Then he said: "And I am not going to let you take it away either, because the lattice has been feeding that machine for seventy-two years and taking the payment out of people who did not consent, and I do not know a single way of opening it that does not kill all of them."

"There's another way," said Tarrant, to his collar. "Dr. Chen?"

"Three," said Mei-Hua Chen's voice. "I have a form. It is a power-of-attorney-plus-authorisation of continued custody, and it requires a person to be *appointed by a living near-relative* of each person in the lattice, and there is not one living near-relative the tribunal will accept of the 1941 intake, and the tribunal's position is that sixty-year absence is a *disqualification* rather than a hardship, and I have been fighting that position in writing since the seventeenth of September and I am going to keep fighting it until somebody with a *gavel* agrees with me."

"So it is the second two," said Tarrant. "It is the two from this year. One of them has been in there since the eleventh and is fifteen years old, and has grown into the substrate of that lattice, and cannot be moved without killing her, and has not been told that anything she says in there is a legally admissible statement. The other has been in there since the eighteenth of May, and is nineteen, and cannot hear, and there is no relative on my sheet and there is no form, and I have left the line blank rather than put a name on it, or a pronoun, and I am aware that a form which cannot be completed without a pronoun is not a form. And I am obliged to tell you, Mr. Harlow, that I have those dates on my sheet and you do not have them on yours."

Harlow closed his eyes.

"Elara Vance," he said.

"Elara Vance," said Blackwood. "Signed the card at the Clinic on the eleventh of September. *Volunteer*, in her own hand, and she was fifteen, and she has a mother in Bellhaven who has been receiving a letter every fortnight from the Clinic explaining that her daughter is in a specialist programme for gifted young people." She put the case down on the corridor floor. "Elena Vance, Marcus, is a person. She is a person with a sister and a mother and a signed card and a statement. And on Thursday the Council is going to be asked whether a machine can be held open by consent or only by the fact that nobody can be *reached*."

Nobody spoke.

"Get me the form," said Harlow.

---

*They gave it to me at nine o'clock on the Tuesday,* he wrote, in the good room, on the seventeenth page, in the space where the letter to Mei would have gone. *A legal pad, and a pen, and a girl of nineteen called Mila Rowntree, who is the Academy's student advocate and who has silver in her hair and who held the paper flat, because I could not get my hand to be steady enough by myself.*

*Elara Vance signed a card at the Clinic on the eleventh. I did not read it. That is the crime. Not that I extracted a girl who refused — she came to me, and she said yes, and she wrote it down herself, and I did not read the card, and I have spent four months telling myself she understood.*

*She understood that she wanted to see. She is fifteen. There is no version of fifteen that understands, and there is no version of a fifteen-year-old that cannot be nineteen when somebody sits in a cage for four hours and is asked what she wants. I do not get to decide which of those two sentences is the true one. I was the one with the machine.*

*Elara Vance has been in a lattice since the eleventh of September. On the twenty-second I telephoned her mother. I did not tell her. I told her that her daughter was safe and that she had been offered a specialist programme and that she would be home in the spring, and the mother thanked me, and I have been sick every day since.*

*I am going to ring her back. I am going to tell her the truth. I am going to say the words* my daughter is a component *to a woman whose name is Vance, because if nobody says the words to her then the words do not exist.*

*And then I am going to sit in this room and wait, because that is all that is left of this, and I have not earned the rest.*

*Mei used to say: some things are supposed to be broken. That is how the light gets in.*

*I have broken something. I do not yet know what the light gets in. I intend to find out, and I have eleven months, which is not very long, and I am not going to be a man who pretends the light is a reward.*

# Chapter 25: Infirmary Reconciliation

*Weave-Quake Counter: 45% instability. Node-failure projection: T+34 d. Rebaselined against a held circuit. The load is off the wall and onto a machine, and the model has never been run on this basis before.*
*Wednesday 25 September.*

The sterile smell of the infirmary had become, over eleven days, a kind of punctuation. It meant: *here is where they put the people the building hurt.*

Lyra had been awake for four hours and out of bed for two, and the two facts were not connected in the way she had expected, because out of bed turned out to be about standing at the end of a bed and holding the rail for eleven minutes, and the world being *horizontal* was not the problem.

The world being horizontal was fine. The world being full of a slightly wrong sort of *detail* was the problem, and it was worst when she was touching things, and it was worst of all in a hospital.

He came in at half past three with a chair.

That was the first thing. He came in with a chair, and then he sat down in it, and then he put a worn leather journal on the cover of the blanket, and Lyra put her hand flat on the blanket by the journal without touching it, and felt about six inches of institutional cotton, and a great deal of *him*.

He was different and he was not different. That was the thing that undid her, in the end, and not the apology.

He smelled like a station platform. He had come on the 14:40, which was a train from Westbrook, which meant he had left the house at six in the morning, which meant he had caught a taxi or walked the length of Maple Street to the station, and Lyra knew what the kitchen looked like at six in the morning because she had been in it four days ago.

He had lost something. She could feel the shape of it in his hand where it rested on the journal — a thin place, a place where somebody had been holding on and had stopped.

He was frightened. She had known that for eleven years. It had never once been on the *surface*.

"Mila," he said, and stopped, and started again the way he did when he had prepared something. "I have read everything. I want to be clear about that first, because I know it sounds like a word, and it is not a word, it is five days. I have read everything in the Clinic correspondence, everything in the Academy's release, and everything of hers that is not under seal, and I have read it in the order she would have read it in, which is chronological, because she was a pedant about order, and I have got quite good at it."

He opened the journal. The paper made its dry sound.

"I want to say three things and I have written them down so that I cannot do the thing I do, which is to make a fourth one that is about me."

"Okay."

"One." He turned a page towards her, and she leaned, and did not touch it. "Your mother was a scientist. That is the sentence I have been not saying for eleven years because I thought it would make it worse, and it does not make it worse, it makes it *possible*, and I am sorry — I am so sorry, Lyra — for eleven years of making it worse."

"She drew pictures of us," Lyra said.

"She drew pictures of us and she wrote down what she could and could not establish, and she numbered her pages, and she has *three* pages in this journal on the ethics of taking a measurement from a child without the child knowing, and the three pages are dated the February before you were born, and they are about a *seer of eleven* she was observing, and there is no note of whose eleven-year-old, and I have spent two days on it, and I am not going to find out, and I want to say out loud that I have looked."

"She was watching somebody's child," Lyra said. "She did it to me."

"Yes."

"Bàba."

"I know."

"She put it in a *journal*."

"I know," said her father, and put his hand over his eyes for a moment, and Lyra felt what that cost him, and did not reach over, because she had learned eleven days ago that reaching over is a thing she had been doing to people for three years.

"Two." He took his hand down. "What I said to you in the corridor, and in the demonstration hall, and on the day of the recital, and at the breakfast table for a decade — that it is mysticism, that it is not testable, that it is not real in the way science demands —"

"Her way of seeing was not testable *that* way," Lyra said. "And neither was yours. You had four publications and she had a *thread dimension*."

"Yes," he said. "And I was not comparing methods, Lyra. That is what I have been telling myself for eleven years, and it is a thing I could say to a colleague. It is not a thing I could say to my wife over a breakfast table, and it is not a thing I was entitled to say to my daughter, and I have apologised for a great many things this week to people who did not ask."

"You were frightened."

"I was *arrogant*," her father said. "Those are different, and the difference is the entire subject of your mother's work, and I had it in my hands for eleven years and I read it as a metaphor about rivers. Because a metaphor about rivers did not require me to change anything."

He turned to a page near the middle and put one finger on it, and Lyra leaned, and read it upside down.

> *控制不是解决方案。共融是唯一的道路。*
>
> *Control is not the solution. Communion is the only path.*
>
> *He says this is sentimental. It is not sentimental. It is the only arithmetic that survives contact with a person. A system that requires a payment from someone who has not agreed to make it is not a system. It is a queue.*

"That is the argument," said her father. "That is the whole of it. It is *arithmetic*. It is not about being nice. A measurement that costs a person something they did not agree to is a *debt*, and you cannot have a science of a thing that goes bankrupt every time somebody finds out what it is made of, and that sentence is *in her journal*, in her hand, in February, and he was a colleague of hers, and he read this, and he built a machine that takes payment from people who did not agree to it, and he called it *necessary*."

They sat with that for a moment.

"Three," said Wei Chen.

He did not look at her. He looked at the journal, at his own handwriting in the margin of a page, in a small cramped script that Lyra did not know and that she recognised absolutely, because it was the script her father used on the backs of grocery lists when he was worried.

"I owe you an apology that is not about your mother and not about the science, and I have been avoiding it since the eleventh of September because I have been telling myself that it is too large to say, which is a thing that fathers say when the apology is not large but *embarrassing*."

He turned the page round and pushed the journal across the blanket, and she put her hand on the cover, and got a fortnight of him through the leather — the train, and a kitchen at six in the morning, and a telephone conversation on Friday with a woman in another state who was being, in his own words, *extremely brave about all of it*.

"I dismissed you," said Wei Chen.

Lyra did not move.

"Not the gift. The *you*. From the day you were six. When you drew a thread between two children in your class and I told you it was a nice drawing and that connections do not work like that, and you went out to the garden, and you came back in and you did not show me — for eleven years." He was looking at the wall. "I made a decision in about four seconds. I did not ask you a single question. I took a thing my child had made — something *she had made* — and I filed it, and I went back to my work, and I have been a professor for twenty-one years, Lyra, and I have never once been wrong about anything the way I was wrong about that."

"Four seconds," Lyra said.

"Four seconds is the lag. I *know* what the lag is now. A girl in a lecture hall told me the number on the twelfth and I have not been able to put it down." He almost laughed. "Four tenths of a second, and I have spent eleven years on the other side of it, and the thing on this side of it was a nine-year-old who stopped bringing her drawings home."

He stopped.

"Mr. Reyes brought me something," he said, after a moment. "On the fifteenth. He asked to take a piece of paper to the registrar and I said no, and he said, *sir, I know it is a stupid thing to ask for*, and I said no again, and he left it in my hand anyway. It was the intake form for a student-safety complaint. It was blank except for the date. He had written *is this the right desk* at the top, and put an arrow pointing at the box marked **INCIDENT**."

Lyra's eyes came up.

"He wrote an arrow," her father said, "pointing at *INCIDENT*, at a desk belonging to a civil servant, at four o'clock in the afternoon, on the fifteenth, because a boy with no gift and a stick could not work out that the complaint he needed to file was not the one he had been told to file. He was not filing it for you. He did not know who to file it for. He was filing it because you were not asking for anything, and he was not going to let that stand."

"There was a note," Lyra said. Her voice had gone somewhere she did not like. "On my door. *Ask somebody.* Zara—"

"You do not need to know who," said her father. "You need to know that a woman who has never once spoken to you, and who is not kind to you, and who is extremely annoying, wrote it on your door. And that you took it off. And that I have been thinking about that for five days, and I have arrived at something, and I am going to say it, and you are not going to like it."

"Okay."

"You did not take it off because you thought you could manage," her father said. "You took it off because you did not believe that anybody who was not paid to care about you would turn up. And the reason you did not believe that is a table in a kitchen and a sentence about a drawing. And the sentence was mine."

He stopped. And waited, and did not rescue her, and did not fill the space with anything at all, which was — Lyra understood, distantly, several days later — the single most remarkable thing her father had ever done.

"Do you want me to say it," she said. "The word."

"I am not going to say it."

"Why not."

"Because you did not *do* anything to me that could be forgiven, so there is nothing for me to be forgiven for, and if I say the word then I have made it about me." He turned the journal back. "What I did was withdraw from a nine-year-old. There is no forgiveness for that. There is only a child who has to live in a house with it, and then a teenager, and then a woman. And the only thing available is what comes after, which is showing up at half past three with a chair for the rest of my life."

"That is not an answer," Lyra said.

"No," her father agreed. "It is a *job*. I am not offering you the end of it. I am offering you the next four months of it, badly, on a train, with a bad back, and I would like it noted that I have a very poor record of doing things I say I will do, and that I have never in my life told you I would do something and then not done it, and that this is the entire extent of my claim to be worth speaking to."

"And then what happened," Lyra said. "On the fifteenth. You were at the front."

Her father put his hands flat on the table.

"I was at the front because a Headmistress with a bad back and no legal training stood up in a corridor at ten past nine in the morning and said, *I am going to be in that room, and I would like it noted that I am not a lawyer, and I am going to sit there anyway, and I am going to ask one question a week for a year.*" He said it carefully. "And then four wardens carried you past me on a trolley, and I said your name, and you were not able to hear me, and I have been told that I was told to. That is the entirety of what happened to me in this building, Lyra. I was a man with a chair in a corridor."

"You should have been."

"I know. That is what I have been writing on the backs of pages for eleven days." He turned the journal round again. "Sit in this chair. Do not be a hero. You are extremely bad at being a hero, and every single thing that has happened in this building since the fourteenth has happened *because* you were bad at it and somebody else had to come and hold the other end. That is not a criticism. That is a job description. Mine."

---

The light through the window had gone the colour of the inside of a peach. Somewhere down the corridor a door closed, and somebody laughed at something, and it was an ordinary sound in an ordinary corridor and Lyra found she could not account for how she had got here.

"Can I ask for one thing," she said.

"Yes."

"Next time," said Lyra, "ask me. Before you decide anything. About anything at all. Even something small."

Wei Chen's hand stopped on the journal.

"I will not be able to do that straight away," he said, "and I would rather tell you the true version than the brave one, because you would see through the brave one, and because it is a thing I will have to practise like a scale, and I have not used one since 1998."

"Then practise it on something small."

"What?"

"Something *small*," Lyra said. "Something that does not matter. Practise on the thing where you decide for me whether to have the last of the rice, and see whether you notice that you have decided. And then do it again, and then one day try it on something that matters." She pushed the journal back across the blanket. "Because I am not — I am not going to be all right with you straight away, Bàba. And I think you have decided that you are going to be forgiven, or that I am going to be *all right*, and neither of those is a thing I can give you, and I would rather tell you now than in six weeks when you have written eleven more pages about it."

Her father was quiet for a long moment.

"Six weeks is about right," he said.

"Then I will say something now, and I want it minuted, because you are a man who minutes things." Lyra put both hands flat on the blanket. "I do not forgive you. I am not going to say that word to you for a long time, possibly ever, and if I ever do say it I will not do it in a hospital."

"That is—"

"I am not finished. I am also not going to be *away* from you. Those are separate decisions and I have thought about which of them I am making." She turned her own hands over, and looked at them, and they looked like her mother's, and had looked like her mother's since about 2008. "You are allowed to be in the room. That is what I am offering. Not the room where I was nine. You get the room where I am sixteen and I am frightened, and I am going to be frightened in front of you for a while, and it is going to be *unpleasant*, and you will not be able to fix any of it, and you will have to be in it anyway. That is what showing up with a chair means. It is not a reward. It is a chore."

"Yes," said Wei Chen.

"Say it back to me. In case I am inventing it."

He did not look away, which cost him something she could feel through the blanket.

"You are not forgiving me," he said. "You are allowing me to be in the room. And it will be unpleasant. And I will not be able to fix it. And I am not going to be away."

"Good." Lyra's throat had closed up and she made herself go on anyway, because she had found out nine days ago that going on was the only skill she had left. "Now the other thing. Which is that it is not just you."

Her father went still.

"Miss Vance is in a study programme in Bellhaven because a man in this building did not read a card. Mr. Reyes cannot hear rooms. A girl called Maya Sandoval has not opened her eyes since the tenth. A boy called James Chen, who is my cousin, has been in Ward C since the twentieth with a signature the model cannot read, and his name has been in a building the entire time, and it has cost nothing, and nobody has said his name out loud to a person who is not an accountant." She heard her own voice go up and did not stop it. "There is one boy in this hospital who has to be told I am sorry, and I have not told him, and I am not going to be able to for weeks. And I would like you to know that I am not going to be able to. And I would like you to *not fix it*, Bàba, because you will want to, and it is not yours."

Wei Chen took his hands off the journal.

"Eleven names," he said.

"Twenty-three," said Lyra. "On Dr. Osei's board. Three colours. And one line in the fourth colour that says **case 41**, and nobody will open it, and before October is out they are going to vote on a machine that is carried by a person, and I am going to go and sit at the back of that room and say what I am allowed to say and no more, and you are going to be in the front, and I would like you to be in the front, and I would like it to be *unbearable* for you."

He bowed his head. It was not a small movement.

"Yes," he said.

---

He stayed until visiting hours were over. He read out loud from the journal — not all of it; there were pages she was not yet ready for, and he did not read them, and did not comment, and the not-commenting was so complete that Lyra noticed it and could not stop noticing it for a week.

At the door he stopped with his hand on the frame.

"She is in there," he said. "In the translator. In the room that is running the whole valley on a current that is not paying anybody. Every minute that machine holds, somebody who is dead on a list somewhere is being *carried*."

"Then that is a debt," Lyra said, "and debts have an end, and the end is not me standing in a corridor feeling bad about it."

He nodded, and went, and the door closed, and the ward went on being the inside of a peach.

---

# Chapter 26: The Ask

*Wednesday 25 September, 21:00.*

> "Thread perception is not limited to sight. When one pathway closes, the Weave often finds another. What the archives of every tradition agree on, and what no textbook in this Academy says, is that the second pathway is never the first one again."
>
> — Lin Chen, "Beyond Visual Thread Perception," unpublished notes, 2001

*Weave-Quake Counter: 46% instability. Node-failure projection: T+34 d.*

## THE TWO OFFERS

There had been two offers on the table in the last week and Lyra had been given both of them by people who meant well, and she had turned both of them down, and she had understood at the time — with a clarity she had privately been very proud of — exactly why.

The first had been Osei: *you can go home, and your grandmother can be told you are sleeping badly, and I will write that the retreat is on medical advice, which is true, and nobody will look at a document I have signed.*

Lyra had said: *Bà would have to lie to me. To my face, for weeks. And she would do it, and she would do it well, and she would never tell me, and I would spend the rest of my life knowing that the one person who has never lied to me is lying to me.*

The second had been Weaver, in person, on the second day: *Room 41, on Sub-level 2. It has a lock and a corridor outside it and a warder on the corridor, and I will put your grandmother in the next room for as long as you want her there, and nobody comes in who is not on a list, and I will be on the list.*

Lyra had said: *Thank you. I want to think about it.*

And what she had come up with, in the end, in a cold room with a bad view of a car park, was this:

*That is a very good offer and it is an offer to be safe, and I am sixteen, and I do not know one single thing about myself any more, and the only person I have ever been able to find out anything with is in a chair on the other side of a corridor with a stick.*

She had said yes to Room 41 on the twenty-fourth of September, which was a Tuesday, and which she would remember for the rest of her life as *the Tuesday I decided to find out what I was.*

---

She had not, in the days between, told anybody that she was the reason the number on the wall had gone from 45 to 51.

She had not told anybody because she had worked out — with the same small, awful, private clarity that had been her companion all fortnight — that it was not true, and that wanting it to be true was a sort of thinking she was going to have to give up, and that the giving-up was going to take longer than everything else she had to do.

*The machine is running on a dead woman,* she would tell herself, on the bad nights, in the phrasing she had constructed on the third night and had been chewing on ever since. *The load is on the lattice. If I come out of it, the load goes somewhere else, and there is nowhere else, and eleven thousand people find out. That is the arithmetic. That is not a debt I can pay by being brave about it in a corridor. It is a debt that has to be *paid*, and the only currency is somebody in the circuit, and I am the only somebody they have found, and I am sixteen and I am in a hospital.*

And underneath that, the thing she would not say, which was:

*And if I go back in there, and this time somebody is holding a document, and I sign it, then I will have done it twice, and twice is a decision.*

---

## THE COUNCIL, DAY 32 — THE TWENTY-SEVENTH OF OCTOBER

The Council of Traditions had sat on the thirty-second day and had not ratified the Protocol.

Lyra was not in that room. She was in a corridor outside it, on a chair, with a folder on her knees, because Dr. Chen had asked whether the person appointed as student advocate for the opening of Case 41 would be the person affected, and had not been told no.

What she had been told afterwards, in the corridor, by a woman of nineteen with silver in her hair who had been in the room for eleven hours:

"They're going back to it. In two days. Because the machine is running and they cannot vote on a machine that is not finished, so they have to finish the machine first, and finishing the machine requires the case, and the case requires an advocate, and the advocate is you." The girl had laughed once, without any humour in it at all. "You have accidentally discovered the entire method of this institution. Nothing happens here except through paperwork, and if you *want* something to happen faster you have to supply the paperwork, and the things that happen fastest are always the things somebody has already written down."

"What did they say about the lattice?"

"Nothing, because they do not know what is in it. They have known since the sixteenth that it is people — Osei's board, Ward C, a hundred and ninety-eight names, Zara's *still connected*." The girl wiped her face with the back of her hand. "They have known it is people for sixteen days and they have not opened it, because the *procedure* for opening it requires next of kin, and thirty-seven of them are unlocatable, and the tribunal's position is that sixty-year absence is a *disqualification* rather than a hardship, and your aunt has been fighting that position in writing since the seventeenth, alone, on a form nobody has countersigned."

"And the two who are left?"

The girl looked at her.

"The two who are *locatable*," she said, "are a fifteen-year-old girl held in a care programme at Bellhaven, with her mother in the same town, and a woman dead since 1941 with a sister still living in Sakai who is eighty-one and has not been told. There is a form. It is *on a desk*. Your aunt says the sister will sign, and the mother will not, because the mother has been receiving a letter every fortnight for six weeks explaining that her daughter is in a specialist programme, and the mother is not going to sign a thing that says the opposite."

The folder was heavy on Lyra's knees. It was a copy of her own deposition, and a request that the Council rule that a signed card obtained by a person in a position of authority is not a consent, and a page of Osei's board transcribed, and eight pages of her mother's journal, and a photograph.

The photograph was Elara Vance at a Clinic open day, in a borrowed coat, squinting, with her hand halfway up as though she had been interrupted mid-wave. She was smiling. She had signed a card, four days later, that nobody had read.

They had not shown it to her mother because they were writing a letter every fortnight saying the opposite, and Lyra had spent a long time in the last four days understanding that the letter was not a deception exactly — it was a *holding operation* — and that a holding operation is a thing you do to a person when you cannot afford the truth yet, and that it works, and that it is still a thing you are doing *to* a person.

*Her name is Elara Vance*, Lyra thought. *And she signed a card and went into a machine and said out loud, in a lattice, over four hours, that she wanted to stay — and the only reason she wanted to stay is that sixteen days in there had grown into her, and I asked her a question she could only answer one way and then I honoured her answer, and both of those were right, and one of them was a horror.*

*And her mother is getting a letter about a specialist programme.*

*And in two days a man in Council grey is going to stand in a room and say that a fifteen-year-old's card is not a consent, and that the girl said what she said, and that the girl is alive, and that the girl will be asked again.*

*And I am going to be allowed to be in that room. Because I wrote the note.*

---

The Council had also, on the thirty-second day, done three other things.

It had adopted Osei's name list, in three colours, and required that every one of the hundred and ninety-eight be contacted **by a person who was not the medical officer** before the end of term. There were a hundred and ninety-eight names and eleven days and four trained people, and it was not going to be enough, and it had been done anyway, and that was the whole of what reform looked like in this building at this stage: a thing that was insufficient, minuted.

It had voted, unanimously and with the Headmistress abstaining, to suspend Professor Harlow's faculty rights *without* prejudging the deposition, which was a distinction that had cost Dr. Chen a week of her life to get into the wording.

And it had set a date, and the date was the reason the folder was so heavy.

---

## THE VISIT

She had seen him once since the fifteenth, in a locked ward on Sub-level 2, for twenty minutes, with a warder on the corridor.

He had stood at the window-panel the entire time, because he had told her he was not going to sit down with her — *you have come here to be talked out of something, and I would rather do it standing up* — and because he had, over nine days in a good room, arrived at a set of opinions about how he would rather be treated than about how he would rather appear.

He had told her four things.

**One.** There were two machines. The one the Council was ratifying ran on Sub-level 2 and had been running since the fifteenth on the load of the lattice alone, because on the twenty-fourth — *last Tuesday, at four in the morning, by my own authority, with no warrant* — he had cancelled the draw on the whole system and the cancellation had held.

**Two.** The other one was under the East Tower, three hundred metres from where she had woken up strapped to a table, and it had been running continuously since the eighteenth of May, and it had a second housing, and it had been taking four people at about one a month.

**Three.** Nobody in the building knew about the second machine except him and the person running it, and that person had been lying to him about it since May in order to keep him funded, and he had believed it, because the alternative was that the network walked and eleven thousand people had a bad week.

**Four.** The four names. Two were on the board in Ward C in the second colour. One was in a care programme in Bellhaven and had been for four months and had cost nothing and had never been spoken, and he was a nineteen-year-old called Tobi Fadipe who had been in Sub-level 3 since the eighteenth of May, and he was the reason Harlow had stood up and not sat down. And the fourth was a boy of fifteen who is alive, and whose mother has been told, and who has not been asked for by name in this building yet.

*That is what the man is for,* Lyra had thought, going down the stairwell. *Not the machine. The man.*

And then, on the fourteenth step, she had understood the rest of it, and had sat down on the step because her legs had stopped being available, and had understood that she now knew three things that nobody in that building had known on the morning of the fifteenth:

that there was a second machine; that somebody was still feeding it; and that a man in a locked ward had cancelled the only system he had legal authority over, on his own signature, and had told nobody, and had then told her.

---

## THE THREE QUESTIONS

She wrote them on the back of a schedule on the thirty-third day, and she has said, in every account she has given since, that writing them down was the entire difference between this and every other bad week of her life.

**Q1.** What is the load, physically, and what happens to it if the person carrying it says no?

**Q2.** Who is holding the trigger on Sub-level 7, and what is the shortest path from a custody ward to a locked door in this building?

**Q3.** If thirty-seven people have been dead since 1941 and two have not, and the Council cannot open the case because it cannot find the next of kin — who is the person the procedure has failed to count?

She did not write a fourth question, and the reason she did not write a fourth question is that the fourth question was *what do I want to happen*, and she did not trust herself to answer it on her own, and she had spent nine days learning that this particular instinct of hers was the one that killed people.

And underneath, in smaller writing, the thing she did not intend to show anybody:

*I am frightened that the answer to Q3 is me, and that I will be extremely good at it, and that being good at it will feel exactly like being right.*

---

On the thirty-fourth day the Council reconvened on the Nexus Stabilization Protocol, and the case was opened, and the machine was either authorised or decommissioned, and Lyra Chen went into the room at the back with a folder on her knee because she had asked to be allowed and Dr. Chen had written a very short letter saying that the student advocate for the opening of the case had been appointed, and had asked whether the person appointed would be the person affected, and had not been told no.

At 11:40 the night before, a note had gone out from the registrar's office, addressed to three people. It said:

***Come to my room. Nine o'clock. Bring the thing you have been not saying.***

And under it, in a different hand, because the registrar had insisted:

***Not from me. From her. She has written it out. She is frightened to send it. I am sending it. — DC***

---
