# Chapter 26B: The Visit

*Thursday 26 September.*

> "You are going to want me to be a better man than I am, because it is more comfortable, and because you are sixteen and it is your job to find reasons. I would rather be audited than comfortable. Auditing is what I am good at. It is why I got here."
>
> — Marcus Harlow, eleventh day of custody

*Weave-Quake Counter: 51% instability. Node-failure projection: T+34 d. Rebaselined against a held circuit. The load is off the wall and onto a machine, and the model has never been run on this basis before.*
*Deposition, eleventh day. The question this week: what was the first thing you did on the fifteenth of September, and who told you to do it?*

## THE WARD ON SUB-LEVEL 2

The warder on the corridor was called Achebe and had been told that the girl coming up the stairs was to be let in without being searched and without a second person present, and had been told that by a Headmistress in a note, and had read the note twice, and had said, "Right," in a way that suggested he had a number of further questions.

Inside: a good room. Cotton sheets. A window that was a panel showing a recorded valley, and it was raining in the panel, and it was the wrong weather for October, and Lyra decided within about four seconds that she was going to lose her mind in there or be fine, and that which of the two happened would probably be decided by what the man in the corner said.

He was standing at the panel, not sitting.

"You should sit down," Lyra said, from the doorway. "You've been standing for the whole time I've been on the stairs."

"I am not going to sit down with you," said Harlow. "You have come here to be talked out of something and I have spent nine days working out what, and I would rather do it standing up. Sit down. You have a stick coming and a bad shoulder and the chair by the table is the only one that will take both."

It was, she found, annoyingly, true.

"You have about twenty minutes," he said. "The warder is not lying to you. I asked them to tell you the truth about it even when it worked in my favour, because I have discovered in the last nine days that I would rather be caught than comfortable, and it turns out that is nearly the same thing."

## WHAT HE COULD NOT STOP

"The Council has a machine," Harlow said. "It is called a translator. It is running. It is held open by a thing in a housing under the floor which I have spent eleven years being unable to read and which a nineteen-year-old student advocate is to read in four hours, in October, while I sat in a good room writing letters."

"I know what the lattice is."

"Yes." He turned from the panel. "Then you know that the machine is running on a person, which means that on Thursday the Council are going to be asked to authorise a machine that is carried by the dead, and that the answer is no, and that when the answer is no, the machine stops."

"And then?"

"And then the load goes back onto a wall under eleven thousand people who have not been told anything." He said it without any drama. "I have run it four thousand times. There is no arrangement of the remaining time in which the wall holds. There is one, and it is you, and you already know that, and you have known it since the seventeenth of September, and you have been chewing on it alone, which is the most predictable thing you have ever done."

"You did not come here to tell me to do it."

"I came here to tell you what I tried to do last Tuesday and what happened, because in eleven days nobody has asked me, and it is the only thing in the last four years that I am not ashamed of, and I would like somebody to hear it and then do as they like." He put his hands on the table. "Last Tuesday I cancelled the draw."

"You what?"

"I cancelled the Protocol's draw schedule. All of it. At four in the morning, in a room under this mountain, by my own authority, with no warrant, on a Monday night." He said it flatly. "It worked. It is still cancelled. The machine on this level has been running on the lattice alone since the twenty-fourth, which is the only reason it is running at all."

Lyra put her hand flat on the table. She was not sure why she had done it. The table was institutional steel and gave her nothing but a faint cold.

"So why hasn't he used it?"

"Because the machine on this level is not the machine on Sub-level 7." Harlow's mouth did something that was not quite a smile. "There are two installations, Lyra, and I built the one on Sub-level 7, and the Council is ratifying the one on this level, and I have spent four months being extremely careful never to say so in a room with a microphone in it."

The cold of the table moved up her arm and settled somewhere behind her sternum, where the warm thing usually was, and the warm thing was not there, because the warm thing had gone into a machine on the fifteenth of September.

"Where is the Sub-level 7 machine."

"Under the East Tower, three hundred metres from where you woke up in a locked ward in September, and it is running, and it is the one with the second housing, and it has been running continuously since the eighteenth of May." He said it in the voice of a man reading a charge sheet. "I have been in a locked room since the fifteenth of September. I did not start it. I cannot stop it. Nobody in this building knows about it, because the one person who knew about it has been lying to me about it since May in order to keep me funded, and I have spent four months believing a woman who has a wrist device and a silk charm and no visible means of support."

"Elara Vance is not the asset," said Lyra slowly. "Elara Vance is fifteen years old and she signed a card she did not understand and she has been in the machine ever since."

"Elara Vance is a *recruitment*," said Harlow. "The Security Chief found her at the Clinic on the eleventh of September and put her in front of me, because I have a well-documented weakness for children and a documented inability to read a consent form. The letter on your sketchbook on the first day of term is not the asset. The asset has never once told you anything." He turned his face, and for a second the mask came off it entirely, and what was underneath was not a monster. "Mirembe Blackwood is the asset. She has been the asset since 2009, and I have known since the twentieth of May, and I have said nothing, because the alternative was that the whole network walks, and eleven thousand people—"

"Eleven thousand people," said Lyra.

"Eleven thousand people," agreed Harlow, "on a wall that has been failing since Kyoto, under a programme that every member of this Academy voted for in 2009 and is going to vote for again in December, and I am not going to stand in that room and tell them that the only reason they slept last night is a lock I turned in a corridor at four in the morning. So I have done nothing, and you have been chewing on the arithmetic by yourself, and your father is on the 14:40 train."

---

She sat in the good room for nine minutes after he finished, and he stood at the panel where the rain was falling in the wrong month, and neither of them said anything for a while.

"You are going to want to know why," he said eventually. "Why I told you. You will have a theory and I would rather pre-empt it than have you construct one."

"Because I am the person you decided about."

"That was in May. Last Tuesday I did it for a different reason and I am not proud of it." He turned round. "I did it because you are going to be in a room in four days and you are sixteen years old, and because whatever you say in that room is the only thing in the entire process that is not going to be written by a man in grey, and I have spent nine days in a good room discovering that I do not want to be the only voice in this."

"That is not an unselfish reason."

"It is the *least* selfish one available to me," said Harlow, "and I am aware of how that sounds, and I would like to draw your attention to the fact that I have not asked you for anything at all, which is a sentence I have not been able to construct since 2009."

"You want me to go into the machine."

"I want you to know what the machine is, and where it is, and who is running it, and that the person running it has been lying to me for four months and to you since the first day of term." He looked at his own hands. "What you do about it is not a thing I am permitted an opinion about, because I am the last person in this building with standing to have one, and because I have already had a standing once and it cost a fifteen-year-old girl everything she had."

There was a bell in the corridor. In nine minutes, a warder named Achebe would open a door.

"Marcus," said Lyra.

He looked up.

"You gave the deposition's third question to the tribunal in your own words," she said. "That is a thing a person does when they have decided to stop managing how they come across. My father says that is a chore and not a reward." She stood up, which took longer than it should have. "It is a chore. It is also the only thing anybody in this building has done since the fifteenth of September that I know was not a technique, and I have been extremely starved for techniques."

"I am sorry," said Harlow, "and I would ask you to be careful with it, because I am extremely good at being sorry and I have done it four hundred times and I have never once been sorry in a way that lasted me past a Tuesday."

"Yes," said Lyra. "That is what I am afraid of."

She got to the door.

"Lyra." He had not moved from the panel. "The other thing has been running since the eighteenth of May, under the East Tower, and in four months it has taken four people. One of them is a third-year seer who came back to this campus in September and has been in a corridor outside my ward twice without knowing I was on the other side of the door."

"Elena."

"Elena."

"And the other three."

"Two of them are on the board in Ward C in the second colour. The third is not on any board and has not been for four months, and his name is Tobi Fadipe, and he was nineteen, and he is in Sub-level 3 in a care programme in Bellhaven, and I have known since the eighteenth of May, and I have done nothing, and I would like it recorded that I have now said the name out loud to a sixteen-year-old who cannot see, in a room, in the middle of the week, instead of saying it in a deposition in a fortnight, because I would like one thing I have done to be *unmanaged*."

The warder opened the door.

---

*Down the stairwell, on the fourteenth step, Lyra sat down, because her legs had stopped being available.*

She had a name. She had had a name since the eighteenth of May and it had been in a building and it had cost nothing, and it had belonged to a nineteen-year-old boy in Bellhaven.

*That is what the man is for,* she thought. *Not the machine. The man. He is the reason the council cannot move. He is a lock in a corridor and he has been a lock for four months and he has just opened himself and I do not know whether to be glad or frightened, and I am going to find out tomorrow at nine o'clock in my room, with three people, because that is what the note says, and because the note is the only thing in this entire building that has ever been on my side.*

---

# Chapter 27: Breach Night

*Friday 27 September, 02:17.*

> "It does not call. That is the part people get wrong, because they want it to call. It is already speaking, and it has been speaking the whole time you have been standing there deciding whether to listen, and what it says is not a name. It says *now*, and it says it in the voice of the thing itself, and it has never once said a word in any language anybody here has."
>
> — Adeyemi, in the Hall of Inherited Practice, on being asked what the Weave does when it is afraid

*Weave-Quake Counter: 51% instability. Node-failure projection: T+34 d. Rebaselined against a held circuit. The load is off the wall and onto a machine, and the model has never been run on this basis before.*

The fragile peace at Threadweaver Academy after the Thread Maze collapse shattered at 2:17 in the morning, and the first thing Lyra noticed was that the corridor floor had gone from *wrong* to *loud*, and that the loud was in her teeth.

She did not need to see any of it. The alarms were the wrong shape for a building — not the chimes, the shriek — and the stone was doing the thing it did when the archway came down, and somewhere east and below something was being turned on that had been off for a long time.

Her bare feet hit cold floor. She went back for Adeyemi's staff, which she had returned that week and had been thinking about every day since. The cowrie shells were warm. She got as far as the stairwell before she understood that she had not taken it because she could use it; she had taken it because it was the only object in that building made by somebody who had not been in the room. In the hallway she collided with Zara, who was already crying and already apologising for crying.

"Syndicate," Eli said, from his door, with a tablet in his hand and the screen doing something he was reading faster than it updated. "They know the building. That is not a breach, that is somebody working from a plan."

Milo came out last, on the crutch he had been told to stop using, moving at a speed the corridor deserved better of.

"I've got nothing," he said, before anybody had asked him anything. "I want that said first, so nobody wastes four seconds. I can't hear any of it. Not the wards, not where it is, not how many. Two weeks ago I would have given you the floor plan and the turnstile count." He stopped, and did not say the rest of it.

Zara had her hand flat on the wall. "East Tower. Big. It's not a person, it's the whole east range. And there's a second thing under the archives, and it's *older* than the first one." She was shaking. "I can feel both. I can't tell which is worse and I'm not going to pretend I can."

Eli, at a screen, said: "Then we take the older one."

"They have a list," Lyra said. "They have had a list for a long time, and everything on it is a room."

The four of them moved through corridors full of students and staff who had been told to stand still and were standing still in the way people do when they have been told to stand still. The operatives came through it in threes and in fives, and they came through it *well*, and the thing Lyra could not get past — the thing that sat under everything else all night — was that they were not frightened. Nobody in that building was frightened of them.

A third-year went down on the East Wing stair with her wards up and the wards came apart, and a man with a wand went past her without slowing, and the nine seconds that bought the fourteen first-years behind her were the whole of what she did that anyone has since written down. Deeper in, somebody from Theoretical held a junction for the length of a corridor and was carried out of it by three fourth-years who are extremely annoyed about it.

The pressure against Lyra's skin was wrong in a way she could not put a word to, and the wrongness was not new; it was the taste of the thing in the housing under the floor, the taste of a person who could do this and had been doing it for years. Her new perception flared with it — heat, behind the eyes, the ringing white static — and she made herself keep walking, because stopping was the thing she was not going to do tonight.

"This way," she said, and took them into a service passage she had found in the days since her sight went, when she had nothing to do but walk the building and learn its shape, and which she had learned entirely by hand.

When forced to engage, Zara's empathic projections put doubts in isolated operatives about whose side they were on, which was worth more and cost her more than she could afford. Eli's barriers put up four seconds of cover each, built from counter-measures he had written at fifteen with a broken arm and no gift. Milo carried the second case, and put a boy of eleven through a doorway, and got them both to the far side, and did it four times; and it was the only thing he was good for all night, and he did not once mention that it was the only thing he was good for.

Under the Archives there was a room the Academy had stopped using, holding a second conduit that had been dormant so long that the interns called it the cold one, and there were four operatives on the door and none of the four were Academy, and inside, two technicians were working on something black.

"Listen," Lyra said, and put her palm flat on the wall, and the stone was screaming, which was not a thing stone did, and which she had felt once before in a corridor on the fourteenth and had not slept properly since. "They have not come for anything in this building. They have come for the cold one and they are turning it on."

"It's not a machine," Eli said. "Look at the cabling. It isn't drawing power, it's *being fed*. Somebody is holding it open from outside and the room is the mouth."

There were four of the academy's own on the floor by the door and none of them had been armed, and in the middle of the room a thing the size of a car was filling up with a light that was the wrong colour and getting wronger, and thick cables ran out of it and into the older stone of the conduit, and it was making a noise Lyra could feel in her back teeth and could not, at that moment, describe to the three people she needed to describe it to.

The light in the room was doing something light does not do, and the wrongness of it was not a colour at all, which was the part Lyra could not explain to anybody afterwards: she could feel that it was *wrong* the way you can tell that a room has somebody in it who is angry, and there was no name for the thing and no shape to point at.

"Open it," said a woman in a black visor, to somebody, in the flat register of somebody reading a checklist. "All of it. I want the signature before the hour."

And Lyra understood, and the understanding arrived in her chest before it arrived anywhere else, as a heat — the silver thing flaring hard enough that she put her hand against the wall to stay upright.

"They are not here for the building," she said. "They have not come for the conduit. They have come for what the conduit is *keyed* to. They have come for the Animus Argenti and they think it is in this room."

The woman's head came round. The visor had a crack in it now, and behind the crack there was a face that was not enjoying itself, and she brought a weapon up that was humming with the same light as the thing on the floor.

"The Silver Soul," she said. "We will have it."

Before she could fire, the ceiling came down over the machine — not all of it, a section, the way a ceiling behaves when something underneath it has been running too hard for too long — and the Registrar of Threadweaver Academy came down through the hole with Elder Adeyemi and four others, and Adeyemi's staff was already making the noise it makes when he means it.

What happened next took about ninety seconds and Lyra has described it four times, in three versions, and none of the four agrees about the order. Adeyemi's staff came down and the shells on it lit and the woman in the visor backed off him, which nobody had expected and which bought the room. Tanaka took the door and did not come past it. Two of the four who came through the ceiling were students, fourth-years, and one of them was crying and fighting at the same time, and the other one was not crying at all and neither has said since why.

"Milo," said Lyra. "The cables. Whatever happens to that thing, do not touch the cables."

"I cannot hear the cables," said Milo. "I am telling you that I cannot hear the cables, and you are going to do it anyway, and I would like it recorded that I said the useful part first."

She went up onto the housing with the regulator in front of her and put both hands on it, and the silver thread went out of her and into the stone — visible, the whole length of it, a cord that anybody in that room with eyes could see and that the four of them had never once seen her do — and what she did with it was not a push.

She has been asked about this the most, of anything, and she has never once given a good answer, and the closest she gets is that the old conduit was a thing that had been *fed* for fifty years by people who were not asking it anything, and that it had a shape, and that she went looking for the shape the way you go looking for a door in a wall you have been touching in the dark.

The machine on the floor made a noise that was not loud and then was very loud. The regulator came off its mount. Something let go in the ceiling, in the floor and in the four people's left ears simultaneously, and the light in the room went out.

"Containment," said the woman in the visor, to nobody, once.

Then the room was a room again.

They went. Not far and not fast — the two technicians went first, and then the woman, and then four men who had come in with a plan and no longer had one. Somebody was dragged. The room was very cold and very quiet and the whole building was screaming its one long note, and the wall-board in the corridor outside had a number on it that was a great deal worse than the one it had carried at two o'clock that morning.

Dr Mei-Hua Chen got to Lyra first. She had a cut over one eye from the ceiling and she did not appear to know it, and she said, quietly, in the voice of somebody reading a label on a jar:

"They were after you. Or what you carry."

Before Lyra could respond, a second alert came up, and Lyra read the header twice and then a third time.

**SECURITY. SUB-LEVEL 2, WARD 41. VISITOR COUNT DISCREPANCY.**

*Twelve days ago,* she thought, *Harlow was moved to Council custody off-site on a warrant countersigned by a woman who is not standing in this corridor. He has not been on this campus since the twenty-fourth. There cannot be a visitor discrepancy in a room that is empty.*

And then, with the awful slowness of somebody fitting together two pieces she did not want to fit:

*Unless the discrepancy is not in Ward 41.*

*Unless somebody has had a count of one in Ward 41 for a fortnight, and has been using the ward to book a lift.*

It was on Council letterhead, and the *off-site* was the only part of it that was not true, and she had known that since the twenty-sixth, in a room with a panel in the window that showed a valley that was not there.

"Not him," Lyra said, and felt the shape of it go cold. "The *route*. He cancelled the draw on his own authority and he published the route. There is a diagram of every door between here and Sub-level 7 in that good room, because he is a man who draws diagrams, and it is signed, and it was read by four Council observers, and I was one of them."

Eli said, very quietly: "And Blackwood has been inside the Council's correspondence chain since May."

Nobody moved for a moment. Somewhere above them a ceiling section settled, and the sound of it going was the most ordinary sound in the world.

"Right," said Milo, and stopped being frightened, which was worse than the fear and which all three of them had seen him do exactly once before. "So we've got until tomorrow night and we know the stairs. Everybody eat something. That's an order from the person who can't hear half of you, so it's the only order in this building that actually counts."

*Weave-Quake Counter: 54% instability. Node-failure projection: T+34 d. Rebaselined against a held circuit. The load is off the wall and onto a machine, and the model has never been run on this basis before.*

# Chapter 28: The Summons & The Descent

*Saturday 28 September, 23:58.*

> "The cruelest betrayals are not those that come from enemies, but from those we trusted to protect us. In that moment of revelation, we discover not just who they truly are, but who we must become to survive their love."
>
> — Lyra Chen, personal journal (written afterwards)

*Weave-Quake Counter: 54% instability. Node-failure projection: T+34 d. Rebaselined against a held circuit. The load is off the wall and onto a machine, and the model has never been run on this basis before.*

## THE SUMMONS

The message came near midnight, delivered by a thread-construct that dissolved the moment Lyra read it: *Come alone. Sub-level 7. Your father's life depends on it. - H*

Lyra stared at the empty air where the message had been, her heart hammering against her ribs. Her father was fading despite the Academy's best medical care. The doctors spoke in hushed tones about "accelerated deterioration" and "unprecedented neural damage," but Lyra knew the truth. Every moment she spent at the Academy, every thread she touched in her studies, was killing him faster.

She wrote to three people at once, at 11:41, and her hands were steady, and she noticed that they were steady, and she has never afterwards been able to decide whether that was courage.

*I am going. Come alone says come alone. I am not going alone. That is not bravery. That is me keeping a piece of procedure that three people wrote into a piece of paper in an infirmary in September. And if I break it tonight I will have broken it for no reason at all, and I have had enough of doing things for no reason at all.*

The three replies came back inside four minutes, which she had not expected and thought about for a long time afterwards.

The Academy's corridors were different at night — shadows deeper, the thread-light dimmer, the air thick with sleeping dreams and unspoken fears. Lyra made her way to the restricted levels. There was nothing to see. She had spent five weeks finding out what that absence was like and she had stopped expecting anything else of a corridor, and the corridor gave her nothing, which was correct, and which she had to keep reminding herself was not a wound.

But she was not alone, and she knew it in the ordinary way, which is to say that she turned round on the third landing because somebody had not shut a door behind her, and that door was the kind of thing Eli did.

"You didn't really think we'd let you face this alone, did you?" Milo's voice came from the shadows, gentle but determined.

"The message said—" Lyra began.

"The message was from someone who's been lying to us for months," Zara interrupted. She had both hands on the stair rail and had had them there since the second landing. "I do not want to go down there. I want that said out loud, before we go, because I don't know what is down there and I would rather you had the fact than the impression."

Eli emerged from behind a support pillar, his scanner already beeping with concerning readings. "Besides — the readings down here are off the charts and I am the only person in this group who can read them. If you go without me, you walk into a room four people have described to you in a corridor, and you will not know which parts of their description are the parts."

They spent eleven minutes in the last landing, which was too long, and which was the most useful eleven minutes of the entire month.

Milo wanted to stay at the top of the stair with the door. Zara wanted to go in and stay in the room. Eli wanted a list.

"He is not going to the top of the stair," Lyra said. "He is not going to be posted anywhere alone, and he is not going to be a contingency, and if I am saying that it is because I did that in September, and it cost a boy his gift, and I am not doing it twice."

"Then say what I *am*," Milo said.

"You are our floor. You are the only one of us who can count a room by sound, which you have been refusing to admit because it is not a thread, and it is exactly what we need it for, and it is worth more than what you lost." He did not look pleased. He looked like a man handed a coat that did not fit and going to wear it anyway. "And if it goes wrong, you go up, and you send for Weaver, and you do not wait for us, and you do not come back down."

"That's the deal," Eli said, writing it on his own hand. "And we go in together and we come out together. And if we can't come out together, whoever has the least to lose goes first, and we all know who that is, and we are not arguing about it in the corridor."

"Thirty-nine minutes," said Zara. "That's how long before the visitor count in Ward 41 turns into a containment order, and then the stair seals and there is no door at all."

"Then we have gone."

## THE DESCENT

The East Tower's sub-basement stretched deeper than any Academy blueprint suggested, carved from living rock and reinforced with thread-infused steel. The emergency lighting was on its second battery. They went down the hidden staircase behind Harlow's laboratory, and the stairwell was colder than any stairwell has a right to be, and Eli said the temperature out loud twice, because he said things out loud twice.

The air was thick with ozone and with something else. Lyra put her palm flat on the stairwell wall going down, because the wall was the only thing in that building she could still read, and the wall said: *two hundred and eleven separate strains, and eleven of them are being held rather than carried.* She kept her hand there for the whole descent, and did not tell them what it said, and Eli noticed, and did not ask, and she was grateful for that in a way she suspects was not entirely to her credit.

"The readings are off the charts," Eli whispered, his scanner beeping frantically. "Whatever's down here, it's generating massive dimensional distortions."

Zara had stopped three steps short of the chamber floor and was standing with her arms crossed, which is what she did instead of holding on to people. "There is something down here," she said. "I cannot tell you what it is. I can tell you that it is old, and that it is not one thing, and that I would rather not go further into this room than I currently want to, and you should take that as data rather than as drama."

Milo had nothing to offer and said so, which was a thing the four of them had agreed about a week ago and had not expected to have to say in a corridor. "I can't do the reading any more," he said. "I get pressure and direction and the room being wrong. That's all I've got and it's not enough and I'd rather say that now than be useful for about a minute and then go down."

They emerged into a vast chamber that defied the Academy's ancient architecture. Crystalline structures rose from the floor like frozen lightning, connected by cables thick as tree trunks. At the centre there was a chair. It was a chair in the way that a cuff is a cuff: a shape with one purpose, made by somebody who had a specification and no feelings about it.

"This is it," Eli breathed, consulting the blueprints they'd stolen from the archives. "The Magnus Conduit."

## UNDER THE BASE PLATE

The machine was in front of her. That was the first thing.

The second was that she could not see it at all, and had to do this the slow way, with her hands, on the housing, at close range, over about four minutes, with a reading that came in layers and had to be taken one at a time or not at all.

The third was Eli, on the far side, at a screen, in a voice that had gone completely flat:

"Lyra. It's not empty."

"I know."

"It's not empty. There's a subsystem under the base plate that is on neither the 1941 schematic nor the 2009 one, and it is drawing, and it is drawing from *inside itself*, and I think—" he stopped, and started again. "I think it has been drawing from inside itself since 1941. I think that is what it is *for*."

Zara was already on the floor with both hands flat on the plate.

She had her eyes open. Her mouth was open. Nothing was coming out of either.

"Zara."

"I can't get all of them," she said. "I can't. There are too many, they're too deep, and they're not — they're not *in* it, that's the thing, they're *under* it, they're older than it—"

The chamber was very cold. Under the base plate there was a lattice of black-silver, and threaded all the way through the lattice a light that was not a colour Lyra knew, and in the light there were shapes.

Lyra knelt and put her hand on it, because there was nothing else to do with her hand, and the reading came up in one piece, all at once, which it had never done before:

*Nobody had ever asked.*

"What do you mean?" Milo asked, and got nothing from the machine, and got instead the one thing he still got, which was that the room was full of people who had not moved in a long time, and that all of them were facing the same way. He put a hand on the wall to keep the floor where it was.

"He's been harvesting from them," Lyra said, her voice breaking. "Every student who's been affected, every thread signature that's been damaged—he's been siphoning their consciousness to power this thing."

Zara had both hands flat on the plate and she was not coming off it, and Lyra went and got hold of her by the shoulder.

"Zara. Look at me."

"They're still in there," Zara said. "That's the part I keep failing to say out loud. I expected a room. It isn't a room. There are thirty-nine of them and they have been in the same position since before I was born and they are not asleep."

"Zara—"

"And they are not the same age," she said, "and I want that written down somewhere, because everybody is going to say *people* and they are not the same. There is one of them who is much younger than the rest, and I can feel that much, because it is the loudest thing in the room and it is the newest."

The machine pulsed, responding to their presence. Displays flickered to life, showing readouts in languages they didn't recognize. But the central screen was clear enough: "ANIMUS ARGENTI DETECTED. INITIATING CONVERGENCE PROTOCOL."

"We have to destroy it," Eli said, his analytical mind struggling to process the horror before them. "We have to free them."

"I'm afraid I can't allow that."

## THE CONFRONTATION

"I am going to be in the room," said a voice from the stair. "Mrs Okonjo is four steps behind me and she is holding a card that says I am permitted to be here, and she did not enjoy reading it out, and I would like it noted that she did not enjoy it."

He came down the last nine steps by himself. He was in the clothes he had worn on Wednesday. There was a receipt for a sandwich in his breast pocket, which he took out and set on the console, because his hands did not seem to know what else to do with them. Lyra thought: *he has been thirteen days down here and somebody has been feeding him, and he has not mentioned it once, and he is not going to.*

He had lost the composure the way a man loses a coat. There was grey at the edge of his jaw and a plaster across the back of his left hand.

"Professor Harlow," Lyra said, her voice steadier than she felt. "We know what you've been doing. We know about Kyoto, about your family, about the students you've been harvesting from."

"Do you," he said. "Not the arithmetic. The part where I decided."

He put his hand flat on the housing, the way he had put it flat on a desk in Kyoto, and did not look at it.

"I am not asking you for the machine," Harlow said. "I have never once asked anybody for the machine. I have asked for a Council vote. A vote is a thing where the answer can be no. If the answer is no on Thursday then the wall goes on carrying it, and the wall is good for six years, and I will have a different conversation with the Council in the spring and I will lose that one as well."

## WHAT HARLOW SAYS FOR HIMSELF

"They're in there," Milo said. "That's the whole of what I've got. They're in there, and they didn't agree to it, and you know they didn't agree to it, because you've got a number for how long each of them has been in. Which means you've been counting."

"I have been counting for four days," Harlow said. "I am not going to describe what I am trying to do by borrowing a word off a child who has not finished school. I have tried for four days and there isn't one, and I am not going to use a bad one tonight because it is the only one to hand."

"They're prisoners," Zara said, her empathic abilities still reeling from the contact with the trapped minds. "They're suffering, Professor. They want to be free."

"They want to be safe," Harlow snapped. "They want to exist in a reality that isn't collapsing around them. And this is the only way to ensure that."

The Conduit's hum changed pitch. Eli looked up at the number, and then at the number's line on the screen, and Lyra watched him decide not to say the second thing he was thinking, which was that the hum and the number were not the same measurement and he did not yet know which of them was lying.

But even as fear gripped her heart, Lyra felt something else stirring — a deep, unshakeable certainty that there had to be another way. The trapped souls weren't just fuel for Harlow's machine; they were people, with their own hopes and dreams and the right to choose their own fate.

*There has to be another way,* she thought. She was aware of her hand going to the jade at her throat and she left it there, because it was warm, and because it was four hundred years old, and because she was aware that a stone is not an argument and was using it as one.

She put her hand back on the plate.

It took her two minutes and eleven seconds, and it was the first time since the fifteenth of September that she had been the most competent person in a room, and she wants that on the record too.

What she found under her fingers was the shape of what the housing was actually doing. It was not a battery. It was a *hand* — a hand that could hold a weight indefinitely, in a fixed shape, without fatigue, and would not put it down, and could not do anything else at the same time. The translator above it was an entirely different machine. The translator could *carry*: it could take the load off the wall and move it and distribute it and be, in every sense, clever. But carrying was a different verb from holding, and the moment the load stopped coming out of the wall and went onto a circuit that had to hold it in place, the translator's cleverness was worth exactly nothing.

*It is a room with a bucket in it,* she thought, *and somebody has to stand in the bucket.*

"Hey." Eli had his hand on her shoulder. "You've gone somewhere. Talk."

"It can carry," Lyra said. "It can't hold. There's a difference and it is the whole difference, and nobody has ever written it down, because nobody has ever had to." She took her hand off the plate. "In four days they are going to vote on whether to switch it on permanently. And if they switch it on, it will be fine for about six months while the wall is still carrying most of it. And then one morning the wall will be carrying nothing and the machine will be carrying everything, and at that point it will do precisely what it is good at, which is move a load around. It will not do the other thing. It cannot. There is nothing in the design that lets it."

Eli wrote it down. He wrote it twice, and then a third time, and then he read it back to himself, and Lyra watched him understand that he had just been handed the most important sentence anybody had said in that building in twenty-four days, and that it had been said by a girl who could not see.

"Then somebody has to hold it," he said.

"Yes."

"And the housing—" He looked at the lattice, and at the cold light, and did not finish the sentence, because there was exactly one word available and none of them wanted to say it.

"The housing holds it," Lyra said. "That is what the lattice is. It is a hold. It is a hold that works by never letting go of a person who never agreed to be held." She wiped her hand on her coat, which achieved nothing. "And I have just been told, out loud, in a language nobody else in this room can hear, that the answer to that is *no*."

*The pendant pulsed once against her sternum, and for a moment — not a voice; she was very careful about that, not a voice, a *texture*, the way a hand feels through a wall — there was something there that was not a memory of her mother and not the Weave, and it was the sensation of a room where somebody had been waiting a very long time.*

*Right,* she thought, in the flat grammar. *So that is the vote. That is what Thursday is actually about.*

## THE MOMENT OF CHOICE

The Conduit was drawing. Eli gave the number out loud twice, because he gave numbers out loud twice, and the second one was higher.

"You can read it," Harlow said, looking at Lyra's hand on the plate. "That is what I have been watching for since the eleventh of September, and it is the only reason I have not spent four days finding out what happened to the people in it."

"You knew they were in there."

"I knew there was something in there that I could not read, for eleven years, and I have known for thirteen days that it was people." He said it without any self-pity, and Lyra found that she respected him a great deal more for that than for anything else he had done. "Do you want the arithmetic, or do you want to know what I did in May? They are the only two questions left, and I have answered both, and only one of them is on the record."

"I know what you did in May," said Lyra.

"Yes," said Harlow. "I thought you might." And then, unexpectedly, he said: "I have not slept. I am not going to tell you to trust me, and I am not going to tell you the machine is the only option, and I am not going to explain myself to you for another four minutes. Here is the one thing, and then I am going to stop talking, and you can put whatever you like on it."

He turned a console so that Eli could read it.

"Four days," he said. "And in four days a Council is going to vote on whether to bring a machine that carries into permanent service, using a housing that holds, and forty-three people are in the housing, and two of them are ours. One of them is fifteen years old and has been in the machine since the eleventh of September, and her mother is in Bellhaven and thinks she is at a specialist programme. The other one has been there since the eighteenth of May, and he is nineteen, and he is the reason this Council is going to spend nine hours discovering that it has no vocabulary for a person, and I would like somebody to write his name on the board before they do that, because he is in a box and he is nineteen and there is no version of nineteen that can be *noted*." He looked up. "I would like to be in the room when it is opened. I am not asking to be in the room when anything else happens. And if you say no to that, I will understand, and I will not appeal it, and you should know that I am saying that because it is the last thing I have that is not a technique, and I would like somebody to check it."

Forty-three, and not thirty-nine, and Eli Park had had the two numbers in his head for two days and had not been able to make them agree, and he made them agree on the second day, from four lines in a finding he had been shown and had not asked to see: *the other one was under the East Tower, and it had been running continuously since the eighteenth of May, and it had a second housing, and it had been taking four people at about one a month.* Thirty-nine were in the lattice, which had lain underneath Sub-level 7 since 1941, and thirty-seven of them had been dead since the war ended, and the other two were the girl and the boy. The other four were in the second housing, and the second housing was under the East Tower, and all four of them were alive, and the Council's figure of forty-three had been arrived at by adding a lattice to a housing and calling the sum a housing, and nobody had asked for the four separately, because a person taken one at a time, about a month apart, in a machine that is about to be voted into permanent service, is not a list.

"Why do you want to be in the room?"

"Because it is my great-grandfather's crime and my hand is on the seal and somebody should be watching me," said Harlow, "and because I have spent sixteen years being certain that I was the only person in this building who had done anything, and I would like to be shown the list."

"This is her nightmare," Lyra said. "And I won't let you use her memory to justify it."

"She was seven." Harlow had put the legal pad down. "You were not. That is the whole of what I have to say to you and I have been saying it badly for an hour."

"Then say it properly or don't say it."

"I am saying it properly now." He did not move from the console. "Your father drove himself to this building on Monday against a doctor's written advice, on a stick, with a woman following him in a hired car, and he has been sitting in the front of the Lumina Lecture Hall since seven this morning being extremely useful with a napkin and a pencil, and he is going to be there this afternoon as well, and he has not once been asked whether he wants to be. That is what I have to say. That is the thing you can say to a Council tomorrow that nobody in this room can say, because nobody in this room has a father."

He put his hand flat on the console, the way he had put it flat on a desk in Kyoto, and did not look at it.

"Or I can say the other thing, which is that he drove himself here because he would not let you go in alone, and that a man who will not let his daughter go in alone is a man who ought to be asked."

Down the corridor, a door opened that nobody opened, and stayed open, and Lyra counted to four before anybody moved.

*Weave-Quake Counter: 58% instability. Node-failure projection: T+34 d. Rebaselined against a held circuit. The load is off the wall and onto a machine, and the model has never been run on this basis before.*

# Chapter 29: The Price of Life

> "The cruelest choices are not the ones where you must choose. They are the ones where somebody has arranged the world so that the choice looks like the only thing left, and has then gone out of the room."
>
> — Eleanor Weaver, on resigning, which she did not

*Weave-Quake Counter: 58% instability. Node-failure projection: T+34 d. Rebaselined against a held circuit. The load is off the wall and onto a machine, and the model has never been run on this basis before.*

*Tuesday 1 October, 00:40. Sub-level 7.*

## THE ARRIVAL

She had been two hours in the lift.

That was the part nobody had thought about, afterwards — not the machine, not the lattice, not the man. Two hours in a service lift, going down eleven floors at the speed of a bottle of wine, with her father beside her and Eli Park holding a case in both hands and Zara Washington standing in the corner with her arms folded and her jaw set and Milo Reyes, who had walked out of the infirmary on a stick at ten o'clock at night against medical advice that had been waived in writing, in the specific way that things in this building were waived in writing.

She had not gone because she had been summoned.

She had gone because at six o'clock that evening, in a doorway outside Ward C, a man in a dark uniform had said *come alone, and come now, and you have eleven hours*, and had then, in a second voice, entirely different, said *and bring the registrar, because there is going to be a form, and the form is the only thing down here that has ever worked.*

Blackwood had sent Mirembe Blackwood, at nineteen minutes past six, and had then sent a second message, at nineteen minutes past six, in which she had written four words: *we are past surnames.*

---

The chamber was not what Lyra had built in her head, and she was faintly ashamed of the head.

It was very large and it was very ordinary. Concrete, a cable tray, a row of lockers, a kettle somebody had plugged in. The Magnus Conduit — the thing she had put her gift through, the thing that had rewritten itself in front of two hundred witnesses sixteen days ago — occupied the middle of it the way a boiler occupies a plant room. It was not beautiful any more. It had been beautiful for about ninety seconds on the Sunday, and then it had gone back to being a piece of equipment, and had stayed that way, humming, with a light on it.

And on the other side of the floor, in a space that had been cleared and had not been cleared properly, there were ten people on the floor on mats, and nine of them were awake, and one of them was not.

Lyra stopped.

"Sit down, please," said Marcus Harlow, from the console. "All of you. It is going to take a long time and I would like to spend it with people on chairs."

Nobody sat down.

"They're the Council's," said Eli, in a voice with no air in it at all. He had the case against his chest. "Those are the *Council's* people. They are wearing Council grey and they are lying on the floor of a machine room and none of them is *shut* —" He stopped. "Why aren't they shut?"

"Because I am not the one who brought them," said Harlow, "and because it is half past twelve in the morning and in about four hours a man is going to stand up in a room and tell this Academy that a translator is carrying a valley on a circuit with no power source, and I wanted you to see them first, and I wanted to see whether you would notice."

## THE ARRANGEMENT

He had laid it out on a bench, in a row, with a clipboard, and he did it the way he did everything, and Lyra found she could not decide whether that was a comfort or the most frightening thing about him.

"Item one. Your father."

She had known it was coming. She had been ready for it for four days. It did not help.

"Sixty-eight per cent coverage at the wrist on the twenty-eighth, and it is a straight line and it is not a curve, and I have eleven weeks in it, and I have told you that and you have written it down." He looked at her, and then at her father, who was standing very still with a cane he had not been given permission to bring. "Professor Chen. You are the only person in this room who has read the transfer function on the east face of that machine. Say the number."

"Sixty-eight," said Wei Chen. "On the twenty-eighth, at about four in the afternoon, from a photograph Osei took with a telephone because we do not own an instrument. Sixty-eight per cent is a coverage figure. It is not a severity. It is the area of skin, and skin is not the question."

"What is the question."

"The question is the gradient." Wei Chen did not move. "The burn is following the lines of the conduit model, Marcus, and you know that, and you have known it for four years, and you have never once put it in a sentence, because the sentence is: *it is following a shape that exists in the architecture of the machine you built*, and if you said that out loud then somebody in this building would ask you why a man's circulatory system has adopted the geometry of a *device*, and there is no answer to that question that is not either *coincidence* or *the machine is doing it*, and you have been living in the gap between those two answers for eleven years and so have I."

Nobody said anything.

"Item two," said Harlow, and his voice did not change at all, which was how Lyra knew. "Your father has a cardiomyopathy that has been quietly getting worse for probably two years and which is at thirty-one per cent of the muscle, and a bleed at nineteen per cent of a load of four, and a heart that is now, in the plainest terms available to me, a clock. Three to nine months in September. Less in October, because he has been doing heroic things, and I want to be extremely precise: I said that to his daughter at four in the morning in a corridor on the twenty-ninth and I do not know how she took it and I have not asked, because asking would have been a way of being forgiven and I am not here for that."

Lyra felt her father's hand arrive on her shoulder, very light, and then leave, because he had understood within about a second what she needed, which was not to be touched.

"Item three," said Harlow. "You can save his life. Not extend it. *Move it.* Not onto this machine. Onto a person. If the silver channel that your daughter is currently standing in is separated from his — cleanly, once, permanently — the bleed stops, and the bleed has been one of the two things shortening his life, and it will stop being one of the two things." He set the clipboard down. "I have run that four thousand times. It is arithmetic. It is the only arithmetic in this room that everybody agrees on."

"And what it costs," said Lyra.

"Her sight," said Harlow. "All of it. I have three prior cases and two of them are alive and neither of them is recognisable to themselves, and I have told her that, in a bookshop, in front of her father, in August, and I have said it again this week in a corridor, and I am now saying it for the third time in front of a witness, and I would like the witness to note that the number is three and the number is the same."

"And in exchange," said Wei Chen.

"Nothing." Harlow's head came up. "That is the difference between the offer I made in the rain and this one. I have learned something in four days and I would like to say what it is, because you have earned it and I have not earned very much this month."

He turned round to the whole of it — the four of them, the ten people on the mats, the kettle, the lockers.

"I spent eleven years being the man who asks a question and then waits, because the waiting is the ethics. I thought that if I never *named* the thing, the thing would not have happened." He put his hand flat on the console, and the silver in his forearm moved, and he did not look at it. "On the fifteenth of September I named it. In a demonstration hall. In front of two hundred people, one of whom is standing in this room. And it happened anyway, and a boy is in a hospital bed in this building who will be a completely ordinary musician for the rest of his life, and the *naming* did not prevent it, and there was no arrangement in which it was prevented, and I have spent four days working out what that means and the answer is very short and I do not like it."

"What does it mean," said Eli Park.

"It means I was not asking. I was *offering*." Harlow looked at him. "I have made eleven offers in eleven years. I have never once been asked for anything. There is a difference between a question and a request, and I have been confusing them since 1997, and it has cost a boy his perception and a girl her childhood and a woman her life, and the last of those is not mine to pay."

## THE PROJECTION

"Then let us talk about the other thing," said Lyra. "The machine."

"The machine is starving," said Harlow. "It has been starving since the moment your silver left the upper matrix and went down. A translator with no power source is a person holding a door. It is not a machine any more. It is a *person*, and the person is fifteen, and she has been holding it for four days, and I want you to understand that she has not been told this, because nobody has been able to work out how to tell her without frightening her, and that is a failure of this building and not of her."

"Then tell me now," said Lyra.

"Tell *her* now," said Harlow, "yes, and I am not the person who does it, and that is not a rhetorical flourish, it is a practical problem, because a subject who has consented to a procedure and is then told that the procedure is the only thing standing between a valley and a rupture will consent to a *great deal*, and four wardens and a compliance officer are not a substitute for the person who asked the question."

Zara Washington said, "Then I will ask her."

Nobody had expected her to speak.

"You have all been talking about her," Zara said, "in the third person, for nine minutes, in a room she is standing in."

And Lyra discovered that this was true, that she had been an object in an argument about herself for the whole of it, and that the discovery was not humiliating; it was the most useful thing that had happened to her in four days.

"Yes," she said. "All right. Ask me."

But it was not then, because there was one more thing on the bench.

## THE RECORDING

"There is a recording," said Harlow. "It is on the console. It is a resonance capture from the fifteenth of April, 1997, made by a man called Weber, who was not on my team and who I have never been able to explain. It is the only surviving record of the garden. I have watched it — " he stopped, and recalibrated, and Lyra watched a man of fifty-four choose a word for the fourth time in nine minutes "— a number of times. I would like you to watch it, and then I would like you to notice the date on the frame, and then I would like you to say the date out loud, because I have needed somebody else to say it for six years."

"Why now?"

"Because on Thursday morning I am going to stand in a room and be asked whether I am a man who fabricates, and I have never been able to answer that question, and I have realised in the last four days that I have been carrying a piece of evidence in a drawer that would have answered it, and that I have kept it in a drawer for six years, and that the reason is that the evidence is also a *reason*, and reasons are what you keep when the evidence has run out." He squared the notes on the console, in a gesture that was so exactly like her father's that her chest hurt. "Watch it. Then say the date. Then we will do the rest of the evening, and it will be a long evening, and I am going to ask all of you the same question at the end of it, and I want you to answer it separately, and I do not want to hear four answers in unison."

---

It was ninety seconds long, and it was not what she had expected.

No collapse. No light. A garden in the last week of April, in a low cold light, shot from about waist height by somebody who was not watching the sky.

A woman with a book she had not opened.

A girl of seven with cherry blossoms in her hair, walking *towards* something out of frame, fast, with both arms out in front of her, the way you walk into a doorway you have decided is a doorway.

And, very briefly, in the extreme left of the frame, a man turning round, and the sound of a man saying a single word.

And then the picture did not end. It carried on for another forty seconds, in the same garden, in the same light, with nothing happening in it, and in the last eleven of those forty seconds a woman of about forty came into frame, sat down on a bench, and stayed there.

*Weber's own footage*, said the caption. *The event does not appear. He is the only witness. He has never given a statement.*

*Frame capture: 15 April 1997, 11:41.*

"Eleven forty-one," said Lyra Chen.

"Yes."

"That's the eleventh of April. The frame is stamped the fifteenth."

"Yes."

"The eleventh of April, 1997, is five years before my mother came to that building, and it is a Friday, and my mother told me once that the eleventh of April was the only day of the year her father let her skip school because of the blossom on the river, and she has never in her life told me a day of the year for any other reason."

"Yes," said Marcus Harlow.

"And in that recording," Lyra said, and her voice had gone somewhere she had never taken it, "my mother is not in it. And I am not in it. And Lin Chen's file in this building has a department number and a project code and four months in it and it is in the spring of 2002, and you have been sitting here for eleven years telling me that the last thing my mother did was go into a chamber under a mountain on the eleventh of September, five years ago, and I want to know whether you have ever once, in eleven years, written the *second* date down."

There were nine people on the floor of Sub-level 7 who had not heard that question before, and two wardens, and a compliance officer, and Marcus Harlow, and he answered it.

"No," he said. "I have never once written the second date down."

"Why not?"

"Because it does not help me," he said, "and because on the eleventh of April, 1997, in a garden in Kyoto, my daughter was alive, and on the eleventh of April, 2002, in a chamber under this mountain, a woman of thirty-two walked in on a Thursday because it was blossom week, and if I write the second date down next to the first one then I have to look at them, and I have not been able to look at them for eleven years, and I would like that in the record, and I would like it in the record in those words, because the record is the only place left where anybody can check a thing about me."

---

## THE QUESTION

He asked it at ten past three in the morning, and he asked it four times, and he wrote the answers down himself, on a legal pad, in a hand that had begun to shake about an hour before he started.

"Seventeen days from now, a node under a populated valley is going to fail, and eleven thousand people live over it, and the only instrument in the world that can hold a circuit open is the thing in this room, and the thing in this room has no power source, and there are four things that can be done about that, and I am going to say all four, and I have spent four days making sure I am not lying in any of them, and then I am going to leave this room and I am not going to be in it again."

He held up the pad.

"**One.** Return case forty-one to the circuit. Thirty-nine people in a lattice, thirty-seven of whom have been dead since 1941 and two of whom are not, and one of those two is fifteen years old. It is a power source. It is the only power source. It will hold the circuit indefinitely and it will hold it cheaply and it will make this building legal by Friday. It is also the thing I have spent eleven years refusing to do in a room where nobody could hear me refuse, and I have now been asked to do it by somebody with a clipboard, which is apparently the only thing that has ever worked on me."

Nobody breathed.

"**Two.** Shut the translator down. The circuit goes open, the load comes off the machine and back onto the wall, and the wall has thirty-one per cent of tolerance in it and it has seventeen days. It will not fail on the seventeenth day. It will fail somewhere in the first four hours, and it will fail without a forecast, because the model for a held circuit has never been run and everybody in this room knows it."

"**Three.** Find a person." He said it very flatly. "Not a volunteer. Not a relative. A person with a *resonance the Weave will hold*, standing in a housing, spending themselves, and a great deal of themselves, and not getting up afterwards. I have looked. There is no version of this that I can construct. I am telling you that as the man who has been trying to construct one for sixteen years, and I would like that minuted."

Lyra's father had not moved. His hand was on the back of a chair.

"**Four.**" Harlow turned the pad round. "Carry it. Split the load. It is the one that has never been on anybody's list because it requires the circuit to be *held* while it is being rebuilt, and holding it requires a person, and a person is three. So: this is not a fourth option. It is a statement about the fourth option, and the statement is that it does not exist, and it does not exist for a reason that has nothing to do with physics, and the reason is that every single one of you would rather be *finished* than be *responsible*, and I include myself, and I have been including myself since 1997, and I would like the four of you to write that down and take it with you."

He put the pen down.

"So. In the morning I am going to be asked whether I would put thirty-nine people back in a machine. I do not know what I am going to say. I have known for four days that I would not, and I have also known for four days that there is going to be a moment, at about half past nine in the morning, when a person with a gavel will describe what eleven thousand people would look like on a Tuesday, and in that moment the arithmetic will be *very* good, and it will be the only good argument in the room, and I will be the only man in the room who knows that." He looked at Lyra. "That is why I asked you to come. Not for the consent. For the witness. There is going to be a moment in the morning when a *number* is the only thing anybody has, and I have spent my life being a man who respects numbers, and I would like, for once, to be in a room with somebody who has read one in the same handwriting as I have."

---

She wrote it on the back of her hand in the dark, after the others had gone up, while her father sat on a chair with his cane across his knees and did not sleep either.

She wrote four things.

1. *He is not lying to me. He is going to be able to do it anyway, and he has told me so, in advance, in a room with witnesses, and that is not a defence and it is not a trick either. It is the first true thing anybody has given me since the fifteenth of September.*
2. *Ask about the bleed. The machine translates. It translated a cage into a conversation. Ask it one more question, about a feedback loop, and put a number on the answer.*
3. *Nine people on mats. Ask them whether they are the Council's or whether they are the lattice's. Both can be true and one of them is a person.*
4. *Four options and one sentence. "Every one of you would rather be finished than responsible." He is wrong about the four of us. He is not wrong about me.*

Then she put her hand over the writing, in case her father could see it in the dark, which he could not, and which she knew he could not, and which she did anyway.

---

*Thread Resonance Note: Sub-level 7, 01:10–03:30. Present: C, L. (no restraint). P, E.C. (no restraint). Reyes, M. (no restraint, discharged against advice in writing at 22:40). Washington, Z. (no restraint). Tarrant, M., compliance. Dr C., M-H., by line. OBSERVER Rowntree, M. (advocate). C, W. (patient, self-transported, declined escort). C-2214 §9 invoked by the subject in person at 00:52, witnessed. Four options minuted verbatim at 03:10 by M.H. and countersigned by Tarrant. Item 3 minuted as "does not exist". This office records that it wrote the words "does not exist" and that it believes them, and that it has written them in a book that a girl of nineteen can read, which is a thing it has never done.*
