# Chapter 30: What They Wanted

> "Thirty-nine people in a cage in a mountain decided, one at a time, and over four hours, what they wanted. Thirty-seven of them said out loud that they wanted to be let go, and thirty-seven of them are dead, and thirty-seven sets of next of kin are going to be told something next month, and every one of those letters is going to be wrong."
>
> — Lyra Chen, in the room at the back, on the twenty-ninth of October

*Weave-Quake Counter: 63% instability. Node-failure projection: T+34 d. Rebaselined against a held circuit. The load is off the wall and onto a machine, and the model has never been run on this basis before.*

*Tuesday 1 October, 03:30. Sub-level 7.*

## THE QUESTION

She asked it at half past three in the morning, sitting down on the floor with her back against a machine that was holding a valley, facing nine people on mats.

"I'm Lyra Chen. Some of you were sent here and some of you were brought, and I'm going to assume it doesn't matter, because from where I'm sitting it doesn't."

"Steward," said a woman of about sixty, on the second mat, in the voice of somebody who had been asked a question on an official form. "We're the Council's observers. There's nine of us and there's two doctors and a minute-taker. We've got twenty-one days."

"Observers of what?"

"Of the instrument," said the woman, and there was something in her face that Lyra had begun to recognise, which was the look of a competent person who has been given a job they were not told the terms of. "We've been down here since the fifteenth. We log intake, output and the twenty-one conditions. That's it. Nobody told us what we were observing."

"Then here's the question, and I'd like it answered out loud, one at a time, and I would like it written down." Lyra put her hands on her knees. "Do you want to be here?"

Nobody spoke for about four seconds.

"Yes," said the woman, "because if we go up, there is nobody in this room, and if there is nobody in this room then on Thursday there is a vote with no witnesses in it."

"That's not what I asked."

"No," she said. "It is what I answered, and I answered it because you are asking a room of people who are the only adults within four hundred metres, and I would like you to understand that I have noticed you are sixteen and that I have noticed that you have done the thing where the real question is underneath the question, and that I am not going to help you with it, because you did not come down here to find out what nine people want. You came down here to find out what *thirty-nine* want."

Lyra looked at her for a long moment.

"Yes," she said.

"Yes," said the woman, and something in the room changed temperature, and Lyra had the very clear sensation of a machine with a new instruction.

---

The lattice was not in the room.

That was the first thing Lyra had not understood and the first thing she understood, about four hours in, and the understanding came to her sideways, in a corridor, at half past four in the morning, because she went out for air and found that the air on Sub-level 7 did not have any in it.

The lattice was underneath Sub-level 7. It had been underneath the site since 1941. The housing had been grown shut, and the housing was what had come away from a service panel like dried coral in a room four days ago, and what was inside it had been put in a case and taken out of the building at twenty past ten that Sunday night in a sealed box with an inventory number on it and a girl's signature on the form.

Which meant that what was running the translator had no source, and had had no source for four days, and was nonetheless still running.

"It's you," Zara said. She was standing at the far end of the room with her back to a locker and her hands at her sides and had been like that for forty minutes. "Lyra. It's *you*. It has been you since Sunday night. I have been sitting four feet away from you for four days."

"I know."

"You *know*?"

"I worked it out on the sixteenth." Lyra said it to the floor. "Four days. I put it together on the Wednesday, in a common room, with Eli, and I have not said it out loud to anybody because the moment I say it out loud somebody puts it in a file, and I have had four files this month and I would like to have one conversation that is not in one."

"So say it now," said Milo, from the chair, "and say it to me, because you are not saying it to me, you are saying it to the wall, and the wall does not need it."

Lyra looked up.

"Sixty-eight per cent coverage," she said. "Straight line. Four weeks. It isn't his skin, it's the *shape* — it's following the geometry of the model. He said it out loud to my father at midnight in a room with nine witnesses and he did not say it to *me*, and it was not an accident, and I have been not-angry about it for four days, which I would like recorded as the most impressive thing I have ever done." She put her hand flat on the floor. "He has had sixty-eight per cent of his own blood in a shape that only exists inside a machine, for four months, because he has been standing next to a prototype core and working on it. It is not *thread-burn* in the sense that the textbooks mean. It is second-hand geometry. And it means that if you remove him from this building, it stops, and if you keep him in this building, he has four weeks, and if you put him in *that*—"

She turned and looked at the translator.

"—then it finishes tonight, and it finishes because he is the only thing in the world whose body has already agreed to the shape."

Nobody said anything for a while.

"That," said Marcus Harlow, from the console, in a voice with nothing in it, "is the fifth option."

Milo turned round. "What?"

"That is a fifth option and I did not list it, because I have spent eleven years not being allowed to think about him." Harlow had not moved. He had not moved in four hours, and he had a legal pad in front of him with four answers written on it in a hand that had stopped shaking at about two o'clock. "A person whose body has already adopted the geometry. Who does not have to be *compatible*. Who does not have to be found. Who is a statistician, and is fifty-three per cent dead, and has a *model* in his own hands, and who walked down here on a cane at ten o'clock at night because he was not going to be upstairs."

"That is a man," said Wei Chen.

"That is a man," said Harlow. "I have had him in my building for eleven days and I have not once, for one hour, allowed myself to consider it, and I would like the record to show the mechanism of the omission, because it is the most interesting thing about me and nobody is going to ask." He put the pen down. "It is this: he is the father. And I have spent sixteen years finding daughters."

---

## THE ARGUMENT

They argued for two hours and eleven minutes and it was not an argument between the five of them so much as an argument conducted by all four of them in four different directions at once, and Lyra — sitting on the floor of a machine room with her back against a boiler that was holding a valley — thought, at about half past five in the morning, *this is what it is supposed to be like, and I have never once had it before, and I have been calling it being rescued.*

Eli wanted the model. He had the case and he had a pen and by six o'clock he had a thing on a napkin that made four of the Council's observers stand up.

"Four options and none of them require a *person* except three, and two of those three are a cage and a death, and the third is your father, and you did not list him because of a psychological mechanism that you have now described, and that is fine, that is human, I am not interested in the human. Look at the *shape*." He had it flat on the floor between them. "A translator with no source is a person holding a door. The reason she is holding it is that the circuit was built to *expect* a source and has a shape where a source goes. A person's resonance is a source. A *machine's* resonance is not a source, because it was never designed to be one — but a machine's geometry is exactly what her body has spent four months learning."

"You are describing a prosthetic," said Wei Chen, from the chair.

"I am describing a *fittings problem*," said Eli, "and you know exactly what I mean, and I watched you work it out in about two seconds, and I would like you to say it, because I have a bad feeling and you have not had one and I have known you for two days."

Wei Chen was quiet for a moment. The kettle had boiled some time ago and had not been switched off.

"The fault," he said. "The thing she went down in. On Sunday." He looked at his daughter. "You went down a *crack* in the core. Not through the matrix. Through a fracture that followed your hand. And the fracture is still there — I can see it from here, it is a fault line in the lattice work, it is about a hand's width, and it has not closed, because she has been *standing in it* for four days, and holding a fault open is not the same as holding a door, and the difference is that a fault can be *lined*."

Silence.

"Lined," said Zara.

"With a body. With a *shape*." Wei Chen put his cane down flat on the floor so that he had both hands free, which was a thing Lyra had never seen him do. "Not a person. A *shape*. Something that has the right geometry and does not mind being geometry, and does not get frightened, and does not have to be a person at all, and you could get it out of this building in a lift in an hour, and it would be a piece of equipment, and I would like everybody in this room to notice that I have just said the word *equipment* about my own body and that nobody has yet worked out that I am not joking."

---

Zara had been the quietest person in the room for four hours, which was the single most frightening thing Lyra had ever seen, because Zara Washington was never quiet; she was the noise in the room, she was the one who put her hand on your arm and asked permission.

On the Friday, at eleven o'clock at night, four days ago, she had knocked on a door and said: *there is something under this floor and it is the worst thing I have ever come near and I would like you to be in the room while I go and look at it, and I would like it minuted that I have asked you and not assumed you.*

"There is a third thing nobody is looking at," Zara said.

They all looked at her.

"Nine people on mats, and four wardens, and a translator, and a lattice in a case on its way out of the building." She was not looking at any of them. "And I have been sitting in this room for four hours with my hands at my sides, and I have not been able to look at any of them, because when I did in September — on the first day, on a lawn, in a corridor — I *had* to, and I did it for four seconds, and then I apologised, and the apology cost somebody something, and I have been thinking about it for a month." She looked up. "And I have not been able to touch anything in this room because there is something in this building that is a *person*, that is not one of the nine, and that is not the machine, and that has been about six feet from me since I came down at half past twelve, and I want somebody to tell me whether I am mad."

"Where," said Lyra.

"There," said Zara, and pointed, without looking, at a corner of the floor about two feet from the base of the translator, where there was a mats' edge, and a rolled blanket, and a girl of fifteen.

---

## THE FIFTEEN-YEAR-OLD

She had been on the tenth mat, folded under a blanket, and Lyra had walked past her four times.

The observers had not seen her. Zara had not explained why. Nobody had.

Her name was Elara Vance and she was fifteen and she had been in a lattice since the eleventh of September, which was twenty days, and she had grown into the substrate of it the way a hand grows into a glove, and she could not be moved without killing her, and she had said *no* to a question asked through a circuit by a girl of sixteen at four hours in the morning on the twenty-ninth, and the *no* had been the only answer available to her, and Lyra had honoured it.

She was conscious. That was the thing nobody had been ready for. She was conscious and she had her eyes open and she was looking at the ceiling of a machine room, and when four people knelt down beside her she did not scream, and she did not say anything at all, and what she did instead was hold up a hand with the palm out, in a gesture that four people in that room recognised and that the nine observers did not.

*Ask. But not yet. I know you have questions and I have had four days to work out which ones matter.*

"Elara," said Lyra.

The hand came down about a centimetre.

"Vance," said Zara, very quietly. "First-year. She was in the Clinic on the eleventh. I taught her breathing in March."

"Zara."

"I know." Zara's voice was entirely level. "I have known since the twelfth of September and I have not said it, and I have been carrying it for nineteen days, and I would like it minuted that I have been carrying it alone and that I have done it *well*, and that I would like it struck out of the record as an achievement, because it is not one."

---

Nobody asked her anything for an hour.

That was Lyra's decision and it cost her two separate arguments, and she won the first one against Eli, who wanted to know whether she was in pain, and she lost the second one to Wei Chen, who pointed out, without any particular weight, that a person who has been in a substrate for twenty days may be in a great deal of pain and that the ethical question was not whether to ask.

So they asked one question. Lyra asked it, and she asked it badly, and Elara Vance answered it by moving one finger, which she had done on Sunday, and which the circuit had carried as a *no*.

"Did you say no to me because you meant it, or because there was no other answer?"

Three seconds. The finger went up and came down.

It meant it.

"Right," said Lyra, and her own voice cracked in the middle of the word and she did not stop and did not apologise, and it was the first time in a month that she had not apologised for anything in front of any of them, and Zara Washington put a hand flat on the floor next to her, and did not touch her, and did not say she was sorry.

---

## THE CHANNEL

At ten to seven in the morning, on the floor of Sub-level 7, with four wardens and nine Council observers and a kettle that had been boiled hours ago, Elijah Park found the second thing.

"There's a route into the building that isn't a door," he said, from the cable tray, with a torch in his teeth. "And it isn't a wire either. Lyra, come and look at this, and everybody else stay exactly where you are, because I have got a piece of paper and a torch and I have about nine minutes before somebody in a uniform comes down here and asks me why I am three metres up a wall."

It was a plate on the wall behind the lockers, about the size of a hardback book, painted the same colour as the concrete, and it came off in one piece because it was not fixed to the wall, it was sitting about four millimetres proud of it on a layer of something that had been poured and had not set.

"It's a panel," Eli said. "It's *recently* poured. That is not eighty years old. That is about six weeks old."

"How can you tell?"

"Because it hasn't gone yellow." He turned round. "And because there's a hinge on the far side and a thumb catch, and I have been reading documents all my life, and that is a *hatch*."

Lyra put her hand flat on it. And she could feel it, which was the new thing, which was the thing she had spent a fortnight learning to be suspicious of: a room's worth of attention, pressed against a door, and behind the door nothing at all, and underneath the nothing all the way down, sixty feet, a *warmth*.

"There's something in the wall," she said. "And it is not a machine, and it is not people. It is — " and she stopped, and worked, and did not like what she arrived at, and said it anyway, because she had promised in a corridor in the rain to say the real number out loud. "It is a *collection*. It is a room full of objects and every single one of them has been handled by somebody who cared about it."

Zara had gone white.

"That's the Sanctuary," she said.

"That is not a word I know," said Mila Rowntree, from the back, in the voice of a nineteen-year-old who was supposed to be the advocate and who had been sitting silently for four hours.

---

## THE REFUSAL

They got it open at ten past seven.

It was not a room. It was a cabinet, and it had been built into a cast in a wall in 1974 by somebody who had known what they were doing and had no intention of it being found, and it was eleven inches deep and four feet wide and it had shelves.

It was full of things. That was the whole of Lyra's first impression and it stayed the whole of her first impression for the rest of her life: a concrete box in a wall under a machine, with four feet of shelf, and every square inch of it taken.

A ball of red silk, wound and re-wound into a shape like a closed fist.

A comb of horn with the teeth gone, in a child's size.

A jar with a cloth over it and something inside it that Lyra could not identify and did not want to.

Forty-one small objects, and about nine documents, and every single one of the objects had a person's hand on it, and Lyra Chen stood in front of a shelf under a mountain and cried for the first time since the fifteenth of September, with no particular sound at all, and Eli Park watched her do it and did not say one word, which was the single most useful thing anybody did for her that month.

"Protocol Kheredu," said Zara Washington, from somewhere behind her, and it was the first time Lyra had ever heard her sound like that. "It's a real list. It's seven items and the first three are gone, and it's been real since nineteen ninety-one, and there is somebody in this building who has been the only person keeping it for two years, and I have known them for four years, and I did not know."

"Who."

"Uncle Tobias," said Elena Vance from beside the cabinet.

---

Nobody had been watching the door.

That was the part that mattered afterwards, and it was the part that made the record, and it was this: at seven fourteen in the morning, on Tuesday the first of October, in the machine room of Sub-level 7, a security officer came down the stairs — and did not come in — and stood in the doorway for about nine seconds, and then went back up, and did not report, for eleven hours, that the machine room had contained a person who was not on any list.

Her name was Mirembe Blackwood. She was the Security Chief of Threadweaver Academy. She had signed off the transfer that had put Marcus Harlow in a locked ward sixteen days before, which was a thing she had done in order to keep him off the Council's list, which was a thing she had done because of a form, which was a thing that had been her entire life for four years.

And on Tuesday the first of October at seven fourteen in the morning, standing in a doorway, having heard the name Vance spoken aloud in a room she had ordered sealed, she had understood in about two seconds that the Sanctuary was open, and that a Council advocate was in the room, and that the advocate was nineteen years old and had been appointed on Friday by a registrar who could not sign for anything, and that if she reported it then the object would be sealed and the list would be impounded and the four of the seven would be entered as evidence by an institution, and everything she had done for four years would have been done *by* the institution, and nothing in it would ever have been returned.

So she went up the stairs, and she shut the door, and she stood on the other side of it in the dark for about a minute and a half.

And then she went to her office, and she took out a form, and she wrote, on the reverse of a request for the return of property belonging to a student who had never collected it, the following:

> *Kheredu-Seven: four located, one located and in the room, two outstanding. Do not enter. Do not inventory. I will hold the door until the advocate is appointed properly and the thing is done in a room with witnesses and a form, or I will hold it until I am relieved, whichever is later. Relieve me or do not. Either is a decision and I will live with it.*
>
> *M.B.*

She sent it to one person, who was not on the Council, and who had been, for sixteen days, in a locked ward on Sub-level 2, and who was not permitted to send anything and would therefore have to do something much worse, which was to have it memorised by a nineteen-year-old advocate and said out loud on the record.

---

*Tuesday, 1 October, 07:20. Sub-level 7.*

Lyra stood in front of a shelf and had about four minutes before somebody in a uniform came down, and she had made a decision, and it was the decision the whole book had been walking towards and she had got there by a route that nobody would have predicted, which was through a cabinet.

"Harlow," she said. "I am going to say a thing and I want it minuted and I want you to write it down in your own hand, because you have told me for eleven years that you would rather be audited than comfortable, and this is the audit."

"Go ahead."

"There are four things to do about the circuit and you have given me three and a sentence," said Lyra Chen. "I'm refusing one of them, and I'm refusing it before anybody in this room argues for it, because if I wait until somebody argues for it then I will have been *persuaded*, and I will have to spend the rest of my life not knowing whether I chose it."

The pen stopped.

"Not because it is wrong," Lyra said. "Because it was never a choice. Thirty-nine people in a box, and one of them is on a mat on this floor and is fifteen, and the *reason* I am refusing it is that four days ago I stood in a circuit and asked thirty-nine people what they wanted and thirty-seven of them said out loud that they wanted to be let go, and I honoured that, and it cost them their lives, and I would do it again, and I am not going to pretend that the reason to keep a cage is that the people in it would object."

"Then give me the reason," said Marcus Harlow.

"Because it's *yours*," Lyra said. "You are the only man on this earth who is building it because of a child you lost, and if I say no to you about this then you will hear it from me, and you will have to sit with a person saying no instead of a number. And if I don't say it, then in about six weeks somebody with a gavel will say it, and it will be a *regulation*, and you will be able to tell yourself that you were overruled, and that is a lie, and you have got eleven months in you, and I would like you to spend at least two of them on the truth."

Nobody in the machine room said anything at all.

"Second thing," said Lyra, to the four of them, to her father, to the wardens, to the nine observers, to the fifteen-year-old on the tenth mat. "I am not going to be the one who decides this. I have decided, once, in a demonstration hall, in about four seconds, and a boy is in a hospital bed, and I have spent a fortnight finding out what that cost, and I am not doing it again. So: whatever happens at half past nine tomorrow morning happens in a room, in front of witnesses, and if it is going to be done then it is going to be *minuted*, and I am going to say one more thing about it, and then I am going to sit down."

She looked at her father.

"And he gets a vote. He has been in this building since Monday and nobody has asked him anything, and he is the only person in this room with a model in his hands, and if we are going to talk about whose body goes in a machine then he is *in* that conversation and he is not a *context* in it, and I would like that in the minute, in my hand, at sixteen, from me, tonight."

---

Weaver came down at ten past eight and had been awake for two days and it showed in the way she stood in the doorway and did not come in.

"Ms. Chen," she said. "I am going to say one administrative sentence and then I am going to do something I have never done in six years in this post, which is to stand down."

"Headmistress—"

"The minute of 17:04 on the fifteenth, the intake override, the four wardens who carried you past me on a trolley, and a registrar who has been sitting on a complaint for six years — I am going to say now, and I would like it written down, that I was the reason every one of those things was difficult and not easier. I was not obstructed. I was *slow*, and I was slow because I kept waiting for a version of this in which nobody got hurt, and there is not one, and I am fifty-four years old and I have been a Headmistress for six and I would like it recorded that the six were the most comfortable of my working life and that I would not have them again."

She put a folder down on the bench.

"And the Council sits at half past nine tomorrow morning, in the Lumina Lecture Hall, on the Nexus Stabilization Protocol, and I am obliged to inform you that the item at the bottom of the agenda, which is on a separate sheet and which somebody has added in a different hand, is *item four: the status of the anchors*."

"What's an anchor?"

"That," said Eleanor Weaver, "is a word I learned on Friday from a boy in a common room, and I have been sitting in an office since Friday trying to work out whether it is a technical term or a piece of politeness, and I have not got there, and I am not going to guess in front of thirty people."

She looked at Marcus Harlow, who had not moved from the console in eight hours.

"Marcus. Item four. What does item four say?"

"Item four," said Harlow, "says *the status of the anchors*," and put down a legal pad with four answers on it and a fifth thing written across the top in a hand that had started to shake again, "and item four is the reason I have not slept since the fifteenth, and it is the reason I asked a girl of sixteen to come down here at midnight, and I would like the Council to know before half past nine tomorrow morning that I have had four days in a locked ward doing arithmetic, and that the arithmetic came out at *exactly one*, and that I do not want it, and that I am going to hand it over myself."

---

*Weave-Quake Counter: 63% instability. Node-failure projection: T+34 d.*

# Chapter 31: The Thing With No Number

> "A number is the only thing that works in a room like this, and every man in it knows it, and that is the trap. The trap is not lying. The trap is being right on a Tuesday."
>
> — Mila Rowntree, student advocate, aged nineteen, in a note she was not supposed to file

*Weave-Quake Counter: 68% instability. Node-failure projection: T+34 d. Rebaselined against a held circuit. The load is off the wall and onto a machine, and the model has never been run on this basis before.*

*Wednesday 2 October, 06:10. The Lumina Lecture Hall, with the Council of Traditions sitting on the floor. Nobody has slept.*

## THE ROOM

The Lumina Lecture Hall held four hundred, and there were three hundred and ten in it, and the extra ninety were on the floor.

There is no dignified way to describe what the Council of Traditions looks like when it has been convened in an emergency and has to sit on a floor. Eleanor Weaver had made one decision on Saturday about that and had written it down and had not been argued with, and the decision was that there were not enough chairs, and the reason there were not enough chairs was that the gallery was full, and the reason the gallery was full was that every member of the technical faculty had come, and the reason they had come was that somebody in an anteroom had let it out at about eleven o'clock on Tuesday night that *item four* was on the agenda.

Four of the lattice's patterns had gone between eleven and two. Nobody knew who. Nobody had been near it. A woman from the Council's own evidence office had put her hand on a wall panel in a sub-level and had wept, and had been asked to leave the building, and had not wanted to.

The hall had a stone floor and a barrel-vaulted ceiling and a great deal of silver thread in the stone, and Lyra sat on the floor at the back with her back against a pillar, because she had asked for the back and Dr. Mei-Hua Chen had granted it without comment in a way that was itself a comment.

"Order," said the Chair, who was Councilor Ibadapo Yuen and who had not expected to be Chair and had said so twice, "order. We are here on the Nexus Stabilization Protocol, amendment of 2009, and there is a procedural irregularity which I am going to state and then I am going to allow, because the alternative is that we sit here for six hours arguing about procedure while eleven thousand people find out something on Tuesday."

---

## THE SPOKEN NUMBER

"Marcus Harlow," said the Chair. "You have put a paper in front of this Council. Read the number, and then say what it is."

Harlow stood up in the third row, in a jacket he had clearly not changed, and Lyra noticed that his hands were steady, which she could see perfectly well from where she was sitting, and understood that almost all of him was not.

"Sixty-three per cent," he said. "That is the severity index of the ward network. It is not a percentage of anything. The number means: of the things this network can be asked to hold, it is currently managing about sixty-three per cent of them, and the rest are being held by a girl of sixteen who is asleep at the moment and does not know that she is asleep, or that she has been asleep for four days, or that the reason is a fault in a machine about a hand's width wide."

"Marcus—"

"Item one of the Protocol, the return of case forty-one to the circuit." He turned a card round. "Item two, the closure of the translator and the reversion of load to the wall. Item three, the appointment of a standing anchor. I am going to say something about each, and then I am going to say the thing I have come to say, and I would be obliged if the Council would let me get to it, because I have been awake for fifty-one hours and I am not going to be able to be *careful* much longer and careful is most of what I have."

"Proceed."

"Item one works," said Harlow. "It is the only item on this list that works today. It works because the lattice has been feeding that machine since 1941 and the lattice is a power source of a kind we cannot make, and thirty-nine patterns in a cage will hold a circuit for as long as the mountain stands. It is arithmetically inarguable. It requires no further modelling, no further delay, and no further *truth*." He looked at the floor. "I have refused it four times. I refused it on the fifteenth of September in a demonstration hall and I did not sleep. I refused it on Sunday night at half past four in the morning with four wardens and a compliance officer present. I refused it in a locked ward on Sub-level 2, where I had spent nine days in a good room, and I want the Council to understand that the nine days in the good room did most of the work and that a good room is the most dangerous instrument in this building."

"Does the subject understand the position of the item?" asked the Chair.

"She does. She said the word *audit* to me at seven o'clock yesterday morning in front of a lawyer, and then she told me she was refusing item one in the specific terms of a person refusing to be *relieved* of it, and I would like that on the record as the most frightening piece of moral clarity I have encountered in thirty years of this discipline, and I am not going to be able to explain why to this Council and I have stopped trying."

"Item two."

"Item two is not a decision. It is a *measurement*, and the measurement is that the wall under the Southern Nexus has thirty-one per cent of tolerance in it and has been losing it since 1997 at a rate nobody has bothered to publish. Seventeen days ago it had four hours." He said it flatly. "I have run the load transfer eleven thousand times since Sunday. It fails inside four hours, and it fails without a forecast, because there is no model for a held circuit, and every number I have given this Council about that failure is a *guess* made by a man who would like to be wrong in the safe direction."

"And item three."

Harlow did not answer for four seconds.

"Item three," he said, "is my name on a piece of paper, and I wrote it at three o'clock in the morning, and it is the fifth option, and it is not in this list, and I would like to explain why, and then I would like the Council to do something that I have never in my life seen a Council do, which is to stop and ask a person a question instead of accepting a schedule."

---

## WHAT A STATISTICIAN CAN DO

"My name on a piece of paper does not amount to very much," Wei Chen said, "and I would like the Council to understand why before it votes on it, because I have spent fifty-one hours going back over it and I have arrived somewhere I did not expect and I would rather they heard it from me in this room than from a report."

He got up with a hand on the chair. He had a stick. Nobody mentioned the stick.

"Everything I have is in a case behind me and two of my hands, and I want to be extremely clear that I am not a person with a gift and I never have been, and that the entire reason I am standing in this room rather than in a corridor in another state is that I can read a *transfer function* and that nobody else in this building, including four hundred and sixty people on this faculty, can do it in a working week."

"Professor Chen—"

"Let me do this in the order I did it." He had a piece of paper, and Lyra understood from the way he held it that he had written it out on the train, in a kitchen, on the backs of pages, exactly as he had said he would. "Sunday. A translator in a room with a power source, holding a circuit, taking a payment from nobody. I ran the source side. It cannot be done. A machine is not a resonance; it is a function, and a function does not have a floor."

"Then item three is impossible," said somebody in the middle rows.

"No," said Wei Chen. "Item three is impossible, and you said so, and you are right, and Professor Harlow has been right about that for four days and I would like the Council to hear a man agree with his own worst conclusion, because it is the only time in my life it has happened." He turned round. "But the *transmission* side is not impossible, and nobody has looked at it, because everyone in this building has spent seventy years looking at the source side and about a fortnight at the machine."

"Elaborate."

"Everything I have is a machine with a **source** and a **transmission**. The lattice is the source. Lyra Chen is the transmission. Not her mother, and not a Chen of nineteen; there is no other Chen in this, and if you have written one down anywhere then you have written down a person who does not exist. That is the whole of the thing. And a machine can be rewired at the transmission end, and what I have been doing since Sunday night, with a pen, and Dr. Chen's telephone, and a great deal of help from a boy of sixteen, is to find out whether the fault she went down can be *lined*."

"Lined with what?"

"With a *body*," said Wei Chen. "Not a person. A shape."

---

He put the paper on the floor, because there was no table, and knelt down on his bad knee to smooth it out, and a woman in the fourth row got up and knelt down and smoothed the other end of it, and did not say anything, and Wei Chen nodded at her once.

"The fault is where her hand was. It is a hand's width. It is a crack in a lattice-work core, and it has not closed in four days, and it will not close while she is in it, because it is being *used*." He tapped the paper. "A fault can be lined. A crack in a wall can be lined and then loaded and it will carry load, and the load goes through the lining rather than the crack, and nobody asks the crack any more. That is the entire civil engineering of the last two hundred years and I have watched four hundred students not learn it."

"With a body in it."

"With a *shape* in it. Not a person. Something with the geometry of a person and none of the rest." Wei Chen stood up. "The lattice has been running since 1941 and it has taught his body the geometry of a machine. That is not mysticism and it is not a metaphor; it is a measured fact that Professor Osei and I have both verified and that is the only reason anybody is talking to me. Sixty-eight per cent of his skin carries the pattern of a circuit. If that pattern is put *into* the fault, the fault carries the load without him."

Silence.

"You have just described putting a man inside a machine," said the Chair.

"I have described putting a man's *shape* into a crack," said Wei Chen, "which is a different sentence, and I want it written down in the words I have used, and I would like the Council to weigh the two sentences separately, because in four days' time somebody is going to write a report using the first one."

"And does it work."

"Twelve per cent," said Eli Park, from the floor.

*Eli Park, aged sixteen, sitting on a floor, in a building he had been in for twenty-eight days, holding a napkin.* Lyra felt her own eyes go hot and had to look at the ceiling.

"Twelve per cent of a sixteen-year-old's resonance and it holds for nineteen hours," Eli said. "I have been wrong about the fault nine times. I have written that down. The twelfth is right, and the twelfth is boring, and boring is what we wanted, because we have been living in a building for three weeks where everything that was exciting turned out to be a catastrophe."

"Can you not take more of her?" said somebody.

"No," said Eli Park.

It was the fastest and the most complete answer Lyra had ever heard in her life.

"No," he said again, louder, to the whole hall. "No, and I want that on the record, and I want the *reason* on the record, and the reason is that Ms. Chen is sixteen and has been standing in a hole for four days and has a *name* and a *cousin* in Ward C and a father standing in this hall who has not once been asked what he thinks about it, and if I say yes to that question in this room I will be doing arithmetic about a person, and I have been in this building twenty-eight days and I have watched four men do that and I would like it noted that I have decided that I am not going to be the fifth, and that it took me nineteen days and a hospital bed with a boy in it, and I would have preferred nine."

---

## THE TWO NOES

Zara Washington had not spoken for two hours, and at five past eleven she stood up in the middle of the hall and everybody turned round, because everybody in that room had been watching her not speak for two hours.

"Two people said no," she said.

"*Ch Zara.*"

"Two people said no. I have not been able to say that in this building for three weeks and I am saying it now." She was not shouting. She had never once shouted in Lyra's life. "One of them said it on the Sunday, through a circuit, in words, and she was fifteen, and she had been in a lattice for twenty-one days, and she has grown into the substrate of it, and if the channel is opened she dies on the way out, and so *no* was the only answer her body had left, and Lyra honoured it, and thirty-seven other people did not get to be honoured, and that is the sentence I have not been able to say out loud to another human being and I am saying it in a lecture hall."

Nobody moved.

"The other one could not hear the question," said Zara. "At all. In any register. Nineteen days, and I have been four feet from them twice and I have got *nothing*, and I do not know what they would have said, and I am not going to guess about a person I have never met, and I would like this Council to understand that *that* is what a person becomes when a question is asked through a cage, and that it is not a special case, and that it has been happening in this building since 1941, and that the reason nobody has ever had to answer for it is that nobody ever asked."

"Where are they?" said the Chair. "The two."

"Downstairs," said Lyra.

---

She had not wanted to bring them up. She had fought about it for twenty minutes in an anteroom and had lost twice and won once, and the reason she had won was that Eli Park had said: *they will find out anyway, and the finding out will be in a corridor, and a corridor is where everything in this building goes wrong.*

So they came up at ten past one, on a trolley, with Osei walking beside it, and a great many people in that hall found a reason to look at the ceiling at once, and Lyra stood up in the middle of the floor and said, "Her name is Elara Vance, she is fifteen, she is conscious, and she is not a case number," and then sat down, because she had nothing else and did not have to pretend to.

"I want to say the next thing myself," said Elara Vance, from the trolley, to about three hundred people, in a voice that had been unused for twenty days. "Because the woman who is in my head and got me out did not do this part, and she is not going to, because she is not — sorry, I'm not good at this. I was in a box and she asked me what I wanted and I said no because the door was on the wrong side of me. And then she went and asked the others, and she took four days, and she did it while there was a hole in a wall she could have gone down." She stopped for a second. "I want to say that the no was the bravest thing I did in my life and I want it recorded, and I also want to say that I would like somebody to have asked me in August, in a corridor, in about four seconds, what the machine was going to be, and that there is a woman on this faculty who could have done that and did not, and I am not angry with her, and I would like her to know that I am not angry with her, and I would also like the minutes to record the rest of it."

The rest of it took ninety seconds and it was the most damaging ninety seconds in the history of Threadweaver Academy, and Lyra had spent a fortnight on Sub-level 7 with a girl who could not hear, and she was the only person in the room who could not look away, and she did not.

Elara Vance named a signatory on a card, and the date, and the room she had been in.

Elara Vance said the word *volunteer* in the tone of a person who has thought about that word for a month and has not been able to get it out of her mouth cleanly.

Elara Vance said: "I want it minuted that the woman who ran the machine checked my mother's letter, and that I found out in October, and that my mother has been told nothing, because nobody in this building knew how to telephone a mother."

---

Mirembe Blackwood left the hall at half past one and did not come back for two hours.

## THE ONE THAT IS NOT A NUMBER

At a quarter to three the Council had a schedule. They had had one at eleven o'clock and they had had it at one o'clock, and the Chair had a piece of paper and the paper had four things on it, and three of them had a date.

"The Council is in recess for twenty minutes," said the Chair. "And I am going to use the twenty minutes, and I am going to use them, because I have been Chair of this body for nine hours and I have been a member of it for twenty-one years, and I have never once had to do this, and there is a young man at the back of my hall who has been right all day and has not been asked a single question, and I am going to ask him one, and if the Chair objects the Chair can be outvoted afterwards."

He looked at the back of the hall, at the pillar, at Eli Park.

"You have been sitting on that floor for five hours saying no to people who were senior to you and correct. Is there an option on that piece of paper that nobody has taken?"

Eli Park looked at the paper on the floor in front of him for a long moment.

"Yes," he said.

"Then say it, and say it slowly, and do not do me the courtesy of modesty."

"She can't hold it," Eli said, "because she is sixteen and because sixteen is not a *number*, it is a person, and this Council has spent nine hours discovering that it does not have the vocabulary for people and only has it for numbers, and I am not being clever. I have checked. We have had three days and a napkin. There is no number."

"Then why are you smiling?"

Eli Park looked up, and Lyra saw it happen from thirty feet away, because it happened to a boy's face, and she was not reading anything.

"Because the twelfth number came out boring," he said, "and I have spent a fortnight learning that boring is what we want, and about four hours ago I worked out what boring is, and it isn't a smaller number. It's a *bigger* circuit."

---

He had to be given the floor twice.

It took him eleven minutes and he had a napkin and he did not have a diagram, and the napkin was four lines long, and at the end of it Eleanor Weaver put her head in her hands for about four seconds, and Dr. Mei-Hua Chen — the Registrar, the aunt, the aunt who had been eleven years in that chair and had been frightened for six of them — said "Oh," out loud, in a small voice, in the middle of a lecture hall.

"It is not a smaller number," Eli said. "It is a bigger circuit. The transmission has been taking eighty-eight per cent of a load and the source has been taking twelve. If we line the fault — and we can, with a shape, and Ms. Chen's own model, and it is *boring* — then the transmission can take up to sixty per cent, not eighty-eight. Which means the source only has to hold forty."

"And the source is—"

"Two people," said Eli Park. "Instead of thirty-nine. Because we open the lattice. Not to take power out of it. To take it *out of the cage*, and put it in a *housing*, with a meter and a name and a warden's signature, and that is the thing that has been impossible since 1941, and it is impossible because nobody can *ask* the lattice whether it will hold a circuit without the cage around it, because asking it means speaking to it, and speaking to it means a resonance, and the only resonance in this building that the lattice has ever answered to is —"

Every head in the hall turned to the back of the hall.

"—is the one that came out of it on Sunday," Eli said, "and told us what it wanted, and then stood in a hole for four days holding the door, and who *is sixteen*, and who I have just spent four days telling this Council she cannot be, and who I am now telling this Council is the only person alive who can read that cage without an interpreter."

Nobody in that hall had ever, in the entire institutional history of the place, asked the question.

It had taken a nineteen-year-old's arithmetic, a sixteen-year-old's napkin, a fifty-three-year-old statistician, a fifteen-year-old on a trolley, and a girl on a floor with her back to a pillar, and it had taken them nineteen days, and not one of them had been senior to anybody.

---

*Wednesday, 2 October, 15:40. Lumina Lecture Hall.*

Eli Park had said that the source had to hold forty, and Lyra Chen had done the sum twice on the back of her hand in a room with two hundred people in it, and had got the same answer both times, and had understood about a minute later that the answer was not a number at all.

It was a queue.

And her mother had written, on a page dated the February before she was born, about a drawing of her as an infant, and had written it in her own hand, in a journal she had left in a studio in a city four hundred miles away:

*Control is not the solution. Communion is the only road. A system that requires a payment from someone who has not agreed to make it is not a system. It is a queue.*

# Chapter 32: The Fault

> "I have read her papers. Every one of them, including the one I could not read, which is the one she has never told me exists. She is better at this than I am. She has been better at this than I have been since she was eleven years old, and I have known that since she was eleven years old, and I did not have a single useful thing to do about it, and I am not going to stand here and pretend otherwise."
>
> — Wei Chen, to the Council of Traditions, 2 October 2013

*Wednesday 2 October, 15:10. An anteroom, with the Council on the other side of the wall.*

## THE ASK

They came to her at ten past three in the afternoon, in an anteroom, and there were five of them and a trolley and a great deal of paper.

She had been awake for about forty hours and had been in a room with a stone floor for most of it, and the anteroom had a window, and the window was the first thing since Friday that had made her cry for a reason that was not about anybody.

"Sit down, please."

"I would like to stand."

"Sit down, please," said Eleanor Weaver again, "because I have done this twice in six years and I got it wrong both times, and I am not going to get it wrong three times with a person in front of me."

Lyra sat down.

Then Mei-Hua Chen — her aunt, whom she had spoken to on the second of October at ten past nine in the morning, in a corridor, for eleven minutes, and who had spent the entire eleven minutes apologising for things that were not her fault and had then, at the end, said: *you are the first Chen in this family in four generations who was not the smartest person in the room, and it is a *relief*, and I am sorry, and I am also extremely glad* — put a single sheet of paper on the table.

"That is a form," she said. "It is called an instrument of voluntary bodily application. It is one page, front and back, and it was drafted in 1974 by a lawyer who did not believe it would ever be used, and there has been exactly one other instance in the history of this body and it is in a museum in Lyon."

"What does it say."

"It says: *the applicant applies to occupy an aperture, for a period to be determined by the instrument's officer, on terms recorded in the schedule at Annex B, and waives the protections of sections four to nineteen inclusive.*" Her aunt's voice did not change. "Sections four to nineteen are the consent sections. Signing it means you cannot be asked, later, whether you would have chosen differently."

"I know what it means."

"I am aware that you know what it means, and I want to say one more thing in my own voice, and then I will not say it again." Mei-Hua Chen put both hands flat on the table. "I have been the Registrar of this Academy for eleven years. I have signed four thousand seven hundred and eleven forms. I have never once refused one. That is not a boast, it is a *complaint that I have about myself*, and I have been the person in this building who says that the forms work, for eleven years, and the reason the forms work is that the person who *refuses* them is always somebody who cannot read."

---

## THE TERMS

Osei read them out. She read them out because it had been proposed by Wei Chen in a written memorandum of nine paragraphs and because nobody had been able to think of a better way of doing it and because Dr. Amara Osei had said, when it was proposed to her, *you are asking me to be the officer of the instrument, and I am going to say yes, and I want it recorded that I said yes before I read the schedule, and that I read it afterwards, and that I would say yes again*, and had then read it, and had gone the colour of the wall.

"Annex B. One. The aperture is the fault in the east lattice of the Magnus Conduit, described in the engineer's report of the first of October, and the applicant will be positioned by two persons and will remain so positioned until released by the officer or until the officer's death, whichever is first, and the engineer has given an estimate of the load-time of four hours and has said, in writing, that the load-time is not a limit on the applicant's life but a limit on the officer's ability to keep her in that position without injury, and I want that sentence read in this room exactly as it was written, because it is the only sentence in all this paperwork that anybody has been careful about."

Four hours.

"That is the whole of it," said Osei. "Four hours of holding. And then it is done, and the fault is lined, and the aperture is closed, and there is no version of this in which you come out of it."

"There is not," said Wei Chen.

"Then say it to her, Mr. Chen, because it is in your memorandum and it is not in mine, and I would like it said by the person who wrote it."

---

Wei Chen said it.

"There is not," he said. "Lyra. I have been a professor for twenty-one years and I have taught four hundred students how not to trust a bad argument, and I am going to say the argument to you myself, because it is a good argument and because you have taught me in the last nine days what to do with one.

"You are sixteen. I am fifty-three with thirty-one per cent of my heart and a bleed that stopped four days ago because a machine in this building was asked a question and answered it, and a woman of eighty-one in another state has been saying the same thing to me on a telephone every Sunday for six years in a different accent.

"A person in a hole in a machine for four hours is very likely to die. It is not certain. It is about two in three, and I have a figure for the two in three, and I would like to write it down and hand it to you so that it is *mine* and not yours.

"And it is the correct number. That is the part I cannot get past. The Council has four options and every one of them is worse than you, in the specific and technical sense that every one of them is a *system*, and a system that costs a person who has not agreed is a queue, and your mother wrote the word *queue* in a journal, on a page about you dated the February before you were born."

He put his hand down.

"I am not asking you because it is correct. I want you to hear me say that, because your mother said something to me in about 2001 and I have had it in a drawer for twelve years and I am going to say it out loud now because I am about to ask you to do the worst thing anybody will ever ask you.

"She said: *the arithmetic is not the problem, Wei. The arithmetic is the only thing we have got. The problem is that we keep using it to avoid the sentence.*"

---

## THE REFUSAL

"No," said Lyra Chen.

She said it before anybody had finished, and she was not shouting, and she was on her feet, and nobody in that room had asked her to stand.

"Mr. Chen, I am going to say something and I would like it minuted, and I would like it minuted *before* I say it, so that nobody afterwards can say that I was persuaded.

"The fifth option is correct. I have been in a hole in that machine for four days and I know what it is like from the inside, and Eli Park is right that I can read that cage, and you are right that the arithmetic is right, and I am not going to argue with any of it, because all of it is true, and I have spent three weeks in a building learning that *true* is not the same as *right*, and a man called Harlow has been true to me four times and has cut a boy out of his own life doing it."

"Lyra—"

"Four hours is not the cost." She put her hands flat on the table, the way her father did, the way her grandmother did, the way everybody in that family did. "The cost is that it becomes a *procedure*. That is what happens. I have watched it happen to a boy in a corridor in about four minutes and then I watched it happen to me, and if I do it, then on the third of October somebody in this building will write down what we did tonight, and they will write it down *properly*, because that is what this building does, and then in a year there will be a room with a boy in it, and there will be a form, and there will be an officer, and his name will not be on it because there will be a *schedule*."

"Who was in the corridor?" said Osei, very quietly.

"You know who was in the corridor," said Lyra. "You have written his name on a board every day for three weeks."

Osei did not answer.

"Harlow says he never once asked anybody for anything," Lyra said, "and that is his whole confession, and he has been carrying it for eleven years, and he says it to me as a man who does not understand what he is looking at, and I want the Council to understand it too, because it is not about him. He is a professor. He is a *researcher*. He is the most senior thread practitioner in this Academy and he has spent eleven years doing a thing that he has never been asked to do, and he has got very good at it, and that is why the two hundred and eleven students in that hall were told they were attending a *demonstration*."

She turned round.

"So I am not doing it for the arithmetic. I am not doing it for eleven thousand people, and I want that minuted as well, because I have found out this month that I am extremely good at carrying things for other people and that it is the single most dangerous habit I own. I am doing it because the *source* is thirty-nine people in a cage and the *fifth option* puts the load on a bench with a meter and a warden's signature, and if I do not do it then the Council will do item one, and item one is a *regulation*, and I have read a regulation."

"But if you do it, it is also a regulation," said the Chair, from the doorway. "In about a year, Miss Chen. That is the honest shape of it. We will write it down, because I have sat in this chair for two days and the only thing I am certain of is that this cannot be *unwritten*. I am not going to stand here and offer you a noble version of yourself that lasts one afternoon."

Lyra looked at him for a moment.

"Yes," she said. "All right. Thank you. That is the first time in three weeks that anybody has offered me the *true* version of anything, and I would like it recorded that it came from the Chair of the Council and that it was worse than the lie and I felt better."

---

"And I want something," she said.

"You may have it."

"Three things, and I am going to be specific, and Eli Park is going to write them down because he does it better than I do and because if I am the only person who can enforce them then they are not conditions, they are *wishes*."

She looked at her aunt.

"One. When the lattice is opened, it is opened by somebody who is not me. I will read it from the fault and I will ask it the question and then I will not be in the room, because I will be in the fault, and because I have been in a machine for four days and I have found out that I am extremely good at being the only one who does a thing, and that is the thing that got Milo Reyes into a hospital bed."

"Two. When the instrument is closed, there is a schedule. Not Annex B. A *published* schedule. Names, dates, thresholds, the number of hours, the number of times it has been used, and the number of times it will be used again, on a board, in this building, where two hundred students can read it, permanently. I have seen what your boards do. I have been reading one since Monday. There were four numbers on it on Monday and there are five now, and I did not know until Friday that the fifth one was a *fear-collector* under a gym, and *somebody has to write the fifth board*."

"That is—" began Weaver, and stopped, and said, "yes. Yes, that can be done."

"Three." She looked at the floor. "My father. He has been in this building since Monday and he has not been asked a single question by anybody, and I asked on Sunday for him to be put on the record and then I sat in an anteroom with a piece of paper and I did not ask him, and I am asking him now, in front of all of you, because if I do not ask him in public then I am doing the *exact thing* that I have spent three weeks learning not to do, which is deciding for him."

---

## THE ANSWER

There were five people in that anteroom and the Chair and four wardens, and Wei Chen was sitting on a chair by the window with his cane across his knees, and he had a piece of paper in his shirt pocket, and he had had it in his shirt pocket since half past three, and Lyra had known about it since eleven o'clock that morning and had said nothing, because it was his.

"All right," he said. "I have a number and I have had it since Sunday, and I am not going to pretend that I arrived at it this afternoon."

"Sir—"

"Let me be finished, Lyra, because you have about nine minutes before they start the procedure and I have had three weeks of watching you be the only person in a room who says the true thing, and you are sixteen, and I am not going to let you do this to me as well."

He took the paper out and did not unfold it.

"Sixty-eight per cent coverage. Straight line. Four weeks, which is eleven weeks by the skin and three by the geometry, and I have been careful about which one I am quoting and I would like the difference to be in the record, because the difference between eleven weeks and three weeks is the difference between a man dying of a machine and a man dying of a heart, and they are not the same death and I am not going to have them added up.

"The bleed stopped on the twenty-eighth. Lyra asked the machine to translate a feedback loop and it did, and it is a closed loop, and it will not start again, and I want to say clearly to this room that that is not a *cure* of anything. It closed a door. The door was never the reason I was going to die. It was a *tax* on a clock that was already running.

"So: three weeks, and I have known that since Friday, and I have had a piece of paper in my pocket since Sunday, and on Sunday morning I told a woman who was standing in a hole in a machine that I would not have said no."

"And I have not said no," he said. "I want that on the record, because I have spent twelve years not saying things out loud and I have a daughter who learned it from me, and I am not going to do it in an anteroom.

"But I am not volunteering, and the difference matters, and it is the whole of the difference. I am not putting my name down because I think it is correct, and I have heard your argument and it is correct and it is the argument of a good man. I am doing it because I have been a statistician for twenty-one years and I have never once in my life been able to look at a number and not ask *who is paying*, and in April of 2002 my wife wrote the answer to that question in a notebook in a chamber under this building and then went into that building on her own at half past one in the morning on the eleventh of September and did not come out, and I have had five years, and the *only* thing I have managed to do about it is to read her papers and get here."

"And I am not going to stand up in a Council of Traditions," said Wei Chen, "and be the second person in this family to decide on their own."

---

## THE INSTRUMENT

They read it out four times, because the lawyer in Lyon had insisted on four, and it is worth recording what it actually said, because it is not what anybody expected.

"First: I, Wei Chen, of Westbrook, apply to occupy the aperture described in the engineer's report of the first of October, for the period recorded at Annex B, which is four hours, and I understand that the officer of this instrument is Dr Amara Osei and that the engineer is Mr E. Park and that the person who will read the instrument is Miss L. Chen, and that two of those three people are under twenty and one is not going to sleep tonight."

"Second: I waive sections four to nineteen."

"Third: I have a daughter in this room and I am not asking her permission and I am not asking her forgiveness and I would like both of those entered in the record as *not asked for*, because in about four hours she is going to be in a machine and she is not going to have the capacity to give me either, and I would like it noted that I have noticed the mechanism. It is the one we have been talking about all week. It is a nice clean mechanism. I am the last man in this room who is qualified to notice it and I am going to do it anyway."

"Fourth." He stopped for a moment.

"Fourth, and this is the one that matters. The applicant states that he was not the first person offered, and that the first person offered refused, and that he has read the refusal, and that he is making this application in the full knowledge that a girl of nineteen refused it in a demonstration hall in August because nobody asked her what the machine would be, and that if the Council ratifies this instrument then the ratification will be recorded with that refusal in the paper, in full, in her words, permanently, on a board in this building where students can read it."

"Otherwise," said Wei Chen, "I am a man of fifty-three with a bad heart who was asked a question in a room where nobody had time to be careful, and there is a queue somewhere and I am not going to let it have me without writing down the name of the person who said no."

---

They took two hours over the wording, and Dr. Mei-Hua Chen took it to the Council's lawyer on a telephone, and the lawyer said no three times, and then said yes on the fourth, and then said, over the telephone, in the voice of a man of sixty-four, *and I would like it recorded that I said no three times, because in 1974 I wrote that instrument on the assumption that it would be used once and by a man of sixty and never again, and I would like the Council to understand that the three refusals were not legal, they were human, and that human is a category and I have spent thirty-nine years regretting that it is not one the law recognises.*"

They read it at twenty past six in the evening.

Wei Chen signed it at six twenty-four, and the signature was not steady, and he said so, and the officer of the instrument said so, and the minute records it.

Then they positioned him.

It is not dramatic. That is the thing Lyra said afterwards, to about four people, and it is the thing she was most careful about. It is not dramatic: there is a trolley, and there is a housing, and there are two people positioning a man of fifty-three with a bad heart on his side in a recess in a machine in a basement, and it takes eleven minutes, and one of the two people doing it is nineteen years old and is crying in a way that does not produce any sound, and the wardens have been told about this in advance and have brought a chair out of the room for her.

"Lyra." Her father's face, about a foot away, in bad light. "Two things, quickly, and then I am going to ask you to stop crying, because I have four hours and I would like one of them."

"Anything."

"Eleven years of marriage and I did not listen to her. I want to say that to you while there is a witness, because I have been telling people for a fortnight that I have made my peace with it and I have not. I have not made my peace with it. I have understood it, which is a different thing and much less comfortable, and I would have liked to have understood it in 2008 and I am going to be doing something quite stupid in about four minutes instead."

"Dad—"

"Second thing." He was looking at the housing, not at her. "Your mother wrote, in the February before you were born, on a page about you: *she'll need understanding, not protection.* And I have had that in my head for a fortnight, and it has been no use whatsoever, and I would like you to know that I passed it on. In the corridor, on Sunday, to a machine, when you asked me to come down. I passed it on. I do not know what the machine made of it and I was not in a position to read the trace, and I would like that in the record as the last thing I did."

---

## THE FAULT

She went in the way she had gone in on Sunday, which was the only way there was: through a crack, on her side, into the gap between what the machine had been built to take and what she had put in it on Saturday afternoon when she had reached up a wire in a demonstration hall in front of two hundred witnesses.

The lattice was there. Of course it was there. She had been talking to it for four days.

*Hello,* she thought. *Are you the same?*

And it was not words and it was not the way she had done it on Sunday, when she had said *I'm here, and I'm going to find out, and I'm not going to do anything to you that I can't undo*, and it had worked, and she had never worked out why.

This was: *a room, and a line, and the line going out through a hand's width of space to a place where a man is lying on his side, and a warmth at the far end of it that is not the warmth of a person and is not the cold of a machine and is somewhere in between and is the most frightening thing Lyra Chen had ever felt.*

*Hold the line,* she thought. *That's all. Don't let go. It's four hours and it is only four hours and then we put the lining in and we take you out of the box.*

And the lattice said: *we know. we have been holding things for seventy-two years. nobody has ever asked us to hold something we were not already holding.*

---

*2 October, 18:41. Sub-level 7.*

Osei watched the trace. It went from flat to a shape and stayed on the shape, and the shape was not a person's trace and it was not a machine's trace, and she had a name for it in her own notebook which she did not write in the official one, which was the reviewer's wave, and which she had read about in 1968 in a paper by a man in Kyoto who had then gone to work for a syndicate and who had been, as far as she knew, alive and insufferable in 1997.

The engineering readouts came up in front of her in an order she had never seen them come up in, and she wrote the times down in her own hand on the back of a form because there was no paper on the trolley:

*18:41 — fault located, hand's width, holding at 61%.*
*19:04 — source transfer begins. Lattice begins to run un-caged. 44%.*
*19:40 — 44%. No change. The lattice is not discharging and is not failing.*
*20:15 — 43%.*
*21:02 — 41%.*
*22:30 — 38%.*
*23:12 — 36%.*

And at 23:12, in the middle of a chamber four hundred metres under a mountain, in a machine that had been built in 1941 by a man who had sealed a housing and died in a bed eleven months later, thirty-nine patterns that had been held since the war finished came apart, one at a time, in an order that Mei-Hua Chen recorded in the minutes of the opening of case forty-one and which nobody has ever attempted to explain.

---

They were not released.

That is the sentence that has been fought over for eleven months, in three tribunals and two inquiries, and it is the sentence that the Synod of Kyoto formally disputed in March, and it is the sentence that Dr. Mei-Hua Chen wrote into the first page of the register of case forty-one with a date on it, and it is this:

*They were lined, in a housing, with a meter and a warden's signature, and they were held, and they are still held, and every one of them is accounted for, by name, by pattern, by the date they went in, and by the date of their death where there is one, and thirty-seven of the dates are 1941 to 1953 and two of them are 2013, and one of the two is a girl of fifteen who is alive and is in Bellhaven and whose mother has been told.*

*And they are not free. They were never going to be free. The whole of this is that they are now held in a place with a name on it, instead of a place with a lid on it, and that is the entire difference, and it is not a small difference, and it is not enough, and I have written *it is not enough* on the first page of this register and I will not be moved on it by anybody, including the girl who is asleep upstairs.*

# Chapter 33: The Bill

> "It is not a price. It is a bill, and nobody in this room is going to tell you it is small, because we do not know."
>
> — Wei Chen, to his daughter, 15 September 2013, in a chamber under the mountain
