## THE FOUR HOURS

He was alive.

That was the first thing, and it was not the thing anybody had braced for, and Lyra stood in the doorway of a machine room at ten past seven in the morning for about four seconds without going in, because she could not work out what a person was supposed to do with that.

"Alive is not the same as recovered," said Osei, from the console, in a voice that had been used for about thirty hours. "Miss Chen, I am going to say four facts and then I am going to ask you a question, and I would like you to notice that I have put the question last and that I have done that on purpose, because the last time somebody in this building put a question last it worked."

"Four facts."

"One. He held the line for three hours and forty-one minutes, which is nineteen minutes short of what we told him it would be, and I would like it in the record that we were the ones who were wrong about the number and not him, and the fault is lined, and the lining took the load, and the lining did what the engineering said it would do." She did not look round. "Two. He is alive. Three. He is not going to recover. Four. The bleed has not restarted and is not going to, because the source of the bleed was a feedback loop between a seer and a man, and the seer is standing in a fault and the man is lying in a housing, and the loop does not close and cannot close and will not close in either of us, ever again."

Nobody said anything.

"And the question," said Dr Amara Osei, "is whether you are going to come into this room, and I am asking it last, and I am asking it in that order, because I have looked after that man for six days and I have watched two hundred and ten people walk past a door he was behind, and I would like to be very clear that I am not blaming you, and I would also like to be very clear that it is going to keep happening."

---

He was grey. That was the first thing.

He had been fifty-three for eight months and he looked about seventy, and his hair had gone white at the sides in eight days, and the skin of his forearms had a pattern in it that Lyra could see with the new thing, the new horrible useful thing, the thing that came in through her palms and told her the temperature of attention: a lattice of fine black lines, a hand's width apart, running up both arms in a pattern that was not a rash and was not a burn and was, unmistakably, a *diagram*.

She sat down on the floor next to the trolley because there was no chair in the machine room and because she had discovered that she could not do the other thing.

"Dad."

"Mila." His voice was about a third of what it had been. "You have got the face you had at eleven."

She did not correct him. He had got it wrong once before, in September, and had stopped in the middle of it and started again, and she had let that go as well.

"That's not a nice thing to say to a person who has not slept."

"It is not a nice thing to say to a person," her father agreed. "I have been thinking about that all night. I have had about four hours of it and I have done nothing else."

"How much?"

"Enough." He shut his eyes. "Lyra, I am going to tell you three things and I am not going to be able to say them the way I would like, so I am going to say them badly.

"One. I knew. Not about the machine. I did not know about the machine. I knew on the Sunday night, in a room with a kettle and a lockers, when a man with silver in his arms told me the arithmetic had come out at exactly one, and I looked at him and I thought — I thought, *he has not asked me, and he is going to ask me, and I have got about nine hours*."

"And you said nothing."

"I said nothing for nine hours," said Wei Chen, "because I have spent twelve years saying nothing in rooms, and because I was frightened, and because I wanted to see whether she would go down there *without* me, which is a sentence I am not proud of and which you are entitled to have, and which I have said out loud now so that it is on the record."

---

"Two." He opened his eyes. "The lining held. And the lining is a piece of equipment, and there is a form, and there is a board in this building with a name on it, and in about four months a committee will write a report about whether the *form* is right. And I would like to say something to you about the report, and I have been working on it since about two o'clock this morning with the assistance of a spinal block and a very good nurse."

"Dad—"

"Let me get to the part. The report will say that the instrument was used once, in an emergency, by a willing adult male, and that the emergency was real, and that the outcome was that a man died and a valley held. And every single sentence of that will be true." He shifted, and it took him a moment. "And in about four years, Lyra, somebody will be in a room with a boy in it, and somebody will be holding a form, and the report will be in a drawer, and the *fact* of this will not be in the room. Not the words. Not the fact that a girl of nineteen said no in a demonstration hall in August and that the Council argued for two days about whether the refusal had to be printed."

"Mr. Park has written it down."

"Mr. Park has written down a great deal this month, in the margins of other people's documents, in a manner I find alarming and correct." Her father shut his eyes again. "I would like you to keep doing it. Not as an act of conscience. As *procedure*. I want it to be a thing that is done, and I want it to be a thing that is done by somebody boring with a good hand, and I want it to be done whether or not anybody is watching. I am a man who has had a career in method and I am telling you that the only thing that will hold this place together is not a girl with a silver thread. It is a form and a name and a date and somebody who turns up."

---

"Three," he said. "And then I am going to be asleep for a while, and they will tell you that it was the strain, and it will be partly the strain, and I would like you to have the other half now while I can still do the shape of it.

"When I went down, I was not brave. I want that on the record, because you will be asked, and because in about a year somebody will tell a story about this in a room and the man in the story will be a different man, and I would like the real one on the record while I can get it down.

"I was not brave. I was *tired*. I have been tired since the eleventh of September five years ago, and I have got up every morning since and done the next thing, and I have done it very well, and I have been dead — " he stopped, and started again, and it was the word that undid her, and he clearly knew it was, and he said it anyway. "I have been dead since 2008 and nobody noticed because I kept turning up, and the reason I was so good at the work is that I was the only person in this family who still *minded*."

"Dad—"

"And on Sunday night, in a corridor, I was asked, and I said yes, and the reason I said yes is that I am the person who minds, and there has been exactly one of me since 2008, and I would like that written down, because the alternative is that the story becomes about a girl with a silver thread and it is not, and it was never, and I have spent the last nine days watching her take a machine apart in a lecture hall in front of three hundred people and I would like it noted that the machine would have been perfectly serviceable on Monday."

---

The room was very quiet, and somewhere up the corridor somebody was doing something with a kettle, and it was the most ordinary sound Lyra Chen had ever heard.

"Did Mum come?" she said.

Her father's face did something she had never seen it do.

"No," he said. "That is the thing I have been getting wrong since Sunday night, and I have had a spinal block and eight hours and I have got it exactly wrong, and I am going to tell you, and then I am going to sleep.

"In this building — and this is not a religious claim, it is a *record* claim, and I have been an expert on record claims for twenty-one years — the pattern of a thing that has been held in a circuit for a long time is stored in the circuit, and the circuit is this room, and there is a great deal in this room. And the pattern of your mother is in here, and there is enough of her in the sub-levels of this building to fill a chamber, because she went into one on her own at half past one in the morning on the eleventh of September and did not come out.

"And I did not want her." His eyes were open. "I have had five years and I have wanted her every day, and last night, when I was lying in an aperture on the wrong side of a thing I built, in the dark, with a lattice in front of me and a nineteen-year-old at the other end of a fault — I did not want her at all. I wanted the *work*. I wanted the equation. I wanted a girl in a hole to be able to read a cage without an interpreter, and I wanted it to work, and I wanted to be the person who finished it.

"Which is what she wrote about on a wall in 2002. *It is a door, and I am not going to be the one who opens it, because everyone who gets here has been told it is the only way.* And I lay in the dark last night and reached for her, and the thing that came was *an answer*." He swallowed. "And it was arithmetic. And it was hers. And it was not her."

Lyra put her hand on the side of the trolley.

"That's not a comforting thing," she said.

"It is not remotely a comforting thing," her father agreed, "and I have been trying to find the version that is, for eight hours, and there is not one, and I have concluded that this is the correct outcome, and that the reason everybody in this family has been in such a mess for two generations is that we have all been waiting for a door."

---

## THE FIFTEEN-YEAR-OLD

They came up at nine.

She came up herself, this time, on her own feet, with a nurse at each elbow and a blanket she had refused to be wrapped in, because she was fifteen and had been in a substrate for twenty-seven days and had developed some opinions.

She came into the machine room where her father was lying and looked at him for a while, and then she said, "He's not breathing properly."

"He is breathing," said Osei. "He is breathing with about forty per cent of his lung."

"I know he's breathing." Elara Vance sat down on the floor, cross-legged, in the middle of a room in which a man was dying, and put her hands on her knees, and looked at the pattern on his forearms for a long time.

"That's the same as mine," she said. "That's exactly the same as mine. I can feel it from here. That's how I know where the line is, because it's the same as — " she stopped. "That's how I know the line is lined now. Because it's the same shape as the fault and the fault is lined, and it was not lined on Friday."

"You can feel the lining," said Eli Park.

"I can feel the *shape*." She looked up at him. "Are you the one with the napkin?"

"I'm the one with the napkin."

"Then can I say something and can you write it down, because you write things down and nobody writes anything down." Elara Vance wiped her face with the back of her hand. "I was in there. And when she was asking — when *she* was asking, on the Sunday, and I said no — I wasn't saying no to the door. There wasn't a door. I've been in a box in the dark since I was six years old and there has never been a *door*, and everybody keeps saying the door and there isn't one."

"Then what was the no for?"

"It was for *her*." Elara Vance said it without any drama at all, which was how the two hardest sentences of Lyra Chen's life were contradicted, and improved, by a girl of fifteen on a floor. "Because she'd been in there about four hours and she'd asked thirty-something of us and we were all saying the same thing and she was *tiring*. And I could tell. And I thought, if I say yes, she'll do whatever it is we all want, and she'll do it on her own, on her own, on her own, and then she'll be *gone* the way everybody else is gone. And I have been watching people go since I was six."

The machine room was extremely quiet.

"So no," said Elara Vance. "It was for her. And I have been sorry for twenty-seven days, and you know what, that's the correct answer, and *you* knew, because you said out loud in that hall on Tuesday that you didn't know what I'd said it for and that you weren't going to guess about a person you'd never met." She looked at Eli Park. "You're allowed to be wrong in the minute. You've got to be allowed to be wrong in the minute. That's the deal. If you have to be right the whole time then you end up alone, and I've been alone since I was six."

---

Lyra Chen did not sleep for two days.

That is the part nobody put in the newspapers and it is the part she would have wanted put in the newspapers. What she did instead, in the two days after her father stopped breathing properly, was sit in two rooms and did not do anything, and was extremely good at it, which everybody in the building had discovered she could be, and which was the single most frightening thing about her for the rest of the year.

She sat in Ward C with him. She did not touch the machine, which was being dismantled in a disciplined way by four engineers from a firm that had been hired through the Council and had not been told what the machine was, and who found it odd, and who were right to.

And on the second morning she went down to Sub-level 2, to a locked ward, where a man of fifty-five with silver scarring up both forearms had been for eleven days, and had not been told anything, because the officer of the instrument had taken a decision, and had written it down, and had told the Headmistress, and the Headmistress had honoured it.

## THE MAN IN THE GOOD ROOM

He was standing at the panel that showed a recorded valley, and it was raining in the panel, and it was the wrong weather for October.

"You don't have to come down here," he said, without turning round. "I have been told that I have four visitors on a list and that you are not on it, and that the woman who built the list is going to be extremely difficult about it."

"I'm on the list," said Lyra Chen. "I got there yesterday. I have a very good hand, apparently."

"It is a very good hand."

She stood about six feet away from him and put her hands behind her back, because she had discovered in the last four days that when she put them in front of her they read as *reaching*, and she had read a great deal of reaching that week.

"The case is lined," she said. "It's in a housing with a meter and a warden's signature. It's on a board. There's a form and there's a schedule and there's a girl on the floor with a napkin who's going to be insufferable about it for forty years."

"That is—" He stopped. Started again. "That is very much more than I expected and rather less than I hoped, and both of those are correct, and I would like to know what you think I did."

"I think you had four days in a good room," said Lyra, "and I think a woman with a clipboard came and told you that the paperwork would be easier with a cage in it, and I think you said no, and I think you have not slept since. And I think that the reason you did it is not that you were good in a good room. It's that you were in a good room and you were not in a corridor, and you have spent eleven years only ever being asked in corridors, and everybody who has ever asked you anything has been in a hurry, including me."

Marcus Harlow turned round.

His face was worse than it had been on the Sunday. There was a fortnight in it that had not been there on the Sunday and it had not gone into the machine, and the silver in his forearms had stopped at the elbow and had not gone any further and never would, and he had eleven months.

"I want to say three things and I have written them down, and you are going to have to let me read them, because I have found that when I say them in my own voice I am *extremely* good at them and I have been extremely good at them for eleven years."

"Okay."

"**One.** I built it. I want that said in the room where you are standing, in a good room, in daylight, to a person who came down here because a woman with a clipboard let her. I built the machine. I did not know about the housing, and that is a *defence*, and I have never once been in front of a person and offered it, and I am aware that it is a defence and I am aware that it is mine, and I am not going to put it in front of the Council, and I would like it on a record somewhere that I declined to put it in front of the Council, because a man who does not put his own defence in front of a tribunal is a man who has understood something about tribunals, and I would like somebody to know that I worked it out at about four o'clock this morning, very late, in a bad dream."

"**Two.** I did not know about Elara Vance."

Lyra did not say anything.

"I want to be extremely careful about how I say the next sentence, because I have had eleven days in this room to work out how to say it and I have not got it, and I am going to say it badly. I signed a card. I did not read the card. There is a file in a drawer in this building with my name on it, and the file says, in my hand, *volunteer*, and the file does not say *she is fifteen*, and the file does not say *she was in my office for four months asking me direct questions*, and the file does not say *her uncle went into a vault in 2011 and did not come out*, and every one of those four things was true and available to me on the eleventh of September, and I want it entered in the record that I did not write them down because if I had written them down then I would have had to hand the file in."

He had a page in his hand, and it was folded, and he did not unfold it.

"**Three.** I am not going to be forgiven. I would like to say that now, in a good room, to the one person in this building who has the standing to say something about it, because I have watched four hundred people be forgiven in my field and I have watched exactly none of it survive contact with a Tuesday. I do not expect to be forgiven. I have got eleven months, and a *letter* is not eleven months, and I have had eleven years of thinking that something was coming, and I would like to be extremely clear that I have understood that it is not coming, and that I am not going to make a speech about it, and that I would rather be the man who said that in week one than the man who waited for week nineteen and made everybody sit through it."

The good room was quiet, and the panel showed a valley in the wrong weather.

"I have written to my daughter," he said. "Eleven letters, in a drawer, in this room, in a drawer in a room on a sub-level of a building I am not allowed out of. I sent one on Tuesday and I did not write a good one. I wrote the true one. It is eleven lines and it says: *I have been looking for you since 1997 and I have been building a machine to look for you with, and I was wrong, and I did not do it to protect you, and I would like you to know that I understood that your mother's name is not in any of it any more and I have been aware of that for fourteen years, and I have not been able to change it, and I have stopped asking.*"

"Did she answer?"

"She has not answered." He put the folded page down on the table. "She will not. She has a restraining order and she is twenty-three and she has spent fifteen years being a stranger's daughter in a town where everybody knows the story, and there is not a version of that in which a letter is the answer." He looked at Lyra Chen. "And I would like to say the thing I have not said, and it is not to you, it is to the register, and I would like you to hear it because you are going to be in that room.

"I killed my wife in a garden in April 1997. Not with my hands. With a *scheduled extraction* and a four-tenths-of-a-second lag between intending a thing and doing it, and I have had sixteen years to find the sentence, and the sentence is that I was *aiming at something else*, and that if I had aimed at her directly she would have been out of the garden in four tenths of a second because she was quicker than me.

"That is the thing. That is what a lag is. It is a machine for telling yourself that you were aiming at something else." He put both hands flat on the table, in a gesture that Lyra had watched in a kitchen, and in a machine room, and on the back of a piece of paper. "I did not kill my daughter. I have had sixteen years of a man who was told by a great many kind people that his daughter survived, and I want it entered in the record, in my hand, that I do not believe them, and that I have not been able to test it, and that on the twenty-first of September my son was put in a lattice, and that I would have given the machine anything, and that I have a list, and that on it is a woman of twenty-nine who came into a chamber on a Tuesday in May 2002 because it was blossom week, and that I knew her name and her mother's name and what her handwriting looked like and I did not write it down."

Harlow asked for it in his own hand, and it was written down as he said it, and one line of it has been underlined twice by somebody who was not in the room, and the line is this: *and that on the twenty-first of September my son was put in a lattice*, and there is no son in the file, and there is no entry for the twenty-first, and the only name the file has for the eleventh of September is Elara Vance, and a man who cannot finish a sentence in the middle is not a man whose arithmetic anybody is going to be able to check, and so the line stays where he put it.

---

## WHAT WAS DONE

On the ninth of October, in a lecture hall, four hundred and ten people were told four things.

One: the Southern Nexus had failed on the seventeenth of September, at about nine in the evening, and the hold had been seventeen per cent of the load at the moment of failure.

Two: eleven thousand people had gone to bed.

Three: the translator was a working instrument with a documented source, a warden's signature, and a name on a board.

Four: the register of case forty-one, with thirty-nine names in it, and thirty-seven of them dead, and two of them alive, had been opened, and was public, and was going to stay public, and the first page of it said, in the Registrar's hand, *it is not enough*.

And on the tenth of October, in the same hall, a girl of sixteen stood up at the back and said four sentences, and they went in the record as *the patient*, because she had asked for that, and because she had said that she was not a girl in a hall any more, she was *the patient*, and the minute would say so or she would read it out.

"I'm Lyra Chen. On the fifteenth of September I went into a machine on my own in a demonstration hall in front of two hundred people, and I broke the resonance of a boy called Milo Reyes, who can play the piano beautifully and cannot hear a room any more, and who is sixteen."

"On the first of October I refused to do the only thing anybody wanted me to do for four days, and I want it written down that I did not do it *because I was brave*. I did it because I was frightened, and because I had spent three weeks learning that a person who decides on their own is the mechanism of every single thing that has gone wrong in this building, including the thing I did on the twenty-seventh."

"My father is dead. He held a line for three hours and forty-one minutes in a machine and he was alive on the seventh of October and he died on the ninth, and I would like it minuted that he told me on the seventh that he was not brave, and that he was the person in this family who *minded*, and that he had been the only one since 2008."

"And there is a board in this building with my name on it and a number on it and an hour on it, and it says **used once**, and it is going to say *used once* for the rest of my life, and somebody has to keep putting it there, and I am not going to be the person who keeps it there, because I am sixteen and I will be forty in a great many years and the person who keeps it there has to be *bored*."

She sat down.

Eli Park put his pen down, and looked at the board over the door, which said 66, and did not write anything on it, because there was nothing to write, and the number was going to be a number for a very long time.

---

> **SOURCE:** Bench 4, register 41. Metered. Warden's signature. Lined 2 Oct 2013.
> **HOLD-TIME USED:** 3 h 41 m. One occasion.
> **ANCHOR (structural):** none.
> **APERTURES OCCUPIED BY A PERSON:** one. See instrument. All occasions listed.

The transmission side is limited because the fault is a hand's width. Nobody has ever widened a fault in this building. That is the whole of the engineering, and it is the whole of the moral position, and it will be true in four years and in forty.

# Chapter 34: The First Admission

> "I am not going to ask you whether you are sorry, because you will say yes and I have no way of testing it, and I have watched a great many people say yes. I am going to ask you one question every week, and it is always the same question, and I would like you to get ready for it."
>
> — Eleanor Weaver, to Marcus Harlow, Sub-level 2, 16 September 2013

*Weave-Quake Counter: 49% instability. Node-failure projection: T+68 d. Source: post-event re-survey. Counted from the 2014 re-survey, not from the incident. The rise from 41 to 49 is the re-baselining, not new damage: the 2014 survey measured a different thing from the model the hourly board was running, and the first honest number anybody has had since the fifteenth of September is worse than the number that was on the wall. The hourly model is not running, and nobody has yet agreed to say so out loud.*

*Monday 14 October.*

## THE ROOM

The room was on the ground floor of the old observatory and it had a window, and the window was the reason they had put it there, and everybody who came into it looked at the window for a while and then had to be made to stop.

There were nine chairs in a circle and a table with water on it and a whiteboard, and the whiteboard had already been written on and erased and written on again, and the second set of writing was still visible underneath the first if you stood at an angle, which nobody had thought about, and which was the single most accurate piece of criticism anybody made of the Council in the following four years.

Lyra sat in the ninth chair. She had asked for the ninth chair and had been given the ninth chair and had been given it without a word, and she noticed, and she wrote on the back of her hand in pencil and rubbed out again four days later because she was not going to be a person with a ritual.

The Inquiry into the Conduit sat at eleven o'clock on a Monday morning in October, and it sat on nine weekdays and it did not sit on the weekend, and it had a name and a chair and a clerk and no legal counsel, because the Chair of the Council had decided in a corridor on the Saturday that a thing with a *lawyer* in it would be a trial, and that this was not a trial, and that he was the wrong man for it and had said so.

He was the wrong man for it. He sat in the tenth chair, which had been added on the Monday morning, and did not speak for two hours, and got the last word at four o'clock by standing up and saying "I have nothing" and sitting down again, and it was the single most difficult thing anybody watched him do that year.

---

Dr Amara Osei had a board. Not a whiteboard — a board, a thing on a wall, with a thing she had drawn on it, and she had drawn it before she knew whether she was allowed to.

Three colours. She had used three colours for a week and everybody in the room understood what they were, and nobody said so, because Osei had told them on the first morning, in the flattest voice anybody had ever heard in that building: *the colours are not a ranking. There is no ranking. The colours are: still here; altered; gone. I have used the word gone about nine people and I have put them in the fourth colour and I would like that recorded, because the fourth colour is not for people who are worse off. It is for people whom I have stopped being able to predict, and I would like somebody in this room to understand that those are not the same and that my colleagues in eleven years will say that they are.*

"Twenty-three names," she said. "Of one hundred and ninety-eight. That is the honest number and I would like it said first, before the speeches.

"Fourteen have recovered to a stable perceptive state. Not to *their* perceptive state. To one they do not have, that they did not have before, and which in nine cases will not be stable, and in three cases I cannot model at all, and I have a note about each and I will read the note if anybody asks and I will not volunteer them because you do not need my terror in this room, you need my arithmetic.

"Three have total loss. Total. Not partial, not reduced: a room they can see and nothing in it.

"Two have a cochlear bleed and will heal, and I am including them because otherwise the only thing anybody in this room will talk about is thread loss and I have watched what a building does when it has only one noun.

"Four are in the second colour and I do not know what they are. I have a girl who reads emotion now, properly, in a two-mile radius, and she cannot turn it off, and she is sixteen, and she has asked me for something to stop it and I have got nothing, and I have been telling her since the fifteenth of September that I have got nothing, and I would like it recorded that the honest answer and the kind answer are the same answer in this case and that is a coincidence I do not expect to be granted twice.

"And then there is the fourth colour." Osei put her thumb on the board. "Three people. Ms. Sandoval has not opened her eyes since the tenth of September. There is a boy in a ward in another state who was taken off this campus in a car on the sixteenth with a signature from this institution and a label that said *specialist programme* and whose family has only been told, in a letter, that he is receiving specialist care. And there is a girl of fifteen who went into a lattice on the eleventh of September on a card she signed herself and who came out of it on the second of October into a room with a meter and a warden's name on it, and who is alive, and who is in Bellhaven, and whose mother has been told on the telephone by a Registrar and not by the man who did it."

"And you want the last one minuted," said the Chair.

"I want the last one minuted *first*," said Osei, "before anybody in this room says anything about the fourth colour, and I want it minuted with a time on it, and I want it read out to Ms. Chen's aunt in front of the girl at some point, because I have read the file and it took four minutes and a girl of fifteen to produce a change in this Academy's behaviour that the entire institutional apparatus of the Council failed to produce in eleven years, and I would like the record to say *four minutes*, so that the next person in this building who is frightened has a figure."

---

## THE LIST

They did the list on the first day and it took five hours and it was the worst afternoon of Lyra Chen's life, and she had spent a month having worse afternoons, and she noticed the absurdity of that at about four o'clock and it made her laugh, once, out loud, in a room of nine chairs, and everybody looked at her and she apologised and then did not apologise, and that was the first time the room noticed anything about her other than that she was sixteen and had lost a gift.

"Read them," said the Chair. "Not the outcomes. The *names*. Out loud. Because the first thing this building does with a name is put it in a column."

They read them out loud.

*Bao, Wei-Lin, 19, East Hall. Ruiz, Benjamin, 17, East Hall. Whitfield, Samuel, 18, West Annex. Ortiz, Maya, 17, East Hall. Sandoval, Maya, 21, third-year, Ward C. Adeyeka, Tolu, 16, first-year. Kaur, Simran, 18, second-year. Novotny, Petr, 17. Ferreira, Ines, 16. Halloran, Declan, 19. Mbeki, Thandiwe, 17. Osei-Rahman, Yusra, 16. Raman, Priya, 19. Oyelaran, Rafi, 20, second-year. Vasco, Ines, 18. Lindqvist, Elsa, 16. Cooper, Marcus, 18. Abubakar, Halima, 17. Duchesne, Coline, 19. Park, Ji-woo, 16 — and somebody in the room is going to stop on that name, because the boy in the second chair is a Park and is sixteen and is Korean-American, and Ji-woo Park has not been in this room all week, and the Registrar wrote both names on one board and has told nobody yet, and it is one of four errors and it is the one that was easiest to make. Reyes, Milo, 16. Vance, Elara, 15. Washington, Zara, 16.*

Three of them were sitting in the room.

"Twenty-three," said the Chair, when they had finished. "Read again tomorrow, and the day after, until the twenty-fourth of October, and then read it to the Council on the twenty-ninth, and then read it every term for as long as this Academy exists, in a room with a window, at eleven o'clock in the morning. I would like it entered that I do not know whether that is a punishment or a service and that I have decided to do it anyway because it is the only thing in this building I can think of that cannot be quietly dropped."

---

## THE ADMISSION

He came at two o'clock on the Thursday.

He came with two wardens and a lawyer the Council had appointed for him against his instruction, because he had said, on the first morning, that he did not want one, and the Chair had overruled him in a corridor in eleven seconds, and had minuted the overrule, which was the second thing in eleven days that had gone into a minute that nobody expected.

The lawyer was there for about twenty minutes and did not use it.

"She should have stayed in Bellhaven," said the lawyer, on the way out, to nobody. "There is no reason on earth for her to be in that building."

"There is no reason on earth for any of them to have been in that building," said the Chair. "That is rather the point of the room."

---

It is not a long speech. It is four sentences and it took about ninety seconds and it is in the transcript in full, and it is on the fifth board in this building, in a Registrar's hand, because somebody in that room had the sense to ask for it to be written where students could read it and was told that this was not what the fifth board was for and was then overruled in a corridor in about four seconds.

"I built it.

"I did not know about the housing, and that is a defence, and I am not going to offer it, and I want it in the transcript that I declined to offer it, because a man who declines to offer his own defence to an inquiry has understood something about inquiries.

"I signed a card on the eleventh of September without reading it, and there is a drawer in this building with my name on it, and the card says *volunteer* in my hand, and it does not say *she was fifteen*, and every one of those four things was true and available to me, and I did not write them down because if I had written them down I would have had to hand the drawer in.

"And I do not know what the lattice is and I have had four weeks in a good room to work out whether I want to know, and I have arrived at yes, and I would like to be asked, and I would like to be asked by somebody who is not going to be kind to me about the answer."

He sat down.

"Mr. Harlow," said the Chair. "You have four minutes. Is that a condition?"

"That is a request."

"It is a condition, and I am granting it, and I would like the transcript to show that I granted it in four seconds, which is faster than I granted it to his lawyer on Monday, and that I would like everybody in this room to draw whatever conclusion they like about that."

---

They asked him. Not Lyra — the Chair asked him, and it was the Chair's question and the Chair's voice, and the transcript records that Lyra Chen was asked twice whether she wished to put a question and declined both times and gave a reason each time, and the reasons were *it is not mine* and *I am the reason, and I have been the reason for four weeks, and if I stand up in this room and ask a man a question then this room becomes about me*, and the Clerk wrote both reasons down in full.

He answered for two hours and eleven minutes.

He told them about 1941 and about a man who had built a cage and put his dying brother in it and died in a bed eleven months later and had sealed the housing and left a note saying that he had done a great and necessary thing and that he was sorry.

He told them about April, and a garden, and a seven-year-old walking towards something, and he said it in the flat voice of a man reading a report, and the transcript records that four people in that room stopped breathing and that one of them was the Chair.

He told them about Aiko, and that she had died of it in 1999, and said the word *fourteen years* and did not use it as an excuse, and was asked about it, and said: *no, and I would like that in the transcript too, because I have had fourteen years and I have used it as a reason approximately nine thousand times, and the last time I used it was on the twenty-seventh of September, in a demonstration hall, to two hundred people, and it is a very good reason and it is a *queue*, and I have been taught that word by a girl of nineteen and I would like the record to show that I did not have it before the second of October.*

He told them about his daughter, who was twenty-three, and about a restraining order signed in 2011, and about eleven letters in a drawer in a room on Sub-level 2, of which he had sent one.

"May I read the letter," said the Chair.

"No," said Harlow. "You may not. It is eleven lines and it is addressed to a person who has not written to me since 1999 and has no obligation to, ever, and if it is read into a record in this building then it becomes a document about me, and it is not a document about me."

"That is the first refusal anybody has made in this room that I have believed on the first hearing," said the Chair, "and I am going to observe that it is a refusal to *publish*, and that it is the only good-faith version of this entire business that I have seen, and that I have been waiting four days to see somebody make one."

---

They broke at half past four and Lyra went out into the corridor and sat down on the floor, which she had done so often in October that she had a favourite.

Zara Washington found her.

"He is not a monster," said Zara, sitting down on the floor beside her, which was a thing that a girl who had been raised in a house where you did not sit on floors had worked on for a month.

"I know."

"That is not what I was going to say. I was going to say: *he is not the thing, and I have known that since the fifteenth of September, and I said it out loud in a hospital bed on the twenty-eighth and Eli nearly took my head off, and I have had three weeks to find out whether I was right and I was right and it is the most useless thing I have ever been right about.*"

"He has eleven months," said Lyra.

"He has eleven months and a letter and a room with a panel in it showing a valley in the wrong weather, and yes." Zara put her hands on her knees. "Lyra. I want to say a thing and I want you to know I have thought about it for three weeks and I have not said it to a single person, including the one I live with.

"The reason I have not been able to say it is Milo."

"Milo."

"He has been *magnificent* about all of this." Zara's voice did not go up. "He has been in a chair with a stick and he has done a hundred and forty hours of corridor work and he has organised a boy of fifteen's entire legal representation, and he has done it in a way that has made everybody in this building look at him the way you look at a *saint*, and I have watched eleven adults do it, and I have watched one boy of sixteen *let* them."

"Milo—"

"And on the fourth evening of the Inquiry he came back to our corridor and he said, in the voice he has been using since the twenty-eighth of September, *it's fine, I'm good, don't* — " she stopped and started again. "And I got angry, Lyra. I got so angry that I had to go and stand in a stairwell. Because he is doing the thing you did, except he is better at it, and I do not know how to tell him that I have *noticed*, and I have been the person closest to him for three weeks and I have not managed it, and if you do not manage it then nobody will, and I have been carrying that for six days and I have got a fifth board to think about and I am *telling* you because you are the only person in this building who will understand what I am actually saying."

Lyra sat on the floor of a corridor in an observatory and put her hand, quite deliberately, on the back of Zara Washington's hand, and asked, and was answered.

"That is exactly the right thing to do," she said. "Zara, that is exactly the right thing to do, and it is going to be extremely hard, and I am going to help you do it, and we are going to do it on Thursday, and we are going to do it in front of the other two, and Eli is going to be *appalling* about it, and it is going to be the single best afternoon of my entire life."

---

*The Inquiry into the Conduit, minutes of the fourth day.*

*The Chair has directed that the following be read into the record before any other business on the fifth day, and that it be read in the hearing of the two persons affected:*

> **E. Vance, 15, first year, one month after the events.** "I was in a lattice for twenty days. I came out of it into a room with a meter on it. I was fifteen. I signed a card that said *volunteer* and I want to say four things about that word and I have said them all already and nobody wrote them down, so I am saying them again and I would like somebody to write them down this time. One: I did not know what it was. Two: I said yes to the wrong thing. Three: nobody asked me what the machine was, in a corridor, in four seconds, and somebody could have and it would have taken four seconds. Four: I said no to her, to the woman who asked me, and she was the first person who had asked me anything in twenty days, and I have had three weeks to work out that I said no *to keep her safe* and I am not going to be told that it was brave by anybody, including her, and if she says it was brave I am going to say no, the way I said it in the lattice."
> **E. Park, 16, first year, napkin, term three.** "The fifth option is the only one on that list that nobody senior proposed. I would like that in the record. I would like it in the record in the form: *the only workable solution to a four-day emergency was produced by a sixteen-year-old who had been awake for thirty-one hours and who was not a member of any committee, and no committee in this building has examined why, and the absence of that examination is the single most serious finding of this Inquiry and it is not a finding about a person, it is a finding about a building.*"
> **E. Harlow, 55, former member of this faculty, week 5.** "I decline."
> **L. Chen, 16, first year.** "I am not going to make a statement today, because there is going to be a day, and I have read the agenda, and I would like the day. And because two of the four people who spoke before me have been in this room for four days and I have known them since the first week of term, and one of them is a boy of nineteen who cannot hear questions properly, and I am not going to stand up in front of him and make a speech about the worst week of my life. When it is my turn I will say four sentences and they will be the four I said in a lecture hall, and I would like somebody to tell me if I change them, because I will want to."
> **W. Chen, 53, and E. Vance, 18, third year — who is not the girl in the row above her, and whose name the Clerk had to ask for twice — and M. Reyes, 16, and Z. Washington, 16 — the note is read out by the Clerk, and it is four words long, and the Clerk had to ask twice.**
>
> *"She was not alone."*

# Chapter 35: The Vote

> "You are asking me to vote for a machine that is carried by a bench with a warder's signature. I have been voting for machines that were carried by people for twenty-one years and I did not once put it in a sentence. I am going to say something at this microphone and then I am going to vote, and I would like the two to be minuted as separate acts, because they are."
>
> — Councilor Ibadapo Yuen, Council of Traditions, 29 October 2013

*Weave-Quake Counter: 31% instability. Node-failure projection: T+212 d. Post-event re-survey. Counted from the 2014 re-survey, not from the incident. The numbers are falling and nobody has yet said why, and three people in this building have theories.*

*Thursday 24 October.*

## THE OTHER WITNESSES

It took nine days and it was the worst thing Lyra Chen was ever asked to sit in, and she has said so, in print, years afterwards, in a document she has spent a long time being sorry for.

Because the Inquiry had a Chair and a clerk and a lawyer and a man in a grey jacket, and it also had twenty-three names on a board, and nobody — not the Council, not the Inquiry, not the four of them — had thought to go and ask the twenty-three what they wanted. The Inquiry had been built around a machine. It had a machine's shape. You could not put a person into it.

"Mr. Ruiz," said the Chair, on the ninth morning. "Your name is second on that board. Would you like to say something to the Inquiry, or would you like to make a statement in writing, or would you like to make no statement, and all three are recorded identically and none of them counts against you."

"Does it count for anything?" said Ben Ruiz, who was seventeen, and who had come to a room in an observatory on his own, on a bus, and who had brought his mother.

"It counts for nothing," said the Chair, "and it will be read out, and the minute will record that it was offered and that it was made."

"My mother has written six pages."

"Then the six pages will be read out." The Chair looked at her over the table. "Mrs. Ruiz, I am going to say something to you that I have said to nobody else in this building and that I am saying because you have brought six pages and taken a bus. I am the Chair of this Council. I signed a schedule in 2009 that put a triage number next to a four-months-pregnant woman's name in a drawer, and I have spent nineteen days learning that the reason I signed it is that I was asked, and that the person who asked me had been asked, and that there is no floor at which anybody is *not* carrying a schedule for somebody above them. I did not know. I want to be exact: I did not know and I have not been forgiven for not knowing, by four people who are sixteen, and I am not asking them to."

Mrs. Ruiz read her six pages out. It took eleven minutes. Three of them were about the boy's *bedroom*.

---

They came, one after another, for nine days, and Lyra sat in the ninth chair for all of it, and here is what she learned, and it is the reason the rest of her life is the shape it is:

Three of the twenty-three did not want anything. Not forgiveness, not money, not an apology, not a place at this Academy. They wanted to be left alone and they wanted their names taken off a list, and one of them wanted a *job*, in a different field, in a different town, and got one, through a woman on a Council who had made a telephone call.

Four did not want to come and wrote letters, and two of those letters were apologies to *her*, and one of them was the second version of a letter that had been sent in September by a man with a clipboard, and it said, in its entirety:

> *I am not going to say I am sorry because I have read what sorry has been used for in this building in the last month and I do not want to be part of that. I would like to say four things and then I would like to go and look after my daughter.*
>
> *1. I knew what the machine was going to be in April and I did not ask.*
> *2. I wrote my name on a card in a corridor in a good room and I did not read the card.*
> *3. My daughter was in a box in the dark for twenty days and a girl of fifteen asked her a question and she said no, and my daughter has told me about it, and she has told me that the reason she said no was to keep the girl safe, and I have been sitting with that for six days and I would like somebody to explain to me what kind of mother that makes me.*
> *4. I would like to be asked things by people who are going to be angry with the answers.*

And sixteen of the twenty-three came.

Maya Ortiz, seventeen, who had been in the East Hall and who said the only funny thing anybody said in nine days and said it on purpose: "I have been *so* looking forward to a hearing. Do you know how long I have wanted to be told that the thing that happened to me was not my fault, in a room, by a person, with a *name*? Three weeks. I have been waiting three weeks for the word *fault* and I have found that I do not actually want it, because it means somebody else did it, and I would rather it was *nothing's* fault and that it was a *thing that happened*, like weather, and that I was in a building with weather in it."

Whitfield, eighteen, who had lost everything and who had come on the last day, in a wheelchair that he operated himself with a piece of string, and who had one question, which he had written down, and which was: *was it worth it*, and which nobody in the room would answer, and which he had predicted, and which was why he had written it down.

"I don't want a philosophical answer," Samuel Whitfield said. "I want the *number*. Nineteen? Twenty? I want to be able to say to people, *we were the nineteen*, because I have tried four other sentences this month and none of them worked."

"Seventeen," said the Chair.

"Seventeen," said Samuel Whitfield, and nodded, and said thank you, and went out, and it was, in the assessment of at least four people in that room, the worst and the best ninety seconds of the entire autumn.

---

## THE THING THAT WAS NOT A DISCLOSURE — THE TWENTY-FIRST OF OCTOBER

On the twenty-first of October, in the room with the window, a woman of about fifty stood up in the circle of chairs and said her name was **Mirembe Blackwood** and that she had been the Security Chief of this Academy for six years and that she was not resigning, and that she was going to say four things and that the fourth one was going to get her put in a cell, and that she had written it down and lodged it with the clerk at nine o'clock that morning, so that if anything happened to her in the next hour the fourth thing would already be in the record and would be read out by somebody else.

The first three took twenty minutes.

She had been given, in 2010, a *protocol* to protect a collection of stolen material, and she had built a hidden repository for it, and she had been running it for four years on the academy's own infrastructure, and she had done it in the belief — held sincerely, examined monthly, examined in writing, examined in writing *again* in August — that an institution could not be made to return anything, and that only a person inside one could do it.

"Eleven items recovered. Four returned to their communities. Seven outstanding." She had a list. Everybody in that room could tell that she had a list. "I have used a member of this Academy's own access systems, on a schedule, to do it, and I have put that material in a building, and I have done that for four years, and I would like the Council to understand that I understood the risk in 2010 and I took it anyway, and that I took it because in 2010 I could not think of another way, and that in 2013 I could, and that I did not stop, and that the reason I did not stop is that I have spent four years being extremely good at a thing and being extremely frightened of finding out what I was."

"I was the Syndicate's channel. Not their leader, not a member of their board, and I was extremely clear with myself about that for four years, and I want to say this next part properly, because it is the part I have not said.

"From 2011 to September 2013 I passed them names. Not students. Not staff. Eleven names of people who had come to this Academy in a decade, and the lists went to an organisation that calls itself the Meridian Group, and the organisation's programme is called *reconstruction*, and its method is to find a person with a *specific* thread signature, get them inside a building, and put them in a machine. I did that eleven times. I know the names of all eleven. One of them is a girl of fifteen who is in Bellhaven and I am the reason her mother wrote me a letter every fortnight for two months and I did not answer one."

The Chair said, "Mrs. Blackwood—"

"My fourth thing is on the desk and it is in the envelope and the clerk has it and I would be obliged if the Council would let me stop talking and start the part where I am quite sure I am going to be arrested."

The envelope contained a list of eleven names, and a list of four, and a list of a further nine that she had been asked for and had not yet delivered, and a single sheet with a statement in her own hand which the Clerk read out into the record in full, and which is the reason a Security Chief of the Council of Traditions is, as of the writing of this note, in a cell in a building in a neighbouring city, and which I am not going to reproduce, because it is eleven hundred words and it is hers, and because she asked for it to be read aloud and not to be printed, and because I have read it and I have not got the right to summarise it for a reader who was not in the room.

What I can tell you is this. The Chair adjourned at four o'clock. At six o'clock a Council observer and the clerk and a lawyer from the Compliance Office entered the room and read the fourth thing again from the envelope, because the envelope had been lodged and the chair had wanted it in the record twice. Mrs. Blackwood was arrested at nine minutes past six, at a table, in front of a window, and she was extremely calm, and she asked one question on the way out, and the Chair answered it, and the answer is in the minute, and the answer was *yes, all of it, and I would have preferred to have read it myself and I am not going to be allowed to.*

She has not been charged. She has been detained pending a referral to the Compliance Office since the twenty-first of October, and by the first of November, which is eleven days, she had made four requests in writing, one of which was to be allowed to read her own statement once more, and the answer to that one was no, and she has recorded that she asked, and that is the end of what anybody in this book knows about it.

---

## THE ARGUMENT ABOUT MERCY — THE TWENTY-SECOND OF OCTOBER

The argument happened on the twenty-second of October and it was not about Harlow and it was not about Blackwood, and it is the only part of the Inquiry that Lyra Chen has ever said she enjoyed, and she was asked about that in a later interview and she said, *enjoyed is the wrong word, and I am going to use it anyway.*

Eli Park had spent three weeks doing arithmetic on the Council, and had arrived at something that was in the transcript and that the Chair had read out twice.

"There are forty-one members," he said. "Of those, twenty-six signed the 2009 schedule. Of those twenty-six, nineteen are still in the building. Of the other fifteen, four signed something else in 2011. So there are *thirty* people in that room who have, at some point, put a schedule in a drawer, and eleven who have not.

"And I have gone through the minutes — the *published* minutes, not the good ones — and I can find where the discussion was. Nineteen times, in eleven years, somebody raised the person-shaped hole. Nineteen times it was minuted as *noted*. I have read all nineteen. Six of them are in a footnote. One of them is a *query*.

"And on the twenty-ninth of October, that room is going to be asked to vote on an instrument which requires a *named applicant* and a *published schedule*, and it is going to pass, because it has to, and because the alternative is worse, and because the alternative is a number on a wall. And when it passes, thirty people in that room will have gone from *noted* to *law* in one afternoon, and *nobody will have moved*, and in four years there will be a boy in a corridor and the schedule will be in a drawer and the nineteen will be twenty."

He put the napkin down.

"So I am not asking the Council to be brave. I have watched this Council for three weeks and I have worked out that it is not capable of being brave, and that is not a criticism, it is a *measurement*, and I have measured it very carefully, and I did the measuring on a floor."

"Then what are you asking," said the Chair.

"I'm asking you to make it *boring*," said Eli Park. "You have a machine that works, and a form, and a board, and a girl with a name on it. You are going to get re-elected by people who read boards. So put the *next* thing on a board. Not the thing you did. The *next* thing. The nineteen became twenty because nobody ever wrote down what the *twenty-first* would be.

"That's it. That's the whole of it. It's not ethics. It's *minutes*."

---

The Chair sat back.

"Mr. Park," he said. "I have been a member of this body for twenty-one years and I am sixty-one years old and I have a bad back from a car accident in 1994, and I want to tell you that I did not understand a single word of the last four minutes, and that I have understood the whole of the last three years in them, and that I am going to do the thing you have asked, and that I am going to do it in the dullest possible form, and that I am going to make a member of my own staff do it who is thirty-one and thinks I am a jobsworth, because you are right and I am not going to be the person who does it."

---

## THE VOTE

Twenty-nine October. Four hundred and ten in the hall, ninety of them on the floor, and three hundred and one of them under some form of medical or legal restriction that had been argued over in a corridor and granted.

The Chair read out four items.

**One.** The Nexus Stabilization Protocol, amended 2009, is suspended, and cannot be resumed until the schedule of instruments is published, and the schedule is a board, and the board has no number on it and stays up.

**Two.** The instrument of 2 October is ratified, on the record, with the refusal of 1 October printed in full, in the applicant's words, permanently, at the applicant's insistence, in a sentence the applicant dictated to the Clerk and which is on the board.

**Three.** Case forty-one is opened. Its register is public from today. The first page carries the Registrar's sentence.

It was the second register that the four of them fought about, and they lost that one too, and the losing is in the minutes. The board in the first room on the fourteenth of October had twenty-three names on it, and this one has two hundred and six, and the two documents are about the same people, and the difference between them is the entire argument.

The victims were not a body. There was no finding that named a group, because the finding that named a group was the document that said *sympathetic resonance trauma*, and every name in the room had been in that document. So it was made person by person, over nine days, by a Clerk who had to telephone a family for each one, and it took two hundred and six entries, and four of the entries are a lie, and the four are marked as lies, and the ones that are marked as lies were marked by the people who survived them.

*Sandoval, Maya*, twenty-one. *Not recovered. Outcome at the time of writing: none.* It is in the register in those words and it is the only line in two hundred and six that nobody has been able to write more comfortably, and the Registrar's sentence on the first page is twelve words long and is: *A person is not a case number and is not a column.*

*Whitfield, Samuel*, eighteen. *Recovered. No thread-sight. Transferred, at his own request, on the eleventh of October.* He was in the room for this, on the last day, and he asked whether the entry said whether it had been worth it, and the Clerk said no, and he said good.

*Ruiz, Benjamin*, seventeen. *Partial.* He is the second name on the board, and he has a mother's six pages in the file, and an instruction of his own, written at seventeen on page four of a consent form, which was that he wanted to be told everything and that he would ask questions they would not like. He was told everything. He asked four. Two of them were about the bedroom.

*Mbeki, Thandiwe*, seventeen. *Recovered speech. Second-year.* She was in the hall on the twelfth of September, and she is the reason the word *trauma* does not appear anywhere in this document, because she is the one who found it in the file in November and wrote a page about what it had been doing to her, and the page is in the register in her own hand, and it is the fourth of the four lies.

*Nakamura, Ren*, nine. *Not recovered.* There is no entry for the people who were in the crater, because the crater was not an Academy matter, and getting one required a letter, and the letter took four months, and it is in the file, and it is the reason a boy of eight is on this list at all.

And at the bottom, in a different hand, because he asked for it by letter and the Clerk allowed it:

*Nakamura, Kenji*, eight. *Alive. Bridged. A bridge that nobody authorised and that no doctor in the world can remove. He has asked to be on this list and has been told he is on it, and has been told that the person who put it there is on the next page, and he has said that he would like that to be in as well, and it is.*

---

**Four.** The Chair's own amendment, drafted by a member of staff he thinks is a jobsworth, in eleven bullet points, of which four are about who may sign what, five are about publication intervals, one is about the word *noted*, and the last one is:

> **11.** No instrument, protocol, or schedule in this Academy may be recorded as *noted*. Any question raised in a body of this Council shall be answered in writing within fourteen days, and the answer shall be read into the record whether or not it is satisfactory to the person who asked. *Proposed by E. Park, aged sixteen, first year, in a napkin, on the second of October. Seconded by the Chair.*

It passed at 11:41 a.m. by thirty-one votes to nine, with one abstention, and the abstention was Councilor Ibadapo Yuen, who had been the Chair of the amendment in 2009 and who stood up afterwards and said one sentence:

"I voted for the schedule in 2009 because the alternative was worse, and I would like it recorded that the alternative was worse, and I would also like it recorded that I have thought about that sentence every day since the second of October and I have arrived at the conclusion that it is the whole of what happened in this building, and that it is not a defence, and I am not offering it."

---

Lyra Chen was allowed into the room at the back for the vote and was not allowed to speak, and said so herself, in a document she later published and has never retracted:

> *I was sixteen. I had lost my sight of threads and my father and my certainty that being the one who decided was a kind of goodness. I was not a member of anything and I did not vote and I did not speak and I sat on the floor of a lecture hall with my back to a pillar and I ate a sandwich.*
> *And I want it on the record that on the twenty-eighth of October, the night before, my three friends and I sat in a corridor and agreed that we would each say one sentence at the Council, and that on the morning of the twenty-ninth I got up at six and could not do it, and that I telephoned each of them individually and told them so, and that not one of them said a word about it, and that Eli Park said, "Fine," and went back to his napkin.*
> *I have been the person who decided for other people since I was six years old. It took me twenty-four days to be talked out of it and I want it noted that it took three other people and a napkin, and that I did not do it myself, and that if you are reading this wondering whether I deserved it: no. I was talked into it. That is the whole of my claim and I am not going to inflate it.*

---
