Note added 2014: the counter fell from 49 to 31 between the fourteenth and the twenty-ninth of October, and the Council's technical officer has stated publicly that the fall is "consistent with a reduction in load," and Ms. Chen has written in the margin of the published version, in pencil, and left it in, and it reads: **ask her what load. That is the fifth board. That is the whole of it. Everything else in this building is a column.***

*Item 11 passed. On the fourteenth of November 2013 the first written answer was filed under it, and it is four words long, and it is addressed to a sixteen-year-old, and it says: **You were right. — I.Y.***

# Interlude: The Repair

> "A wound in the Weave is a wound in the world. It cannot be mended by one hand alone, nor by one tradition. True healing is a chorus, and a chorus is not a speech: it is eleven people saying slightly different things in the same room, and none of them being the tune."
>
> — Collected sayings of the First Council of Traditions, quoted without attribution by an eighty-one-year-old woman in a kitchen on Ellery Street, who said she had made it up, and who was believed

*Weave-Quake Counter: 22% instability. Node-failure projection: T+341 d. Counted from the 2014 re-survey, not from the incident. It is not published hourly and has not been since November. The Council's minute of 7 November is four words long: **It is not useful.***

*Wednesday 6 November.*

## WHAT A BUILDING LOOKS LIKE AFTER

Nobody had told Lyra what a day like this was like, and she had spent a month assuming there would be something to look at.

There was a great deal to look at. There was a queue at the registrar's desk from seven in the morning. There were two hundred and eleven people at the Nexus Concord at nine o'clock in the evening. There was a queue outside the room where the register of case forty-one was open to the public, and it was a queue of people who had come to read thirty-nine names.

"What are you all doing here?" asked Lyra Chen, to a man of about sixty in a car park, on the Thursday, genuinely, because she could not work it out.

"Reading the names," he said.

"Have you got somebody in there?"

"No." He looked slightly embarrassed, in the way of a man who has arrived at a building with a purpose and has been told it is not the purpose. "My wife's aunt. 1949. She was — nobody ever told anybody."

That was nine times on the first day, and Lyra counted, because she had not yet worked out that she was allowed to stop counting, and a man from a village four hundred miles away told her on the second day that she was the first person he had spoken to about it in sixty-four years and that he had got in his car and driven two hours for it, and that he was going to have to tell his wife, and that his wife did not know either.

The register is public. It is a bound book on a table in a room with a window, and there is a clerk, and anybody may read it, and there is a *second* book beside it in which a visitor may write — not names, not grievances, but the *details* that were lost: a date somebody remembered wrongly, a village nobody had written down, the name of a plant, a song.

"Eleven hundred and six entries by December," said the Registrar's deputy, in a report nobody asked for. "Nine hundred of them are wrong. That is not a criticism of the visitors. That is the finding. *The Academy's records for 1941 to 1953 are not merely incomplete, they are wrong in nine hundred places, and every one of the nine hundred is a place, and the record was written by people who had never been to the places.*"

---

## THE REPAIRS

There were nine, and they were all dull, and Eleanor Weaver gave them to a reporter in a corridor in about four minutes and then spent four months being disliked for them.

One: the schedules. Published. Weekly, in the same place, with the same format, whether or not there is anything to say, and a schedule that says *nothing this week* is a schedule.

Two: the Act. The Authority for the Care of Persons of Perceived Thread, section nine — the section that has been in force since 1974 and that has, in forty years, been observed eleven times out of eleven opportunities, in a building, in a demonstration hall, in front of sixty-one people, with no second responder and a rug — was amended in a fortnight. It is a short amendment. It requires a named second responder at any outreach; it requires the second responder to be able to say *no* to the first; and it requires the word *no* to be recorded whether or not it is subsequently reversed, which is the entire of it and which four hundred people had argued for in eleven years and which was got in a fortnight by a girl with a folder and a woman with a telephone.

Three: the Council. Yuen removed from the Council, and from the 2009 working group, and from every committee touching human subjects, and appointed, at his own request and over the objection of four members, to a body that reviews *appeals*, which has no powers whatever and meets in a room with a bad chair. He asked for it and the Chair said no and Yuen asked again in writing and the Chair, who is that man, granted it, and minuted that he had refused once first, so that the record showed that the request had not been a formality.

Four: the injury fund. Not named for anybody. Forty-one thousand two hundred and eleven accounts, opened in nine days, from an institution that has a *five-year renewable charter* and has had one for sixty years, and every renewal of which was signed without discussion. It is not a fund for the twenty-three. It is a fund for *whoever is next*, and Dr. Osei's single most quoted sentence in the next decade is in a footnote on page nine of a report and reads:

> *I have been a physician here for eleven years and I have treated four hundred and eleven people for a condition caused by the fact that somebody in this building was near them with a number. The fund does not treat anybody. It buys time. Time is what we did not have on the twenty-seventh of September and I would like somebody to remember, when they are tempted to make it a good press release, that the thing that was missing was not money.*

Five: the Sanctuary. Opened. Formally, publicly, in the room with the window, on the eleventh of October, by the Registrar, with a Security Chief two hundred miles away by video link, at her own insistence and on the Registrar's condition, that she had to be talked into using. Four of the seven items in Protocol Kheredu-Seven have gone back. Three are disputed and are going to a hearing in a country Lyra has never been to, and the Registrar has been made a party to it by a lawyer from that country who has read four pages of the register and then written a letter saying, *you have kept a hundred names and no stories, and we will give you the stories, and some of them will be difficult, and you will have to sit in a room with people who are not going to forgive you for seventy years, and you have to go anyway.*

Four hundred and nine people came to the opening. Two hundred and eleven to the Concord. And a man from a village four hundred miles away came twice, and left four entries in the second book, and the third of them was the name of his wife's aunt, which was not in the first book, and which is now in every copy.

Six: the Maze. The archway. Rebuilt, and it is the only thing in this book that anybody has ever got wrong twice, and it went wrong again in a way that is worth recording because the record is the point: the 2014 archway, built under Weaver's own instruction, put the Chinese knots back exactly as they had been in 1954, because that was what the 1954 drawings showed, and a first-year from the third week's course went and looked at it, and then went and looked at the left-hand post, and then found a woman of ninety in a care home in the next valley who had made the 1941 version out of silk and had kept it in a tin.

She had kept it in a tin for seventy-two years. It was four inches of silk and it took eleven months to make and it was *wrong on purpose*, in three places, and the third wrongness was the loose knot on the left-hand post, and when they asked her about it she said:

"My husband did that. In nineteen forty-one, with a knife, while he was making the other one, because he said the second one had come out too perfect and he was frightened of it. And then he died, and I kept the wrong one, and I have thought about that for seventy-two years, and I would like somebody to write down that a mistake made by a frightened man in one hour survived two wars and an institution and a demolition order and it is the only thing I have ever made that anybody looked at for seventy years."

The left-hand post of the archway of Threadweaver Academy is loose. It is deliberately, provably, documented loose. It is on the maintenance schedule. It has been on the maintenance schedule since 2014 and it is the single most visited object in the building.

Seven: the transcript. The entire Inquiry record, all nine days, published in full after the final sitting, including the four hundred and eleven pages that nobody had wanted. Four thousand copies went out in the first week, to every family on the board, and to every academy in the Council's list, and to eleven countries on request, and it has been required reading in four teacher-training courses and it is out of print and reprinted twice and it is, according to a survey in 2019, the second-most-cited document in the history of the Council after the 1974 Act, and it is a document about a machine, and a rug, and a card nobody read.

Eight, nine, ten, eleven: there were eleven, and by the end of the year there were nineteen, and by 2016 there were forty, and the Chair of the Council, who was then a different man, put the number on a wall in the vestibule of the Lumina Hall with no other explanation, and it is the only number in that building that is not a severity, and it is the one that visitors photograph.

## THE CONCORD

*Wednesday 6 November, nine o'clock. The Great Loom Chamber.*

They did it in the evening, which nobody expected, and in the Loom, which everybody did.

"There is a thing this institution is *for*," said Eleanor Weaver, "and it is not on a plaque anywhere in this building, and I have been Headmistress for six years and I have used the phrase *academic standards* four hundred times and I have never once used this one, and I am going to use it now and then I am going to hand the floor to somebody who has been in this valley for nine days and has not been to a staff meeting.

"The Loom is a nexus anchor. It holds this valley's share of the network. It has been holding it since nineteen forty-one and before that since the eighteen-seventies, and it has been holding it in the same way for a hundred and forty years, which is to say: alone, and at a cost, and with a lid on it.

"Two months ago something in this building did the arithmetic of a hundred and forty years in about ninety seconds, in a demonstration hall, and got it wrong by fourteen per cent, and there were four hundred people in the room. We are not going to be able to unlearn that. The only thing available is to hold it *differently*, and to be honest that we do not know what differently means yet, and to ask."

---

The first person to speak was an old woman in a wheelchair who had been at the back and had asked for no microphone, and who was introduced only as *Bà Sáu*, and who was eighty-four, and who had come from a house on a street in a city four thousand miles away, and who had not been asked to come and had come anyway, at enormous cost, in a wheelchair, through an airport, with a woman of nineteen who had held the door for her.

"There is no loom in my tradition," she said, in English, slowly, and the room understood every word of it and about half of the meaning, and there was a granddaughter beside her who translated the other half and would not be asked to translate it again.

"There are currents. There have always been currents, under this street and every street my family has lived on, and the oldest of them is under a market in a place you have never heard of, and it is not a *loom*. A loom is a machine that makes something. What is under the market is a *belly*. It is not making anything. It is carrying, and it has been carrying since before anybody here had a word for the carrying.

"Your Loom has been carrying alone for a hundred and forty years because somebody, in nineteen forty-one, made a lid. And your Loom is not *ill*. It is *load-bearing*, and there has never been enough of it in this room to say so out loud, and that is my whole contribution, and I am going to sit down now."

They did it in eleven parts and it took four hours and ten minutes, and Lyra was the last, and she was sixteen, and the Chair had to explain to the room who she was, and did it badly and apologised for it afterwards.

She did not describe a technique. There is no technique. She put her hand on a stone that had been part of the archway since 1781 and that was, as of 2014, on a maintenance schedule for being deliberately loose, and she said four things.

"One. I cannot see threads any more. I want that said first, every time, in every room, for the rest of my life, and I want it said by somebody else as well, and I would like it on the Concord record that the person who is doing this cannot do the thing she learned to do when she was six, and that she is doing it anyway, and that this is not inspiring and should not be reported as inspiring.

"Two. The current under this building is carrying about sixty per cent of a load and the other forty is on a bench with a warder's name on it, and eleven of us know that and nine hundred and ninety-nine other people in this valley do not, and we are going to keep that on a board, and that is the whole of the institutional part of this evening.

"Three. I am not going to say anything about communion. Everybody in this room has been using the word for two months and it has been used in a great many ways and about half of them have been ways of not saying a number. My mother wrote it in a notebook and then wrote, four lines later, *a system that requires a payment from someone who has not agreed is not a system, it is a queue.* Both sentences are hers and they are four lines apart and the four lines are the argument, and I would like the argument entered and the word left where it is, on a wall, being useful, meaning five different things to five different people including me.

"Four. There is a woman of eighty-four who came four thousand miles and has just told us that our central organ is a *belly* and not a *loom*, and that she is right, and that I have spent a month being taught that by a fifteen-year-old, and that I am not going to credit myself for it."

---

The Loom settled at about eleven o'clock. There is no other word and everybody who was there used it.

Osei, afterwards, to a reporter who was standing on the steps and who was clearly going to print the word *miracle*: "It went down nine per cent and it stayed down for four hours and then it went up one, which is within tolerance, and which is what I would have predicted, and which I predicted at four o'clock that afternoon in a corridor, and which nobody wrote down at the time, and which I have been slightly bitter about for a month and have now stopped being bitter about, because a miracle is not a miracle, it is a *measurement we did not make in time*, and if this building learns one thing from me in the next forty years I would like it to be that sentence, and I would like it on a board, and Eli Park can have the job."

---

## THE FOUR OF THEM

They did it in an anteroom, at half past midnight, because four teenagers who had not been in a room together without a crisis since September found that they did not know how to have a conversation any more and had to be told.

"Thirty per cent of our year group did not come back from August," Eli Park said. "That is a fact. It is a fact that will be on a form somewhere, and I have been avoiding it for four days, and I would like to say it and then I would like to talk about something else for a while."

Nobody argued with him about it. Zara put her hand flat on the table in a way that was the whole of her entire personality.

"Alexandria," said Zara Washington. "My mother's mother. I am not going to study *empathic resonance* — she has a word for it that has no English in it and I refuse to write it down in a Latin alphabet. I want to learn the thing that she learned, which is a set of household practices for *attending* to a person, and they have a name, and the name is not a discipline, and every programme in this building that I have been offered in the last three weeks is a discipline." She looked at the table. "And I want a word for what I can do. Because I can read the emotional weather of a two-mile radius and I cannot switch it off and Dr. Osei has got nothing, and I am going to spend my twenties learning to live inside a thing that is going to be there when I am forty, and I would like somebody to have a *name* for it that is not *complication*."

"Lyon," said Eli Park. "A formal place, a real supervisor, a two-year contract, and access to the Lyon lattice, which is the fourth one in the world, and which is in a car park, and which is the only one of the four that is still — " he stopped. "Which is still *in a car park*. Which is a thing I did not know was true until October and which I think about four times a day."

"And?"

"And when I get there I want to write it up," said Eli Park. "Not the machine. The *question*. Eleven people in this room have been asked to describe what they want in the next four years and nine of them said a place and two of them said a *method*, and I have noticed that the two who said a method are the two who have been given the least. I would like somebody to write up the noticing."

---

Milo Reyes did not say anything for a while.

"I am going to be an ordinary musician," he said. "I have made my peace with it and I worked it out on the fourteenth of October in about eleven minutes, which everybody thinks was a long time and was actually the fastest decision I have ever made, and I want it recorded that it was fast, because people keep giving me a *look* about it, as though I had chewed on a piano, and I have not, and I am not going to."

"That is not a decision," said Eli Park. "That is a *fact about you*. I am not being cruel. I am saying that those are different sentences and that since the first week of term I have watched about forty people write the second one down when they meant the first, including two adults who sit on a Council."

"Then here is the first sentence," said Milo Reyes. "I am going to be an ordinary musician, *and* I can still feel a room. Not hear it. Feel it. Which is not what it was and is not nothing, and it costs me about a headache every four hours and a stick, and I have been putting it in a log, and I have got fifty-two days of it, and I have got a *pattern*, and the pattern is that I am only good at it for about eleven minutes at a time and then I go stupid.

"And I want somebody to tell me what happens in the twelfth minute. Because *somebody* is going to write that down one day, and it is going to be in a drawer, and there is going to be a number on it." He looked round. "That is why I am telling you *now*, while I am sixteen, and while there is no instrument at all. Put it in the log. Put it on the board. There is no girl in a hole in this one. There is just a boy on a stick with a notebook and eleven minutes and a question, and I would like it entered that I raised it *myself*, in advance, and that nobody made me."

They wrote it down. All four of them, in four hands, on the back of a form, and it went into the record of the Concord at ten past one in the morning, under item nine, and there is a copy in a file in a building in this valley and there is a copy in a car park in Lyon and there is a copy on a piano in Alexandria and there is a copy in a corridor in a high school in Westbrook where two people who were sixteen in September now teach.

---

Lyra Chen did not say her own thing at half past midnight.

She said it on the fourth of November, in the room with the window, in eleven words, into a recorder, with the Clerk present, and she gave the recording to the Clerk and not to the four of them, and Eli Park did not know about it for six weeks and was extremely angry for about a minute and a half and then was not.

"Do you want me to read it back?" said Mila Rowntree, who had been the Clerk to that Inquiry for nine days and the student advocate for five weeks, and who had by then learned considerably more about minutes than about threads.

"No," said Lyra Chen. "That's the point. Nobody reads it back. That's what you write a minute for."

---

*The room with the window, 4 November 2013. Recorded, with the consent of the speaker.*

> *I am going to live at home for a year and then I am going to go and sit in a lecture hall in a foreign city with a folder and a napkin and say nothing for about nine minutes while somebody else who is younger than I was in September does the talking.*
>
> *I am not being modest. I have worked out that I am extremely good at three things and all three of them are the same thing, and the thing is deciding.*
>
> *And I am going to learn to do a fourth, and the fourth is going to take about forty years, and Eli Park has told me that the technique is that you turn up on Fridays, and that it is boring, and that it is the whole of it.*
>
> *I would like the last thing in the record to be a name that is not mine.*

---

*The Clerk, in the margin of the minute of the meeting of 4 November, and the Clerk's name is on the record of every day of that Inquiry and of two of the Concord sessions, and she was twenty years old for the last of them:*

> *The speaker asked for the last item to be a name and not hers, and there were five people in the room, and the Clerk has read the names aloud, and the speaker has chosen.*
>
> *It is: **M. Reyes, aged 16, who raised a question about his own condition on the fourth of November, unprompted, in advance of any instrument, and asked for it to be written down where anybody could find it.***

# Chapter 36: The Thread Continues

> "Sight is not a thing you have. Sight is a thing you are doing, in a particular language, at a particular distance, with a particular cost. My daughter has lost one of those. I have spent forty years explaining to people that this is not a tragedy, and I have been half right, and being half right is the most common condition in this building."
>
> — Anmei Chen, in a kitchen on Ellery Street, to a woman from a magazine who had come to ask about silk and stayed for the tea

*Weave-Quake Counter: 11% instability. Node-failure projection: T+402 d. Counted from the 2014 re-survey, not from the incident.*

*Friday 8 November 2013.*

## THE MEMORIAL

They did it in the Great Hall, and they took the podium out, which was the whole of the design and the whole of the argument, and about forty people had a bad night about it and about eleven had a good one.

Seventeen thousand words had been read into a record in the Great Hall. A hundred and forty people had spoken. The Council had met in the Lumina Lecture Hall with ninety people on the floor.

And on the eighth of November they moved eleven chairs into a circle in the middle of the Great Hall and sat down in it, and the thing that happened was that a man of sixty-one with a bad back from a car accident in 1994 said, for about eleven minutes, the names.

Not all of them. She read the twenty-three, because those were the ones in the room, and then she read a further one hundred and eighty-three names, at a measured pace, in a hall with a barrel-vaulted ceiling that has a great deal of silver thread in the stone, and it took forty minutes, and nobody in that hall of nine hundred moved for more than about eleven seconds at a time, which is what a human being can manage, and which is the entire physiological budget.

The last name she read was a man called **Kenji Nakamura**, aged eight, of no fixed address, of Hokkaido, who had been in a crater three point two kilometres from that building on the fourteenth of September and had come out of it, and whose sister had not.

"There is a girl in the third row," said the Chair, afterwards, "who was in a hole in a wall under this building — one of the thirty-nine, and she is the reason the four in the second housing were ever counted separately at all, because when the Council made its figure of forty-three it had added a lattice to a housing and nobody had asked for the four — and who is alive, and whose mother has been told, and who is fifteen. She has asked to speak and I am going to say something I have never said at one of these and I am sorry, Ms. Vance, I am not going to let you."

"That's twice," said Elara Vance.

"Yes," said the Chair. "Twice."

---

He did not speak. He sent a letter, and the Clerk read it out, and it was nine lines long and the second line was this:

> *I am not going to thank anybody because the only person who has ever asked me anything was sixteen and she did it by accident. I want to say that my mother has been to a place called Ellery Street in another state and that a woman of eighty-one made her tea and asked her what she needed, and it was not a thing anybody in this building did, and I have thought about that for two months and I have decided that it is the correct response to what happened to me and that nobody in an institution can do it and that the fact that it took a grandmother eleven thousand miles away is the actual finding of this entire Inquiry and I have written it on the board myself.*

## THE HOUSE

Her father had written, in February of 1996, on the back of a form — a *form*, at their kitchen table, with a pen — this:

> *L., if I am not here: do not sell the house on Hậu Giang. Do not let anyone "arrange" it. Do not have it cleared out. The room at the back is the one. Ask N. about the room at the back and do not ask her in front of anyone. A.H.*

Anmei Chen had not asked him about the room at the back. She had told her granddaughter about it in April, in a kitchen, in about nine seconds, and had then spent four days not being able to say anything else about it at all.

The room was nine feet by eleven and it had a window with a grille and a stone floor and a smell that had survived the demolition order, the war, and four decades of tropical damp, which was linseed oil and turpentine and something else that Lyra had never been able to name and that her grandmother identified, on arrival, in about four seconds, by walking in and standing still.

"Turpentine and the resin off the leaf," said Anmei Chen. "Your grandmother's grandmother used the leaf on a burn. It is in the wall because I could not take it out. The wall is in the wrong place now. Everything in this room is in the wrong place. Sit down, you are all standing up like visitors."

The room is not a school. Lyra Chen has not decided what it is going to be and has written on the door, in pencil, in a hand that took six weeks to get right: *what is in this room: a person, a place, and what has happened between them, in that order. what is not in this room: a technique, a specialism, a modality, a spectrum, a category, or a number.*

The line under it is her father's, and it is not translated, and it is the only line in it she has ever asked anybody to leave alone: **what you will be examined on is whether you can be wrong in front of somebody.**

She has not worked out yet who examines people in this room, or how often, or for money. She has got as far as a pencil, and a waiting list of people who want to sit on the floor of it, and no decision at all.

---

She was nineteen, and she was Kenji Nakamura's cousin, and she has a name, which is **Kailani Akana**, and she has asked twice to be called something else and has not been asked twice, and that is a thing the four of them noticed and did not fix, and one of them has written down that she noticed.

She has no gift, no training, and no certification, and a cousin of eight with a diagram on his forearms who is alive, and a sister who is not in this book.

Kailani Akana has asked twice to be called something else. Nobody has asked her twice. That is a thing the four of them noticed and did not fix, and one of them has written down that she noticed, and the writing down is the entire remedy available to them at present.

## THE CHARM

*Saturday 18 January 2014. Ellery Street.*

Her grandmother gave it to her in a kitchen on Ellery Street, in a tin, and it was a knot of red silk wound and re-wound so many times it had gone into a shape like a closed fist.

"It is four hundred years old or it is a piece of red silk from a shop, and I have never been able to establish which and I have stopped trying, and that is the last piece of my own mother's business that I will hand on and I am handing it on because I am eighty-two and because the person it belongs to is you.

"It was made by a woman in a lane in Chợ Lớn in nineteen forty-three, on a night when the rice was being taken, and her granddaughter brought it out and it came up the line and it came out of Saigon in a suitcase in nineteen seventy-five, and it has been in a tin ever since, in a kitchen, for forty years, in a house that was not even ours.

"There is no enchantment in it. I want to say that, because you are going to think there is. There is no enchantment in it and it has never once done anything, and I have kept it for forty years because a woman I loved held it in her hand on the night she came to tell me she was going to do something stupid and brave, and because it is *proof* — and this is the whole of it, and I have had four generations to get to this and I have not found a better sentence — it is proof that somebody, a very long time ago, in a completely different century, with no idea at all what was coming, made a thing out of red thread and then *wound it up tight and knotted it* and put it in a tin.

"That is the whole craft. That is what your great-grandmother did, and she did it in a night of rice and fear, and she did it for no reason that anybody has ever been able to write down.

"She made a knot to remember that she had made a decision. That is all a tradition *is*, Lyra. It is a knot somebody tied to remember that they tied it. It is not a technique and it is not a power and it is not wisdom. It is a *receipt*." Her grandmother's hands were not entirely steady, and she dealt with it, as she had dealt with everything since 2008, by being extremely practical about the next thing. "Which is why I have taught you to keep minutes, and why the boy with the elbow keeps minutes, and why the woman in the observatory kept the best minutes in the building for eleven years and did not know anybody was reading them."

"Unknot it," said Lyra.

"Unknot it," said Anmei Chen. "Slowly. And do not throw it away, and do not put it in a museum, and do not give it to a student. Give it to whoever cleans the room. Give it to the person who does the floors. That is what it is *for*, and if you cannot see that then you have not understood your great-grandmother and you should read her one more time, and she is in the red book on the top shelf, in Vietnamese, and you will not be able to read it, and I am eighty-two, and one day somebody will have to teach you."

---

She unknotted it over the course of nine minutes on a kitchen table on Ellery Street, and nothing happened, exactly as her grandmother had said, and at minute six the silk came apart into four separate threads and one of them had a knot in it that had been there for four hundred years, and that one she kept, and the other three she put in a tin and gave to the woman who cleans that floor, whose name is **Nadia Wanjiru**, and who was in the room.

That is the last fact about the silk that this book contains, and it is the correct one.

## THE SEVEN WEEKS

They all left on the same day, which was not planned and which everybody has since described as the single most embarrassing thing about the whole of it.

Eli Park left on the six o'clock train from a station two hundred miles away, with a case, a napkin file, and a legal pad, for a car park in Lyon, where somebody had offered him a desk and not a job.

Zara Washington left for Alexandria, in a house with a courtyard, to learn a set of household practices for attending to a person which has a name in Arabic and which she has refused, four times, to write down in a Latin alphabet, in a document she signed and which is in a file in a building in this valley.

Milo Reyes left for a conservatory, on a stick, with fifty-four days of a log and a question about the twelfth minute, which is unanswered, and which is on a board in two countries, and which nobody has yet picked up.

And Lyra Chen went to Hậu Giang Street.

---

She went because there were eleven people in a house on a street four thousand miles away who had been, for four generations, the only people in the world who knew how to do the thing her great-grandmother had done on a night in October 1943, and who had been described, in an institutional record, as *a practice with no measurable outcomes*, and who had a waiting list of forty, and who were seventy-nine and eighty-one and eighty-four years old.

She went because her grandmother had said, on a Sunday in a kitchen in Ellery Street, in the voice she used for things she had decided: "You are not going to come back here and run a school. You are going to go and sit in a room with three old women and a well and you are going to be the *fourth thing that is difficult*."

She went because she could not see threads any more, and had not been able to for three months, and had stopped waking up at four in the morning about it in about the second week of November, and had never once described that to anybody.

That is what this book knows about her, and it stops there.

---

*Friday 8 November 2013, 4:40 p.m. The valley.*

The bells went at four forty in the afternoon, because that is when they go, and because a ceremony that finishes at noon is a ceremony about a building.

Lyra Chen stood in the colonnade and let them ring and did not try to work out what she felt, because she had discovered in October that she could not, and that not being able to was a *skill* she had acquired, and that she was extremely proud of it in a way she suspected was not entirely healthy, and that she was going to work on it for about forty years.

Milo Reyes was in a corridor with fifty-four days of a log.

Eli Park was on a train.

Zara Washington was on a telephone in a car park, laughing about something, and would be on a plane in the morning, and neither of those facts was a metaphor for anything.

Dr. Mei-Hua Chen was in her office with a book open in front of her that she had not read in four years and was reading now, and it was her sister-in-law's, and it was the fourth volume, and it was the one with the map in it.

And in a good room on Sub-level 2, a man of fifty-five with eleven months and a letter that had not been answered was watching a panel that showed a valley in the wrong weather, and had been watching it for fifty-four days, and had asked, in the last week, whether it could be fixed, and had been told, kindly, by a woman with a very good hand, that it could not, and had said: *I know. I have been reading it for two months as though it were a discipline. It is a panel. Somebody put it there because I was going to be in a room.*

---

*Saturday 9 November 2013. 7:15 a.m. The east corridor, by a door, which is not a metaphor.*

There was a note on it in Lyra Chen's handwriting, in a hand she had been practising for six weeks under the direction of a nineteen-year-old Clerk who had told her, on the first attempt, that it looked like a *diagnosis*, and on the fourteenth attempt that it looked like a person, and had then said, "That one," and gone back to her minutes.

It said:

> *Ask somebody. — L.C., and M. Reyes, and E.P., and M. Rowntree, and Z.W., and E.V., and J.C., and M.S., and B.R., and S.W., and R.O., and P.H., and the twenty-two other people whose names are on the board.*

And underneath, in a different hand entirely, in a nine-year-old's capitals:

> *I ASKED SOMEONE.*

# Epilogue: The Thing That Is Not Finished

> "A cord laid along the load will hold. That is not a metaphor and I would like it on the wall in plain language, because the plain language is what people read at eleven o'clock in the morning while they are waiting for a desk."
>
> — Bà Sáu, aged eighty-four, in the Great Loom Chamber, 6 November 2013

*Weave-Quake Counter: 4% instability. Node-failure projection: not published. There is a board. On the board there is a number, and the number is not a severity.*

*Two years later.*

## THE ELEVEN MINUTES

She had got very good at the eleven minutes and she had never once been able to explain it to anybody, and the two people she tried hardest to explain it to — Eli Park, in a letter from a car park in Lyon, and Zara Washington, on a telephone from Alexandria — had both written back the same thing, in different words, and she had framed both letters and hung them in a room four thousand miles away.

Eli Park's said: *I think it is because you are the only person in the building who is not looking at a number. I have watched you do it four times now and every time somebody reads a figure you wait until they have finished and then you say one sentence. I think you learned it from your grandmother counting your breaths and I think you learned the *reason* from a woman in a corridor in eleven minutes, in October, who spent all eleven of them apologising for things that were not her fault. You are extremely rude to figures. It is your best quality.*

Zara Washington's said: *You are not calm. I want to be extremely clear that I can feel what you actually are from eleven hundred miles away through a bad telephone line, and what you actually are is a person having an enormous opinion about a room, continuously, at a volume you have trained yourself to keep under your breath. It is not calm. It is containment. Those are different and you have been pretending they are the same for two years and the pretending is what the eleven minutes is.*

Lyra Chen wrote back and said that both of them were wrong, and that Eli was right about the numbers, and that Zara was right about everything else, and that she had known Zara was right since about December and had not said so because she was eighteen and it was convenient.

---

The thing that is not finished came to her in a corridor, in October 2015, at about eleven o'clock in the morning, and it came to her in the form of a *scheduled* item, which is the only form in which anything in that building has ever arrived that she did not already know about.

There was a girl on the second floor doing a practical, and the girl was fifteen, and she was doing the eleven minutes.

That was the whole of it. That was the entire discovery, and it took Lyra Chen four and a half seconds, standing on a stair landing, and what she felt was not recognition, because the girl was not like her, and the girl did not resemble anybody she had known. What she felt was *vertigo*, of a kind she had only felt twice before, both times in that building, both times in a corridor, and both times immediately before a great deal of trouble.

The girl was fifteen and she was doing the eleven minutes, and Lyra Chen stood on a stair landing and understood with total clarity, in about four seconds, that **it is learnable**.

That is the horror. That is the entire horror of the second half of her life, and she has said so, in print, more than once, and it is the sentence she is most quoted for and the one she most wishes she had not said:

> *It is not a gift and it is not a gift, and I have said that in eleven countries and I am going to say it again. It is not rare. It is not special. It is a* skill, *and it is taught, and it is taught badly, and it is taught to children of fifteen by teachers who have been told by a doctor that it is not a gift because a doctor in a corridor in September needed a number, and the number was wrong, and the number went into a system, and the system has been running for two years, and it has produced eleven children in two countries who can sit in a room and hold their nerve and say one thing at the end of it, and every single one of those eleven children is going to be offered something.*

She went down the stairs and said it out loud to a person of twenty-six in a corridor, and it took four minutes, and the person of twenty-six was a former student of hers who had a schedule and a supervisor and a small amount of money and a great deal of charm, and who was, at that moment, eleven days into a nine-year plan to go and sit in a room in a foreign city with a folder and a napkin.

"I am not asking you not to do it," she said. "I did it. I have the board. I want you to understand exactly what you are being told and exactly how little I am asking."

"Then what are you asking?"

"I am asking you to look at the item number on the sheet you are going to hand in, and to notice whether anybody has asked the girl what she would like to be called on a form."

---

## THE LETTER

It came in the last week of November 2015, to a house on Hậu Giang Street, and it was forwarded by a solicitor in Lyon. The girl who sent it was fifteen, and she had found the address on a form in a building in Westbrook and had had the extraordinary luck, or the extraordinary *sense*, to ask somebody in that building whether the address was right, and had been given the address by a woman of eighteen who worked at a desk and who had thought about it for four days before she did it.

It was nine lines, and not one of the nine was hers.

The words were a man's. He had had twenty-four days in a good room and a bad back, and he had been working through them one at a time, and he had written nine lines, and he could not send them, because every route to that address ran through an institution that had a file on him and would have opened it. So he had asked a fifteen-year-old to write them out for him, and he had taught her the hand first, which took four days, and the hand was the only part of the letter that was hers. It was in a hand that had been taught to write by a man with silver scars up both forearms and a limp he does not entirely have and did, in 2013, have entirely.

> *I did not read your book. I am not going to. I have four days and I am going to spend them on the fourth line, so I will write the other three first so that they are out of the way.*
>
> *1. I was wrong about a great many things and I have had twenty-four days in a good room and a bad back to work through them and I have got to three of them and I am not going to write down the other forty-one because the minutes exist and Ms. Rowntree keeps them and I have asked her for a copy of the whole file and she has said yes, which I did not expect, and I am going to read it in January when she sends it and it is eleven hundred pages.*
>
> *2. You were right, in August, in a demonstration hall, about the four tenths of a second, and nobody in this building has ever said so out loud except a sixteen-year-old with a napkin, and I would like somebody to have said it to me, so: you were right, and it was the only true thing anybody said to me that day, and I was in the middle of doing the other thing at the time.*
>
> *3. I have read the register of case forty-one. All of it. It took two evenings and I had to stop on the first one and take something for it, which I want recorded because I have spent twenty-four days finding out that the reason I could not sleep was not grief.*
>
> *4. I want to write to you about the eleventh of April 2002. There is a map in the third volume and it is not what I thought it was for eleven years and I have had it in a drawer in a good room and I have taken it out, and I would like to tell you what I have found, and I would like to know whether you would want to know, and I am asking rather than assuming, which is a thing I have been told I have never once done in my life and which I am going to start on a Tuesday in a house in another country with eleven lines and no heading.*
>
> *Do not reply to this address.*

---

She replied to the solicitor in Lyon.

Four lines, and the fourth one was not an answer to the question, and she has never explained that to anybody, and the letter is in a tin in a kitchen on Ellery Street with three pieces of red silk in it.

> *Item 4: yes. I would want to know. I am eighteen and I have no gift and I have spent a month standing on a stair landing understanding that a thing I learned at fifteen is being taught, and I would like to know who taught it, and I would like to know whether they were taught, or whether they were found, and I think those are different, and I think it is the whole of the difference and I think it is worth an island nobody has looked at.*

She did not, in the end, go to the island.

She went, in the end, to a house on a street in a city of eleven million people, to a room nine feet by eleven, to nine years, and she was there in March 2016 and she is there still, and there are eleven students in the room and a waiting list of forty and three old women and a well, and the well is not a *nexus* and the wall is in the wrong place and the smell of turpentine has got into the mortar and will, according to a conservator from Lyon, outlast everything else in the building.

*2017.*

And there is a map in a tin, and there are nine lines in a letter, and there is an island that does not appear on most charts, and the charts that have it disagree with each other about its position, and nobody has been there since a woman of thirty-two stood under a stone archway on the eleventh of April 2002 and tried to work out where a thread dimension *converges* rather than where anybody had decided to count it.

And in a room in a valley, there is a board, and on the board there is a number, and the number is not a severity, and the number has been eleven since 2013, and nobody has worked out yet whether eleven is a good number or a bad one, and a man of twenty has written on the margin of the published figure, in pencil, and left it in:

> *Ask somebody. I asked. It took four years. It is not the same as the number going down and I would like somebody in this building to stop suggesting that it is. I said the same thing at sixteen and nobody heard me then either. — E.P.*

---
